Luke — Saints Holy Bible cross-references
1137 completed source verses; 1148 retained pairs; 1149 source units read; 12 deferred units.
First unfinished: Luke 1:21. Next new source review in this book: none. All original source units in this book have been read. Overall next new source: 1 Corinthians 8:1.
Completed means an initial curated cross-reference pass: the complete supplied English source unit and each retained target passage were read, and each pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean exhaustive coverage or independent specialist approval.
Original Saints numbering is retained.

Luke 1:1
Since many have undertaken to compile an account of the events fulfilled among us,
  Acts 1:1 — thematic parallel: Both opening notices refer to composing an account. Luke mentions multiple prior undertakings concerning fulfilled events; Acts addresses its own prior account of Jesus' deeds and teaching. This is a literary-preface comparison, not a claim that Acts identifies every account Luke mentions.
    Acts 1:1 In my first account, Theophilus, I wrote about all that Jesus began both to do and to teach

Luke 1:2
just as those who from the beginning were eyewitnesses and servants of the word handed them down to us,
  John 15:27 — thematic parallel: Both connect testimony with people present from the beginning. Luke describes transmitted eyewitness testimony and service of the word; John records Jesus telling the disciples that they testify because they have been with Him from the beginning.
    John 15:27 And you also bear witness, because you have been with Me from the beginning.

Luke 1:3
it seemed good to me also, having followed everything carefully from the beginning, to write an orderly account for you, most excellent Theophilus,
  Acts 1:1 — thematic parallel: Both address Theophilus in connection with a written account. Luke describes careful investigation and an orderly account; Acts refers to the first account about Jesus. The connection uses the linked addressee-and-account context rather than a loose match of a personal name alone.
    Acts 1:1 In my first account, Theophilus, I wrote about all that Jesus began both to do and to teach

Luke 1:4
so that you may know the certainty of the matters in which you have been instructed.
  1 John 5:13 — thematic parallel: Both writers explain their writing as enabling readers to know something with confidence. Luke concerns certainty about received instruction; John concerns believers knowing they have eternal life. The subject matter and intended assurance are not treated as identical.
    1 John 5:13 I have written these things to you who believe in the name of the Son of God, so that you may know that you have eternal life.

Luke 1:5
In the days of Herod, king of Judea, there was a priest named Zechariah, of the division of Abijah. He had a wife from the daughters of Aaron, and her name was Elizabeth.
  1 Chronicles 24:10 — allusion: Luke identifies Zechariah with the priestly division of Abijah; Chronicles lists Abia as the eighth priestly allotment in its surrounding division list. The name forms remain as supplied, and this does not identify Zechariah with an unrelated prophet of the same name.
    1 Chronicles 24:10 the seventh for Kos, the eighth for Abia,

Luke 1:6
Both were righteous before God, walking blamelessly in all the Lord’s commandments and ordinances.
  Philippians 3:6 — thematic parallel: Both describe blamelessness in relation to observance of the law. Luke praises the couple's conduct in the Lord's commandments and ordinances; Paul describes his own former standing concerning righteousness in law. The persons and the surrounding arguments differ.
    Philippians 3:6 with respect to zeal, persecuting the Church; with respect to righteousness in law, having become blameless.

Luke 1:7
They had no child, because Elizabeth was barren, and both were advanced in years.
  Genesis 11:30 — narrative parallel: Both describe a marriage without children because the wife is barren. Genesis names Sara; Luke names Elizabeth and additionally specifies the couple's advanced age. The two couples are not identified as one.
    Genesis 11:30 And Sara was barren, and bore no children.

Luke 1:8
While he was serving as priest before God in the order of his division,
  1 Chronicles 24:19 — allusion: Luke's priest serving in his division corresponds to the ordered priestly ministry entering the Lord's house described in Chronicles. Chronicles supplies institutional background, not a report of Zechariah's particular service.
    1 Chronicles 24:19 This was their inspection according to their ministry, to enter the Lord’s house according to their ordinance through Aaron their father, as the Lord, the God of Israel, had commanded.

Luke 1:9
according to the priesthood’s custom, he was chosen by lot to enter the Lord’s sanctuary and burn incense.
  Exodus 30:7–30:8 — allusion: Exodus prescribes the priestly burning of incense before the Lord morning and evening. Luke narrates Zechariah's incense duty in the sanctuary; the Exodus passage does not establish Luke's separate lot-selection detail.
    Exodus 30:7 Aaron shall burn finely ground compounded incense upon it morning by morning, when he tends the lamps.
    Exodus 30:8 When Aaron lights the lamps in the evening, he shall burn incense upon it, a continual incense before the Lord throughout their generations.

Luke 1:10
The whole multitude of the people was praying outside at the hour of incense.
  Psalms 140:2 — thematic parallel: Both associate prayer with incense before God. Luke describes people actually praying during the incense hour; the psalm likens prayer to incense and raised hands to evening sacrifice. It does not describe Luke's crowd or fix its hour.
    Psalms 140:2 Let my prayer be directed as incense before Thee, the lifting of my hands as an evening sacrifice.

Luke 1:11
An angel of the Lord appeared to him, standing at the right of the altar of incense.
  Judges 13:3 — narrative parallel: Both birth-announcement settings begin with an angel of the Lord appearing before the promise of a child. Judges addresses a barren woman; Luke addresses the priest Zechariah by the incense altar. Judges does not supply the altar or its right-hand position.
    Judges 13:3 And the messenger of the Lord appeared to the woman and said to her, “Behold, you are barren and have borne no child; but you shall conceive and bear a son.

Luke 1:12
Zechariah was troubled when he saw him, and fear fell upon him.
  Luke 2:9 — narrative parallel: This same-book comparison links fear at an angelic appearance in two infancy scenes. Zechariah fears when he sees the angel; the shepherds fear at the angel and surrounding glory. The people and scenes remain distinct.
    Luke 2:9 An angel of the Lord stood beside them, and the Lord’s glory shone around them; they feared with a great fear.

Luke 1:13
But the angel said to him, “Do not fear, Zechariah, for your petition has been heard. Your wife Elizabeth will bear you a son, and you shall name him John.
  Genesis 17:19 — narrative parallel: Both announce that a named wife will bear a son and give the child's name in advance. God names Isaac for Abraham and Sarah; Luke's angel names John for Zechariah and Elizabeth. The promises and children are not conflated.
    Genesis 17:19 And God said to Abraham, “Yes; behold, Sarah your wife shall bear you a son, and you shall call his name Isaac. And I will establish My covenant with him as an everlasting covenant, and with his seed after him.

Luke 1:14
You will have joy and gladness, and many will rejoice at his birth,
  Luke 1:58 — promise and fulfillment: This same-chapter promise of rejoicing at John's birth corresponds to the later account of Elizabeth's neighbors and relatives rejoicing with her. It is an internal promise-and-narrative link, not independent attestation of the birth.
    Luke 1:58 Her neighbors and relatives heard that the Lord had magnified His mercy toward her, and they rejoiced with her.

Luke 1:15
for he will be great before the Lord. He will certainly drink neither wine nor strong drink, and he will be filled with the Holy Spirit even from his mother’s womb.
  1 Samuel 1:11 — allusion: John's prohibition on wine and strong drink recalls Hannah's vow that her promised son will drink neither wine nor intoxicating drink in the supplied Saints Samuel text. Samuel also mentions no iron on the head; Luke does not add that restriction or explicitly call John a Nazirite. Luke's Holy-Spirit statement is additional.
    1 Samuel 1:11 She vowed a vow to the Lord, saying, “Adonai, Lord, Eloai Sabaoth, if Thou wilt indeed look upon Thy handmaid’s humiliation and remember me, and give Thy handmaid male offspring, then I will give him before Thee as a gift until the day of his death. He shall drink neither wine nor intoxicating drink, and no iron shall come upon his head.”

Luke 1:16
He will turn many of the sons of Israel to the Lord their God.
  Acts 13:24 — promise and fulfillment: Luke predicts John's turning many Israelites toward the Lord; Acts recalls John's proclamation of a baptism of repentance to all Israel before Jesus' arrival. The later ministry notice supplies a concrete counterpart to the preparatory mission, without quoting every word of the prediction.
    Acts 13:24 after John had proclaimed, before His coming, a baptism of repentance to all the people of Israel.

Luke 1:17
He himself will go before Him in Elijah’s spirit and power, to turn fathers’ hearts toward children and the disobedient to the understanding of the righteous, to prepare for the Lord a people made ready.”
  Malachi 3:22–3:23 — allusion: Luke describes John going in Elijah's spirit and power and turning fathers' hearts toward children. Saints Malachi announces Elijah and restoration of a father's heart toward a son and a person's heart toward a neighbor. The different second relationships are preserved, and John is not declared literally the historical Elijah.
    Malachi 3:22 “And look, I am sending you Elijah the Tishbite before the coming of the Lord’s great and manifest day,
    Malachi 3:23 who will restore a father’s heart toward a son and a person’s heart toward his neighbor, lest I come and strike the land utterly.”

Luke 1:18
Zechariah said to the angel, “How will I know this? For I am an old man, and my wife is advanced in years.”
  Genesis 17:17 — narrative parallel: Both men respond to a promised birth by raising the problem of advanced parental age. Abraham laughs and names his and Sarah's ages; Zechariah asks how he will know and describes himself and his wife as old. Their reactions and the later divine responses are not made identical.
    Genesis 17:17 And Abraham fell upon his face and laughed, and said in his mind, “Shall a child be born to a man a hundred years old? And shall Sarah, who is ninety years old, bear a child?”

Luke 1:19
The angel answered him, “I am Gabriel, who stands before God, and I was sent to speak to you and announce this good news to you.
  Daniel 8:16 — allusion: Both name Gabriel in a divine communication. Daniel hears a voice commissioning Gabriel to explain a vision; Luke has Gabriel identify himself and his mission to announce good news. Daniel's verse does not state Luke's description of standing before God.
    Daniel 8:16 I heard a human voice in the midst of Ulai, calling, “Gabriel, give that one understanding of the vision.” The man cried out and said, “The vision concerns that command.”

Luke 1:20
And behold, you will be silent and unable to speak until the day these things happen, because you did not believe my words, which will be fulfilled at their appointed time.”
  Ezekiel 3:26–3:27 — thematic parallel: Both describe a divinely governed period of inability to speak followed by an opening of the mouth. Luke makes Zechariah's silence a response to unbelief until the announced events occur; Ezekiel's silence and later speech concern a different prophetic commission and an embittering house.
    Ezekiel 3:26 I will bind your tongue, and you will become mute; you will not be a man rebuking them, for it is an embittering house.
    Ezekiel 3:27 But when I speak to you, I will open your mouth, and you will say to them, ‘Thus says the Lord: Let the one who hears hear, and let the one who disobeys disobey,’ for it is an embittering house.”

Luke 1:22
When he came out, he could not speak to them, and they realized that he had seen a vision in the sanctuary. He kept making signs to them and remained mute.
  Daniel 10:15–10:16 — narrative parallel: Both vision settings involve silence and subsequent communication. Luke explicitly says Zechariah cannot speak and makes signs; Daniel falls silent and then opens his mouth after a touch to his lips. The comparison does not assign the same cause or duration to their silence.
    Daniel 10:15 As he spoke these commands with me, I set my face toward the earth and fell silent.
    Daniel 10:16 Something like a human hand touched my lips. I opened my mouth, spoke, and said to the one standing opposite me, “My lord, something like a vision turned upon my side, upon me, and there was no strength in me.

Luke 1:24
After those days his wife Elizabeth conceived, and she kept herself secluded for five months, saying,
  Genesis 21:1–21:2 — narrative parallel: Both narrate conception in fulfillment of a preceding promise concerning an elderly couple. Genesis describes the Lord's promised visitation to Sarah and her conception; Luke describes Elizabeth conceiving and adds five months of seclusion, which Genesis does not reproduce.
    Genesis 21:1 And the Lord visited Sarah as He had said, and the Lord did to Sarah as He had spoken.
    Genesis 21:2 And Sarah conceived, and bore Abraham a son in his old age, at the season of which the Lord had spoken to him.

Luke 1:25
“This is what the Lord has done for me in these days, when He looked upon me to take away my disgrace among people.”
  Genesis 30:23 — allusion: Elizabeth's statement about God removing disgrace recalls Rachel's declaration that God has taken away her reproach after conceiving and bearing a son. The women and births are distinct, and the supplied disgrace/reproach wording is retained.
    Genesis 30:23 And she conceived and bore Jacob a son. And Rachel said, “God has taken away my reproach.”

Luke 1:26
In the sixth month, the angel Gabriel was sent from God to a city of Galilee named Nazareth,
  Luke 1:19 — narrative parallel: This same-chapter connection identifies Gabriel as the envoy previously introduced to Zechariah. The later sending to Nazareth begins a distinct announcement to Mary; the earlier self-introduction does not independently establish the sixth month or destination.
    Luke 1:19 The angel answered him, “I am Gabriel, who stands before God, and I was sent to speak to you and announce this good news to you.

Luke 1:27
to a virgin betrothed to a man named Joseph, of David’s house. The virgin’s name was Mary.
  Matthew 1:18–1:20 — narrative parallel: Both connect Mary with betrothal to Joseph, and Matthew's angel addresses Joseph as son of David alongside Luke's identification of his Davidic house. Matthew narrates an already discovered pregnancy; Luke introduces the virgin before the announcement. The different narrative stages remain distinct.
    Matthew 1:18 The origin of Jesus Christ was like this. When His mother Mary was betrothed to Joseph, before they came together she was found to be with child from the Holy Spirit.
    Matthew 1:19 Joseph her husband, being righteous and unwilling to expose her to public disgrace, decided to divorce her quietly.
    Matthew 1:20 As he considered these things, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to him in a dream and said, “Joseph, son of David, do not fear to take Mary your wife home, for what has been conceived in her is from the Holy Spirit.

Luke 1:28
And he entered and said to her, “Rejoice, you who have been favoured! The Lord is with you.”
  Judges 6:12 — allusion: Both angelic greetings assure the person addressed that the Lord is with them. Luke greets Mary as favored; Judges addresses Gideon as a mighty man of strength. The recipients and commissions are different.
    Judges 6:12 The Lord’s messenger appeared to him and said, “The Lord is with you, mighty man of strength.”

Luke 1:29
She was greatly troubled at the saying and considered what sort of greeting this might be.
  Luke 1:12 — narrative parallel: This same-chapter comparison links the troubled responses to the two angelic appearances. Mary is troubled by the saying and considers the greeting; Zechariah is troubled on seeing the angel and fears. The distinct causes of the reactions are preserved.
    Luke 1:12 Zechariah was troubled when he saw him, and fear fell upon him.

Luke 1:30
The angel said to her, “Do not fear, Mary, for you have found grace with God.
  Luke 1:13 — narrative parallel: Both announcements begin with the angel telling the named recipient not to fear. Mary's assurance concerns grace found with God; Zechariah's concerns his heard petition and Elizabeth's promised son. This is a repeated same-chapter annunciation pattern, not one announcement.
    Luke 1:13 But the angel said to him, “Do not fear, Zechariah, for your petition has been heard. Your wife Elizabeth will bear you a son, and you shall name him John.

Luke 1:31
And behold, you will conceive in your womb and bear a Son, and you shall name Him Jesus.
  Matthew 1:21 — narrative parallel: Both announce Mary's bearing a Son and direct that He be named Jesus. Luke addresses Mary before conception; Matthew's angel addresses Joseph and adds salvation from sins as the naming explanation.
    Matthew 1:21 She shall bear a Son, and you shall call His name Jesus, for He shall save His people from their sins.”

Luke 1:32
He will be great and will be called Son of the Most High. The Lord God will give Him the throne of His father David,
  2 Samuel 7:12–7:13 — promise and fulfillment: Luke's promise of David's throne recalls God's promise to David concerning his seed, the prepared kingdom and a throne established forever. The Samuel verses supply the royal promise, not Luke's exact title Son of the Most High.
    2 Samuel 7:12 When your days are fulfilled and you sleep with your fathers, I will raise up your seed after you, who will be from your own body, and prepare his kingdom.
    2 Samuel 7:13 He will build Me a house for My name, and I will set his throne upright for ever.

Luke 1:33
and He will reign over Jacob’s house forever, and His kingdom will have no end.”
  2 Samuel 7:16 — promise and fulfillment: Both promise an enduring royal house or kingdom and throne. Luke applies an unending reign to Mary's Son over Jacob's house; Samuel speaks of the royal house and kingdom in the Davidic promise. The named houses are not silently made the same expression.
    2 Samuel 7:16 His house and his kingdom will be made sure for ever before Me, and his throne will be set upright for ever.’”

Luke 1:34
Mary said to the angel, “How will this be, since I do not know a man?”
  Matthew 1:18 — narrative parallel: Both identify conception in the absence of the prior marital relations at issue. Mary asks prospectively because she does not know a man; Matthew narrates pregnancy before Mary and Joseph came together. The question and the narrated discovery occur at different stages.
    Matthew 1:18 The origin of Jesus Christ was like this. When His mother Mary was betrothed to Joseph, before they came together she was found to be with child from the Holy Spirit.

Luke 1:35
The angel answered her, “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; and so the holy One to be born will be called Son of God.
  Matthew 1:20 — narrative parallel: Both attribute Mary's conception to the Holy Spirit. Luke's angel explains it to Mary with overshadowing and Son-of-God language; Matthew's angel explains the already conceived child to Joseph without repeating those additional phrases.
    Matthew 1:20 As he considered these things, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to him in a dream and said, “Joseph, son of David, do not fear to take Mary your wife home, for what has been conceived in her is from the Holy Spirit.

Luke 1:36
And behold, your relative Elizabeth has also conceived a son in her old age, and this is the sixth month for her who was called barren,
  Genesis 21:2 — narrative parallel: Both describe a son conceived or born in an elderly couple's fulfillment of promise. Luke names Elizabeth as Mary's relative and specifies the sixth month; Genesis names Sarah and Abraham and does not supply those relational or timing details.
    Genesis 21:2 And Sarah conceived, and bore Abraham a son in his old age, at the season of which the Lord had spoken to him.

Luke 1:37
for no word from God will be powerless.”
  Genesis 18:14 — allusion: Both insist on the efficacy of God's word in the setting of a surprising promised birth. Genesis asks whether any word is impossible with God and names Sarah's future son; Luke says no word from God will be powerless. The births and question/assertion forms differ.
    Genesis 18:14 Is any word impossible with God? At this season I will return to you, when the time comes round; and Sarah shall have a son.”

Luke 1:38
Mary said, “Behold, I am the Lord’s servant. May it happen to me according to your word.” Then the angel departed from her.
  2 Samuel 7:25 — thematic parallel: Both speakers accept God's promise in the language of His servant and ask for the spoken word to be made effective. Mary consents to the announcement about her Son; David asks confirmation of the word concerning his servant and house. These are different responses to different promises.
    2 Samuel 7:25 Now, my Lord, Lord, confirm for ever the word Thou hast spoken concerning Thy servant and his house, Lord Almighty, God of Israel. And now, as Thou hast spoken,

Luke 1:39
In those days Mary arose and went in haste into the hill country, to a city of Judah.
  2 Samuel 6:2–6:3 — possible narrative echo: Mary's rising and going to Judah's hill country is compared, cautiously, with David and the people's rising and going on an ascent to bring the ark from a house on a hill. The larger visitation narrative supplies the household and blessing setting. This is a possible journey-pattern echo; Samuel's Judah wording concerns its rulers, and neither an identical route or city nor an identification of Mary with the ark follows.
    2 Samuel 6:2 David and all the people with him rose and went from Judah’s rulers on an ascent to bring up from there the ark of God, over which is called the name of the Lord of hosts, enthroned upon the cherubim above it.
    2 Samuel 6:3 He placed the ark of the Lord on a new cart and brought it from Abinadab’s house on the hill. Uzzah and his brothers, Abinadab’s sons, were driving the cart

Luke 1:40
She entered Zechariah’s house and greeted Elizabeth.
  Luke 1:44 — narrative parallel: Elizabeth later explicitly recalls the sound of Mary's greeting entering her ears and the infant leaping with gladness. That speech refers back to the greeting narrated here at Zechariah's house. This is an internal action-and-recollection link within one visit, not a separate witness or evidence for words of the greeting that Luke does not record.
    Luke 1:44 For behold, when the sound of your greeting entered my ears, the infant in my womb leaped with gladness.

Luke 1:41
When Elizabeth heard Mary’s greeting, the infant leaped in her womb, and Elizabeth was filled with the Holy Spirit.
  Genesis 25:22 — narrative parallel: Both describe movement of children in a mother's womb. Rebekah's children leap within her and prompt her troubled question; Elizabeth's infant leaps at Mary's greeting, with Elizabeth later interpreting the movement as joy. The pregnancies and meanings of the movements differ.
    Genesis 25:22 And the children leaped within her; and she said, “If it is to be this way with me, why is this happening to me?” And she went to inquire of the Lord.

Luke 1:42
She cried out with a loud shout and said, “Blessed are you among women, and blessed is the fruit of your womb!
  Judges 5:24 — allusion: Elizabeth's blessing of Mary among women recalls the blessing of Jael among women in Deborah's song. Luke additionally blesses the fruit of Mary's womb; Judges praises Jael in a different setting and does not assign her Mary's maternal role.
    Judges 5:24 Blessed among women be Jael, the wife of Heber the Kenite; among women in a tent may she be blessed.

Luke 1:43
And how is it that the mother of my Lord should come to me?
  2 Samuel 6:9 — allusion: Elizabeth asks how the mother of her Lord should come to her, recalling David's question about how the Lord's ark will come to him. This is a possible verbal visitation allusion across distinct narratives; it does not explicitly identify Mary as the ark or equate the surrounding events.
    2 Samuel 6:9 David feared the Lord that day, saying, “How can the ark of the Lord come to me?”

Luke 1:44
For behold, when the sound of your greeting entered my ears, the infant in my womb leaped with gladness.
  Luke 1:41 — narrative parallel: This internal narrative-and-speech connection links the narrated leap at Mary's greeting with Elizabeth's explanation that the infant leaped with gladness when she heard it. The second account adds the expressed interpretation of joy; it is not an independent witness report.
    Luke 1:41 When Elizabeth heard Mary’s greeting, the infant leaped in her womb, and Elizabeth was filled with the Holy Spirit.

Luke 1:45
And blessed is she who believed that there would be a fulfillment of the things spoken to her from the Lord.”
  Hebrews 11:11 — thematic parallel: Both associate believing God's promise with a remarkable conception. Elizabeth blesses Mary's belief in fulfillment of the Lord's words; Hebrews describes Sarah's faith in the faithful One who promised. The women, promises and circumstances are distinct.
    Hebrews 11:11 By faith even Sarah herself, though barren, received power to conceive offspring even beyond the proper age, since she considered the One who promised faithful.

Luke 1:46
Mary said, “My soul magnifies the Lord,
  Psalms 33:4 — allusion: Mary's soul magnifying the Lord recalls the psalm's call to magnify the Lord and exalt His name together. The psalm invites shared praise; Mary begins her own song. This is shared worship language, not an explicit quotation attribution.
    Psalms 33:4 Magnify the Lord with me; let us exalt His name together.

Luke 1:47
and my spirit rejoiced in God my Savior,
  1 Samuel 2:1 — allusion: Mary's rejoicing in God her Savior recalls Hannah's rejoicing in the Lord's salvation. Both maternal songs praise God, while the speakers and the occasions of their songs differ.
    1 Samuel 2:1 She said, “My heart is established in the Lord; my horn is exalted in my God. My mouth is opened wide against enemies; I rejoice in Thy salvation.

Luke 1:48
because He looked upon the lowly condition of His servant. For behold, from now on all generations will call me blessed,
  1 Samuel 1:11 — allusion: Mary's praise that God looked upon His servant's lowly condition recalls Hannah's request that the Lord look upon His servant's humiliation. Hannah petitions for a child; Mary praises grace already announced and adds future generations calling her blessed.
    1 Samuel 1:11 She vowed a vow to the Lord, saying, “Adonai, Lord, Eloai Sabaoth, if Thou wilt indeed look upon Thy handmaid’s humiliation and remember me, and give Thy handmaid male offspring, then I will give him before Thee as a gift until the day of his death. He shall drink neither wine nor intoxicating drink, and no iron shall come upon his head.”

Luke 1:49
because the Mighty One has done great things for me, and holy is His name.
  Psalms 110:9 — allusion: Both praise God's holy name in connection with His mighty or saving action. Mary names the Mighty One's great deeds for her; the psalm names redemption for His people and His commanded covenant. It does not describe Mary's personal experience.
    Psalms 110:9 He sent redemption to His people; He commanded His covenant for ever; holy and fearsome is His name.

Luke 1:50
His mercy is for generations and generations upon those who fear Him.
  Psalms 102:17 — allusion: Both speak of the Lord's enduring mercy toward those who fear Him across generations. Luke says generations and generations; the psalm says from eternity to eternity and additionally speaks of righteousness for children's children.
    Psalms 102:17 But the Lord’s mercy is from eternity to eternity toward those who fear Him, and His righteousness toward children’s children,

Luke 1:51
He has performed a mighty deed with His arm; He scattered the proud in the thought of their heart.
  Psalms 88:11 — allusion: Both join the Lord's mighty arm with the defeat or scattering of opposing pride. Luke speaks of scattering the proud in their heart's thought; the psalm describes humbling a proud one and scattering enemies with the arm of power. The objects and imagery are not silently made identical.
    Psalms 88:11 Thou didst humble a proud one like a wounded man, and with the arm of Thy power Thou didst scatter Thine enemies.

Luke 1:52
He brought rulers down from thrones and raised up the lowly.
  1 Samuel 2:7–2:8 — allusion: Both songs describe the Lord lowering and raising people in a reversal of status. Luke mentions rulers brought from thrones and lowly people raised; Hannah's song mentions humbling, exalting and raising the poor to sit with rulers on a throne. The examples differ.
    1 Samuel 2:7 The Lord makes poor and makes rich; He humbles and raises high.
    1 Samuel 2:8 He raises the needy from the earth and lifts the poor from the dung heap, to seat them with rulers of peoples and grant them a throne of glory as their inheritance,

Luke 1:53
He filled the hungry with good things and sent the rich away empty.
  Psalms 106:9 — allusion: Both praise God for filling hungry people with good things. Luke pairs that action with the rich being sent away empty; the psalm's paired description concerns an empty soul and a hungry soul, not Luke's rich.
    Psalms 106:9 for He filled an empty soul and filled a hungry soul with good things.

Luke 1:54
He helped Israel His servant, remembering mercy,
  Psalms 97:3 — allusion: Both connect remembered divine mercy with Israel. Luke describes helping Israel His servant; the psalm describes the Lord remembering mercy to Jacob and truth to Israel's house and adds the earth seeing His salvation.
    Psalms 97:3 He remembered His mercy to Jacob and His truth to the house of Israel. All the ends of the earth saw the salvation of our God.

Luke 1:55
as He spoke to our fathers, to Abraham and his offspring forever.”
  Micah 7:20 — allusion: Both connect God's mercy or promise to Abraham with what was spoken or sworn to the fathers. Luke mentions Abraham and his offspring forever; Micah names mercy to Abraham, truth to Jacob and the ancient oath. The differing clauses remain distinct.
    Micah 7:20 Thou wilt grant truth to Jacob, mercy to Abraham, just as Thou didst swear to our fathers in the former days.

Luke 1:56
Mary stayed with her about three months and returned to her home.
  2 Samuel 6:11 — qualified literary echo: three-month household stays: Mary's stay of about three months with Elizabeth is compared with the ark remaining three months in Obed-edom's house. The surrounding visitation narrative in Luke contains blessing language, while Samuel explicitly describes the household being blessed. The shared duration-and-household pattern is a limited literary comparison, not an identity of Mary and the ark or proof that Mary attended John's birth.
    2 Samuel 6:11 The ark of the Lord remained in the house of Obed-edom the Gittite for three months. The Lord blessed Obed-edom’s whole household and all that was his.

Luke 1:57
When Elizabeth’s time to give birth had come, she bore a son.
  Luke 1:13 — promise and fulfillment: The birth of Elizabeth's son fulfills the angel's earlier same-chapter announcement that Elizabeth would bear Zechariah a son. The name commanded in the promise is addressed in the subsequent naming scene.
    Luke 1:13 But the angel said to him, “Do not fear, Zechariah, for your petition has been heard. Your wife Elizabeth will bear you a son, and you shall name him John.

Luke 1:58
Her neighbors and relatives heard that the Lord had magnified His mercy toward her, and they rejoiced with her.
  Luke 1:14 — promise and fulfillment: The relatives' and neighbors' rejoicing with Elizabeth provides the same-chapter narrative counterpart to the promise that many would rejoice at John's birth. Luke also interprets the event as the Lord magnifying mercy toward Elizabeth.
    Luke 1:14 You will have joy and gladness, and many will rejoice at his birth,

Luke 1:59
On the eighth day they came to circumcise the child and were calling him Zechariah after his father’s name.
  Leviticus 12:3 — allusion: Luke's eighth-day circumcision corresponds to the eighth-day circumcision prescribed in Leviticus. The law does not prescribe the proposed name Zechariah or narrate this particular naming gathering.
    Leviticus 12:3 And on the eighth day he shall circumcise the flesh of his foreskin.

Luke 1:60
But his mother answered, “No; he shall be called John.”
  Luke 1:13 — promise and fulfillment: Elizabeth's insistence that the child be called John agrees with the angel's prior command to give that name. This internal link does not assert how Elizabeth learned the command, which the cited verses do not explain.
    Luke 1:13 But the angel said to him, “Do not fear, Zechariah, for your petition has been heard. Your wife Elizabeth will bear you a son, and you shall name him John.

Luke 1:61
They said to her, “No one among your relatives is called by this name.”
  Luke 1:13 — narrative parallel: The relatives object that the proposed name John has no family precedent; the earlier angelic instruction explicitly tells Zechariah to name the child John. The comparison explains the narrative contrast between family expectation and the announced name without identifying other people named John as relatives or treating the objection as independent proof of the angel's appearance.
    Luke 1:13 But the angel said to him, “Do not fear, Zechariah, for your petition has been heard. Your wife Elizabeth will bear you a son, and you shall name him John.

Luke 1:62
They made signs to his father to ask what he wanted the child to be called.
  Luke 1:22 — narrative parallel: Both parts of the same narrative use signs during Zechariah's inability to speak. Earlier Zechariah makes signs to the people; at the naming others make signs to him. The gestures alone are not treated as proof that he was deaf.
    Luke 1:22 When he came out, he could not speak to them, and they realized that he had seen a vision in the sanctuary. He kept making signs to them and remained mute.

Luke 1:63
He asked for a writing tablet and wrote, “His name is John.” And they all wondered.
  Luke 1:13 — promise and fulfillment: Zechariah's written declaration that the child's name is John follows the name prescribed by the angel earlier in the same chapter. It is an internal naming-fulfillment connection, with the later wonder of the observers additional.
    Luke 1:13 But the angel said to him, “Do not fear, Zechariah, for your petition has been heard. Your wife Elizabeth will bear you a son, and you shall name him John.

Luke 1:64
Immediately his mouth and tongue were opened, and he spoke, blessing God.
  Luke 1:20 — promise and fulfillment: Zechariah's restored speech at the naming resolves the same-chapter announcement that he would be unable to speak until the promised events occurred. The later verse adds his blessing God rather than repeating the angel's rebuke.
    Luke 1:20 And behold, you will be silent and unable to speak until the day these things happen, because you did not believe my words, which will be fulfilled at their appointed time.”

Luke 1:65
Fear came upon all who lived around them, and all these matters were discussed throughout the whole hill country of Judea.
  Luke 7:16–7:17 — narrative parallel: Both scenes combine fear after a remarkable divine action with a report spreading through a region. Here the account concerns John's birth and Zechariah's restored speech; Luke 7 concerns Jesus raising a widow's son and the people's declaration of God's visitation. They are different events.
    Luke 7:16 Fear seized everyone, and they glorified God, saying, “A great prophet has arisen among us,” and, “God has visited His people.”
    Luke 7:17 This report about Him spread throughout all Judea and all the surrounding region.

Luke 1:66
Everyone who heard took these matters to heart, saying, “What then will this child become?” For the Lord’s hand was indeed with him.
  Acts 11:21 — thematic parallel: Both use the Lord's hand being with a person or group to indicate divine aid. Luke concerns the child's future; Acts concerns people proclaiming the Lord Jesus and many believing. The common expression does not identify John with those later missionaries.
    Acts 11:21 The Lord’s hand was with them, and a great number believed and turned to the Lord.

Luke 1:67
His father Zechariah was filled with the Holy Spirit and prophesied, saying,
  Acts 19:6 — thematic parallel: Both connect the Holy Spirit with prophetic speech. Zechariah is filled and prophesies; Acts describes the Spirit coming upon people after Paul's laying on hands, followed by tongues and prophecy. The recipients and accompanying actions differ.
    Acts 19:6 When Paul laid his hands upon them, the Holy Spirit came upon them, and they spoke in tongues and prophesied.

Luke 1:68
“Blessed is the Lord, the God of Israel, because He has visited and accomplished redemption for His people,
  Psalms 110:9 — allusion: Zechariah praises the Lord for accomplishing redemption for His people; the psalm says He sent redemption to His people. Luke adds visitation and the blessing of Israel's God, while the psalm adds the commanded covenant and God's holy name.
    Psalms 110:9 He sent redemption to His people; He commanded His covenant for ever; holy and fearsome is His name.

Luke 1:69
and has raised up a horn of salvation for us in the house of David His servant,
  Psalms 131:17 — allusion: Luke's horn of salvation in David's house recalls the Lord causing a horn to spring up for David in the psalm. Luke adds salvation and the raising-up statement; the psalm also describes a lamp for the anointed. This royal image is not used to make the Aaronic child John a Davidic heir.
    Psalms 131:17 There I will make a horn spring up for David; I have prepared a lamp for My anointed.

Luke 1:70
as He spoke through the mouth of His holy prophets from long ago:
  Acts 3:21 — allusion: Both speak of God speaking through the mouth of His holy prophets from long ago. Zechariah introduces promised salvation; Acts speaks of restoration and heaven's reception of Jesus. The shared prophetic formula does not collapse the differing surrounding statements.
    Acts 3:21 whom heaven must receive until the times of restoration of all things of which God spoke through the mouth of His holy prophets from long ago.

Luke 1:71
salvation from our enemies and from the hand of all who hate us,
  Psalms 105:10 — allusion: Both speak of salvation from the hand of those who hate God's people and redemption or rescue from enemies. The psalm recalls deliverance at the sea in its context; Zechariah speaks prophetically of salvation without naming all those enemies as Egypt.
    Psalms 105:10 He saved them from the hand of those hating them and redeemed them from the hand of the enemy.

Luke 1:72
to show mercy to our fathers and remember His holy covenant,
  Psalms 105:45 — allusion: Both associate remembered covenant with divine mercy toward God's people. Luke refers to mercy to the fathers and the holy covenant; the psalm describes remembering His covenant and relenting according to abundant mercy during the people's distress.
    Psalms 105:45 He remembered His covenant and changed His purpose according to His abundant mercy,

Luke 1:73
the oath He swore to Abraham our father, to grant us
  Genesis 22:16–22:18 — allusion: Luke recalls the oath God swore to Abraham; Genesis supplies God's oath by Himself and His blessing and offspring promise to Abraham. The rescue-and-service wording continuing Luke's sentence is not presented as a verbatim part of Genesis' oath.
    Genesis 22:16 saying, “By Myself I have sworn, says the Lord: because you have done this thing, and have not spared your beloved son for My sake,
    Genesis 22:17 surely I will bless you, and greatly multiply your seed as the stars of the heaven, and as the sand upon the shore of the sea; and your seed shall inherit the cities of their adversaries.
    Genesis 22:18 And in your seed all the nations of the earth shall be blessed, because you have listened to My voice.”

Luke 1:74
that, rescued from our enemies’ hand, we might serve Him without fear,
  Exodus 3:12 — thematic parallel: Both link deliverance of God's people with subsequent service to God. Exodus promises that Moses and the people brought from Egypt will serve God on the mountain; Luke speaks of rescue from enemies to serve without fear. The enemies and settings are not identified as the same.
    Exodus 3:12 And God said to Moses, “I will be with you; and this shall be the sign to you that I send you: when you have brought My people out of Egypt, you shall serve God upon this mountain.”

Luke 1:75
in holiness and righteousness before Him all our days.
  Ephesians 4:24 — thematic parallel: Both join holiness and righteousness in the life oriented to God. Luke describes ongoing service before Him; Ephesians describes putting on the new person created according to God in righteousness and holiness of truth. This is an ethical-language comparison, not the same argument.
    Ephesians 4:24 and put on the new human being, created according to God in righteousness and holiness of truth.

Luke 1:76
And you also, child, will be called prophet of the Most High, for you will go before the Lord to prepare His ways,
  Malachi 3:1 — allusion: Zechariah says the child will go before the Lord to prepare His ways; Malachi announces a messenger who will look to a way before God's face in the supplied wording. Luke assigns John a preparatory role and adds prophet of the Most High, without identifying him with every messenger figure in Malachi's verse.
    Malachi 3:1 “Look, I am sending out My messenger, and he will look to a way before My face. Suddenly the Lord whom you seek will come to His own sanctuary, and the messenger of the covenant whom you desire. Look, he is coming,” says the Lord Almighty.

Luke 1:77
to give His people knowledge of salvation in the forgiveness of their sins,
  Luke 3:2–3:3 — promise and fulfillment: This same-book link connects Zechariah's prediction concerning salvation in forgiveness with the later ministry of John explicitly identified as Zechariah's son. John proclaims a baptism of repentance for forgiveness of sins; the later passage supplies the public ministry, not every phrase of the prediction.
    Luke 3:2 during the high priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas, God’s word came to John, Zechariah’s son, in the wilderness.
    Luke 3:3 He went into all the region around the Jordan, proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins,

Luke 1:78
through the tender mercy of our God, by which the dawn from on high will visit us,
  Malachi 3:20 — allusion: Luke's dawn from on high visiting through God's mercy recalls Malachi's rising sun of righteousness with healing. This is a proposed light-and-rising image allusion, not an explicit quotation; Luke does not reproduce Malachi's healing wings or its audience description.
    Malachi 3:20 But for you who fear My name, a sun of righteousness will rise, and healing in its wings; you will go out and leap like little calves released from bonds.

Luke 1:79
to shine upon those who sit in darkness and death’s shadow, to direct our feet into a way of peace.”
  Isaiah 9:1 — allusion: Both describe light reaching people in darkness and death's shadow. Isaiah speaks of a great light shining on the people walking or dwelling there; Luke adds the directing of feet into peace. The added peace clause is not claimed as a quotation of this verse.
    Isaiah 9:1 You people who walk in darkness, behold a great light; you who dwell in a land and shadow of death, a light will shine upon you.

Luke 1:80
The child grew and became strong in spirit. He remained in the wilderness until the day of his public appearance to Israel.
  Luke 2:40 — narrative parallel: The same-book childhood summaries both describe a child growing and becoming strong. John grows strong in spirit and remains in the wilderness until his manifestation to Israel; Jesus is described with wisdom and God's grace. These are deliberately distinguished children and developmental summaries.
    Luke 2:40 The Child grew and became strong, being filled with wisdom, and God’s grace was upon Him.

Luke 2:1
In those days a decree went out from Caesar Augustus for all the inhabited world to be registered.
  Acts 18:2 — narrative parallel: Both narratives name an imperial command that shapes people's movement. Luke names Augustus and a registration decree, with travel narrated next; Acts names Claudius and the departure of Jews from Rome. The emperors, subjects, places and commands remain different; this is not corroboration of one decree or of a universal census procedure.
    Acts 18:2 He found a Jew named Aquila, a native of Pontus who had recently come from Italy with his wife Priscilla, because Claudius had ordered all the Jews to leave Rome. Paul went to them,

Luke 2:2
This first registration took place while Quirinius was governing Syria.
  Acts 5:37 — thematic parallel: Luke dates this first registration by Quirinius's governing Syria; Acts places Judas the Galilean's uprising in the days of the registration. These are concrete registration notices in Luke and Acts. Acts does not name Quirinius or Augustus here, so this link alone neither establishes that the registrations are identical nor resolves Luke's chronology.
    Acts 5:37 After him Judas the Galilean arose in the days of the registration and drew people away after him. He too perished, and all who followed him were scattered.

Luke 2:3
Everyone went to be registered, each to his own city.
  Luke 2:4–2:5 — narrative parallel: Joseph's following journey supplies the narrative's particular example of going to be registered: Bethlehem is related to his Davidic house and family, and Mary accompanies him. This clarifies the local own-city statement without proving that every Roman registration required travel to an ancestral town or supplying an unstated administrative rule.
    Luke 2:4 Joseph also went up from Galilee, from the city of Nazareth, into Judea, to David’s city, which is called Bethlehem, because he belonged to David’s house and family,
    Luke 2:5 to be registered with Mary, who was betrothed to him and was pregnant.

Luke 2:4
Joseph also went up from Galilee, from the city of Nazareth, into Judea, to David’s city, which is called Bethlehem, because he belonged to David’s house and family,
  Matthew 1:20 — identity parallel: Both identify Joseph through Davidic ancestry. Luke makes his belonging to David's house the reason for the Bethlehem journey; Matthew's angel addresses him as Joseph, son of David, without narrating this census journey.
    Matthew 1:20 As he considered these things, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to him in a dream and said, “Joseph, son of David, do not fear to take Mary your wife home, for what has been conceived in her is from the Holy Spirit.

Luke 2:5
to be registered with Mary, who was betrothed to him and was pregnant.
  Matthew 1:18 — narrative parallel: Both describe Mary as betrothed to Joseph and pregnant. Matthew additionally attributes conception to the Holy Spirit and says it occurred before they came together; Luke's cited registration notice does not repeat those explanations.
    Matthew 1:18 The origin of Jesus Christ was like this. When His mother Mary was betrothed to Joseph, before they came together she was found to be with child from the Holy Spirit.

Luke 2:6
While they were there, the time came for her to give birth.
  Luke 1:57 — same-book structural parallel: Both infancy accounts describe the time to give birth arriving. Luke1 concerns Elizabeth and immediately reports her son's birth; Luke2 concerns Mary in Bethlehem and narrates the birth next. This is a repeated birth-transition pattern, not the same mother or event.
    Luke 1:57 When Elizabeth’s time to give birth had come, she bore a son.

Luke 2:7
She bore her firstborn Son, wrapped Him in swaddling cloths, and laid Him in a manger, because there was no place for them in the lodging place.
  Luke 2:12 — internal sign-and-event link: The later angelic sign repeats the infant's swaddling cloths and manger from the narrated birth. This connects the sign with the precise circumstances already given, without supplying independent testimony to the lodging-place detail.
    Luke 2:12 This is the sign for you: you will find an infant wrapped in swaddling cloths and lying in a manger.”

Luke 2:8
In that same region there were shepherds living in the fields and keeping watch over their flock through the watches of the night.
  Genesis 31:38–31:40 — scriptural background: nighttime flock labor: Both passages place tending flocks in the conditions of nighttime labor. Luke narrates shepherds keeping night watches over their flock; Jacob describes responsibility for the flock, losses by night, frost and sleeplessness. This is concrete occupational background for night shepherding, not an identification of Jacob with the shepherds, their region, the birth occasion or the angelic encounter.
    Genesis 31:38 “These twenty years I have been with you; your sheep and your goats have not miscarried, and I have not eaten the rams of your flock.
    Genesis 31:39 “I brought you no animal taken by wild beasts; I myself paid for what was stolen by day and what was stolen by night.
    Genesis 31:40 “By day I was consumed by the heat, and by night by the frost; and sleep departed from my eyes.

Luke 2:9
An angel of the Lord stood beside them, and the Lord’s glory shone around them; they feared with a great fear.
  Luke 1:11–1:12 — same-book narrative parallel: Both infancy scenes describe an angel appearing and the recipients becoming afraid. Luke2 adds the Lord's surrounding glory and concerns shepherds in the fields; Luke1 concerns Zechariah at the incense altar. The places and recipients remain distinct.
    Luke 1:11 An angel of the Lord appeared to him, standing at the right of the altar of incense.
    Luke 1:12 Zechariah was troubled when he saw him, and fear fell upon him.

Luke 2:10
The angel said to them, “Do not fear, for behold, I announce to you good news of great joy, which will be for all the people:
  Luke 1:13–1:14 — same-book teaching parallel: Both angelic announcements begin with reassurance against fear and associate a child's birth with joy. Luke2 promises good news of great joy for the people; Luke1 addresses Zechariah about John and many rejoicing at his birth.
    Luke 1:13 But the angel said to him, “Do not fear, Zechariah, for your petition has been heard. Your wife Elizabeth will bear you a son, and you shall name him John.
    Luke 1:14 You will have joy and gladness, and many will rejoice at his birth,

Luke 2:11
today in David’s city a Savior has been born for you, who is Christ the Lord.
  Matthew 1:21 — teaching parallel: Both angelic announcements connect the Child's birth with salvation. Luke calls the newborn Savior, Christ and Lord in David's city; Matthew commands the name Jesus because He will save His people from sins. The additional titles and future-versus-born announcement remain distinct.
    Matthew 1:21 She shall bear a Son, and you shall call His name Jesus, for He shall save His people from their sins.”

Luke 2:12
This is the sign for you: you will find an infant wrapped in swaddling cloths and lying in a manger.”
  Luke 2:7 — internal sign-and-event link: The angel's sign specifically matches the swaddled infant lying in the manger in the earlier birth notice. It identifies the narrative's sign rather than creating an independent account.
    Luke 2:7 She bore her firstborn Son, wrapped Him in swaddling cloths, and laid Him in a manger, because there was no place for them in the lodging place.

Luke 2:13
Suddenly there was with the angel a multitude of the heavenly army, praising God and saying,
  Psalms 148:2 — thematic parallel: Luke describes the heavenly army praising God, while the psalm calls all God's angels and powers to praise Him. The shared heavenly praise is explicit; the psalm's powers wording is not rewritten as a second narrative of this angelic appearance.
    Psalms 148:2 Praise Him, all His angels; praise Him, all His powers.

Luke 2:14
“Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace among people of His favour.”
  Luke 19:38 — same-book praise parallel: Both Lukan acclamations join glory in the highest with peace. Here the heavenly praise names peace on earth among people of God's favor; the entry acclamation names peace in heaven. The speakers, occasions and placement of peace remain distinct.
    Luke 19:38 saying, “Blessed is the King who comes in the name of the Lord! Peace in heaven, and glory in the highest!”

Luke 2:15
When the angels had departed from them into heaven, the shepherds said to one another, “Let us go over to Bethlehem and see this event that has happened, which the Lord has made known to us.”
  Matthew 2:9–2:11 — narrative parallel: The shepherds decide to go see the announced birth after the angels depart; Matthew's visitors follow the star and enter to see the Child with Mary. Both narratives connect heavenly guidance with going to see Jesus as a child. The shepherds and magi, the guiding signs, the locations described, and the actions are distinct; no simultaneous arrival is inferred.
    Matthew 2:9 After hearing the king, they went on their way. And behold, the star they had seen at its rising went ahead of them until it came and stood over the place where the Child was.
    Matthew 2:10 When they saw the star, they rejoiced with exceedingly great joy.
    Matthew 2:11 They entered the house and saw the Child with Mary His mother. Falling down, they bowed before Him; and opening their treasures, they offered Him gifts: gold, frankincense, and myrrh.

Luke 2:16
They came in haste and found Mary and Joseph, and the infant lying in the manger.
  Luke 2:12 — internal sign-and-fulfillment link: The shepherds find the infant lying in the manger, matching the angel's announced sign. Mary and Joseph are added in the discovery; the earlier sign also specifies swaddling cloths.
    Luke 2:12 This is the sign for you: you will find an infant wrapped in swaddling cloths and lying in a manger.”

Luke 2:17
When they saw Him, they made known the saying spoken to them about this Child.
  Luke 24:9 — same-book thematic parallel: Both scenes describe witnesses reporting revealed news concerning Jesus after an angelic encounter in the surrounding account. Here shepherds report the birth saying after seeing the Child; later women report the tomb news to the Eleven and others. This compares different birth and resurrection testimony scenes.
    Luke 24:9 and returned from the tomb and reported all these things to the Eleven and to all the rest.

Luke 2:18
Everyone who heard wondered at what the shepherds told them.
  Luke 1:63–1:66 — internal infancy report-and-reception pattern: Both infancy narratives join reports about an unusual child with a community response. John's naming and Zechariah's restored speech bring wonder, fear and discussion among hearers; the shepherds' report brings wonder about Jesus. The children, speakers, signs and reactions are explicitly distinct. This compares the paired infancy narratives' report-and-reception pattern, not the same testimony or audience.
    Luke 1:63 He asked for a writing tablet and wrote, “His name is John.” And they all wondered.
    Luke 1:64 Immediately his mouth and tongue were opened, and he spoke, blessing God.
    Luke 1:65 Fear came upon all who lived around them, and all these matters were discussed throughout the whole hill country of Judea.
    Luke 1:66 Everyone who heard took these matters to heart, saying, “What then will this child become?” For the Lord’s hand was indeed with him.

Luke 2:19
But Mary kept all these matters, pondering them in her heart.
  Luke 2:51 — same-book structural parallel: Both notices describe Mary keeping all these matters in her heart. Luke2:19 adds pondering after the shepherds' news; the later notice follows the twelve-year-old Jesus' temple episode. The repeated maternal response is not the same occasion.
    Luke 2:51 He went down with them and came to Nazareth, and He remained subject to them. His mother kept all these matters in her heart.

Luke 2:20
The shepherds returned, glorifying and praising God for everything they had heard and seen, just as it had been spoken to them.
  Luke 24:52–24:53 — same-book thematic parallel: Both scenes conclude an encounter concerning Jesus with return and praise of God. The shepherds return praising for what they heard and saw; the later disciples return to Jerusalem with joy and bless God in the temple. The groups, places and events differ.
    Luke 24:52 And they, having worshiped Him, returned to Jerusalem with great joy,
    Luke 24:53 and were continually in the temple, blessing God.

Luke 2:21
When eight days were completed for His circumcision, He was named Jesus, the name given by the angel before He was conceived in the womb.
  Leviticus 12:3 — law and observance: The circumcision on the eighth day follows the law's eighth-day circumcision instruction. Leviticus does not supply Jesus' naming or the angelic naming promise.
    Leviticus 12:3 And on the eighth day he shall circumcise the flesh of his foreskin.
  Luke 1:31 — internal promise-and-fulfillment link: Jesus' name at circumcision matches the angel's earlier command that Mary name her promised Son Jesus. The earlier announcement precedes conception in its narrative context, as the naming notice recalls.
    Luke 1:31 And behold, you will conceive in your womb and bear a Son, and you shall name Him Jesus.

Luke 2:22
When the days of their purification according to Moses’ law were completed, they brought Him up to Jerusalem to present Him to the Lord,
  Leviticus 12:6–12:7 — law and observance: Luke connects completed purification days with Moses' law and presentation in Jerusalem. Leviticus specifies the mother's purification offering at the Tent of Testimony. Luke's plural their and the Child's presentation are retained; Leviticus is not made to require purification of everyone included in that plural.
    Leviticus 12:6 And when the days of her purification for a son or daughter are completed, she shall bring unto the priest at the door of the Tent of Testimony an unblemished year-old lamb for a whole burnt offering and a young pigeon or turtledove for sin.
    Leviticus 12:7 And the priest shall present it before the Lord, and make atonement for her, and cleanse her from the fountain of her blood. This is the law for her that bears a male or a female.

Luke 2:23
as it is written in the Lord’s law: “Every male opening the womb shall be called holy to the Lord,”
  Exodus 13:2 — quotation: Luke's law citation about the womb-opening firstborn being holy to the Lord corresponds to the command to consecrate every firstborn opening the womb as belonging to God. Exodus includes animals as well as humans; Luke's citation specifies every male.
    Exodus 13:2 “Consecrate to Me every firstborn, the first offspring opening every womb among the sons of Israel, both human and animal. It is Mine.”

Luke 2:24
and to give a sacrifice according to what is said in the Lord’s law: “A pair of turtledoves or two young pigeons.”
  Leviticus 12:8 — quotation: Both specify two turtledoves or two young pigeons as the offering. Leviticus explains this provision when the person's resources cannot supply a lamb and distinguishes the two sacrifices; Luke quotes the permitted birds without reproducing the full rule.
    Leviticus 12:8 If her hand cannot provide enough for a lamb, she shall take two turtledoves or two young pigeons, one for a whole burnt offering and one for sin. The priest shall make atonement for her, and she shall be cleansed.

Luke 2:25
And behold, there was a man in Jerusalem named Simeon. This man was righteous and devout, awaiting Israel’s consolation, and the Holy Spirit was upon him.
  Isaiah 40:1–40:2 — thematic parallel: Simeon's expectation of Israel's consolation corresponds to Isaiah's command to comfort God's people and Jerusalem. Isaiah is a restoration promise, not an independent biographical notice about Simeon or his Spirit-endowment.
    Isaiah 40:1 Comfort, comfort My people, says God.
    Isaiah 40:2 You priests, speak to the heart of Jerusalem; comfort her. For her humiliation is fulfilled, her sin is loosed; for from the hand of the Lord she has received double for her sins.

Luke 2:26
It had been revealed to him by the Holy Spirit that he would not see death before he saw the Lord’s Christ.
  Luke 9:27 — same-book teaching parallel: Both promises postpone death until a specified sight of God's messianic work. Simeon is promised sight of the Lord's Christ; Jesus later tells some followers they will see God's kingdom before tasting death. The recipients and promised sights are not equated, and no common fulfillment date is assigned.
    Luke 9:27 But truly I tell you, some standing here will certainly not taste death until they see God’s kingdom.”

Luke 2:27
He came in the Spirit into the temple. When the parents brought in the Child Jesus to do for Him what was customary under the law,
  Luke 2:22 — internal narrative continuity: The parents' arrival with Jesus for the law's custom continues the earlier notice of bringing Him to Jerusalem for presentation. This identifies the narrated visit that intersects Simeon's Spirit-led arrival; it is not an independent witness to the visit or to the Spirit's guidance.
    Luke 2:22 When the days of their purification according to Moses’ law were completed, they brought Him up to Jerusalem to present Him to the Lord,

Luke 2:28
he received Him into his arms, blessed God, and said,
  Mark 9:36–9:37 — thematic comparison: receiving a child and the role of Jesus: Luke describes Simeon receiving the Child Jesus into his arms and blessing God. Mark describes Jesus embracing a child and teaching that receiving such a child in His name is receiving Him. The shared child-reception motif is compared with the differing roles kept explicit: Jesus is received in Luke and receives the child in Mark; the blessing and teaching actions are also distinct.
    Mark 9:36 He took a child, stood the child among them, and, embracing the child, said to them,
    Mark 9:37 “Whoever receives one such child in My name receives Me; and whoever receives Me does not receive Me but the One who sent Me.”

Luke 2:29
“Now, Master, Thou art releasing Thy servant in peace according to Thy word,
  Genesis 46:30 — thematic parallel: Both speakers connect death or release with seeing the awaited person. Israel says he may die after seeing Joseph alive; Simeon acknowledges being released in peace according to God's word, after seeing salvation as the surrounding hymn states. The persons and circumstances remain distinct; Luke does not state Simeon's age.
    Genesis 46:30 And Israel said to Joseph, “Now I may die, since I have seen your face, for you are still alive.”

Luke 2:30
because my eyes have seen Thy salvation,
  Isaiah 52:10 — allusion: Simeon's eyes seeing God's salvation correspond to Isaiah's promise that earth's ends will see salvation from God. Luke gives one person's present testimony about the Child, while Isaiah announces a public, worldwide revelation.
    Isaiah 52:10 The Lord will uncover His holy arm before all nations, and all the ends of earth will see the salvation that comes from God.

Luke 2:31
which Thou didst prepare in the sight of all peoples:
  Isaiah 52:10 — allusion: Both place God's salvation before the nations or peoples. Luke describes salvation prepared in all peoples' sight; Isaiah describes the Lord uncovering His holy arm before all nations and the earth's ends seeing salvation.
    Isaiah 52:10 The Lord will uncover His holy arm before all nations, and all the ends of earth will see the salvation that comes from God.

Luke 2:32
a light for revelation to the nations and glory for Thy people Israel.”
  Isaiah 49:6 — allusion: Both combine Israel's restoration with light and salvation for nations. Isaiah names Jacob's tribes and Israel's dispersion before the servant's light for nations; Luke names revelation to nations and glory for Israel, preserving the different formulations.
    Isaiah 49:6 and He said to me, “It is a great thing for you to be called My servant, to establish Jacob’s tribes and bring back Israel’s dispersion. Behold, I have appointed you as a covenant for a race, a light for nations, so that you may bring salvation to the earth’s extremity.”

Luke 2:33
His father and mother were wondering at what was being said about Him.
  Luke 2:18 — internal response pattern: wonder at testimony about Jesus: The parents wonder at what Simeon is saying about Jesus, while earlier hearers wonder at what the shepherds tell them about the same Child. The repeated response is specifically to testimony about the infant Jesus in the same infancy narrative. The witnesses, words and audiences differ; parental wonder is not equated with ignorance or made a second account of the shepherds' visit.
    Luke 2:18 Everyone who heard wondered at what the shepherds told them.

Luke 2:34
Simeon blessed them and said to His mother Mary, “Behold, this One is appointed for the fall and rising of many in Israel, and for a sign spoken against—
  Isaiah 8:14–8:15 — allusion: Both passages associate God's intervention with contrasting outcomes in Israel and the fall of many. This supplied Isaiah text contrasts a sanctuary for trust with Jacob's trap and people's falling; Simeon speaks of the Child's appointment for falling and rising. Isaiah's conditional wording is retained rather than replaced by another edition's stone wording.
    Isaiah 8:14 If you trust in Him, He will become a sanctuary for you, and you will not encounter Him as the stumbling of a stone or as the falling of a rock. But Jacob’s house is in a trap, and those settled in Jerusalem in a pit.
    Isaiah 8:15 Therefore many among them will be powerless, fall and be broken; people who are secure will draw near and be captured.”

Luke 2:35
and a sword will pass through your own soul also—so that thoughts from many hearts may be revealed.”
  Hebrews 4:12 — thematic imagery comparison: Both join a penetrating sword image with the soul and disclosure or judgment of heart-thoughts. Luke's statement concerns Mary's own soul and the revelation of many hearts; Hebrews concerns God's living word. The comparison does not identify Mary's sword with the word or claim identical experiences.
    Hebrews 4:12 For the word of God is living and active and sharper than any two-edged sword, penetrating as far as the division of soul and spirit, of joints and marrow, and able to judge the thoughts and intentions of the heart.

Luke 2:36
There was also a prophetess, Anna, daughter of Phanuel, from the tribe of Asher. She was very old. She had lived with her husband for seven years after marrying as a virgin,
  2 Kings 22:14 — thematic parallel: Luke identifies Anna as a prophetess in the Jerusalem temple scene; Kings identifies Olda as a prophetess living in Jerusalem and names her household connections. This compares specifically named female prophetic figures associated with Jerusalem. Olda is not Anna, and Kings supplies no confirmation of Anna's father, tribe, age, or marriage history.
    2 Kings 22:14 Chelkias the priest, Achikam, Achobor, Sapphan, and Asaias went to Olda the prophetess, wife of Sellem son of Thekoue son of Araas, keeper of the garments. She lived in Jerusalem in the masena. They spoke to her.

Luke 2:37
and had been a widow until the age of eighty-four. She did not leave the temple, serving with fasts and petitions night and day.
  1 Timothy 5:5 — thematic parallel: Both associate widowhood with petitions and prayer night and day. Luke describes Anna's temple service and fasting; Timothy describes a widow left alone who hopes in God. Their settings and social descriptions are not treated as the same community status.
    1 Timothy 5:5 Now the woman who is truly a widow and left alone has set her hope on God and continues in petitions and prayers night and day.

Luke 2:38
Coming up at that very hour, she gave thanks to God and spoke about Him to everyone awaiting Jerusalem’s redemption.
  Luke 24:21 — same-book thematic parallel: Both connect Jesus with hope for Israel's redemption. Anna speaks about the Child to those awaiting Jerusalem's redemption; the later travelers describe having hoped Jesus would redeem Israel after the passion. The different moments and degrees of understanding are preserved.
    Luke 24:21 But we were hoping that He was the One about to redeem Israel. Yet indeed, besides all these things, this is the third day since these things happened.

Luke 2:39
When they had completed everything according to the Lord’s law, they returned to Galilee, to their own city of Nazareth.
  Matthew 2:22–2:23 — narrative parallel: Both infancy accounts place the family in Galilee at Nazareth. Luke describes return after completing the Lord's law; Matthew narrates withdrawal after the Egyptian stay and fear of Archelaus. No single merged journey or chronology is imposed.
    Matthew 2:22 But hearing that Archelaus reigned over Judea in place of his father Herod, he feared to go there. Warned in a dream, he withdrew to the regions of Galilee.
    Matthew 2:23 He came and settled in a city called Nazareth, so that what was spoken through the prophets might be fulfilled: “He will be called a Nazarene.”

Luke 2:40
The Child grew and became strong, being filled with wisdom, and God’s grace was upon Him.
  Luke 1:80 — same-book structural parallel: Both childhood summaries say the child grew and became strong. Jesus' summary adds wisdom and God's grace; John's adds strength in spirit and wilderness residence until his public appearance. The children and developmental settings remain distinct.
    Luke 1:80 The child grew and became strong in spirit. He remained in the wilderness until the day of his public appearance to Israel.

Luke 2:41
His parents went each year to Jerusalem for the Festival of Passover.
  Exodus 23:14–23:17 — law and observance: The annual Passover-family journey fits the law's recurring pilgrimage festivals, including Unleavened Bread and appearing before the Lord. Exodus specifies male attendance and other festivals, without naming Jerusalem here or requiring Mary's attendance in particular; Luke reports both parents' actual custom.
    Exodus 23:14 Three times a year you shall keep a feast to Me.
    Exodus 23:15 Keep the feast of unleavened bread; for seven days you shall eat unleavened bread, as I commanded you, at the appointed time in the month of new grain; for in it you came out of Egypt. You shall not appear before Me empty-handed.
    Exodus 23:16 And you shall keep the feast of harvest, of the firstfruits of your labours which you sow in your field; and the feast of completion as the year goes out, when you gather your labours out of your field.
    Exodus 23:17 Every male of yours shall appear three times in the year before the Lord your God.

Luke 2:42
When He became twelve years old, they went up according to the festival’s custom.
  Exodus 23:14–23:17 — thematic parallel: The family's customary festival journey, at Jesus' age of twelve, is compared with the law's recurring festival obligations, including unleavened bread and male appearance before the Lord. This supplies scriptural background to the festival custom identified as Passover in Luke's preceding verse. Exodus gives no age-twelve rule or later initiation custom.
    Exodus 23:14 Three times a year you shall keep a feast to Me.
    Exodus 23:15 Keep the feast of unleavened bread; for seven days you shall eat unleavened bread, as I commanded you, at the appointed time in the month of new grain; for in it you came out of Egypt. You shall not appear before Me empty-handed.
    Exodus 23:16 And you shall keep the feast of harvest, of the firstfruits of your labours which you sow in your field; and the feast of completion as the year goes out, when you gather your labours out of your field.
    Exodus 23:17 Every male of yours shall appear three times in the year before the Lord your God.

Luke 2:43
When the festival days were over and they were returning, the boy Jesus remained in Jerusalem, and His parents did not know.
  1 Samuel 2:11 — narrative parallel: In Luke the parents return while the boy remains in Jerusalem without their knowledge; in Samuel the family departs for Armathaim while the child remains ministering before the Lord with Eli. Both scenes distinguish a child's remaining at the place of worship from the household's departure. Samuel's stay is deliberate dedication, unlike Luke's unrecognized separation.
    1 Samuel 2:11 They left him there before the Lord and departed to Armathaim, while the child ministered in the Lord’s presence before Eli the priest.

Luke 2:46
After three days they found Him in the temple, sitting among the teachers, listening to them and questioning them.
  John 7:14–7:15 — thematic parallel: Both place Jesus in the temple in a learning-and-teaching encounter. Luke describes the twelve-year-old among teachers, listening and asking questions; John describes the adult teaching during a feast and people questioning His knowledge of writings. The ages, roles and occasions are not identified.
    John 7:14 When the feast was already halfway through, Jesus went up into the temple and taught.
    John 7:15 So the Jews marveled, saying, “How does this man know the writings without having studied?”

Luke 2:47
All who heard Him were astonished at His understanding and answers.
  John 7:15 — thematic parallel: Both describe amazement at Jesus' understanding or knowledge. Luke's hearers marvel at the child's understanding and answers; John's hearers question the adult's knowledge without formal study. John does not narrate the childhood episode.
    John 7:15 So the Jews marveled, saying, “How does this man know the writings without having studied?”

Luke 2:48
When His parents saw Him, they were overwhelmed. His mother said to Him, “Child, why hast Thou treated us this way? Behold, Thy father and I have been searching for Thee in anguish.”
  Luke 2:49–2:50 — narrative parallel: The mother's reference to Thy father and I is answered by Jesus' reference to My Father and the necessity of being in what belongs to Him; the parents do not understand the saying. This explicit paternal-reference contrast interprets their exchange without replacing the broader wording with a compulsory house/business rendering or identifying another Mary.
    Luke 2:49 He said to them, “Why were you searching for Me? Did you not know that I must be in what belongs to My Father?”
    Luke 2:50 But they did not understand the saying He spoke to them.

Luke 2:49
He said to them, “Why were you searching for Me? Did you not know that I must be in what belongs to My Father?”
  John 2:16 — teaching parallel: Both connect Jesus' temple activity with His Father. Luke preserves the phrase what belongs to My Father; John explicitly names My Father's house during the cleansing. House is not silently substituted into Luke's supplied wording.
    John 2:16 And He said to those selling the doves, “Take these things away from here. Do not make My Father’s house a house of trade.”

Luke 2:50
But they did not understand the saying He spoke to them.
  Luke 18:34 — same-book narrative pattern: Both notices describe close followers failing to understand Jesus' saying. Here they are His parents after the temple response; later they are disciples after the passion prediction. The repeated incomprehension pattern does not make the sayings or hearers identical.
    Luke 18:34 But they understood none of these things. This saying was hidden from them, and they did not understand what was being said.

Luke 2:51
He went down with them and came to Nazareth, and He remained subject to them. His mother kept all these matters in her heart.
  Ephesians 6:1 — thematic parallel: Jesus' remaining subject to His parents corresponds to the instruction that children obey their parents in the Lord. Ephesians gives a general command; Luke narrates Jesus' childhood conduct and additionally returns to Mary's keeping matters in her heart.
    Ephesians 6:1 Children, obey your parents in the Lord, for this is right.

Luke 2:52
Jesus advanced in wisdom and age, and in favor with God and people.
  1 Samuel 2:26 — allusion: Both childhood summaries describe growth and favor with God or the Lord and with people. Luke adds wisdom and age; Samuel's summary names Samuel and continued growth. This compares the stated childhood pattern without equating the children.
    1 Samuel 2:26 The child Samuel continued to grow and was in favour both with the Lord and with people.

Luke 3:1
In the fifteenth year of Tiberius Caesar’s rule, when Pontius Pilate governed Judea, Herod was tetrarch of Galilee, his brother Philip tetrarch of the region of Iturea and Trachonitis, and Lysanias tetrarch of Abilene,
  Luke 23:6–23:7 — narrative parallel: The dating notice names Pilate over Judea and Herod as Galilee's tetrarch. Later Pilate asks whether Jesus is Galilean and sends Him to Herod on learning that He belongs to Herod's jurisdiction. The later scene illuminates those particular jurisdictional roles; it does not verify the Tiberius-year date or the other rulers and territories in the earlier list.
    Luke 23:6 When Pilate heard this, he asked whether the man was a Galilean.
    Luke 23:7 And learning that He belonged to Herod’s jurisdiction, he sent Him to Herod, who was himself also in Jerusalem in those days.

Luke 3:2
during the high priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas, God’s word came to John, Zechariah’s son, in the wilderness.
