  Mark 1:4 — narrative parallel: Both introduce John in the wilderness at the beginning of his baptismal ministry. Luke additionally names his father, the priestly setting, and God's word coming to him; Mark summarizes his appearing and proclamation.
    Mark 1:4 John appeared, baptizing in the wilderness and proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins.

Luke 3:3
He went into all the region around the Jordan, proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins,
  Mark 1:4–1:5 — narrative parallel: Both describe John's baptism of repentance for forgiveness of sins in the Jordan setting. Luke describes movement through its region; Mark describes people coming to him and confessing sins in the river.
    Mark 1:4 John appeared, baptizing in the wilderness and proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins.
    Mark 1:5 The whole Judean countryside and all the people of Jerusalem were going out to him. They were being baptized by him in the Jordan River, confessing their sins.

Luke 3:4
as it is written in the book of the words of Isaiah the prophet: “A voice of one crying in the wilderness: Prepare the Lord’s way; make His paths straight.
  Isaiah 40:3 — quotation: Luke quotes the wilderness voice commanding preparation of the Lord's way and straight paths. Saints Isaiah says paths of our God where Luke says His paths.
    Isaiah 40:3 A voice of one crying in the wilderness: Prepare the way of the Lord; make straight the paths of our God.

Luke 3:5
Every ravine will be filled, and every mountain and hill brought low. The crooked will become straight, and the rough ways smooth roads,
  Isaiah 40:4 — quotation: Both announce filled valleys, lowered mountains and hills, straightened crooked places, and smoothed rough terrain. Ravine, roads, country, and plains remain the supplied English expressions rather than a harmonized quotation.
    Isaiah 40:4 Every valley will be filled, and every mountain and hill will be brought low; all the crooked places will become straight, and the rough country will become plains.

Luke 3:6
and all flesh will see God’s salvation.”
  Isaiah 40:5 — quotation: All flesh seeing God's salvation repeats the corresponding Isaiah clause. Isaiah also mentions the Lord's glory and His spoken word, which Luke's supplied excerpt does not repeat.
    Isaiah 40:5 And the glory of the Lord will be seen, and all flesh will see the salvation of God; for the Lord has spoken.

Luke 3:7
So he said to the crowds coming out to be baptized by him, “Brood of vipers! Who warned you to flee the coming wrath?
  Matthew 3:7 — teaching parallel: Both give John's brood-of-vipers address and question about fleeing coming wrath. Luke addresses crowds coming for baptism; Matthew specifically names Pharisees and Sadducees. The audiences are not silently equated.
    Matthew 3:7 When he saw many of the Pharisees and Sadducees coming to his baptism, he said to them, “Brood of vipers! Who warned you to flee from the coming wrath?

Luke 3:8
Produce fruits worthy of repentance, and do not begin saying among yourselves, ‘We have Abraham as father.’ For I tell you that God can raise up children for Abraham from these stones.
  Matthew 3:8–3:9 — teaching parallel: Both require repentance-worthy fruit and reject reliance on Abrahamic ancestry, saying God can raise Abraham's children from stones. Luke uses fruits and Matthew fruit; the shared argument remains clear.
    Matthew 3:8 Bear fruit, then, worthy of repentance,
    Matthew 3:9 and do not suppose you can say among yourselves, ‘We have Abraham as father.’ For I tell you that God can raise up children for Abraham from these stones.

Luke 3:9
Already the axe also lies at the root of the trees. Therefore every tree that does not produce good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.”
  Matthew 3:10 — teaching parallel: Both place the axe at the trees' root and warn that a tree without good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.
    Matthew 3:10 Already the axe lies at the root of the trees. Every tree, therefore, that does not bear good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.

Luke 3:10
The crowds asked him, “What then should we do?”
  Acts 2:37–2:38 — thematic parallel: Both have listeners ask what they should do in response to preaching. Acts names Peter and the apostles and answers with repentance and baptism; Luke's John scene develops practical ethical answers. These are different audiences and occasions.
    Acts 2:37 When they heard this, they were pierced to the heart and said to Peter and the other apostles, “Men, brothers, what should we do?”
    Acts 2:38 Peter said to them, “Repent, and let each one of you be baptized upon the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of your sins, and you will receive the gift of the Holy Spirit.

Luke 3:11
He answered them, “Let the one who has two tunics share with the one who has none, and let the one who has food do likewise.”
  Isaiah 58:7 — teaching parallel: Both require practical provision of food and clothing to people in need. Luke specifies sharing a second tunic and food; Isaiah names hungry and naked people and additionally includes housing the homeless.
    Isaiah 58:7 Break your bread for the hungry and bring homeless poor people into your house. If you see someone naked, clothe him; do not overlook those belonging to your own family.

Luke 3:12
Tax collectors also came to be baptized and said to him, “Teacher, what should we do?”
  Luke 7:29 — narrative parallel: Luke's later reflection explicitly includes tax collectors baptized by John, corresponding to their coming for baptism here. The later verse does not repeat their question, so this is a same-book ministry link rather than independent testimony to the dialogue.
    Luke 7:29 When all the people and the tax collectors heard, they acknowledged God’s righteousness, having been baptized with John’s baptism.

Luke 3:13
He said to them, “Collect nothing beyond what has been appointed for you.”
  Luke 19:8 — thematic parallel: John forbids collection beyond authorized amounts; Zacchaeus, identified as a chief tax collector in that later scene, promises fourfold restitution for anything extorted. Both address unjust taking in tax-collection settings, without equating their speakers or occasions.
    Luke 19:8 But Zacchaeus stood and said to the Lord, “Behold, Lord, I give half of my possessions to the poor, and if I have extorted anything from anyone, I repay it fourfold.”

Luke 3:14
Soldiers also asked him, “And we, what should we do?” He said to them, “Extort from no one, do not make false accusations, and be content with your pay.”
  Hebrews 13:5 — teaching parallel: Both require contentment rather than acquisitive conduct. Luke applies it to soldiers' pay alongside bans on extortion and false accusation; Hebrews calls for a disposition free from love of money and contentment, without repeating those military-specific instructions.
    Hebrews 13:5 Let your way of life be free from the love of money, being content with what you have; for He Himself has said, “I will certainly not leave you, nor will I ever forsake you.”

Luke 3:15
As the people were waiting expectantly and everyone was considering in their hearts whether John might be the Christ,
  John 1:19–1:20 — narrative parallel: Both raise the question of John's identity relative to the Christ. Luke describes the people's inward speculation; John narrates emissaries questioning him and his explicit denial. The questioners and modes of inquiry differ.
    John 1:19 And this is the testimony of John, when the Jews sent priests and Levites from Jerusalem to him, to ask him, “Who are you?”
    John 1:20 And he confessed and did not deny, and confessed, “I am not the Christ.”

Luke 3:16
John answered them all, “I baptize you with water, but One stronger than I is coming, whose sandal strap I am not worthy to untie. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.
  Mark 1:7–1:8 — teaching parallel: Both announce a stronger coming One, John's unworthiness to untie His sandal strap, and water baptism contrasted with Holy Spirit baptism. Luke adds fire; Mark's supplied wording does not.
    Mark 1:7 He proclaimed, saying, “The One stronger than I is coming after me. I am not worthy to stoop down and untie the strap of His sandals.
    Mark 1:8 I baptized you with water, but He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit.”

Luke 3:17
His winnowing fork is in His hand, to cleanse His threshing floor thoroughly and gather the wheat into His storehouse; but the chaff He will burn with unquenchable fire.”
  Matthew 3:12 — teaching parallel: Both picture the coming One clearing a threshing floor, storing wheat, and burning chaff with unquenchable fire. Winnowing fork and shovel remain the edition's distinct English terms.
    Matthew 3:12 His winnowing shovel is in His hand, and He will thoroughly clear His threshing floor and gather His wheat into the storehouse; but He will burn the chaff with unquenchable fire.”

Luke 3:18
With many other exhortations also, he announced good news to the people.
  Acts 13:24 — narrative parallel: Both summarize John's proclamation to the people. Luke calls it good news with many exhortations; Acts specifically describes repentance-baptism proclamation to Israel before Jesus' coming, not an exact repetition of every exhortation.
    Acts 13:24 after John had proclaimed, before His coming, a baptism of repentance to all the people of Israel.

Luke 3:19
But Herod the tetrarch, rebuked by him concerning Herodias, his brother’s wife, and all the evil things Herod had done,
  Mark 6:17–6:18 — narrative parallel: Both connect John's rebuke of Herod with Herodias, his brother's wife. Mark names Philip and quotes the marriage objection; Luke additionally refers to all Herod's evil acts without listing them here.
    Mark 6:17 For Herod himself had sent men to seize John and bind him in prison because of Herodias, the wife of his brother Philip, because he had married her.
    Mark 6:18 For John had been saying to Herod, “It is not lawful for you to have your brother’s wife.”

Luke 3:20
added this also to them all: he shut John up in prison.
  Mark 6:17 — narrative parallel: Both attribute John's imprisonment to Herod. Mark narrates seizure and binding in the Herodias context; Luke frames the imprisonment as an additional evil after the rebuke.
    Mark 6:17 For Herod himself had sent men to seize John and bind him in prison because of Herodias, the wife of his brother Philip, because he had married her.

Luke 3:21
When all the people were baptized, and Jesus also had been baptized and was praying, heaven was opened,
  Matthew 3:16 — narrative parallel: Both place an opening of heaven after Jesus' baptism. Luke explicitly describes Jesus praying with the people baptized; Matthew describes His immediate emergence from the water without the prayer detail.
    Matthew 3:16 When Jesus was baptized, He immediately came up from the water. And behold, the heavens were opened, and He saw the Spirit of God descending like a dove and coming upon Him.

Luke 3:22
and the Holy Spirit descended upon Him in bodily form like a dove. A voice came from heaven: “Thou art My beloved Son; in Thee I have taken delight.”
  Mark 1:10–1:11 — narrative parallel: Both describe dove-like Spirit descent and direct heavenly address to the beloved Son. Luke says upon Him in bodily form; Mark says into Him. Delight and well pleased remain the supplied voice wordings.
    Mark 1:10 Immediately as He came up out of the water, He saw the heavens being torn open and the Spirit descending like a dove into Him.
    Mark 1:11 A voice came from the heavens: “Thou art My beloved Son; in Thee I am well pleased.”

Luke 3:23
Jesus Himself was about thirty years old when He began His ministry. He was the son, as was supposed, of Joseph, of Heli,
  Matthew 1:16 — genealogy: Both connect Jesus with Joseph while qualifying the relationship. Luke says as was supposed and names Heli; Matthew calls Joseph Mary's husband, names Jacob before Joseph, and says Jesus was born of Mary. Neither a maternal-line solution nor an identity of Heli and Jacob is asserted.
    Matthew 1:16 and Jacob begot Joseph, the husband of Mary, of whom was born Jesus, who is called Christ.

Luke 3:27
of Joanan, of Rhesa, of Zerubbabel, of Shealtiel, of Neri,
  Matthew 1:12–1:13 — genealogy: Both place Zerubbabel next to Shealtiel within Jesus' genealogy. Luke continues toward Neri and through Rhesa; Matthew toward Jechoniah and through Abiud. This compares the overlapping names and differing branches without declaring the full ancestries identical.
    Matthew 1:12 After the deportation to Babylon, Jechoniah begot Shealtiel; Shealtiel begot Zerubbabel;
    Matthew 1:13 Zerubbabel begot Abiud; Abiud begot Eliakim; Eliakim begot Azor;

Luke 3:31
of Melea, of Menna, of Mattatha, of Nathan, of David,
  2 Samuel 5:13–5:14 — genealogy: Samuel names Nathan among David's Jerusalem-born sons, corresponding to Luke's Nathan-David link. It does not independently name Melea, Menna, or Mattatha or establish their intervening links, and Nathan is not identified merely by sharing a prophet's name.
    2 Samuel 5:13 David took more wives and concubines from Jerusalem after coming from Hebron, and David had more sons and daughters.
    2 Samuel 5:14 These are the names of those born to him in Jerusalem: Shammua, Shobab, Nathan, and Solomon;
  Matthew 1:6 — genealogy: Both genealogies include David but take different onward branches: Luke names Nathan, while Matthew follows David through Solomon. The comparison records the difference rather than making the branches one or inventing a reconciliation.
    Matthew 1:6 Jesse begot David the king. David begot Solomon by the wife of Uriah;

Luke 3:32
of Jesse, of Obed, of Boaz, of Sala, of Nahshon,
  Ruth 4:20–4:22 — genealogy: The genealogies overlap in Nahshon, Boaz, Obed, and Jesse in the same succession, read in opposite directions. Luke has Sala where Ruth has Salman; that actual name difference is preserved rather than silently renamed.
    Ruth 4:20 and Amminadab begot Nahshon, and Nahshon begot Salman,
    Ruth 4:21 and Salman begot Boaz, and Boaz begot Obed,
    Ruth 4:22 and Obed begot Jesse, and Jesse begot David.

Luke 3:33
of Amminadab, of Admin, of Arni, of Hezron, of Perez, of Judah,
  Ruth 4:18–4:20 — genealogy: Both connect Perez, Hezron, and Amminadab in the ancestral sequence. Luke includes Admin and Arni between Amminadab and Hezron, while Ruth has Arran there. The two Luke names are not treated as automatically one name-form of Arran or Ram.
    Ruth 4:18 Now these are the generations of Perez: Perez begot Hezron,
    Ruth 4:19 and Hezron begot Arran, and Arran begot Amminadab,
    Ruth 4:20 and Amminadab begot Nahshon, and Nahshon begot Salman,

Luke 3:34
of Jacob, of Isaac, of Abraham, of Terah, of Nahor,
  Genesis 11:24–11:26 — genealogy: Genesis gives the Nahor-Terah-Abram succession corresponding to Luke's reversed Nahor-Terah-Abraham portion. The supplied Abram and Abraham forms remain visible; this target does not independently establish Luke's Jacob-Isaac portion.
    Genesis 11:24 And Nahor lived seventy-nine years, and begot Terah.
    Genesis 11:25 And Nahor lived one hundred and twenty-nine years after he begot Terah, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
    Genesis 11:26 And Terah lived seventy years, and begot Abram, Nahor, and Haran.
  Matthew 1:2 — genealogy: Both genealogies connect Abraham with Isaac and Jacob. Matthew continues to Judah and his brothers; Luke's verse continues farther back through Terah and Nahor. This pair supports the shared patriarchal portion without asserting identical complete genealogies.
    Matthew 1:2 Abraham begot Isaac; and Isaac begot Jacob; and Jacob begot Judah and his brothers;

Luke 3:35
of Serug, of Reu, of Peleg, of Eber, of Shelah,
  Genesis 11:14–11:20 — genealogy: Genesis explicitly gives the Shelah-Eber-Peleg-Reu-Serug succession, which Luke lists in reverse direction. The Genesis age statements are preserved in the target but are not turned into ages claimed by Luke.
    Genesis 11:14 And Shelah lived one hundred and thirty years, and begot Eber.
    Genesis 11:15 And Shelah lived three hundred and thirty years after he begot Eber, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
    Genesis 11:16 And Eber lived one hundred and thirty-four years, and begot Peleg.
    Genesis 11:17 And Eber lived three hundred and seventy years after he begot Peleg, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
    Genesis 11:18 And Peleg lived one hundred and thirty years, and begot Reu.
    Genesis 11:19 And Peleg lived two hundred and nine years after he begot Reu, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
    Genesis 11:20 And Reu lived one hundred and thirty-two years, and begot Serug.

Luke 3:36
of Cainan, of Arphaxad, of Shem, of Noah, of Lamech,
  Genesis 11:10–11:13 — genealogy: Genesis gives Shem, Arphaxad, and Cainan, with Cainan fathering Shelah inside the original 11:13 unit. This matches the post-Flood Cainan position across Luke 3:35–36; it is not confused with the pre-Flood Cainan in Luke 3:37.
    Genesis 11:10 These are the generations of Shem. Shem was one hundred years old when he begot Arphaxad, in the second year after the flood.
    Genesis 11:11 And Shem lived five hundred years after he begot Arphaxad, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
    Genesis 11:12 And Arphaxad lived one hundred and thirty-five years, and begot Cainan.
    Genesis 11:13 And Arphaxad lived four hundred and thirty years after he begot Cainan, and begot sons and daughters, and died. And Cainan lived one hundred and thirty years, and begot Shelah. And Cainan lived three hundred and thirty years after he begot Shelah, and begot sons and daughters, and died.
  1 Chronicles 1:3–1:4 — genealogy: Chronicles lists Lamech, Noah, and Noah's son Shem, corresponding to that portion of Luke's reverse ancestry. The target does not supply Luke's post-Flood Cainan, so it is not treated as confirming every name in the verse.
    1 Chronicles 1:3 Enoch, Methuselah, Lamech,
    1 Chronicles 1:4 Noah. Noah’s sons: Shem, Ham, Japheth.

Luke 3:37
of Methuselah, of Enoch, of Jared, of Mahalalel, of Cainan,
  1 Chronicles 1:2–1:3 — genealogy: Both lists contain the pre-Flood sequence through Mahalalel, Jared, Enoch, and Methuselah, with Luke read in reverse. Chronicles has Kenan where Luke has Cainan; the name form is preserved and not confused with the post-Flood Cainan linked to Arphaxad in Luke 3:36.
    1 Chronicles 1:2 Kenan, Mahalalel, Jared,
    1 Chronicles 1:3 Enoch, Methuselah, Lamech,

Luke 3:38
of Enosh, of Seth, of Adam, of God.
  Genesis 5:1–5:6 — genealogy: Genesis describes God creating Adam, Adam fathering Seth, and Seth fathering Enosh, corresponding to Luke's final reverse ancestry. Genesis's creation wording is not recast as biological generation by God merely because Luke ends with of God.
    Genesis 5:1 This is the book of the generations of mankind. On the day when God made Adam, He made him in the image of God.
    Genesis 5:2 Male and female He made them, and He blessed them, and named them Adam on the day when He made them.
    Genesis 5:3 And Adam lived two hundred and thirty years, and begot a son in his own likeness and image, and named him Seth.
    Genesis 5:4 And the days of Adam after he begot Seth were seven hundred years, and he begot sons and daughters.
    Genesis 5:5 And all the days that Adam lived were nine hundred and thirty years, and he died.
    Genesis 5:6 And Seth lived two hundred and five years, and begot Enosh.

Luke 4:1
Jesus, full of the Holy Spirit, returned from the Jordan and was led in the Spirit in the wilderness
  Matthew 4:1 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Jesus being led by the Spirit into the wilderness for testing by the devil. Luke additionally describes His fullness of the Spirit and return from the Jordan.
    Matthew 4:1 Then Jesus was led up into the wilderness by the Spirit to be tempted by the devil.

Luke 4:2
for forty days, being tested by the devil. He ate nothing during those days, and when they were completed He was hungry.
  Matthew 4:2 — narrative parallel: Both place hunger after forty days without food. Matthew explicitly adds forty nights; Luke describes the testing during those days and says He ate nothing.
    Matthew 4:2 After fasting forty days and forty nights, He became hungry.

Luke 4:3
The devil said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, tell this stone to become bread.”
  Matthew 4:3 — narrative parallel: Both temptations challenge Jesus as God's Son to make bread from stone. Luke speaks of this stone; Matthew speaks of these stones.
    Matthew 4:3 The tempter came and said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, command these stones to become loaves of bread.”

Luke 4:4
Jesus answered him, “It is written: ‘A human being shall not live on bread alone.’”
  Deuteronomy 8:3 — quotation: Jesus cites the teaching that a human does not live on bread alone. Deuteronomy continues with every word proceeding through God's mouth and its manna setting; Luke's supplied answer ends after bread alone.
    Deuteronomy 8:3 And He afflicted you, and made you hunger, and fed you with manna, which your fathers did not know, that He might declare unto you that man shall not live by bread alone, but man shall live by every word that proceeds through the mouth of God.

Luke 4:5
Taking Him up, the devil showed Him all the kingdoms of the inhabited world in a moment of time.
  Matthew 4:8 — narrative parallel: Both describe the devil showing the world's kingdoms and their glory in Matthew's wording. Matthew specifies a very high mountain; Luke specifies a moment of time. This test occupies a different position in the two accounts.
    Matthew 4:8 Again the devil took Him to an exceedingly high mountain and showed Him all the kingdoms of the world and their glory.

Luke 4:6
The devil said to Him, “I will give Thee all this authority and their glory, because it has been handed over to me, and I give it to whomever I wish.
  Matthew 4:9 — narrative parallel: Both contain the devil's offer to give Jesus the displayed kingdoms. Luke additionally claims that their authority and glory were handed over to the devil and that he gives them at will; Matthew does not repeat that claim.
    Matthew 4:9 He said to Him, “I will give Thee all these things if Thou fall down and worship me.”

Luke 4:7
Therefore, if Thou dost bow down before me, it will all be Thine.”
  Matthew 4:9 — narrative parallel: Both make the devil's offered gift conditional on Jesus bowing down to worship him. Luke's wording is before me; Matthew also says falling down. No truth is granted to the tempter's claims.
    Matthew 4:9 He said to Him, “I will give Thee all these things if Thou fall down and worship me.”

Luke 4:8
Jesus answered him, “It is written: ‘You shall worship the Lord your God, and Him alone shall you serve.’”
  Deuteronomy 6:13 — quotation: Jesus' answer invokes worship and exclusive service of the Lord. The supplied Deuteronomy commands fearing and serving Him, clinging to Him and swearing by His name. The formulations differ; Luke is not presented as reproducing every clause.
    Deuteronomy 6:13 You shall fear the Lord your God, and you shall serve Him, and you shall cleave unto Him, and you shall swear by His name.

Luke 4:9
He brought Him to Jerusalem, placed Him on the temple’s pinnacle, and said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, throw Thyself down from here,
  Matthew 4:5–4:6 — narrative parallel: Both describe the temple-pinnacle challenge to God's Son to throw Himself down. Luke names Jerusalem and puts this test after the kingdom offer; Matthew says the holy city and places it earlier.
    Matthew 4:5 Then the devil took Him into the holy city and set Him on the pinnacle of the temple,
    Matthew 4:6 and said to Him, “If Thou art God’s Son, throw Thyself down. For it is written: ‘He will command His angels concerning Thee,’ and ‘They will lift Thee on their hands, lest Thou strike Thy foot against a stone.’”

Luke 4:10
for it is written: ‘He will command His angels concerning Thee, to guard Thee,’
  Psalms 90:11 — quotation: The tempter cites God's command to angels concerning the one guarded. The psalm additionally speaks of guarding in all the person's ways; Luke's supplied citation does not repeat that phrase.
    Psalms 90:11 For He will command His angels concerning you, to guard you in all your ways.

Luke 4:11
and, ‘They will lift Thee upon their hands, lest Thou strike Thy foot against a stone.’”
  Psalms 90:12 — quotation: Both describe angels lifting the person upon their hands so that the foot does not strike a stone. The psalm is here cited within the devil's test; citation does not endorse the proposed jump.
    Psalms 90:12 On their hands they will lift you, lest you strike your foot against a stone.

Luke 4:12
Jesus answered him, “It has been said: ‘You shall not put the Lord your God to the test.’”
  Deuteronomy 6:16 — quotation: Jesus answers with the prohibition on putting the Lord to the test. Deuteronomy additionally recalls testing Him at Testing in its supplied wording; Luke's answer does not repeat that historical clause.
    Deuteronomy 6:16 You shall not put the Lord your God to the test, as you tested Him at Testing.

Luke 4:13
Having completed every test, the devil departed from Him until an opportune time.
  Matthew 4:11 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the temptation scene with the devil leaving Jesus. Luke adds until an opportune time; Matthew adds angels arriving and ministering. Those additions are not treated as the same statement.
    Matthew 4:11 Then the devil left Him, and behold, angels came and ministered to Him.

Luke 4:14
Jesus returned in the Spirit’s power into Galilee, and a report about Him spread throughout the whole surrounding region.
  Matthew 4:12 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus' return or withdrawal into Galilee after the preceding introductory scenes. Luke emphasizes the Spirit's power and spreading report; Matthew mentions hearing that John had been handed over. Neither account is made to supply the other's stated reason.
    Matthew 4:12 When He heard that John had been handed over, He withdrew to Galilee.

Luke 4:15
He taught in their synagogues, being glorified by everyone.
  Matthew 4:23 — narrative parallel: Both summarize Jesus teaching in synagogues during His Galilean activity. Luke adds universal praise; Matthew adds kingdom proclamation and healing. The added clauses are not attributed to both verses.
    Matthew 4:23 He went throughout Galilee, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the good news of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every infirmity among the people.

Luke 4:16
He came to Nazareth, where He had been brought up, and according to His custom entered the synagogue on the Sabbath day. He stood up to read.
  Mark 6:1–6:2 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus teaching in His homeland synagogue on a Sabbath. Luke explicitly names Nazareth, His upbringing, custom and standing to read; Mark mentions His disciples and the hearers' astonishment. No merged chronology of the visits is imposed.
    Mark 6:1 He left there and came to His hometown, and His disciples followed Him.
    Mark 6:2 When the Sabbath came, He began teaching in the synagogue. The many who heard Him were astonished, saying, “Where did this Man get these things? What is this wisdom given to Him? And such mighty works taking place through His hands!

Luke 4:17
The scroll of the prophet Isaiah was given to Him. Unrolling it, He found the place where it was written:
  Isaiah 61:1–61:2 — quotation introduction: Luke identifies the scroll as Isaiah before the quotation in the following verses. Isaiah supplies the anointing, good news, release, sight and acceptable-year language; Luke's scroll-handling detail is not independently narrated there.
    Isaiah 61:1 The Lord’s spirit is upon me, because He anointed me. He sent me to bring good news to the poor, to heal the brokenhearted, to proclaim release to captives and recovery of sight to the blind,
    Isaiah 61:2 to announce the Lord’s acceptable year and a day of recompense, to comfort all who mourn,

Luke 4:18
“The Spirit of the Lord is upon Me, because He anointed Me to proclaim good news to the poor. He has sent Me to proclaim release to captives and recovery of sight to the blind, to send the oppressed away in release,
  Isaiah 61:1 — quotation: Both speak of the Lord's Spirit, anointing, good news to the poor, release to captives and sight for the blind. Isaiah also mentions healing the brokenhearted; Luke's supplied verse instead continues with releasing the oppressed.
    Isaiah 61:1 The Lord’s spirit is upon me, because He anointed me. He sent me to bring good news to the poor, to heal the brokenhearted, to proclaim release to captives and recovery of sight to the blind,
  Isaiah 58:6 — allusion: Luke's sending the oppressed away in release corresponds to Isaiah's instruction to release the crushed among its commands to loosen injustice. This clause-level connection does not make the entire Nazareth reading identical to Isaiah58.
    Isaiah 58:6 ‘It is not such a fast that I chose,’ says the Lord. ‘Instead, loosen every bond of injustice, undo the strangling ties of coercive agreements, release the crushed, and tear up every unjust written contract.

Luke 4:19
to proclaim the Lord’s acceptable year.”
  Isaiah 61:2 — quotation: Luke's proclamation of the Lord's acceptable year corresponds to Isaiah's acceptable-year announcement. Isaiah continues with recompense and comfort; Luke's reading stops before those additional clauses.
    Isaiah 61:2 to announce the Lord’s acceptable year and a day of recompense, to comfort all who mourn,

Luke 4:20
He rolled up the scroll, gave it back to the attendant, and sat down. The eyes of everyone in the synagogue were fixed on Him.
  Luke 4:17 — narrative parallel: Receiving and unrolling the Isaiah scroll in the earlier unit is matched by rolling it up and returning it here. The concrete object and reversed actions frame this reading before its interpretation. This internal frame does not independently establish a universal synagogue protocol or explain the whole congregation's inner thoughts.
    Luke 4:17 The scroll of the prophet Isaiah was given to Him. Unrolling it, He found the place where it was written:

Luke 4:21
He began to say to them, “Today this Scripture has been fulfilled in your ears.”
  Isaiah 61:1–61:2 — promise and fulfillment: In the context of His Isaiah reading, Jesus declares the Scripture fulfilled today. The referenced Isaiah passage supplies the anointing and mission language He has just read; the fulfillment claim is Luke's presentation of Jesus' declaration.
    Isaiah 61:1 The Lord’s spirit is upon me, because He anointed me. He sent me to bring good news to the poor, to heal the brokenhearted, to proclaim release to captives and recovery of sight to the blind,
    Isaiah 61:2 to announce the Lord’s acceptable year and a day of recompense, to comfort all who mourn,

Luke 4:22
All bore witness to Him and wondered at the words of grace coming from His mouth. They said, “Is this not Joseph’s son?”
  Mark 6:2–6:3 — narrative parallel: Both hometown scenes combine wonder at Jesus with reference to His familiar family. Luke asks whether He is Joseph's son; Mark calls Him the carpenter and Mary's son and names other relatives. The family descriptions are retained as different formulations.
    Mark 6:2 When the Sabbath came, He began teaching in the synagogue. The many who heard Him were astonished, saying, “Where did this Man get these things? What is this wisdom given to Him? And such mighty works taking place through His hands!
    Mark 6:3 Is this not the carpenter, the Son of Mary and brother of James, Joses, Judas, and Simon? Are not His sisters here with us?” They took offense at Him.

Luke 4:23
He said to them, “Surely you will tell Me this proverb: ‘Physician, heal yourself.’ ‘Do here in Thy homeland also everything we heard happened in Capernaum.’”
  Matthew 27:41–27:42 — thematic parallel: The physician-heal-yourself proverb anticipates a demand that Jesus demonstrate at home the works heard of elsewhere. At the cross the mockers similarly contrast His saving others with an alleged inability to save Himself. This compares the challenge to prove power on one's own behalf, with hometown demands and crucifixion mockery kept distinct; neither proverb nor mockery is endorsed as true.
    Matthew 27:41 Likewise the chief priests, mocking with the scribes and elders, said,
    Matthew 27:42 “He saved others; Himself He cannot save. He is King of Israel! Let Him come down from the cross now, and we will believe in Him.

Luke 4:24
He said, “Amen, I tell you, no prophet is acceptable in his homeland.
  Mark 6:4 — teaching parallel: Both state a prophet's lack of honor or acceptance in his homeland. Mark additionally mentions relatives and household; Luke's supplied saying concerns the homeland.
    Mark 6:4 Jesus said to them, “A prophet is not without honor except in his hometown, among his relatives, and in his own house.”

Luke 4:25
But in truth I tell you, there were many widows in Israel in Elijah’s days, when heaven was shut for three years and six months and a great famine came over the whole land.
  James 5:17 — allusion: Both associate Elijah with a rainless period of three years and six months. Luke emphasizes the closed heaven and resulting famine; James emphasizes Elijah's prayer and human likeness. James does not repeat Luke's widow comparison.
    James 5:17 Elijah was a human being with experiences like ours, and he prayed earnestly that it might not rain; and it did not rain upon the earth for three years and six months.

Luke 4:26
Yet Elijah was sent to none of them except to a widow at Zarephath in Sidon’s territory.
  1 Kings 17:9 — allusion: Jesus' reference to Elijah being sent to the Zarephath widow corresponds to God's command that he go there, where a widow was appointed to sustain him. The place-name forms remain those of the supplied passages.
    1 Kings 17:9 “Rise and go to Zarephath in Sidonian territory. Behold, I have commanded a widow there to sustain you.”

Luke 4:27
And there were many leprous people in Israel in the time of Elisha the prophet, but none of them was cleansed except Naaman the Syrian.”
  2 Kings 5:14–5:15 — allusion: Luke recalls Naaman's cleansing in Elisha's time; Kings narrates his washing, restored flesh and return to the man of God. Kings' account supplies this cleansing, not an exhaustive claim about every leprous person in Israel.
    2 Kings 5:14 And Naaman went down, and dipped himself in the Jordan seven times, according to the word of Elisha; and his flesh was restored as the flesh of a little child, and he was cleansed.
    2 Kings 5:15 He returned to Elisha, he and all his company. He came and stood and said, ‘Behold, now I know that there is no God in all the earth except in Israel. Now accept the blessing from your slave.’

Luke 4:28
Everyone in the synagogue was filled with anger when they heard these things.
  Acts 22:21–22:22 — narrative parallel: The synagogue becomes angry after Jesus' examples of aid to the Sidonian widow and the Syrian Naaman; Acts' crowd turns against Paul at the reported commission to Gentiles. Both scenes place a hostile response after speech about God's activity beyond Israel. The speakers, audiences, and occasions differ, and this does not reduce every listener's motives to an unstated explanation.
    Acts 22:21 He said to me, ‘Go, for I will send you far away to the Gentiles.’”
    Acts 22:22 They listened to him until this statement, then raised their voice and said, “Remove such a man from the earth, for he ought not to live.”

Luke 4:29
They rose, threw Him out of the city, and led Him to the brow of the hill on which their city was built, intending to hurl Him down.
  Acts 7:57–7:58 — narrative parallel: Luke's crowd takes Jesus outside the city and toward a hill to hurl Him down; Acts' crowd rushes Stephen and drives him outside the city to stone him. The concrete pattern is hostile collective expulsion for lethal violence. The persons, cities, methods, and outcomes differ; Stephen's stoning does not establish that Jesus was thrown from the hill.
    Acts 7:57 They cried out with a loud voice, stopped their ears, and rushed upon him together.
    Acts 7:58 They drove him outside the city and began stoning him. The witnesses laid their garments at the feet of a young man called Saul.

Luke 4:30
But He passed through their midst and went on His way.
  John 10:39 — narrative parallel: Luke says Jesus passes through the hostile crowd and departs; John says those seeking to seize Him do not retain Him and He goes out from their hand. These are distinct narrated escapes from hostile attempts to take Him. John does not reproduce the hilltop method, and neither account is used to add a miraculous mechanism or an identical occasion to the other.
    John 10:39 So they sought again to seize Him, and He went out from their hand.

Luke 4:31
He went down to Capernaum, a city of Galilee, and was teaching them on the Sabbaths.
  Mark 1:21 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus teaching in the Capernaum synagogue on the Sabbath. Luke says He went down to Capernaum and names it a Galilean city; Mark describes entry into the synagogue.
    Mark 1:21 They entered Capernaum, and immediately on the Sabbath He went into the synagogue and taught.

Luke 4:32
They were overwhelmed at His teaching, because His word was with authority.
  Mark 1:22 — narrative parallel: Both describe astonishment at Jesus' authoritative teaching. Mark contrasts Him with the scribes; Luke directly says His word possessed authority.
    Mark 1:22 They were astonished at His teaching, for He taught them as one having authority, and not as the scribes.

Luke 4:33
In the synagogue was a man who had the spirit of an unclean demon. He cried out with a loud voice,
  Mark 1:23 — narrative parallel: Both introduce a man with an unclean spirit crying out in the synagogue. Luke describes the cry as loud and calls the spirit an unclean demon; Mark's introduction is shorter.
    Mark 1:23 Immediately there was in their synagogue a man with an unclean spirit, and he cried out,

Luke 4:34
“Ah! What have we to do with Thee, Jesus of Nazareth? Hast Thou come to destroy us? I know who Thou art: God’s Holy One.”
  Mark 1:24 — narrative parallel: Both give the spirit's challenge to Jesus of Nazareth, question about destruction and recognition of Him as God's Holy One.
    Mark 1:24 “What have we to do with Thee, Jesus of Nazareth? Hast Thou come to destroy us? I know who Thou art—the Holy One of God!”

Luke 4:35
Jesus rebuked him, saying, “Be silent and come out of him!” The demon threw him down in their midst and came out of him without harming him.
  Mark 1:25–1:26 — narrative parallel: Both contain Jesus' silencing-and-expulsion command followed by the spirit leaving. Luke describes the man thrown down without injury; Mark describes convulsion and a loud cry. The physical descriptions are not silently made identical.
    Mark 1:25 Jesus rebuked him, saying, “Be muzzled, and come out of him!”
    Mark 1:26 The unclean spirit convulsed him, cried out with a loud voice, and came out of him.

Luke 4:36
Amazement came upon everyone, and they said to one another, “What is this word? With authority and power He commands the unclean spirits, and they come out!”
  Mark 1:27 — narrative parallel: Both report amazement at the authority with which Jesus commands unclean spirits and their obedience or departure. Mark additionally calls it new teaching; Luke also names power.
    Mark 1:27 They were all amazed, so that they discussed among themselves, saying, “What is this? A new teaching! With authority He commands even the unclean spirits, and they obey Him.”

Luke 4:37
A report about Him went out into every place in the surrounding region.
  Mark 1:28 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the synagogue expulsion with a report spreading through the surrounding region. Mark explicitly calls the region Galilee; Luke's immediate context places the event in Galilean Capernaum.
    Mark 1:28 Immediately the report about Him went out everywhere through the whole surrounding region of Galilee.

Luke 4:38
Leaving the synagogue, He entered Simon’s house. Simon’s mother-in-law was gripped by a high fever, and they asked Him about her.
  Mark 1:29–1:30 — narrative parallel: Both move from the synagogue to Simon's house and describe his mother-in-law's fever being reported to Jesus. Mark also names Andrew, James and John; Luke calls the fever high.
    Mark 1:29 Immediately after leaving the synagogue, they went into the house of Simon and Andrew, with James and John.
    Mark 1:30 Simon’s mother-in-law was lying down with a fever, and immediately they told Him about her.

Luke 4:39
Standing over her, He rebuked the fever, and it left her. Immediately she rose and served them.
  Mark 1:31 — narrative parallel: Both describe the fever leaving and Simon's mother-in-law serving. Luke has Jesus rebuke the fever while standing over her; Mark has Him take her hand and raise her. The actions are preserved separately.
    Mark 1:31 He came to her, took her by the hand, and raised her up. The fever left her, and she served them.

Luke 4:40
As the sun was setting, everyone who had people sick with various diseases brought them to Him. He laid His hands on each one and healed them.
  Mark 1:32–1:34 — narrative parallel: Both place the bringing of sick people and healings at sunset or evening. Luke describes laying hands on each and healing them; Mark includes the gathered city, many healings and expulsions without those individual hand details.
    Mark 1:32 When evening came, after the sun had set, they brought to Him all who were ill and those possessed by demons.
    Mark 1:33 The whole city had gathered at the door.
    Mark 1:34 He healed many who were ill with various diseases and cast out many demons. He would not allow the demons to speak, because they knew Him.

Luke 4:41
Demons also came out of many, crying, “Thou art God’s Son!” He rebuked them and would not let them speak, because they knew He was the Christ.
  Mark 1:34 — narrative parallel: Both describe demons expelled and forbidden to speak because they knew Jesus. Luke supplies their Son-of-God cry and identifies their recognition as His being the Christ; Mark does not quote that cry here.
    Mark 1:34 He healed many who were ill with various diseases and cast out many demons. He would not allow the demons to speak, because they knew Him.

Luke 4:42
When day came, He went out to a deserted place. The crowds searched for Him, came to Him, and tried to keep Him from departing from them.
  Mark 1:35–1:37 — narrative parallel: Both describe departure to a deserted place and a search for Jesus. Mark says it was very early, He prayed, and Simon's party searched; Luke says day came and crowds tried to prevent His departure. The searchers and details differ.
    Mark 1:35 Very early, while it was still dark, He arose, went out, and departed to a deserted place, and there He prayed.
    Mark 1:36 Simon and those with him searched for Him
    Mark 1:37 and found Him. They said to Him, “Everyone is looking for Thee.”

Luke 4:43
But He said to them, “I must announce the good news of God’s kingdom to the other cities also, because for this I was sent.”
  Mark 1:38 — narrative parallel: Both explain that Jesus must proclaim elsewhere as the purpose of His coming or mission. Luke names God's kingdom and other cities; Mark names neighboring towns and the reason He came out.
    Mark 1:38 He said to them, “Let us go elsewhere, into the neighboring towns, so that I may proclaim there also, for that is why I came out.”

Luke 4:44
And He continued proclaiming in the synagogues of Judea.
  Mark 1:39 — narrative parallel: Both summarize proclamation in synagogues. Luke's supplied text says Judea; Mark says all Galilee and also mentions demon expulsion. The different geographical wording is preserved without emendation.
    Mark 1:39 He went throughout all Galilee, proclaiming in their synagogues and casting out the demons.

Luke 5:1
As the crowd pressed upon Him to hear God’s word, He was standing beside the lake of Gennesaret.
  Mark 4:1 — narrative parallel: Both set teaching beside the lake or sea with a pressing or gathered crowd. Mark proceeds to boat teaching, as Luke does shortly afterward. This compares teaching settings without identifying the occasion with Luke's fish catch.
    Mark 4:1 Again He began teaching beside the sea. A very great crowd gathered around Him, so that He got into a boat on the sea and sat down, while the whole crowd was on the land beside the sea.

Luke 5:2
He saw two boats beside the lake; the fishermen had left them and were washing their nets.
  Mark 1:19–1:20 — narrative parallel: Luke introduces fishermen's boats and nets being washed before Jesus' call; Mark introduces James and John in a boat putting the nets in order before their call. This compares fishing-work settings around the disciples' calling, while retaining washing versus putting in order and Luke's fishermen outside the boats versus Mark's named men in a boat.
    Mark 1:19 Going a little farther, He saw James son of Zebedee and John his brother, who were also in the boat, putting the nets in order.
    Mark 1:20 Immediately He called them. They left their father Zebedee in the boat with the hired workers and went after Him.

Luke 5:3
He entered one of the boats, which belonged to Simon, and asked him to put out a little from the land. Sitting down, He taught the crowds from the boat.
  Mark 4:1 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus sitting in a boat to teach the crowd on land. Luke identifies Simon's boat and the request to move out a little; Mark does not. The boat-teaching occasions are not merged.
    Mark 4:1 Again He began teaching beside the sea. A very great crowd gathered around Him, so that He got into a boat on the sea and sat down, while the whole crowd was on the land beside the sea.

Luke 5:4
When He finished speaking, He said to Simon, “Put out into the deep water, and let down your nets for a catch.”
  John 21:6 — narrative parallel: Both fishing scenes turn on Jesus directing where to cast or lower the net. Luke specifies deep water; John specifies the boat's right side after the resurrection. These are distinct occasions and instructions.
    John 21:6 And He said to them, “Cast the net on the right side of the boat, and you will find some.” So they cast it, and were no longer strong enough to draw it in because of the multitude of fish.

Luke 5:5
Simon answered, “Master, we labored through the whole night and caught nothing, but at Thy word I will let down the nets.”
  John 21:3 — narrative parallel: Both report a night's unsuccessful fishing. Luke has Simon say this before agreeing to Jesus' instruction; John narrates the night's failure before the later shore-side encounter. The distinct settings are preserved.
    John 21:3 Simon Peter says to them, “I am going fishing.” They say to him, “We also are coming with you.” They went out and got into the boat, and during that night they caught nothing.

Luke 5:6
When they did this, they enclosed a great multitude of fish, and their nets began to tear.
  John 21:6 — narrative parallel: Both narrate a large catch after Jesus' instruction. Luke says the nets began tearing; John says the net could not be drawn because of the multitude. The different descriptions do not make this one catch.
    John 21:6 And He said to them, “Cast the net on the right side of the boat, and you will find some.” So they cast it, and were no longer strong enough to draw it in because of the multitude of fish.

Luke 5:7
They signaled to their partners in the other boat to come and help them. They came and filled both boats, so that they began to sink.
  John 21:6–21:8 — narrative parallel: Both Jesus-directed catches produce so many fish that coordinated handling becomes necessary: Luke's partners bring the other boat and both boats become dangerously loaded, while John's disciples cannot draw the net and the others come in the little boat dragging it. John's scene is after the resurrection and says nothing about two boats sinking; the catches and handling sequences remain distinct.
    John 21:6 And He said to them, “Cast the net on the right side of the boat, and you will find some.” So they cast it, and were no longer strong enough to draw it in because of the multitude of fish.
    John 21:7 Then that disciple whom Jesus loved says to Peter, “It is the Lord.” So Simon Peter, hearing that it is the Lord, tied his outer garment around himself—for he was naked—and threw himself into the sea.
    John 21:8 But the other disciples came in the little boat, dragging the net of fish, for they were not far from the land, but about two hundred cubits away.

Luke 5:8
When Simon Peter saw it, he fell at Jesus’ knees, saying, “Depart from me, Lord, because I am a sinful man.”
  Isaiah 6:5 — thematic parallel: Both respond to an overwhelming encounter by confessing unworthiness. Simon calls himself a sinful man before Jesus; Isaiah laments unclean lips after seeing the Lord. Their words, settings and experiences are distinct.
    Isaiah 6:5 And I said, Woe is me, for I am pierced to the heart! For I am a man of unclean lips, and I dwell among a people of unclean lips; and my eyes have seen the King, the Lord Sabaoth.

Luke 5:9
For amazement had seized him and everyone with him at the catch of fish they had taken,
  John 21:6 — narrative parallel: Both scenes emphasize a multitude of fish unexpectedly caught after Jesus' direction. Luke explicitly describes amazement among the fishermen; John describes the net's weight. The reaction and the post-resurrection setting are not equated.
    John 21:6 And He said to them, “Cast the net on the right side of the boat, and you will find some.” So they cast it, and were no longer strong enough to draw it in because of the multitude of fish.

Luke 5:10
and likewise James and John, Zebedee’s sons, who were Simon’s partners. Jesus said to Simon, “Do not fear. From now on you will be catching people alive.”
  Mark 1:17 — teaching parallel: Both connect Jesus' call with a future mission pictured through catching people. Luke says catching people alive and names Simon's partners in the surrounding sentence; Mark says fishers of men to Simon and Andrew.
    Mark 1:17 And He said to them, “Come after Me, and I will make you become fishers of men.”

Luke 5:11
They brought the boats to land, left everything, and followed Him.
  Mark 1:18 — narrative parallel: Both describe fishermen leaving their prior equipment or livelihood and following Jesus. Luke adds boats brought ashore and leaving everything; Mark names leaving the nets immediately. No combined timetable is inferred.
    Mark 1:18 Immediately they left their nets and followed Him.

Luke 5:12
While He was in one of the cities, behold, there was a man full of leprosy. Seeing Jesus, he fell on his face and petitioned Him, saying, “Lord, if Thou art willing, Thou canst cleanse me.”
  Mark 1:40 — narrative parallel: Both describe a leprous man's petition that Jesus can cleanse him if willing. Luke says he fell on his face and was full of leprosy; Mark describes kneeling and entreaty.
    Mark 1:40 A leper came to Him, pleading with Him, kneeling, and saying, “If Thou art willing, Thou canst cleanse me.”

Luke 5:13
Stretching out His hand, He touched him and said, “I am willing; be cleansed.” Immediately the leprosy departed from him.
  Mark 1:41–1:42 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus stretching out His hand, touching the man, affirming His willingness and commanding cleansing, followed by immediate removal of the leprosy.
    Mark 1:41 And moved with compassion, He stretched out His hand and touched him, and says to him, “I will; be cleansed.”
    Mark 1:42 Immediately the leprosy departed from him, and he was cleansed.

Luke 5:14
He commanded him to tell no one: “But go, show yourself to the priest, and make the offering for your cleansing as Moses commanded, for a testimony to them.”
  Mark 1:44 — narrative parallel: Both forbid public speech and direct the cleansed man to the priest with the offering Moses commanded, as testimony. Neither verse here repeats the full offering regulations.
    Mark 1:44 and said to him, “See that you say nothing to anyone. But go, show yourself to the priest, and offer for your cleansing what Moses commanded, as a testimony to them.”

Luke 5:15
Yet the report about Him spread all the more, and large crowds gathered to hear and to be healed of their illnesses.
  Mark 1:45 — narrative parallel: Both describe spreading publicity and crowds coming. Luke adds hearing and healing; Mark attributes publicity to the man and describes Jesus outside cities. Luke's verse is not made to identify that same publisher explicitly.
    Mark 1:45 But he went out and began proclaiming it widely and spreading the report, so that Jesus could no longer enter a city openly. He remained outside in deserted places, and they came to Him from every direction.

Luke 5:16
But He kept withdrawing to deserted places and praying.
  Mark 1:35 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus withdrawing to a deserted place to pray. Luke presents repeated withdrawals; Mark narrates one early-morning departure. The summary and individual episode remain distinct.
    Mark 1:35 Very early, while it was still dark, He arose, went out, and departed to a deserted place, and there He prayed.

Luke 5:17
On one of those days He was teaching, and Pharisees and teachers of the law were sitting there. They had come from every village of Galilee and Judea and from Jerusalem; and the Lord’s power was present for Him to heal.
  Mark 2:1–2:2 — narrative parallel: Both introduce teaching before the paralytic is brought in. Mark describes a crowded Capernaum house; Luke names Pharisees and law teachers from several regions and the Lord's healing power, details Mark does not independently state.
    Mark 2:1 When He entered Capernaum again after some days, it was heard that He was at home.
    Mark 2:2 Many gathered, so that there was no longer room, not even near the door, and He was speaking the word to them.

Luke 5:18
And behold, men were carrying on a bed a man who was paralyzed. They sought to bring him in and place him before Him.
  Mark 2:3 — narrative parallel: Both introduce people carrying a paralyzed man to Jesus. Mark specifies four carriers; Luke specifies the bed and their wish to place him before Jesus without numbering them.
    Mark 2:3 They came, bringing to Him a paralyzed man carried by four men.

Luke 5:19
Finding no way to bring him in because of the crowd, they went up onto the roof and lowered him through the tiles with his little bed into their midst, before Jesus.
  Mark 2:4 — narrative parallel: Both describe the crowd preventing access and the man being lowered through the roof with his bed. Luke names tiles; Mark describes uncovering and digging through the roof. Those construction descriptions remain distinct.
    Mark 2:4 Since they could not bring him to Jesus because of the crowd, they removed the roof above the place where He was. After digging through it, they lowered the mat on which the paralyzed man lay.

Luke 5:20
Seeing their faith, He said, “Man, your sins are forgiven you.”
  Mark 2:5 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus see their faith and pronounce the man's sins forgiven. Luke addresses him as man; Mark addresses him as child.
    Mark 2:5 Jesus saw their faith and said to the paralyzed man, “Child, your sins are forgiven.”

Luke 5:21
The scribes and Pharisees began to reason, saying, “Who is this who speaks blasphemies? Who can forgive sins except God alone?”
  Mark 2:6–2:7 — narrative parallel: Both report the scribal objection of blasphemy because only God can forgive sins. Luke also names Pharisees; Mark introduces scribes sitting and reasoning in their hearts.
    Mark 2:6 Some of the scribes were sitting there and reasoning in their hearts,
    Mark 2:7 “Why does this man speak this way? He is blaspheming! Who can forgive sins except the one God?”

Luke 5:22
Jesus recognized their thoughts and answered them, “What are you reasoning in your hearts?
  Mark 2:8 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus recognize the critics' inward reasoning and ask why they reason in their hearts. Mark explicitly describes His recognition in His spirit.
    Mark 2:8 Immediately Jesus knew in His spirit that they were reasoning this way within themselves. He said to them, “Why are you reasoning these things in your hearts?

Luke 5:23
Which is easier: to say, ‘Your sins are forgiven you,’ or to say, ‘Rise and walk’?
  Mark 2:9 — narrative parallel: Both ask whether declaring sins forgiven or commanding the man to rise and walk is easier. Mark's walking command also includes taking the bed.
    Mark 2:9 Which is easier: to say to the paralyzed man, ‘Your sins are forgiven,’ or to say, ‘Arise, take your mat, and walk’?

Luke 5:24
But so that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins”—He said to the paralyzed man—“I tell you, rise, take up your little bed, and go home.”
  Mark 2:10–2:11 — narrative parallel: Both declare the Son of Man's authority on earth to forgive sins and then command the paralytic to rise, carry his bed and go home.
    Mark 2:10 But so that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins”—He said to the paralyzed man—
    Mark 2:11 “I say to you, arise, take your mat, and go to your house.”

Luke 5:25
Immediately he rose before them, took up what he had been lying on, and went home glorifying God.
  Mark 2:12 — narrative parallel: Both narrate immediate rising, taking the bed and departure before observers. Luke expressly has the healed man glorify God; Mark explicitly attributes glorifying God to the amazed onlookers.
    Mark 2:12 He arose, immediately took up the mat, and went out before everyone. They were all amazed and glorified God, saying, “We have never seen anything like this!”

Luke 5:26
Astonishment seized everyone, and they glorified God and were filled with fear, saying, “We have seen extraordinary things today.”
  Mark 2:12 — narrative parallel: Both describe communal astonishment and glorification of God at the healing. Luke adds fear and extraordinary things today; Mark says they had never seen anything like it.
    Mark 2:12 He arose, immediately took up the mat, and went out before everyone. They were all amazed and glorified God, saying, “We have never seen anything like this!”

Luke 5:27
After this He went out and saw a tax collector named Levi sitting at the tax booth, and said to him, “Follow Me.”
  Mark 2:14 — narrative parallel: Both identify Levi at the tax booth and Jesus' call to follow. Mark adds son of Alphaeus. No equation with another named tax collector is required by this pair.
    Mark 2:14 As He passed by, He saw Levi son of Alphaeus sitting at the tax station. He said to him, “Follow Me.” He arose and followed Him.

Luke 5:28
Leaving everything, he rose and followed Him.
  Mark 2:14 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Levi rising and following Jesus. Luke expressly adds leaving everything; Mark does not use that phrase in the cited verse.
    Mark 2:14 As He passed by, He saw Levi son of Alphaeus sitting at the tax station. He said to him, “Follow Me.” He arose and followed Him.

Luke 5:29
Levi gave a great reception for Him in his house. A large crowd of tax collectors and others was reclining with them.
  Mark 2:15 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus reclining with many tax collectors and others at a house meal. Luke explicitly identifies Levi as the host of a great reception; Mark's house pronoun is not used to establish an independent owner identification.
    Mark 2:15 As He reclined at table in his house, many tax collectors and sinners reclined with Jesus and His disciples, for there were many who followed Him.

Luke 5:30
The Pharisees and their scribes grumbled to His disciples, saying, “Why do you eat and drink with tax collectors and sinners?”
  Mark 2:16 — narrative parallel: Both have Pharisaic scribes question the disciples about eating with tax collectors and sinners. Luke adds drinking and describes grumbling; Mark frames the complaint after seeing the meal.
    Mark 2:16 The scribes of the Pharisees saw that He was eating with sinners and tax collectors and said to His disciples, “Why does He eat with tax collectors and sinners?”

Luke 5:31
Jesus answered them, “Those who are healthy have no need of a physician, but those who are ill do.
  Mark 2:17 — teaching parallel: Both answer through a physician saying: Luke's healthy and Mark's strong do not need a physician, but the ill do. Mark continues with the call of sinners in the same verse.
    Mark 2:17 Jesus heard and said to them, “The strong have no need of a physician, but those who are ill do. I did not come to call the righteous, but sinners.”

Luke 5:32
I have not come to call the righteous, but sinners to repentance.”
  Mark 2:17 — teaching parallel: Both state Jesus came to call sinners rather than the righteous. Luke expressly adds to repentance; that phrase is not silently inserted into Mark's supplied text.
    Mark 2:17 Jesus heard and said to them, “The strong have no need of a physician, but those who are ill do. I did not come to call the righteous, but sinners.”

Luke 5:33
They said to Him, “John’s disciples fast often and offer petitions, and likewise those of the Pharisees, but Thine eat and drink.”
  Mark 2:18 — narrative parallel: Both contrast fasting by John's disciples and Pharisees with Jesus' disciples. Luke also mentions petitions and eating and drinking; Mark asks why His disciples do not fast.
    Mark 2:18 John’s disciples and the Pharisees were fasting. They came and said to Him, “Why do John’s disciples and the disciples of the Pharisees fast, but Thy disciples do not fast?”

Luke 5:34
Jesus said to them, “Can you make the bridegroom’s companions fast while the bridegroom is with them?
  Mark 2:19 — teaching parallel: Both use the bridegroom's companions being with the bridegroom to explain their not fasting. Luke asks whether others can make them fast; Mark asks whether they can fast.
    Mark 2:19 Jesus said to them, “Can the bridegroom’s companions fast while the bridegroom is with them? As long as they have the bridegroom with them, they cannot fast.

Luke 5:35
But days will come, and when the bridegroom is taken from them, then they will fast in those days.”
  Mark 2:20 — teaching parallel: Both predict the bridegroom being taken away and fasting then. Luke says in those days; Mark's supplied formulation refers to that day.
    Mark 2:20 But days will come when the bridegroom is taken away from them, and then they will fast on that day.

Luke 5:36
He also told them a parable: “No one tears a patch from a new garment and puts it on an old garment. Otherwise he will tear the new one, and the patch from the new will not match the old.
  Mark 2:21 — teaching parallel: Both compare new cloth with an old garment. Luke describes tearing a patch from a new garment and mismatch with the old; Mark describes an unshrunk patch pulling away and worsening the tear. The mechanics remain different.
    Mark 2:21 No one sews a patch of unshrunk cloth onto an old garment. Otherwise the new patch pulls away from the old, and a worse tear results.

Luke 5:37
And no one puts new wine into old wineskins. Otherwise the new wine will burst the skins, and it will be poured out and the skins destroyed.
  Mark 2:22 — teaching parallel: Both warn that new wine in old wineskins bursts the skins and loses wine and skins. Mark also gives the positive fresh-skins conclusion in this verse.
    Mark 2:22 No one puts new wine into old wineskins. Otherwise the wine will burst the skins, and the wine is lost along with the skins. New wine belongs in fresh wineskins.”

Luke 5:38
But new wine must be put into fresh wineskins.
  Mark 2:22 — teaching parallel: Both conclude that new wine belongs in fresh wineskins. Mark includes the preceding warning about old skins within the same verse.
    Mark 2:22 No one puts new wine into old wineskins. Otherwise the wine will burst the skins, and the wine is lost along with the skins. New wine belongs in fresh wineskins.”

Luke 5:39
And no one who has drunk old wine desires new, for he says, ‘The old is good.’”
  Sirach 9:10 — thematic imagery parallel: Both use the value of aged wine. Luke says the old-wine drinker does not desire new wine because the old is good; Sirach likens a new friend to new wine enjoyed with gladness after aging and urges loyalty to an old friend. The uses and arguments differ; no explicit quotation is claimed.
    Sirach 9:10 Do not abandon an old friend, for a recent one is not his equal. A new friend is new wine; when it has aged, you will drink it with gladness.

Luke 6:1
On a Sabbath He was passing through grainfields. His disciples plucked the ears, rubbed them in their hands, and ate.
  Mark 2:23 — narrative parallel: Both describe passing through grainfields on a Sabbath and the disciples plucking ears. Luke adds rubbing them in their hands and eating; Mark's cited verse does not describe the rubbing.
    Mark 2:23 He was passing through the grainfields on the Sabbath, and His disciples began to make their way, plucking the heads of grain.

Luke 6:2
Some of the Pharisees said, “Why are you doing what is not lawful on the Sabbaths?”
  Mark 2:24 — narrative parallel: Both have Pharisees object that the disciples' action is unlawful on the Sabbath. Luke addresses the disciples directly; Mark addresses Jesus about them.
    Mark 2:24 The Pharisees said to Him, “Look! Why are they doing what is not lawful on the Sabbath?”

Luke 6:3
Jesus answered them, “Have you not even read what David did when he and those with him were hungry,
  Mark 2:25 — teaching parallel: Both answer by asking whether the critics have read about David's hunger and the people with him. Mark additionally describes need.
    Mark 2:25 He said to them, “Have you never read what David did when he was in need and hungry, he and those with him:

Luke 6:4
how he entered God’s house, took and ate the loaves of the presentation, and gave them to those with him, although no one except the priests alone may lawfully eat them?”
  Mark 2:26 — teaching parallel: Both recall David entering God's house, eating the presentation loaves reserved for priests and giving to companions. Mark additionally names Abiathar as high priest; Luke does not supply that name.
    Mark 2:26 how he entered God’s house in the time of Abiathar the high priest and ate the loaves of presentation, which only priests may lawfully eat, and also gave them to those who were with him?”

Luke 6:5
He said to them, “The Son of Man is Lord of the Sabbath.”
  Mark 2:28 — teaching parallel: Both explicitly call the Son of Man Lord of the Sabbath. Mark includes even in its formulation.
    Mark 2:28 So the Son of Man is Lord even of the Sabbath.”

Luke 6:6
On another Sabbath He entered the synagogue and taught. A man was there whose right hand was withered.
  Mark 3:1–3:2 — narrative parallel: Both introduce a man with a withered hand in a synagogue in a Sabbath-healing setting. Luke explicitly says another Sabbath and the right hand; Mark does not specify which hand.
    Mark 3:1 He entered a synagogue again, and a man with a withered hand was there.
    Mark 3:2 They watched Him closely to see whether He would heal him on the Sabbath, so that they might accuse Him.

Luke 6:7
The scribes and Pharisees watched closely to see whether He would heal on the Sabbath, so that they might find a way to accuse Him.
  Mark 3:2 — narrative parallel: Both describe watching Jesus for a Sabbath healing in order to accuse Him. Luke explicitly names the scribes and Pharisees.
    Mark 3:2 They watched Him closely to see whether He would heal him on the Sabbath, so that they might accuse Him.

Luke 6:8
But He knew their thoughts and said to the man with the withered hand, “Rise and stand in the middle.” He rose and stood.
  Mark 3:3 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus command the man with the withered hand into the middle. Luke additionally narrates His knowledge of their thoughts and the man's compliance; Mark gives the command without those additional narrated details.
    Mark 3:3 He said to the man with the withered hand, “Arise and come into the midst.”

Luke 6:9
Jesus said to them, “I ask you: Is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or do evil, to save a life or destroy it?”
  Mark 3:4 — teaching parallel: Both ask whether Sabbath law permits doing good or evil and saving a life or destroying or killing it. Mark additionally narrates the observers' silence.
    Mark 3:4 He said to them, “Is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or to do evil, to save a life or to kill?” But they remained silent.

Luke 6:10
Looking around at all of them, He said to him, “Stretch out your hand.” He did so, and his hand was restored.
  Mark 3:5 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus looking around, commanding the man to stretch out his hand and its restoration. Mark adds anger and grief at hardened hearts; Luke does not reproduce those emotions here.
    Mark 3:5 He looked around at them with anger, grieving at the hardness of their hearts, and said to the man, “Stretch out your hand.” He stretched it out, and his hand was restored.

Luke 6:11
They were filled with mindless anger and discussed with one another what they might do to Jesus.
  Mark 3:6 — narrative parallel: Both narrate hostile deliberation after the hand's restoration. Luke describes mindless anger and discussion of what to do; Mark specifies Pharisees with Herodians planning to destroy Jesus.
    Mark 3:6 The Pharisees went out and immediately took counsel with the Herodians against Him, as to how they might destroy Him.

Luke 6:12
In those days He went out to the mountain to pray, and spent the night in prayer to God.
  Mark 3:13 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus on a mountain before the choice or appointment of the Twelve in context. Luke specifies a night spent praying; Mark specifies summoning those He wanted and does not independently state the overnight prayer.
    Mark 3:13 He went up the mountain and called to Himself those He wanted, and they came to Him.

Luke 6:13
When day came, He called His disciples and chose twelve from them, whom He also named apostles:
  Mark 3:13–3:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe calling disciples, appointing twelve and naming them apostles. Mark additionally connects the appointment with being with Jesus and being sent to proclaim.
    Mark 3:13 He went up the mountain and called to Himself those He wanted, and they came to Him.
    Mark 3:14 He appointed twelve, whom He also named apostles, to be with Him and to be sent out by Him to proclaim

Luke 6:14
Simon, whom He also named Peter, and his brother Andrew, and James and John, and Philip and Bartholomew,
  Mark 3:16–3:18 — identity parallel: Both apostle lists include Simon named Peter, Andrew, James, John, Philip and Bartholomew. Their sequence differs, and Mark also supplies the Zebedee brothers' designation and further names within this contiguous range.
    Mark 3:16 He appointed the Twelve: Simon, to whom He gave the name Peter;
    Mark 3:17 James son of Zebedee and John, James’s brother, to whom He gave the name Boanerges, that is, Sons of Thunder;
    Mark 3:18 Andrew, Philip, Bartholomew, Matthew, Thomas, James son of Alphaeus, Thaddaeus, Simon the Cananaean,

Luke 6:15
and Matthew and Thomas, and James son of Alphaeus, and Simon called the Zealot,
  Mark 3:18 — identity parallel: Both lists name Matthew, Thomas, James of Alphaeus and Simon. Luke calls Simon the Zealot; Mark calls him the Cananaean and includes Thaddaeus. The supplied names and epithets are preserved.
    Mark 3:18 Andrew, Philip, Bartholomew, Matthew, Thomas, James son of Alphaeus, Thaddaeus, Simon the Cananaean,

Luke 6:16
and Judas son of James, and Judas Iscariot, who became a traitor.
  Mark 3:19 — identity parallel: Both lists identify Judas Iscariot with Jesus' betrayal. Luke additionally names Judas of James; this comparison does not identify that Judas with another differently named apostle in Mark.
    Mark 3:19 and Judas Iscariot, who also handed Him over.

Luke 6:17
He came down with them and stood on a level place. There was a large crowd of His disciples and a great multitude of the people from all Judea, Jerusalem, and the coastal region of Tyre and Sidon.
  Mark 3:7–3:8 — narrative parallel: Both crowd summaries include Judea, Jerusalem, Tyre and Sidon. Luke describes a level place after descent; Mark describes withdrawal toward the sea and names further regions. The geographical summaries are not turned into identical scenes.
    Mark 3:7 Jesus withdrew with His disciples to the sea, and a great multitude from Galilee followed; and from Judea,
    Mark 3:8 Jerusalem, Idumea, beyond the Jordan, and the region of Tyre and Sidon, a great multitude heard all He was doing and came to Him.

Luke 6:18
They had come to hear Him and be healed of their diseases; those troubled by unclean spirits were also healed.
  Mark 3:10–3:11 — narrative parallel: Both surrounding summaries describe healing and the presence of unclean spirits. Luke says the troubled people were healed; Mark describes many healings and spirits falling before Jesus and identifying Him. The distinct statements about spirits are not rewritten as identical actions.
    Mark 3:10 For He healed many, so that all who had afflictions pressed upon Him to touch Him.
    Mark 3:11 Whenever the unclean spirits saw Him, they fell before Him and cried, “Thou art the Son of God!”

Luke 6:19
The whole crowd sought to touch Him, because power went out from Him and healed everyone.
  Mark 3:10 — narrative parallel: Both connect the crowd's effort to touch Jesus with healing. Luke expressly mentions power going out and healing everyone; Mark describes many healed and those with afflictions pressing toward Him.
    Mark 3:10 For He healed many, so that all who had afflictions pressed upon Him to touch Him.

Luke 6:20
Lifting His eyes toward His disciples, He said, “Blessed are you who are poor, because yours is God’s kingdom.
  Matthew 5:3 — teaching parallel: Both pronounce the poor blessed with possession of the kingdom. Luke directly addresses you who are poor and says God's kingdom; Matthew says poor in spirit and the kingdom of the heavens. The qualifications are preserved.
    Matthew 5:3 “Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of the heavens.

Luke 6:21
Blessed are you who hunger now, because you will be filled. Blessed are you who weep now, because you will laugh.
  Matthew 5:6 — teaching parallel: Both promise filling to the hungry. Luke says those hungry now; Matthew specifies hunger and thirst for righteousness. This comparison concerns the first clause; Luke's laughter promise is not found in Matthew's verse.
    Matthew 5:6 Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be filled.
  Matthew 5:4 — teaching parallel: Luke's blessing on those weeping now corresponds to Matthew's blessing on mourners. Luke promises laughter; Matthew promises comfort. This comparison concerns the second clause without making those promises verbatim.
    Matthew 5:4 Blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted.

Luke 6:22
Blessed are you when people hate you, when they exclude you, reproach you, and cast out your name as evil because of the Son of Man.
  Matthew 5:11 — teaching parallel: Both bless people reproached because of Jesus. Luke also names hatred, exclusion and rejection of their name because of the Son of Man; Matthew names persecution and false evil speech because of Me.
    Matthew 5:11 Blessed are you when they revile you and persecute you and falsely speak every evil thing against you for My sake.

Luke 6:23
Rejoice on that day and leap, for behold, your reward is great in heaven; their fathers treated the prophets in the same way.
  Matthew 5:12 — teaching parallel: Both command rejoicing because of a great heavenly reward and compare the treatment of earlier prophets. Luke adds leaping and calls the persecutors their fathers.
    Matthew 5:12 Rejoice and be glad, for great is your reward in the heavens; for thus they persecuted the prophets before you.

Luke 6:24
But woe to you who are rich, because you have received your consolation.
  Luke 16:25 — thematic parallel: The woe over rich people having received their consolation corresponds to the rich man having received good things in life before the reversal with Lazarus. This is a same-book warning-and-parable comparison, not an identification of every rich hearer with that character.
    Luke 16:25 But Abraham said, ‘Child, remember that you received your good things during your life, and Lazarus likewise bad things. But now he is comforted here, and you are in pain.

Luke 6:25
Woe to you who are filled now, because you will hunger. Woe to you who laugh now, because you will mourn and weep.
  Isaiah 65:13–65:14 — thematic parallel: Both use eating, hunger, joy and crying to describe contrasting outcomes. Luke warns the now-filled and laughing of future hunger and mourning; Isaiah contrasts the future lots of God's servants and His opponents. The temporal reversal and the contrast between groups are distinguished.
    Isaiah 65:13 Therefore thus says the Lord: “Behold, those serving Me will eat, but you will hunger. Those serving Me will drink, but you will thirst. Those serving Me will rejoice, but you will be ashamed.
    Isaiah 65:14 Behold, those serving Me will exult with joy, but you will cry out because of your heart’s pain and wail from a broken spirit.

Luke 6:26
Woe when all people speak well of you, for their fathers treated the false prophets in the same way.
  Jeremiah 5:31 — allusion: Luke's warning about acclaim and earlier false prophets corresponds to Jeremiah's unjust prophets and a people loving that situation. Jeremiah supplies a concrete popular-approval pattern, not a rule that every well-regarded teacher must be false.
    Jeremiah 5:31 The prophets prophesy unjust things, and the priests applauded with their hands, and My people loved it so. And what will you do after these things?

Luke 6:27
But I say to you who hear: Love your enemies; do good to those who hate you.
  Matthew 5:44 — teaching parallel: Both command loving enemies. Luke also explicitly says to do good to those who hate you; Matthew's supplied verse adds prayer for persecutors rather than that exact clause.
