    Matthew 5:44 But I tell you: love your enemies, and pray for those who persecute you,

Luke 6:28
Bless those who curse you; pray for those who mistreat you.
  Matthew 5:44 — teaching parallel: Both command prayer for hostile people. Luke additionally commands blessing those who curse; Matthew's supplied text commands loving enemies and praying for persecutors without the same blessing clause.
    Matthew 5:44 But I tell you: love your enemies, and pray for those who persecute you,

Luke 6:29
Offer the other cheek also to the one striking you on the cheek; and from the one taking your cloak, do not withhold your tunic also.
  Matthew 5:39–5:40 — teaching parallel: Both urge offering another cheek and yielding a second garment. Matthew specifies the right cheek and a lawsuit for the tunic followed by the cloak; Luke puts taking the cloak before not withholding the tunic.
    Matthew 5:39 But I tell you: do not resist the evildoer; but whoever strikes you upon your right cheek, turn the other to him also.
    Matthew 5:40 And if anyone would sue you and take your tunic, let him have your cloak also.

Luke 6:30
Give to everyone who asks you, and do not demand back your belongings from the one who takes them.
  Matthew 5:42 — teaching parallel: Both command giving to the one who asks. Luke additionally forbids demanding taken belongings back; Matthew additionally forbids turning away the would-be borrower. Those second instructions are distinct.
    Matthew 5:42 Give to the one who asks you, and do not turn away from the one who would borrow from you.

Luke 6:31
As you wish people to do to you, do likewise to them.
  Matthew 7:12 — teaching parallel: Both instruct doing to others what one wants them to do to oneself. Matthew adds that this is the law and prophets; Luke's supplied verse does not add that explanation.
    Matthew 7:12 So in everything, whatever you wish people to do for you, you also do likewise for them; for this is the Law and the Prophets.

Luke 6:32
If you love those who love you, what credit is that to you? For even sinners love those who love them.
  Matthew 5:46 — teaching parallel: Both question the merit of loving only those who love you. Luke uses credit and sinners; Matthew uses reward and tax collectors. The comparison groups remain as supplied.
    Matthew 5:46 For if you love those who love you, what reward do you have? Do not even the tax collectors do the same?

Luke 6:33
If you do good to those who do good to you, what credit is that to you? Even sinners do the same.
  Matthew 5:47 — teaching parallel: Both challenge ordinary reciprocity as insufficiently distinctive. Luke speaks of doing good to benefactors and sinners doing the same; Matthew speaks of greeting only one's brothers and the Gentiles doing likewise. The concrete actions differ.
    Matthew 5:47 And if you greet only your brothers, what are you doing beyond others? Do not even the Gentiles do the same?

Luke 6:34
If you lend to those from whom you hope to receive, what credit is that to you? Even sinners lend to sinners to receive the equivalent back.
  Matthew 5:42 — teaching parallel: Both teachings concern lending beyond ordinary self-interest. Luke questions lending only when equivalent repayment is expected; Matthew says not to turn away the person wanting to borrow. Neither verse is turned into a separate interest-rate rule.
    Matthew 5:42 Give to the one who asks you, and do not turn away from the one who would borrow from you.

Luke 6:35
But love your enemies, do good, and lend, expecting nothing back. Your reward will be great, and you will be sons of the Most High, because He is kind to the ungrateful and evil.
  Matthew 5:44–5:45 — teaching parallel: Both connect love for enemies with becoming children of the heavenly Father and His goodness to evil people. Luke adds generous lending; Matthew adds prayer and the examples of sun and rain. Those examples are not attributed to Luke.
    Matthew 5:44 But I tell you: love your enemies, and pray for those who persecute you,
    Matthew 5:45 that you may become sons of your Father who is in the heavens; for He makes His sun rise upon the evil and the good, and sends rain upon the righteous and the unrighteous.

Luke 6:36
Be merciful, just as your Father is merciful.
  Matthew 5:48 — teaching parallel: Both exhort a life patterned on the Father. Luke says merciful as your Father is merciful; Matthew says perfect as the heavenly Father is perfect. Mercy and perfection are not silently substituted for one another.
    Matthew 5:48 You shall therefore be perfect, as your heavenly Father is perfect.

Luke 6:37
Do not judge, and you will certainly not be judged. Do not condemn, and you will certainly not be condemned. Release, and you will be released.
  Matthew 7:1–7:2 — teaching parallel: Both warn that judging is reciprocated. Luke additionally forbids condemning and urges release with corresponding release; Matthew continues with judgment and measure. The additional release wording is retained as supplied.
    Matthew 7:1 Do not judge, so that you may not be judged.
    Matthew 7:2 For with the judgment you use, you will be judged, and with the measure you use, it will be measured to you.

Luke 6:38
Give, and it will be given to you. They will put into your lap a good measure, pressed down, shaken together, overflowing; for by the measure you measure, it will be measured back to you.”
  Mark 4:24 — teaching parallel: Both say the measure used will be measured back. Luke places this in giving and a generously filled lap; Mark frames it with attention to what is heard and adds that more will be given. The contexts differ.
    Mark 4:24 He said to them, “Pay attention to what you hear. With the measure you use, it will be measured to you, and more will be added to you.

Luke 6:39
He also told them a parable: “Can a blind person guide a blind person? Will they not both fall into a pit?
  Matthew 15:14 — teaching parallel: Both picture a blind person guiding another blind person and both falling into a pit. Matthew applies the image to blind guides in its surrounding controversy; Luke introduces it as a parable.
    Matthew 15:14 Leave them; they are blind guides of the blind. If a blind person guides a blind person, both will fall into a pit.”

Luke 6:40
A disciple is not above the teacher, but everyone fully trained will be like his teacher.
  Matthew 10:24–10:25 — teaching parallel: Both say a disciple is not above the teacher and compare the disciple becoming like the teacher. Luke speaks of complete training; Matthew also compares a slave and master in the surrounding persecution teaching.
    Matthew 10:24 A disciple is not above the teacher, nor a slave above his master.
    Matthew 10:25 It is enough for the disciple to become like his teacher, and the slave like his master. If they called the master of the house Beelzebul, how much more his household members!

Luke 6:41
Why do you look at the speck in your brother’s eye but fail to notice the beam in your own eye?
  Matthew 7:3 — teaching parallel: Both question noticing a speck in a brother's eye while failing to attend to a beam in one's own eye.
    Matthew 7:3 Why do you see the splinter in your brother’s eye, but do not notice the beam in your own eye?

Luke 6:42
How can you say to your brother, ‘Brother, let me take out the speck in your eye,’ when you yourself do not see the beam in your own eye? Hypocrite! First take the beam out of your eye, and then you will see clearly to take the speck out of your brother’s eye.
  Matthew 7:4–7:5 — teaching parallel: Both expose offering to remove another's speck while having one's own beam, call that hypocrisy and require removing the beam first to see clearly.
    Matthew 7:4 Or how will you say to your brother, ‘Let me remove the splinter from your eye,’ when behold, the beam is in your own eye?
    Matthew 7:5 Hypocrite! First remove the beam from your own eye, and then you will see clearly to remove the splinter from your brother’s eye.

Luke 6:43
For no good tree produces rotten fruit, nor again does a rotten tree produce good fruit.
  Matthew 7:17–7:18 — teaching parallel: Both deny good fruit from a bad tree or bad fruit from a good tree. Matthew also positively states each tree's ordinary produce before the impossibility statement.
    Matthew 7:17 In the same way, every good tree bears good fruit, but the rotten tree bears evil fruit.
    Matthew 7:18 A good tree cannot bear evil fruit, nor can a rotten tree bear good fruit.

Luke 6:44
Each tree is known by its own fruit. People do not gather figs from thorns, nor harvest grapes from a bramble.
  Matthew 7:16 — teaching parallel: Both say trees or people are known by fruit and use thorn plants with figs and grapes. Luke pairs figs with thorns and grapes with a bramble; Matthew's question pairs grapes with thorns and figs with thistles.
    Matthew 7:16 You will recognize them by their fruits. Do people gather grapes from thornbushes, or figs from thistles?

Luke 6:45
The good person brings out good from the good treasure of his heart, and the evil person brings out evil from the evil treasure; for his mouth speaks from the heart’s abundance.
  Matthew 12:34–12:35 — teaching parallel: Both connect good or evil heart-treasure with what a person brings out and the mouth with the heart's abundance. Matthew directly addresses a brood of vipers before its general treasure saying; Luke's supplied verse contrasts good and evil people.
    Matthew 12:34 Brood of vipers! How can you speak good things when you are evil? For out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks.
    Matthew 12:35 The good person brings forth good things from the good treasure, and the evil person brings forth evil things from the evil treasure.

Luke 6:46
Why do you call Me ‘Lord, Lord,’ and not do what I say?
  Matthew 7:21 — teaching parallel: Both challenge merely saying Lord, Lord without the required obedience. Luke asks why people do not do His sayings; Matthew contrasts such speech with doing the heavenly Father's will for entering the kingdom.
    Matthew 7:21 Not everyone who says to Me, ‘Lord, Lord,’ will enter the kingdom of the heavens, but the one who does the will of My Father in the heavens.

Luke 6:47
Everyone who comes to Me, hears My words and does them—I will show you whom he resembles.
  Matthew 7:24 — teaching parallel: Both introduce the person hearing Jesus' words and doing them through a house-building comparison. Matthew already identifies the wise builder and rock; Luke introduces the comparison before those details follow.
    Matthew 7:24 Therefore everyone who hears these words of Mine and puts them into practice will be likened to a prudent man who built his house upon the rock.

Luke 6:48
He resembles a man building a house, who dug and went deep and laid a foundation on the rock. When a flood came, the river burst against that house but could not shake it, because it had been well built.
  Matthew 7:25 — teaching parallel: Both describe a house with a rock foundation withstanding flood. Luke emphasizes deep digging and a river's assault; Matthew adds rain and winds and states the house did not fall because of the rock foundation.
    Matthew 7:25 The rain came down, the rivers came, and the winds blew and fell upon that house, and it did not fall, for it had been founded upon the rock.

Luke 6:49
But the one who heard and did not act resembles a man who built a house on the ground without a foundation. The river burst against it, and immediately it collapsed; the ruin of that house was great.”
  Matthew 7:26–7:27 — teaching parallel: Both contrast hearing without doing with a house collapsing greatly under flood. Luke describes ground without a foundation; Matthew describes sand, rain and winds. The ground descriptions and weather details remain distinct.
    Matthew 7:26 And everyone who hears these words of Mine and does not put them into practice will be likened to a foolish man who built his house upon the sand.
    Matthew 7:27 The rain came down, the rivers came, and the winds blew and struck that house, and it fell; and its fall was great.”

Luke 7:1
When He had finished all His sayings in the people’s hearing, He entered Capernaum.
  Matthew 8:5 — narrative parallel: Both move Jesus into Capernaum before the centurion's request. Luke introduces the move after completed sayings; Matthew describes the centurion approaching and petitioning. The accounts' differing introductions are retained.
    Matthew 8:5 When He entered Capernaum, a centurion came to Him, pleading with Him

Luke 7:2
A certain centurion had a slave who was ill and about to die, and who was precious to him.
  Matthew 8:6 — narrative parallel: Both describe a centurion's ill servant. Luke says he was precious to the centurion and near death; Matthew specifies paralysis and severe suffering. Neither description is silently inserted into the other.
    Matthew 8:6 and saying, “Lord, my servant lies at home paralyzed, terribly tormented.”

Luke 7:3
Hearing about Jesus, he sent elders of the Jews to Him, asking Him to come and save his slave.
  Matthew 8:5–8:6 — narrative parallel: Both introduce the centurion's appeal for the servant. Luke explicitly sends Jewish elders after hearing about Jesus; Matthew narrates the centurion approaching and speaking. No unrecorded delegation or harmonized dialogue is invented.
    Matthew 8:5 When He entered Capernaum, a centurion came to Him, pleading with Him
    Matthew 8:6 and saying, “Lord, my servant lies at home paralyzed, terribly tormented.”

Luke 7:4
When they reached Jesus, they urged Him earnestly, saying, “He is worthy for Thee to grant him this,
  Luke 7:6 — internal contrast: Within the same healing account, the elders urge that the centurion is worthy, while his later message declares him unworthy to receive Jesus under his roof. The different speakers and assessments are compared, not treated as a contradiction by Jesus.
    Luke 7:6 Jesus went with them. When He was already not far from the house, the centurion sent friends, saying to Him, “Lord, do not trouble Thyself, for I am not worthy for Thee to enter under my roof.

Luke 7:5
for he loves our nation, and he himself built our synagogue for us.”
  Acts 10:1–10:2 — thematic parallel: Luke's elders commend their centurion's love for the nation and construction of their synagogue. Acts identifies Cornelius as a centurion who gives many alms to the people and prays continually. These are specific depictions of centurions' benefaction toward the people, not identification of the two men; Acts does not say Cornelius built Luke's synagogue.
    Acts 10:1 In Caesarea there was a man named Cornelius, a centurion of the cohort called the Italian,
    Acts 10:2 devout and fearing God with his whole household, giving many alms to the people and praying to God continually.

Luke 7:6
Jesus went with them. When He was already not far from the house, the centurion sent friends, saying to Him, “Lord, do not trouble Thyself, for I am not worthy for Thee to enter under my roof.
  Matthew 8:8 — narrative parallel: Both give the centurion's declaration of unworthiness for Jesus to enter his roof. Luke conveys it through sent friends as Jesus approaches; Matthew presents his reply directly. The modes of delivery remain distinct.
    Matthew 8:8 The centurion answered, “Lord, I am not fit for Thee to enter under my roof. Only speak a word, and my servant will be healed.

Luke 7:7
Therefore I did not even consider myself worthy to come to Thee. But speak a word, and let my servant be healed.
  Matthew 8:8 — narrative parallel: Both express confidence that a word from Jesus can heal the servant. Luke additionally explains why the centurion did not consider himself worthy to come personally; Matthew does not repeat that explanation.
    Matthew 8:8 The centurion answered, “Lord, I am not fit for Thee to enter under my roof. Only speak a word, and my servant will be healed.

Luke 7:8
For I too am a man placed under authority, with soldiers under me. I tell this one, ‘Go,’ and he goes; another, ‘Come,’ and he comes; and my slave, ‘Do this,’ and he does it.”
  Matthew 8:9 — narrative parallel: Both illustrate the centurion's confidence through his being under authority, commanding soldiers to go and come and directing a slave's work.
    Matthew 8:9 For I too am a man under authority, with soldiers under me. I say to this one, ‘Go,’ and he goes; to another, ‘Come,’ and he comes; and to my slave, ‘Do this,’ and he does it.”

Luke 7:9
When Jesus heard this, He marveled at him. Turning to the crowd following Him, He said, “I tell you, not even in Israel have I found such faith.”
  Matthew 8:10 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus marveling and declaring He has not found such faith in Israel. Luke specifically turns to the following crowd; Matthew introduces His statement to those following.
    Matthew 8:10 When Jesus heard this, He marveled and said to those following, “Truly I tell you, with no one in Israel have I found such faith.

Luke 7:10
Those who had been sent returned to the house and found the slave in good health.
  Matthew 8:13 — narrative parallel: Both conclude with the servant healed. Luke narrates the messengers' return and discovery of good health; Matthew gives Jesus' answer and healing in that hour. The return and precise timing are not treated as independently stated by both.
    Matthew 8:13 Jesus said to the centurion, “Go; as you have believed, let it happen for you.” And his servant was healed at that hour.

Luke 7:11
Afterward He went to a city called Nain, and His disciples and a large crowd traveled with Him.
  1 Kings 17:8–17:10 — narrative parallel: Jesus' journey to Nain introduces the following encounter with a widow whose son is restored; Elijah's journey to Zarephath brings him to a widow at the city gateway, before the later restoration of her son. This compares the opening movements in the wider widow-and-son narratives. The towns, time before the son's death, accompanying crowds, and manner of restoration are not equated.
    1 Kings 17:8 The Lord’s word came to Elijah:
    1 Kings 17:9 “Rise and go to Zarephath in Sidonian territory. Behold, I have commanded a widow there to sustain you.”
    1 Kings 17:10 He rose and went to Zarephath, to the city gateway. Behold, a widow was there gathering wood. Elijah called after her and said to her, “Please bring me a little water in a vessel, so I may drink.”

Luke 7:12
As He approached the city’s gate, behold, a dead man was being carried out, his mother’s only son. She was a widow, and a considerable crowd from the city was with her.
  1 Kings 17:17 — allusion: Both raising narratives concern a widow's dead son. Kings narrates the son's illness and loss of breath; Luke begins with his funeral at the city gate and identifies him as an only son. Kings does not independently supply Luke's funeral details.
    1 Kings 17:17 After these things, the son of the woman, the mistress of the house, became ill. His illness was very severe, until no breath remained in him.

Luke 7:13
When the Lord saw her, He was moved with compassion for her and said, “Do not weep.”
  Luke 8:52 — narrative parallel: Both raising scenes include Jesus telling mourners not to weep. Here He compassionately addresses a widow; later He addresses those mourning a girl and adds the sleep saying. The children and consolations' full wording remain distinct.
    Luke 8:52 Everyone was weeping and beating themselves in mourning for her. He said, “Do not weep, for she has not died, but is sleeping.”

Luke 7:14
He approached and touched the bier; the bearers stood still. He said, “Young man, I tell you, rise.”
  Mark 5:41 — narrative parallel: Both raising scenes include Jesus addressing the dead child or young person with a command to rise. Luke has Him touch the bier; Mark has Him take the girl's hand and supplies her Aramaic address. The recipients and gestures differ.
    Mark 5:41 Taking the child’s hand, He said to her, “Talitha koum,” which translated means, “Little girl, I say to you, arise!”

Luke 7:15
The dead man sat up and began speaking, and He gave him to his mother.
  1 Kings 17:23 — allusion: Both raising accounts culminate in returning the living son to his mother. Kings describes Elijah carrying the child downstairs and announcing he lives; Luke describes the young man sitting up and speaking before Jesus gives him to his mother.
    1 Kings 17:23 He brought him down from the upper room into the house and gave him to his mother. Elijah said, “Behold, your son lives!”

Luke 7:16
Fear seized everyone, and they glorified God, saying, “A great prophet has arisen among us,” and, “God has visited His people.”
  Luke 1:68 — allusion: The crowd's declaration that God has visited His people recalls Zechariah's praise that God visited and accomplished redemption for His people. This is a same-book visitation-language connection, not one crowd or occasion.
    Luke 1:68 “Blessed is the Lord, the God of Israel, because He has visited and accomplished redemption for His people,

Luke 7:17
This report about Him spread throughout all Judea and all the surrounding region.
  Matthew 9:26 — narrative parallel: Both raising scenes conclude with the report spreading through a region. Luke concerns the widow's son at Nain and names Judea and surrounding country; Matthew concerns the ruler's daughter. The children, events and geographical wording remain distinct.
    Matthew 9:26 This report went out into all that land.

Luke 7:18
John’s disciples reported all these things to him. John called two of his disciples
  Matthew 11:2 — narrative parallel: Both introduce John's hearing of Jesus' deeds and sending disciples. Luke says two and continues their commissioning in the next verse; Matthew explicitly locates John in prison and does not reproduce that number here.
    Matthew 11:2 John, hearing in prison about the works of the Christ, sent through his disciples

Luke 7:19
and sent them to the Lord, saying, “Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?”
  Matthew 11:3 — narrative parallel: Both send John's question whether Jesus is the coming One or another should be expected. Luke explicitly introduces the two disciples being sent to the Lord.
    Matthew 11:3 and said to Him, “Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?”

Luke 7:20
When the men came to Him, they said, “John the Baptist sent us to Thee, saying, ‘Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?’”
  Matthew 11:3 — narrative parallel: Luke repeats the messengers' delivery of John's question; Matthew gives the same question within the sending account. The repeated delivery in Luke is not counted as another independent inquiry.
    Matthew 11:3 and said to Him, “Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?”

Luke 7:21
At that hour He healed many of diseases, afflictions and evil spirits, and graciously gave sight to many blind people.
  Matthew 11:5 — narrative parallel: Luke narrates healings and restored sight at that hour; Matthew's answer lists restored sight and other healing signs. Matthew's list does not independently narrate Luke's precise hour or reproduce the evil-spirit clause.
    Matthew 11:5 the blind receive sight and the lame walk, lepers are cleansed and the deaf hear, the dead are raised and the poor have good news proclaimed to them.

Luke 7:22
He answered them, “Go and report to John what you have seen and heard: the blind recover sight, the lame walk, leprous people are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead are raised, and the poor receive good news.
  Matthew 11:4–11:5 — narrative parallel: Both tell the messengers to report what is seen or heard and list blind sight, lame walking, leprosy cleansed, deaf hearing, dead raised and poor receiving good news. The reported signs are not used to merge every individual healing.
    Matthew 11:4 Jesus answered them, “Go and report to John what you hear and see:
    Matthew 11:5 the blind receive sight and the lame walk, lepers are cleansed and the deaf hear, the dead are raised and the poor have good news proclaimed to them.

Luke 7:23
And blessed is whoever does not stumble because of Me.”
  Matthew 11:6 — teaching parallel: Both pronounce blessed the person who does not stumble because of Jesus.
    Matthew 11:6 And blessed is whoever does not stumble because of Me.”

Luke 7:24
When John’s messengers had departed, He began to speak to the crowds about John: “What did you go out into the wilderness to behold? A reed shaken by the wind?
  Matthew 11:7 — narrative parallel: Both begin Jesus' address about John after the messengers depart and ask whether the crowd went into the wilderness to see a wind-shaken reed.
    Matthew 11:7 As they went away, Jesus began to speak to the crowds about John: “What did you go out into the wilderness to behold? A reed shaken by the wind?

Luke 7:25
But what did you go out to see? A man clothed in soft garments? Behold, those who live in splendid clothing and luxury are in royal palaces.
  Matthew 11:8 — teaching parallel: Both reject the expectation of finding John in soft clothing and place such clothing in royal houses. Luke additionally mentions splendid clothing and luxury.
    Matthew 11:8 But what did you go out to see? A man dressed in soft clothing? Behold, those wearing soft clothing are in kings’ houses.

Luke 7:26
But what did you go out to see? A prophet? Yes, I tell you, and more than a prophet.
  Matthew 11:9 — teaching parallel: Both answer the question about seeing a prophet by calling John more than a prophet.
    Matthew 11:9 But why did you go out? To see a prophet? Yes, I tell you, and more than a prophet.

Luke 7:27
This is the one about whom it is written: ‘Behold, I send My messenger before Thy face, who will prepare Thy way ahead of Thee.’
  Matthew 11:10 — quotation: Both apply the written messenger saying to John, with My messenger before Thy face preparing Thy way. This is a direct Gospel counterpart to Jesus' application.
    Matthew 11:10 This is the one about whom it is written: ‘Behold, I send My messenger before Thy face, who will prepare Thy way before Thee.’
  Malachi 3:1 — allusion: Luke's messenger preparing the addressed person's way recalls Malachi's messenger who will look to a way before God's face in the supplied wording. The My-face versus Thy-face formulations and Malachi's additional sanctuary and covenant-messenger clauses are preserved.
    Malachi 3:1 “Look, I am sending out My messenger, and he will look to a way before My face. Suddenly the Lord whom you seek will come to His own sanctuary, and the messenger of the covenant whom you desire. Look, he is coming,” says the Lord Almighty.

Luke 7:28
I tell you, among those born of women no one is greater than John; yet the lesser one in God’s kingdom is greater than he.”
  Matthew 11:11 — teaching parallel: Both contrast John's greatness among those born of women with greater status in the kingdom. Luke says the lesser one in God's kingdom; Matthew says the least in the kingdom of the heavens and explicitly names John the Baptist.
    Matthew 11:11 Truly I tell you, among those born of women none greater than John the Baptist has arisen; yet the least in the kingdom of the heavens is greater than he.

Luke 7:29
When all the people and the tax collectors heard, they acknowledged God’s righteousness, having been baptized with John’s baptism.
  Matthew 21:32 — thematic parallel: Both contrast receptive tax collectors with resistant religious leaders in response to John. Luke specifically describes acknowledgment of God's righteousness after John's baptism; Matthew says tax collectors and prostitutes believed John. The groups and actions are not made identical.
    Matthew 21:32 For John came to you in the way of righteousness and you did not believe him, but tax collectors and prostitutes believed him. Even after seeing this, you did not afterward change your minds and believe him.

Luke 7:30
But the Pharisees and experts in the law rejected God’s purpose for themselves, not having been baptized by him.
  Matthew 21:32 — thematic parallel: Both describe religious leaders rejecting John's ministry. Luke specifies Pharisees and law experts not being baptized and rejecting God's purpose for themselves; Matthew emphasizes unbelief and failure to change their minds after others believed.
    Matthew 21:32 For John came to you in the way of righteousness and you did not believe him, but tax collectors and prostitutes believed him. Even after seeing this, you did not afterward change your minds and believe him.

Luke 7:31
“To what, then, shall I compare the people of this generation, and what are they like?
  Matthew 11:16 — teaching parallel: Both introduce a comparison for the generation through the image of children in a marketplace. Luke asks first what the people are like; Matthew introduces the image within its question and answer.
    Matthew 11:16 To what shall I compare this generation? It is like children sitting in the marketplaces who call to the others,

Luke 7:32
They are like children sitting in the marketplace and calling to one another, saying, ‘We played the flute for you, and you did not dance; we lamented, and you did not weep.’
  Matthew 11:17 — teaching parallel: Both give the children's complaint that flute-playing did not produce dancing and lament did not produce the expected mourning. Luke specifies weeping; Matthew specifies beating the breasts.
    Matthew 11:17 ‘We played the flute for you, and you did not dance; we sang a lament, and you did not beat your breasts.’

Luke 7:33
For John the Baptist has come neither eating bread nor drinking wine, and you say, ‘He has a demon.’
  Matthew 11:18 — teaching parallel: Both report the accusation that John has a demon in response to his abstinent conduct. Luke explicitly names bread and wine; Matthew says neither eating nor drinking without those objects.
    Matthew 11:18 For John came neither eating nor drinking, and they say, ‘He has a demon.’

Luke 7:34
The Son of Man has come eating and drinking, and you say, ‘Behold, a glutton and wine-drinker, a friend of tax collectors and sinners.’
  Matthew 11:19 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the Son of Man's eating and drinking with the accusation of gluttony, wine-drinking and friendship with tax collectors and sinners. Matthew also includes its wisdom conclusion in the same verse.
    Matthew 11:19 The Son of Man came eating and drinking, and they say, ‘Behold, a gluttonous man and a wine drinker, a friend of tax collectors and sinners!’ Yet wisdom was vindicated by her works.”

Luke 7:35
Yet wisdom was justified by all her children.”
  Matthew 11:19 — teaching parallel: Both conclude by defending wisdom. Luke says wisdom was justified by all her children; Matthew says wisdom was vindicated by her works. The supplied formulations are retained rather than harmonized.
    Matthew 11:19 The Son of Man came eating and drinking, and they say, ‘Behold, a gluttonous man and a wine drinker, a friend of tax collectors and sinners!’ Yet wisdom was vindicated by her works.”

Luke 7:36
One of the Pharisees asked Him to eat with him. He entered the Pharisee’s house and reclined at table.
  Luke 11:37 — narrative parallel: Both same-book scenes begin with a Pharisee inviting Jesus to a meal and His entering and reclining. They are distinct meals with different ensuing controversies; the inviter is not assumed to be the same man.
    Luke 11:37 As He was speaking, a Pharisee invited Him to dine with him. He went in and reclined at the table.

Luke 7:37
And behold, a woman in the city who was a sinner learned that He was reclining in the Pharisee’s house. She brought an alabaster vessel of perfume
  Matthew 26:7 — narrative parallel: Both anointing accounts introduce a woman with an alabaster vessel of perfume. Luke calls the woman a sinner in a Pharisee's house; Matthew's surrounding account names Simon the leper and describes head anointing. The women and meals are not identified.
    Matthew 26:7 a woman came to Him with an alabaster flask of very costly perfume and poured it upon His head as He reclined at table.

Luke 7:38
and stood behind Him at His feet, weeping. She began to wet His feet with her tears and wiped them with the hair of her head. She kept kissing His feet and anointing them with the perfume.
  John 12:3 — narrative parallel: Both describe anointing Jesus' feet and wiping them with a woman's hair. Luke's unnamed woman wets them with tears and kisses them; John names Mary and describes costly perfume. Luke's woman is not assigned Mary's identity.
    John 12:3 Then Mary took a litra of costly perfume of genuine nard, anointed Jesus’ feet, and wiped His feet with her hair. And the house was filled with the fragrance of the perfume.

Luke 7:39
When the Pharisee who had invited Him saw it, he said within himself, “If this man were a prophet, He would know who and what sort of woman is touching Him, that she is a sinner.”
  John 4:18–4:19 — thematic contrast: Both involve a judgment of Jesus as prophet in relation to knowledge of a woman's life. Luke's host doubts His prophetic knowledge; John's woman recognizes it after Jesus describes her relationships. The women and their histories are not identified or given the same moral description.
    John 4:18 for you have had five husbands, and the one you have now is not your husband. This you have spoken truly.”
    John 4:19 The woman said to Him, “Sir, I see that Thou art a prophet.

Luke 7:40
Jesus answered him, “Simon, I have something to say to you.” He said, “Teacher, say it.”
  Luke 5:22 — narrative parallel: In the context of Simon's inward objection, Jesus answers him; Luke5 explicitly describes Jesus recognizing inward thoughts and answering critics. The two challenges and respondents are distinct, and this Simon is not identified with Simon Peter.
    Luke 5:22 Jesus recognized their thoughts and answered them, “What are you reasoning in your hearts?

Luke 7:41
“A certain lender had two debtors. One owed five hundred denarii and the other fifty.
  Matthew 18:24–18:27 — parable parallel: Both parables use an unpayable debt and a creditor's release. Luke introduces two debts of five hundred and fifty denarii; Matthew introduces one ten-thousand-talent debt. The amounts, people and ensuing morals are not made identical.
    Matthew 18:24 And when he began to settle them, one was brought to him who owed ten thousand talents.
    Matthew 18:25 And since he did not have the means to repay, his master commanded that he be sold, with his wife and children and all that he had, and that payment be made.
    Matthew 18:26 The slave therefore fell down and did him homage, saying, ‘Have patience with me, and I will repay you everything.’
    Matthew 18:27 And the master of that slave, moved with compassion, released him and forgave him the loan.

Luke 7:42
Since they had nothing with which to repay, he graciously forgave them both. Which of them, then, will love him more?”
  Matthew 18:25–18:27 — parable parallel: Both parables describe inability to repay followed by cancellation of debt. Luke forgives two debtors and asks about their love; Matthew describes a master's compassion and release of one slave. The different plot and moral remain distinct.
    Matthew 18:25 And since he did not have the means to repay, his master commanded that he be sold, with his wife and children and all that he had, and that payment be made.
    Matthew 18:26 The slave therefore fell down and did him homage, saying, ‘Have patience with me, and I will repay you everything.’
    Matthew 18:27 And the master of that slave, moved with compassion, released him and forgave him the loan.

Luke 7:43
Simon answered, “I suppose the one to whom he forgave more.” He said to him, “You judged correctly.”
  Luke 7:47 — internal parable interpretation: Simon's answer that greater forgiveness leads to greater love corresponds to Jesus' conclusion contrasting much and little forgiveness and love. This links the same episode's parable and application without making an independent witness.
    Luke 7:47 For this reason I tell you: her many sins are forgiven, because she loved much. But the one who is forgiven little loves little.”

Luke 7:44
Turning toward the woman, He said to Simon, “Do you see this woman? I entered your house; you gave Me no water for My feet, but she wet My feet with her tears and wiped them with her hair.
  John 12:3 — narrative parallel: Both describe wiping Jesus' feet with hair. Luke contrasts the host's missing water with the unnamed woman's tears; John describes Mary's perfume. The liquid, named identity and occasions remain distinct.
    John 12:3 Then Mary took a litra of costly perfume of genuine nard, anointed Jesus’ feet, and wiped His feet with her hair. And the house was filled with the fragrance of the perfume.

Luke 7:45
You gave Me no kiss, but from the time I entered she has not stopped kissing My feet.
  Luke 22:47–22:48 — thematic parallel: The woman's repeated kisses of Jesus' feet are contrasted with Simon's omitted greeting kiss in this scene; Judas later approaches Jesus for a kiss and Jesus questions handing over the Son of Man with a kiss. These are contrasting uses of a kiss in Luke, devotion and hospitality versus betrayal, not the same kind of kiss, person, or incident.
    Luke 22:47 While He was still speaking, behold, a crowd came, and the one called Judas, one of the Twelve, was going before them. He drew near to Jesus to kiss Him.
    Luke 22:48 But Jesus said to him, “Judas, do you hand over the Son of Man with a kiss?”

Luke 7:46
You did not anoint My head with oil, but she anointed My feet with perfume.
  John 12:3 — narrative parallel: Both describe perfume applied to Jesus' feet. Luke contrasts that action with Simon not anointing His head with oil; John narrates Mary's foot anointing. This does not identify Luke's woman with Mary or merge the meals.
    John 12:3 Then Mary took a litra of costly perfume of genuine nard, anointed Jesus’ feet, and wiped His feet with her hair. And the house was filled with the fragrance of the perfume.

Luke 7:47
For this reason I tell you: her many sins are forgiven, because she loved much. But the one who is forgiven little loves little.”
  Luke 7:42–7:43 — internal parable interpretation: Jesus' much-or-little forgiveness and love conclusion corresponds to the two-debtor story and Simon's judgment about the one forgiven more. The link preserves Luke's supplied because she loved much wording without imposing an additional account of how forgiveness is earned.
    Luke 7:42 Since they had nothing with which to repay, he graciously forgave them both. Which of them, then, will love him more?”
    Luke 7:43 Simon answered, “I suppose the one to whom he forgave more.” He said to him, “You judged correctly.”

Luke 7:48
He said to her, “Your sins are forgiven.”
  Luke 5:20 — teaching parallel: Both have Jesus directly pronounce a person's sins forgiven. Here He addresses the woman at the meal; earlier He addresses the paralyzed man after seeing faith. The recipients and occasions are distinct.
    Luke 5:20 Seeing their faith, He said, “Man, your sins are forgiven you.”

Luke 7:49
Those reclining with Him began to say within themselves, “Who is this who even forgives sins?”
  Luke 5:21 — narrative parallel: Both scenes respond to Jesus' forgiveness pronouncement with a question about who can forgive sins. Luke7 has meal guests ask about Him; Luke5 names scribes and Pharisees and their claim that God alone forgives.
    Luke 5:21 The scribes and Pharisees began to reason, saying, “Who is this who speaks blasphemies? Who can forgive sins except God alone?”

Luke 7:50
And He said to the woman, “Your faith has saved you; go in peace.”
  Mark 5:34 — teaching parallel: Both end with Jesus saying that faith has saved the woman and directing her to go in peace. Mark concerns the woman healed of bleeding and adds freedom from her affliction; Luke concerns the forgiven woman at the meal. They are not identified.
    Mark 5:34 And He said to her, “Daughter, your faith has saved you; go in peace, and be well, free from your affliction.”

Luke 8:1
After this He traveled through cities and villages, proclaiming and announcing the good news of God’s kingdom. The Twelve were with Him,
  Matthew 4:23 — ministry-summary parallel: Both summarize Jesus traveling and proclaiming the kingdom's good news. Matthew additionally names Galilean synagogues and healing; Luke names cities, villages and the accompanying Twelve.
    Matthew 4:23 He went throughout Galilee, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the good news of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every infirmity among the people.

Luke 8:2
and certain women who had been healed of evil spirits and illnesses: Mary called Magdalene, from whom seven demons had gone out,
  Mark 16:9 — narrative callback: Both identify Mary Magdalene as having been delivered from seven demons. Mark's target belongs to the edition's double-bracketed longer ending; it describes a resurrection appearance, not this ministry journey or all the women Luke lists.
    Mark 16:9 ⟦After He had risen early on the first day of the week, He appeared first to Mary Magdalene, from whom He had cast out seven demons.

Luke 8:3
Joanna, wife of Chuza, Herod’s steward, Susanna, and many others, who served them out of their own belongings.
  Mark 15:40–15:41 — narrative parallel: Both describe women accompanying and serving Jesus during His ministry. Luke specifies provision from their belongings and names Joanna and Susanna; Mark's named group differs and does not independently identify Chuza or those women.
    Mark 15:40 There were also women watching from a distance, among them Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James the Little and Joses, and Salome.
    Mark 15:41 When He was in Galilee, they had followed Him and served Him; there were also many other women who had come up with Him to Jerusalem.

Luke 8:4
When a large crowd was gathering and people from each city were coming to Him, He spoke through a parable:
  Mark 4:1–4:2 — narrative parallel: Both introduce parabolic teaching before a large gathering. Mark describes a seaside boat setting; Luke describes people coming from cities without giving that boat arrangement here.
    Mark 4:1 Again He began teaching beside the sea. A very great crowd gathered around Him, so that He got into a boat on the sea and sat down, while the whole crowd was on the land beside the sea.
    Mark 4:2 He taught them many things in parables, and in His teaching He said to them:

Luke 8:5
“The sower went out to sow his seed. As he sowed, some fell beside the road, was trampled, and the birds of the sky ate it.
  Mark 4:3–4:4 — parable parallel: Both begin with a sower and roadside seed eaten by birds. Luke additionally says it was trampled; Mark does not include that detail.
    Mark 4:3 “Listen! Look, the sower went out to sow.
    Mark 4:4 As he sowed, some fell beside the road, and the birds came and ate it up.

Luke 8:6
Other seed fell on the rock, and when it sprouted it withered because it had no moisture.
  Mark 4:5–4:6 — parable parallel: Both describe rocky-ground seed sprouting and withering. Luke explains lack of moisture; Mark describes shallow soil, scorching sun and lack of root. The explanations are preserved separately.
    Mark 4:5 Other seed fell on rocky ground, where it had little soil, and immediately sprang up because the soil had no depth.
    Mark 4:6 When the sun rose, it was scorched, and because it had no root, it withered.

Luke 8:7
Other seed fell among thorns, and the thorns grew up with it and choked it.
  Mark 4:7 — parable parallel: Both describe seed among thorns being choked as the thorns grow. Mark additionally states that it produced no fruit.
    Mark 4:7 Other seed fell among the thorns. The thorns grew up and choked it, and it gave no fruit.

Luke 8:8
Other seed fell into good soil, sprouted and produced fruit a hundredfold.” As He said these things, He called out, “Let the one who has ears to hear listen.”
  Mark 4:8–4:9 — parable parallel: Both describe fruitful good soil and call hearers with ears to listen. Luke names a hundredfold yield; Mark also names thirtyfold and sixtyfold.
    Mark 4:8 Other seeds fell into the good soil and gave fruit, growing up and increasing; they yielded thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and a hundredfold.”
    Mark 4:9 He said, “Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear.”

Luke 8:9
His disciples asked Him what this parable meant.
  Mark 4:10 — narrative parallel: Both describe an inquiry about the parable. Luke names the disciples; Mark describes those around Jesus together with the Twelve and a private setting.
    Mark 4:10 When He was alone, those around Him, together with the Twelve, asked Him about the parables.

Luke 8:10
He said, “To you it has been given to know the mysteries of God’s kingdom, but to the rest in parables, so that seeing they may not see, and hearing they may not understand.
  Mark 4:11–4:12 — teaching parallel: Both contrast granted knowledge of the kingdom's mystery or mysteries with outsiders' parables and unperceiving sight and hearing. Mark adds turning and forgiveness language absent from Luke's excerpt.
    Mark 4:11 He said to them, “To you the mystery of the kingdom of God has been given. But for those outside, everything comes in parables,
    Mark 4:12 so that, looking, they may look and not see, and hearing, they may hear and not understand, lest they turn back and be forgiven.”

Luke 8:11
This is the parable: the seed is God’s word.
  Mark 4:14 — parable explanation: Both identify the sown material with the word. Luke explicitly calls the seed God's word; Mark says the sower sows the word.
    Mark 4:14 The sower sows the word.

Luke 8:12
Those beside the road are the ones who have heard; then the devil comes and takes the word from their heart, so that they may not believe and be saved.
  Mark 4:15 — parable explanation: Both describe the evil one taking away the word after roadside hearers receive it. Luke calls him the devil and adds prevention of believing and salvation; Mark calls him Satan.
    Mark 4:15 These are the ones beside the road where the word is sown: whenever they hear, Satan immediately comes and takes away the word sown into them.

Luke 8:13
Those on the rock are the ones who, when they hear, receive the word with joy. They have no root; they believe for a time and fall away in a time of testing.
  Mark 4:16–4:17 — parable explanation: Both describe joyful initial reception without lasting root. Luke speaks of falling away in testing; Mark specifies distress or persecution because of the word.
    Mark 4:16 Likewise, these are the ones sown on the rocky places: whenever they hear the word, they immediately receive it with joy,
    Mark 4:17 but they have no root in themselves and last only for a time. Then, when distress or persecution comes because of the word, they immediately stumble.

Luke 8:14
What fell among thorns represents those who have heard, but as they go along they are choked by life’s cares, wealth and pleasures, and do not bring fruit to maturity.
  Mark 4:18–4:19 — parable explanation: Both explain the thorns through cares and riches choking the word's fruitfulness. Luke adds life's pleasures and failure to mature; Mark names the deceitfulness of riches and desires for other things.
    Mark 4:18 Others are the ones sown among the thorns. These are the ones who have heard the word,
    Mark 4:19 but the cares of the age, the deceitfulness of riches, and desires for other things enter and choke the word, and it becomes unfruitful.

Luke 8:15
What is in the good soil represents those who hear the word in a noble and good heart, hold it fast, and bear fruit with endurance.
  Mark 4:20 — parable explanation: Both explain good soil as hearing and fruit-bearing. Luke emphasizes a noble good heart, holding fast and endurance; Mark describes acceptance and thirtyfold, sixtyfold and hundredfold fruit.
    Mark 4:20 Those sown on the good soil are the ones who hear the word, accept it, and bear fruit: thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and a hundredfold.”

Luke 8:16
No one lights a lamp and covers it with a vessel or puts it under a bed, but places it on a lampstand, so that those entering may see the light.
  Mark 4:21 — teaching parallel: Both oppose hiding a lamp under something or under a bed to placing it on its stand. Luke names a vessel and the visibility for entrants; Mark names the modius basket.
    Mark 4:21 He said to them, “Does the lamp come to be put under the modius basket, or under the bed? Is it not to be put on the lampstand?

Luke 8:17
For nothing is hidden that will not become manifest, nor concealed that will not be known and come into the open.
  Mark 4:22 — teaching parallel: Both declare that hidden and secret things will become revealed and open. Their supplied sentences express the same disclosure principle without requiring an identical setting.
    Mark 4:22 For nothing is hidden except to be revealed, nor has anything become secret except to come into the open.

Luke 8:18
Therefore watch how you hear. For whoever has will be given more, and whoever does not have will have even what he thinks he has taken from him.”
  Mark 4:24–4:25 — teaching parallel: Both connect attention to hearing with more being given to one who has and removal from one who lacks. Luke says how one hears and what one thinks one has; Mark says what one hears and what one has.
    Mark 4:24 He said to them, “Pay attention to what you hear. With the measure you use, it will be measured to you, and more will be added to you.
    Mark 4:25 For whoever has will be given more; and whoever does not have, even what he has will be taken from him.”

Luke 8:19
His mother and brothers came to Him but could not reach Him because of the crowd.
  Mark 3:31–3:32 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus' mother and brothers outside while a crowd surrounds Him. Luke explicitly says the crowd prevented their reaching Him; Mark reports their standing outside and sending for Him.
    Mark 3:31 His mother and His brothers came. Standing outside, they sent to Him and called Him.
    Mark 3:32 A crowd was sitting around Him. They said to Him, “Look! Thy mother and Thy brothers and Thy sisters are outside looking for Thee.”

Luke 8:20
It was reported to Him, “Thy mother and Thy brothers are standing outside, wanting to see Thee.”
  Mark 3:32 — narrative parallel: Both report to Jesus that His mother and brothers are outside seeking Him. Mark's supplied report additionally names His sisters.
    Mark 3:32 A crowd was sitting around Him. They said to Him, “Look! Thy mother and Thy brothers and Thy sisters are outside looking for Thee.”

Luke 8:21
He answered them, “My mother and My brothers are these who hear God’s word and do it.”
  Mark 3:35 — teaching parallel: Both define Jesus' family through obedience to God. Luke names hearing and doing God's word; Mark names doing God's will and explicitly includes sister.
    Mark 3:35 For whoever does the will of God is My brother and sister and mother.”

Luke 8:22
On one of those days He entered a boat with His disciples and said to them, “Let us cross to the other side of the lake.” They put out.
  Mark 4:35–4:36 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus and His disciples entering the lake-crossing episode after His instruction to cross. Mark supplies evening, departure from the crowd and other boats; Luke says one of those days.
    Mark 4:35 On that day, when evening came, He said to them, “Let us cross to the other side.”
    Mark 4:36 Leaving the crowd, they took Him with them, just as He was, in the boat. Other boats were with Him.

Luke 8:23
As they sailed, He fell asleep. A windstorm came down upon the lake, and they were being swamped and were in danger.
  Mark 4:37–4:38 — narrative parallel: Both combine a dangerous windstorm filling the boat with Jesus sleeping. Mark locates Him in the stern on a cushion, details Luke does not give.
    Mark 4:37 A great windstorm arose, and the waves broke into the boat, so that the boat was already filling.
    Mark 4:38 He was in the stern, asleep on the cushion. They woke Him and said to Him, “Teacher, dost Thou not care that we are perishing?”

Luke 8:24
They came and woke Him, saying, “Master, Master, we are perishing!” He awoke and rebuked the wind and the surging water. They ceased, and there was calm.
  Mark 4:38–4:39 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples waking Jesus over perishing and His rebuking the wind into calm. Their addresses differ, and Mark additionally quotes His command to the sea.
    Mark 4:38 He was in the stern, asleep on the cushion. They woke Him and said to Him, “Teacher, dost Thou not care that we are perishing?”
    Mark 4:39 And He awoke, rebuked the wind, and said to the sea, “Be silent; be still.” And the wind ceased, and there was a great calm.

Luke 8:25
He said to them, “Where is your faith?” Afraid, they marveled and said to one another, “Who then is this, that He commands even the winds and the water, and they obey Him?”
  Mark 4:40–4:41 — narrative parallel: Both join Jesus' question about faith with frightened amazement that wind and water obey Him. Luke asks where their faith is; Mark asks about cowardice and still having no faith.
    Mark 4:40 He said to them, “Why are you cowardly? Do you still have no faith?”
    Mark 4:41 They were greatly afraid and said to one another, “Who then is this, that even the wind and the sea obey Him?”

Luke 8:26
They sailed into the country of the Gerasenes, which lies opposite Galilee.
  Mark 5:1 — narrative parallel: Both locate the arrival in the country of the Gerasenes. Luke additionally places it opposite Galilee; the supplied name is retained rather than replaced by another edition's locality.
    Mark 5:1 They came to the other side of the sea, to the country of the Gerasenes.

Luke 8:27
When He stepped onto the land, a man from the city who had demons met Him. For a considerable time he had worn no garment and had not lived in a house, but among the tombs.
  Mark 5:2–5:3 — narrative parallel: Both describe a demon-afflicted man meeting Jesus on landing and living among tombs. Luke additionally describes his city origin, prolonged nakedness and lack of a house; Mark does not supply those particulars.
    Mark 5:2 As He stepped out of the boat, a man with an unclean spirit immediately met Him from the tombs.
    Mark 5:3 He made his dwelling among the tombs, and no one could bind him anymore, even with a chain.

Luke 8:28
Seeing Jesus, he cried out, fell before Him, and said with a loud voice, “What have I to do with Thee, Jesus, Son of the Most High God? I beg Thee, do not torment me!”
  Mark 5:6–5:7 — narrative parallel: Both describe the man bowing or falling before Jesus, loudly naming Him Son of the Most High God and pleading against torment. Mark adds running from a distance and an adjuration by God.
    Mark 5:6 Seeing Jesus from far away, he ran and bowed before Him.
    Mark 5:7 He cried out with a loud voice and said, “What hast Thou to do with me, Jesus, Son of God Most High? I adjure Thee by God: do not torment me!”

Luke 8:29
For He was commanding the unclean spirit to leave the man. It had seized him many times; he was bound with chains and shackles and kept under guard, but he broke the bonds and was driven by the demon into deserted places.
  Mark 5:3–5:4 — narrative parallel: Both describe repeated attempts to bind the man and his breaking the restraints. Luke adds the command to the spirit, guards and being driven into deserted places; this target specifically supports the restraint-and-breaking portion.
    Mark 5:3 He made his dwelling among the tombs, and no one could bind him anymore, even with a chain.
    Mark 5:4 He had often been bound with fetters and chains, but he had torn the chains apart and shattered the fetters. No one had the strength to subdue him.

Luke 8:30
Jesus asked him, “What is your name?” He said, “Legion,” because many demons had entered him.
  Mark 5:9 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' question about the name and the answer Legion because of plurality. Luke explains that many demons had entered him; Mark's answer says we are many.
    Mark 5:9 He asked him, “What is your name?” He said to Him, “Legion is my name, because we are many.”

Luke 8:31
They begged Him not to command them to go away into the abyss.
  Mark 5:10 — narrative parallel: Both describe a plea not to be sent away. Luke names the abyss; Mark names departure from the country. Those destinations are not silently treated as interchangeable wording.
    Mark 5:10 He begged Him earnestly not to send them out of the country.

Luke 8:32
A herd of many pigs was feeding there on the hillside. They begged Him to permit them to enter those pigs, and He permitted them.
  Mark 5:11–5:13 — narrative parallel: Both describe a hillside or mountain herd, the spirits' request to enter the pigs and Jesus' permission. Mark additionally gives the subsequent entry and drowning in the same target range.
    Mark 5:11 Now a large herd of pigs was feeding there near the mountain.
    Mark 5:12 They begged Him, saying, “Send us into the pigs, so that we may enter them.”
    Mark 5:13 He allowed them. The unclean spirits came out and entered the pigs, and the herd rushed down the steep bank into the sea—about two thousand—and they drowned in the sea.

Luke 8:33
The demons came out of the man and entered the pigs. The herd rushed down the steep bank into the lake and was drowned.
  Mark 5:13 — narrative parallel: Both describe the demons entering pigs that rush down a steep bank and drown in the water. Mark gives approximately two thousand and calls the water the sea; Luke gives no number and calls it the lake.
    Mark 5:13 He allowed them. The unclean spirits came out and entered the pigs, and the herd rushed down the steep bank into the sea—about two thousand—and they drowned in the sea.

Luke 8:34
When the herdsmen saw what had happened, they fled and reported it in the city and in the fields.
  Mark 5:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe the herdsmen fleeing and reporting the event in the city and fields. Mark continues with people coming to investigate.
    Mark 5:14 Those tending them fled and reported it in the city and in the fields. People came to see what had happened.

Luke 8:35
People came out to see what had happened. They came to Jesus and found the man from whom the demons had gone out sitting at Jesus’ feet, clothed and in his right mind; and they were afraid.
  Mark 5:15 — narrative parallel: Both describe visitors seeing the formerly afflicted man sitting clothed and sane, and becoming afraid. Luke places him at Jesus' feet; Mark explicitly recalls the legion.
    Mark 5:15 They came to Jesus and saw the demon-possessed man sitting, clothed and in his right mind—the one who had had the legion—and they were afraid.

Luke 8:36
Those who had seen reported to them how the demon-possessed man had been saved.
  Mark 5:16 — narrative parallel: Both describe eyewitnesses explaining the afflicted man's changed condition. Mark also names what happened to the pigs; Luke describes how the man had been saved.
    Mark 5:16 Those who had seen it told them what had happened to the demon-possessed man and about the pigs.

Luke 8:37
The whole multitude of the surrounding country of the Gerasenes asked Him to depart from them, because they were gripped by great fear. He entered a boat and returned.
  Mark 5:17–5:18 — narrative parallel: Both describe people asking Jesus to leave and His boarding a boat. Luke explicitly attributes the request to great fear and narrates His return; Mark moves to the restored man's request.
    Mark 5:17 They began begging Him to leave their territory.
    Mark 5:18 As He was boarding the boat, the man who had been demon-possessed begged Him to let him be with Him.

Luke 8:38
The man from whom the demons had gone out begged to be with Him, but He sent him away, saying,
  Mark 5:18–5:19 — narrative parallel: Both describe the restored man's request to accompany Jesus being refused before he receives a homeward commission. Luke says Jesus sent him away; Mark explicitly says He did not allow him.
    Mark 5:18 As He was boarding the boat, the man who had been demon-possessed begged Him to let him be with Him.
    Mark 5:19 He did not allow him, but said to him, “Go home to your own people and report to them how much the Lord has done for you and how He has had mercy on you.”

Luke 8:39
“Return to your home and recount everything God has done for you.” He went away proclaiming throughout the whole city everything Jesus had done for him.
  Mark 5:19–5:20 — narrative parallel: Both commission homeward testimony and narrate proclamation of what Jesus had done. Luke says God in the commission and the whole city in its result; Mark says the Lord, adds mercy, and names the Decapolis.
    Mark 5:19 He did not allow him, but said to him, “Go home to your own people and report to them how much the Lord has done for you and how He has had mercy on you.”
    Mark 5:20 He went away and began proclaiming in the Decapolis how much Jesus had done for him, and everyone marveled.

Luke 8:40
When Jesus returned, the crowd welcomed Him, for they were all awaiting Him.
  Mark 5:21 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus' return across the water and a crowd meeting Him. Luke says they welcomed and awaited Him; Mark describes a large crowd gathered by the sea without those explicit responses.
    Mark 5:21 When Jesus had crossed again in the boat to the other side, a large crowd gathered around Him, and He was beside the sea.

Luke 8:41
And behold, a man named Jairus came, who was a ruler of the synagogue. Falling at Jesus’ feet, he begged Him to enter his house,
  Mark 5:22–5:23 — narrative parallel: Both name Jairus, a synagogue ruler, falling at Jesus' feet and urgently asking His intervention for the daughter. Mark specifies laying hands so she may live; Luke asks Him to enter the house.
    Mark 5:22 One of the synagogue rulers came, named Jairus. Seeing Him, he fell at His feet
    Mark 5:23 and begged Him earnestly, saying, “My little daughter is at the point of death. Please come and lay Thy hands on her, so that she may be saved and live.”

Luke 8:42
because he had an only daughter, about twelve years old, and she was dying. As He went, the crowds pressed closely around Him.
  Mark 5:23–5:24 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jairus's daughter as dying and the crowd pressing as Jesus goes. Luke adds only daughter and about twelve years old here; this target does not repeat those details.
    Mark 5:23 and begged Him earnestly, saying, “My little daughter is at the point of death. Please come and lay Thy hands on her, so that she may be saved and live.”
    Mark 5:24 He went with him. A large crowd followed Him and pressed against Him.

Luke 8:43
A woman had suffered a flow of blood for twelve years. She had spent her whole livelihood on physicians, but could not be healed by anyone.
  Mark 5:25–5:26 — narrative parallel: Both describe twelve years of bleeding and exhausting resources on physicians without recovery. Mark adds suffering under treatment and worsening; Luke says no one could heal her.
    Mark 5:25 A woman who had had a flow of blood for twelve years,
    Mark 5:26 and had suffered much under many physicians and spent everything she had, without any benefit but instead growing worse,

Luke 8:44
Coming up behind Him, she touched the fringe of His garment, and immediately her flow of blood stopped.
  Mark 5:27–5:29 — narrative parallel: Both describe the woman approaching from behind, touching Jesus' clothing and immediately losing the blood flow. Luke specifies the garment's fringe; Mark additionally reports her prior thought about touching.
    Mark 5:27 heard about Jesus. She came behind Him in the crowd and touched His garment.
    Mark 5:28 For she was saying, “If I touch even His clothes, I will be saved.”
    Mark 5:29 Immediately the source of her blood dried up, and she knew in her body that she was healed of the affliction.

Luke 8:45
Jesus said, “Who touched Me?” When everyone denied it, Peter said, “Master, the crowds surround Thee and press against Thee.”
  Mark 5:30–5:31 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' question about touching and the reply about the pressing crowd. Luke attributes the reply to Peter after denials; Mark names the disciples and specifies His clothes.
    Mark 5:30 Immediately Jesus, recognizing in Himself that power had gone out from Him, turned around in the crowd and said, “Who touched My clothes?”
    Mark 5:31 His disciples said to Him, “Thou seest the crowd pressing against Thee, and Thou sayest, ‘Who touched Me?’”

Luke 8:46
Jesus said, “Someone touched Me, for I knew that power had gone out from Me.”
  Mark 5:30 — narrative parallel: Both explicitly say Jesus knew or recognized power had gone out from Him. Mark combines this with His question; Luke presents His explanation after Peter's response.
    Mark 5:30 Immediately Jesus, recognizing in Himself that power had gone out from Him, turned around in the crowd and said, “Who touched My clothes?”

Luke 8:47
When the woman saw that she had not escaped notice, she came trembling and fell before Him. Before all the people she reported why she had touched Him and how she had been healed immediately.
  Mark 5:33 — narrative parallel: Both describe the trembling woman falling before Jesus and telling what happened. Luke explicitly places her account before all the people and names its reason and immediate healing.
    Mark 5:33 The woman, afraid and trembling, knowing what had happened to her, came and fell before Him and told Him the whole truth.

Luke 8:48
He said to her, “Daughter, your faith has saved you; go in peace.”
  Mark 5:34 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' address Daughter, declaration that her faith saved her and dismissal in peace. Mark adds release from her affliction.
    Mark 5:34 And He said to her, “Daughter, your faith has saved you; go in peace, and be well, free from your affliction.”

Luke 8:49
While He was still speaking, someone came from the synagogue ruler’s house, saying, “Your daughter has died. Do not trouble the Teacher any longer.”
  Mark 5:35 — narrative parallel: Both interrupt Jesus' speech with a report from the ruler's house that the daughter died and the Teacher should no longer be troubled. Luke names one messenger; Mark describes people coming.
    Mark 5:35 While He was still speaking, people came from the synagogue ruler’s house, saying, “Your daughter has died. Why trouble the Teacher any further?”

Luke 8:50
Jesus heard and answered him, “Do not fear; only believe, and she will be saved.”
  Mark 5:36 — narrative parallel: Both answer the death report with do not fear, only believe. Luke adds that she will be saved; Mark does not repeat that promise in the cited unit.
    Mark 5:36 Jesus, overhearing the word being spoken, said to the synagogue ruler, “Do not fear; only believe.”

Luke 8:51
When He came to the house, He allowed no one to enter with Him except Peter, John and James, and the child’s father and mother.
  Mark 5:37–5:40 — narrative parallel: Both restrict Jesus' accompanying group to Peter, James and John and bring the child's parents into the room. Mark distributes the restrictions over the approach and later entry after dismissing mourners; Luke states them at the house.
    Mark 5:37 He allowed no one to accompany Him except Peter, James, and John the brother of James.
    Mark 5:38 They came to the synagogue ruler’s house, and He saw a commotion, with people weeping and wailing loudly.
    Mark 5:39 He went in and said to them, “Why are you making a commotion and weeping? The child has not died but is sleeping.”
    Mark 5:40 They laughed at Him. But He put them all outside, took the child’s father and mother and those with Him, and went in where the child was.

Luke 8:52
Everyone was weeping and beating themselves in mourning for her. He said, “Do not weep, for she has not died, but is sleeping.”
  Mark 5:38–5:39 — narrative parallel: Both describe weeping mourners and Jesus saying the child is sleeping rather than dead. Luke additionally names beating in mourning and commands them not to weep.
    Mark 5:38 They came to the synagogue ruler’s house, and He saw a commotion, with people weeping and wailing loudly.
    Mark 5:39 He went in and said to them, “Why are you making a commotion and weeping? The child has not died but is sleeping.”

Luke 8:53
They laughed at Him, knowing that she had died.
  Mark 5:40 — narrative parallel: Both describe the onlookers laughing at Jesus' statement. Luke explicitly says they knew she had died; Mark moves to putting the others out.
    Mark 5:40 They laughed at Him. But He put them all outside, took the child’s father and mother and those with Him, and went in where the child was.

Luke 8:54
But He took her hand and called, “Child, rise.”
  Mark 5:41 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus taking the child's hand and commanding her to rise. Mark preserves Talitha koum and its translation; Luke gives Child, rise directly.
    Mark 5:41 Taking the child’s hand, He said to her, “Talitha koum,” which translated means, “Little girl, I say to you, arise!”

Luke 8:55
Her spirit returned, and she rose immediately. He ordered that she be given something to eat.
  Mark 5:42–5:43 — narrative parallel: Both describe her immediately rising and a command to feed her. Luke additionally says her spirit returned; Mark names walking and her age rather than that phrase.
    Mark 5:42 Immediately the little girl rose and walked around, for she was twelve years old. Immediately they were overcome with great amazement.
    Mark 5:43 He gave them strict orders that no one should know this, and said that she should be given something to eat.

Luke 8:56
Her parents were astonished, but He commanded them to tell no one what had happened.
  Mark 5:42–5:43 — narrative parallel: Both conclude with amazement and strict secrecy. Luke specifies astonished parents; Mark records great amazement and an order that no one know.
    Mark 5:42 Immediately the little girl rose and walked around, for she was twelve years old. Immediately they were overcome with great amazement.
    Mark 5:43 He gave them strict orders that no one should know this, and said that she should be given something to eat.

Luke 9:1
Calling the Twelve together, He gave them power and authority over all demons and to heal diseases.
  Matthew 10:1 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus summoning the Twelve and granting authority over unclean spirits and healing diseases. Matthew explicitly includes every disease and infirmity.
    Matthew 10:1 He summoned His twelve disciples and gave them authority over unclean spirits, to cast them out and to heal every disease and every infirmity.

Luke 9:2
He sent them to proclaim God’s kingdom and heal the sick.
  Matthew 10:7–10:8 — mission-teaching parallel: Both commission kingdom proclamation and healing. Matthew names the kingdom of the heavens and adds raising dead, cleansing lepers, exorcism and freely giving; Luke's brief commission does not list all these here.
    Matthew 10:7 As you go, proclaim, ‘The kingdom of the heavens has drawn near.’
    Matthew 10:8 Heal the sick, raise the dead, cleanse lepers, cast out demons. Freely you received; freely give.

Luke 9:3
He said to them, “Take nothing for the journey: neither a staff nor a bag nor bread nor silver; neither are you to have two tunics each.
  Matthew 10:9–10:10 — mission-teaching parallel: Both restrict money, bag, extra tunics and staff for the journey. Luke additionally names bread; Matthew lists sandals and several metals. These prohibitions are not rewritten to match Mark's distinct staff permission.
    Matthew 10:9 Do not acquire gold, silver, or copper for your belts,
    Matthew 10:10 nor a bag for the journey, nor two tunics, nor sandals, nor a staff; for the worker is worthy of his food.

Luke 9:4
Whatever house you enter, remain there and depart from there.
  Mark 6:10 — mission-teaching parallel: Both direct the travelers to remain in the house they enter until departure from that place.
    Mark 6:10 He said to them, “Wherever you enter a house, remain there until you leave that place.

Luke 9:5
Wherever people do not receive you, as you leave that city shake the dust from your feet as a testimony against them.”
  Mark 6:11 — mission-teaching parallel: Both direct a dust-shaking testimony when a place refuses reception. Mark explicitly adds refusal to listen, while Luke describes leaving the city.
    Mark 6:11 Whatever place does not receive you, and whose people do not listen to you, shake off the dust beneath your feet as you leave there, as a testimony to them.”

Luke 9:6
They went out and passed through the villages, announcing good news and healing everywhere.
  Mark 6:12–6:13 — narrative parallel: Both narrate the mission being carried out through proclamation and healing. Mark specifies repentance, demons expelled and anointing with oil; Luke summarizes good news and healing throughout the villages.
    Mark 6:12 They went out and proclaimed that people should repent.
    Mark 6:13 They cast out many demons, and anointed many sick people with oil and healed them.

Luke 9:7
Herod the tetrarch heard of everything that was happening and was perplexed, because some were saying that John had been raised from the dead,
  Mark 6:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe Herod hearing and reports that John had risen. Luke says he was perplexed; Mark connects the report to Jesus' public name and mighty powers.
    Mark 6:14 King Herod heard of it, for His name had become known. People were saying, “John the baptizer has been raised from the dead, and that is why mighty powers are at work in Him.”

Luke 9:8
others that Elijah had appeared, and others that one of the ancient prophets had arisen.
  Mark 6:15 — narrative parallel: Both report public identifications with Elijah or a prophet. Luke specifies an ancient prophet arisen; Mark says a prophet like one of the prophets.
    Mark 6:15 Others said, “He is Elijah.” Still others said, “He is a prophet, like one of the prophets.”

Luke 9:9
Herod said, “John I beheaded. But who is this about whom I hear such things?” And he sought to see Him.
  Mark 6:16 — narrative parallel: Both have Herod acknowledge beheading John. Luke then asks who Jesus is and seeks to see Him; Mark has Herod assert that the beheaded John has risen. The different responses remain distinct.
    Mark 6:16 But when Herod heard, he said, “John, whom I beheaded—he has been raised!”

Luke 9:10
When the apostles returned, they recounted to Him everything they had done. Taking them with Him, He withdrew privately to a city called Bethsaida.
  Mark 6:30–6:32 — narrative parallel: Both describe returning apostles reporting and Jesus withdrawing with them privately. Luke names Bethsaida; Mark describes a boat journey to a deserted place and a proposed rest. The geographical notices are not forced into a single wording.
    Mark 6:30 The apostles gathered around Jesus and reported to Him everything they had done and taught.
    Mark 6:31 He said to them, “Come away by yourselves to a deserted place and rest a little.” For many were coming and going, and they did not even have an opportunity to eat.
    Mark 6:32 They went away privately in the boat to a deserted place.

Luke 9:11
The crowds learned of it and followed Him. He welcomed them, spoke to them about God’s kingdom, and healed those needing a cure.
  Matthew 14:13–14:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe crowds following Jesus' withdrawal and His healing their sick. Matthew describes following on foot and compassion; Luke adds welcome and teaching about God's kingdom.
    Matthew 14:13 When Jesus heard this, He withdrew from there by boat to a deserted place privately. Hearing of it, the crowds followed Him on foot from the cities.
    Matthew 14:14 Coming out, He saw a great crowd, was moved with compassion for them, and healed their sick.

Luke 9:12
The day was beginning to draw to a close. The Twelve approached and said to Him, “Dismiss the crowd, so that they may go into the surrounding villages and countryside, find lodging and obtain provisions, because we are here in a deserted place.”
  Mark 6:35–6:36 — narrative parallel: Both describe late-day advice to dismiss a crowd from a deserted place to surrounding settlements for provisions. Luke additionally names lodging.
    Mark 6:35 When the hour was already late, His disciples came to Him and said, “The place is deserted, and the hour is already late.
    Mark 6:36 Dismiss them, so that they can go into the surrounding fields and villages and buy themselves something to eat.”

Luke 9:13
He said to them, “You give them something to eat.” They said, “We have no more than five loaves and two fish, unless we ourselves go and buy food for all these people.”
  Mark 6:37–6:38 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' instruction that the disciples feed the crowd, their objection about buying food and the five loaves and two fish. Mark specifies two hundred denarii and asks how much bread they have.
    Mark 6:37 He answered them, “You give them something to eat.” They said to Him, “Should we go and buy two hundred denarii worth of bread and give them something to eat?”
    Mark 6:38 He said to them, “How many loaves do you have? Go and see.” When they found out, they said, “Five, and two fish.”

Luke 9:14
For there were about five thousand men. He said to His disciples, “Have them recline in groups of about fifty each.”
  Mark 6:39–6:40 — narrative parallel: Both arrange the crowd in reclining groups, including fifties. Mark also names hundreds and green grass; this range supports the grouping rather than Luke's separate five-thousand count.
    Mark 6:39 He ordered them to have everyone recline in dining groups on the green grass.
    Mark 6:40 They reclined in rows, by hundreds and by fifties.

Luke 9:15
They did so and had everyone recline.
  Mark 6:40 — narrative parallel: Both narrate the disciples' arrangement being carried out as the people recline. Mark specifies rows of hundreds and fifties.
    Mark 6:40 They reclined in rows, by hundreds and by fifties.

Luke 9:16
Taking the five loaves and the two fish, He looked up to heaven, blessed them, broke them, and kept giving them to the disciples to set before the crowd.
  Mark 6:41 — narrative parallel: Both describe five loaves and two fish, looking to heaven, blessing, breaking and distributing through the disciples to the people.
