    Mark 6:41 He took the five loaves and the two fish, looked up to heaven, pronounced a blessing, and broke the loaves. He kept giving them to His disciples to set before the people, and He divided the two fish among them all.

Luke 9:17
They all ate and were filled, and the leftover fragments were gathered up: twelve baskets.
  Mark 6:42–6:43 — narrative parallel: Both describe universal satisfaction and twelve baskets of leftover fragments. Mark additionally mentions fish in the leftovers.
    Mark 6:42 They all ate and were satisfied.
    Mark 6:43 They took up broken pieces, twelve baskets full, and some of the fish.

Luke 9:18
While He was praying apart, His disciples were with Him, and He asked them, “Who do the crowds say I am?”
  Mark 8:27 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Jesus' question to the disciples about people's views of His identity. Luke locates it during private prayer with them; Mark names the road toward Caesarea Philippi's villages. Those settings are preserved.
    Mark 8:27 Jesus and His disciples went out to the villages of Caesarea Philippi. On the way He questioned His disciples, saying to them, “Who do people say that I am?”

Luke 9:19
They answered, “John the Baptist; others say Elijah; and others that one of the ancient prophets has arisen.”
  Mark 8:28 — narrative parallel: Both give John the Baptist, Elijah and a prophet among the popular answers. Luke specifies an ancient prophet arisen; Mark says one of the prophets.
    Mark 8:28 They answered Him, saying, “John the Baptist; others, Elijah; and still others, one of the prophets.”

Luke 9:20
He said to them, “But you, who do you say I am?” Peter answered, “God’s Christ.”
  Mark 8:29 — narrative parallel: Both turn the identity question directly to the disciples and give Peter's answer identifying Jesus as the Christ. Luke says God's Christ.
    Mark 8:29 He asked them, “But you—who do you say that I am?” Peter answered Him, “Thou art the Christ.”

Luke 9:21
He sternly commanded them to tell this to no one,
  Mark 8:30 — narrative parallel: Both describe a stern instruction not to tell others this identification of Jesus.
    Mark 8:30 He strictly warned them to tell no one about Him.

Luke 9:22
saying, “The Son of Man must suffer many things, be rejected by the elders, chief priests and scribes, be killed, and on the third day be raised.”
  Mark 8:31 — passion-prediction parallel: Both predict the Son of Man's suffering, rejection by elders, chief priests and scribes, death and resurrection. Luke says on the third day; Mark says after three days. Neither phrase is silently replaced.
    Mark 8:31 He began teaching them that the Son of Man must suffer many things, be rejected by the elders, the chief priests, and the scribes, be killed, and rise after three days.

Luke 9:23
He said to everyone, “If anyone wishes to come after Me, let him deny himself, take up his cross daily, and follow Me.
  Mark 8:34 — teaching parallel: Both require self-denial, cross-bearing and following Jesus from those who wish to follow Him. Luke explicitly adds daily.
    Mark 8:34 Calling the crowd to Him with His disciples, He said to them, “If anyone wants to follow behind Me, let him deny himself, take up his cross, and follow Me.

Luke 9:24
For whoever wishes to save his life will lose it, but whoever loses his life for My sake will save it.
  Mark 8:35 — teaching parallel: Both contrast losing life through trying to save it with saving it by losing it for Jesus. Mark additionally names the gospel.
    Mark 8:35 For whoever wants to save his life will lose it; but whoever loses his life for My sake and for the gospel will save it.

Luke 9:25
For what benefit is there for a person to gain the whole world but destroy or forfeit himself?
  Mark 8:36 — teaching parallel: Both ask what benefit gaining the world offers if a person forfeits life or self. Luke also says destroys himself; the supplied expressions remain distinct.
    Mark 8:36 For what does it benefit a person to gain the whole world and forfeit his life?

Luke 9:26
Whoever is ashamed of Me and My words, of him the Son of Man will be ashamed when He comes in His glory and that of the Father and the holy angels.
  Mark 8:38 — teaching parallel: Both warn that the Son of Man will be ashamed of those ashamed of Jesus and His words when He comes in glory with holy angels. Luke explicitly includes His own glory and the Father's; Mark additionally names the sinful generation.
    Mark 8:38 Whoever is ashamed of Me and My words in this adulterous and sinful generation, the Son of Man will also be ashamed of him when He comes in the glory of His Father with the holy angels.”

Luke 9:27
But truly I tell you, some standing here will certainly not taste death until they see God’s kingdom.”
  Mark 9:1 — teaching parallel: Both promise some present will see God's kingdom before tasting death. Mark adds that it has come in power; neither passage supplies a calendar date here.
    Mark 9:1 He said to them, “Truly I tell you, some standing here will certainly not taste death until they see that the kingdom of God has come in power.”

Luke 9:28
About eight days after these sayings, He took Peter, John and James and went up the mountain to pray.
  Mark 9:2 — transfiguration parallel: Both name Peter, James and John accompanying Jesus up a mountain. Luke says about eight days after the sayings and names prayer; Mark says after six days. No counting scheme is invented to erase the difference.
    Mark 9:2 After six days, Jesus took Peter, James, and John with Him and brought them up a high mountain privately, alone. He was transfigured before them,

Luke 9:29
As He prayed, the appearance of His face became different, and His clothing became white and flashing like lightning.
  Mark 9:3 — transfiguration parallel: Both describe brilliantly white clothing. Luke also describes the changed face during prayer; Mark adds comparison with a bleacher's inability to whiten clothes.
    Mark 9:3 and His clothes became gleaming, exceedingly white, as no cloth bleacher on earth could whiten them.

Luke 9:30
And behold, two men were speaking with Him, who were Moses and Elijah.
  Mark 9:4 — transfiguration parallel: Both name Moses and Elijah speaking with Jesus. Their named order differs, and Luke first describes them as two men.
    Mark 9:4 Elijah appeared to them with Moses, and they were talking with Jesus.

Luke 9:31
Appearing in glory, they spoke of His departure, which He was about to fulfill in Jerusalem.
  Luke 18:31–18:33 — same-book thematic link: Both connect Jesus' approaching Jerusalem with fulfillment. The later prediction explains the coming suffering, death and resurrection as fulfillment of what prophets wrote; the mountain scene names His departure and Moses and Elijah speaking. This does not independently attest their dialogue or silently replace departure with one narrower word.
    Luke 18:31 Taking the Twelve aside, He said to them, “Behold, we are going up to Jerusalem, and everything written through the prophets will be accomplished for the Son of Man.
    Luke 18:32 For He will be handed over to the nations and will be mocked and insulted and spat upon.
    Luke 18:33 And after flogging Him, they will kill Him, and on the third day He will rise.”

Luke 9:32
Peter and those with him were weighed down with sleep, but when fully awake they saw His glory and the two men standing with Him.
  2 Peter 1:16–1:18 — transfiguration testimony link: Both describe witnesses seeing Jesus' glory or majesty in the mountain context. Peter's passage also recalls the heavenly voice; it does not attest the sleep detail or independently describe the two men in Luke's unit.
    2 Peter 1:16 For we did not follow cleverly devised myths when we made known to you the power and coming of our Lord Jesus Christ, but we became eyewitnesses of His majesty.
    2 Peter 1:17 For He received honor and glory from God the Father when a voice such as this was borne to Him by the Majestic Glory: “This is My Son, My Beloved, in whom I have taken pleasure”—
    2 Peter 1:18 and we ourselves heard this voice borne from heaven when we were with Him on the holy mountain.

Luke 9:33
As they were parting from Him, Peter said to Jesus, “Master, it is good for us to be here. Let us make three tents, one for Thee, one for Moses and one for Elijah,” not knowing what he was saying.
  Mark 9:5–9:6 — transfiguration parallel: Both give Peter's proposal of three tents for Jesus, Moses and Elijah and say he did not know what to say. Luke locates it as the men were parting; Mark emphasizes the disciples' terror.
    Mark 9:5 Peter answered Jesus, “Rabbi, it is good for us to be here. Let us make three tents: one for Thee, one for Moses, and one for Elijah.”
    Mark 9:6 For he did not know what to answer, because they had become terrified.

Luke 9:34
While he said this, a cloud came and overshadowed them, and they were afraid as they entered the cloud.
  Mark 9:7 — transfiguration parallel: Both describe an overshadowing cloud. Luke adds entering the cloud and fear; Mark's cited unit proceeds directly to the voice.
    Mark 9:7 A cloud came, overshadowing them, and a voice came from the cloud: “This is My beloved Son; listen to Him.”

Luke 9:35
A voice came from the cloud, saying, “This is My Son, the chosen One; listen to Him.”
  Mark 9:7 — transfiguration parallel: Both identify Jesus as God's Son in the cloud-voice and command listening to Him. Luke says the chosen One; Mark says My beloved Son.
    Mark 9:7 A cloud came, overshadowing them, and a voice came from the cloud: “This is My beloved Son; listen to Him.”

Luke 9:36
When the voice came, Jesus was found alone. They kept silent and in those days reported to no one anything they had seen.
  Mark 9:8–9:9 — transfiguration parallel: Both end with Jesus alone and silence about the vision. Mark explicitly records Jesus' secrecy order until resurrection; Luke describes their silence in those days without giving that limit here.
    Mark 9:8 Suddenly, looking around, they no longer saw anyone with them except Jesus alone.
    Mark 9:9 As they came down from the mountain, He ordered them to tell no one what they had seen until the Son of Man had risen from the dead.

Luke 9:37
On the following day, when they came down from the mountain, a large crowd met Him.
  Mark 9:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus meeting a large crowd following the mountain scene. Luke specifies the following day; Mark places the crowd around the disciples and names scribes disputing, without that day interval.
    Mark 9:14 When they came to the disciples, they saw a large crowd around them and scribes disputing with them.

Luke 9:38
And behold, a man from the crowd cried out, “Teacher, I beg Thee to look upon my son, because he is my only child.
  Mark 9:17 — narrative parallel: Both give a father's appeal to Jesus about his son. Luke calls him an only child and asks Jesus to look; Mark specifies the speechless spirit and bringing the boy.
    Mark 9:17 One from the crowd answered Him, “Teacher, I brought Thee my son, who has a speechless spirit.

Luke 9:39
Behold, a spirit seizes him, and suddenly he cries out. It convulses him with foaming and scarcely leaves him, bruising him.
  Mark 9:18 — narrative parallel: Both describe the spirit seizing the child with foaming. Mark names dashing down, teeth-grinding and rigidity; Luke names crying, convulsing and bruising with scarce relief.
    Mark 9:18 Wherever it seizes him, it dashes him down; he foams, grinds his teeth, and becomes rigid. I told Thy disciples to cast it out, but they did not have the strength.”

Luke 9:40
I begged Thy disciples to cast it out, but they could not.”
  Mark 9:18 — narrative parallel: Both report that the father's request to the disciples to cast out the spirit had failed.
    Mark 9:18 Wherever it seizes him, it dashes him down; he foams, grinds his teeth, and becomes rigid. I told Thy disciples to cast it out, but they did not have the strength.”

Luke 9:41
Jesus answered, “O faithless and distorted generation, how long shall I be with you and bear with you? Bring your son here.”
  Mark 9:19 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' lament over an unbelieving generation, question about bearing with them and command to bring the boy. Luke additionally says distorted.
    Mark 9:19 He answered them, “O unbelieving generation, how long shall I be with you? How long shall I bear with you? Bring him to Me.”

Luke 9:42
While he was still approaching, the demon dashed him down and convulsed him. Jesus rebuked the unclean spirit, healed the boy, and gave him back to his father.
  Mark 9:20–9:27 — narrative parallel: Both narrate the child's convulsion on approach and Jesus' rebuke and successful intervention. Mark expands the exchange with the father and the spirit's departure, then Jesus raises the boy by the hand; Luke summarizes healing and giving him back to his father.
    Mark 9:20 They brought him to Him. When the spirit saw Him, it immediately convulsed the boy; falling on the ground, he rolled about, foaming.
    Mark 9:21 He asked his father, “How long has this been happening to him?” He said, “From childhood.
    Mark 9:22 Often it has thrown him both into fire and into water, to destroy him. But if Thou canst do anything, have compassion on us and help us.”
    Mark 9:23 Jesus said to him, “‘If Thou canst’! All things are possible for the one who believes.”
    Mark 9:24 Immediately the father of the child cried out and said, “I believe; help my unbelief!”
    Mark 9:25 Seeing that a crowd was running together, Jesus rebuked the unclean spirit, saying to it, “Speechless and deaf spirit, I command you: come out of him, and enter him no more.”
    Mark 9:26 Crying out and convulsing him violently, it came out. The boy became like a dead person, so that most of them said, “He has died.”
    Mark 9:27 But Jesus took him by the hand and raised him, and he stood up.

Luke 9:43
Everyone was overwhelmed at God’s majesty. While they all marveled at everything He was doing, He said to His disciples,
  Luke 7:15–7:16 — internal restoration-and-divine-acclaim pattern: Both Lukan scenes give an acclaim of God after Jesus restores a son to a parent. Luke9 follows the healed boy being given back to his father and names God's majesty; Luke7 gives the raised son to his mother and reports fear and glorifying God. Healing and raising, the children and parents, and the reported praise differ; the later handover prediction is not inserted into Luke7.
    Luke 7:15 The dead man sat up and began speaking, and He gave him to his mother.
    Luke 7:16 Fear seized everyone, and they glorified God, saying, “A great prophet has arisen among us,” and, “God has visited His people.”

Luke 9:44
“You, place these words in your ears: the Son of Man is about to be handed over into human hands.”
  Mark 9:31 — passion-prediction parallel: Both predict the Son of Man's being handed into human hands. Mark continues with death and resurrection; Luke's present unit gives the handover prediction and a command to attend to it.
    Mark 9:31 For He was teaching His disciples and saying to them, “The Son of Man is being handed over into human hands, and they will kill Him. After He is killed, He will rise after three days.”

Luke 9:45
But they did not understand this saying; it was veiled from them so that they might not perceive it, and they were afraid to ask Him about it.
  Mark 9:32 — narrative parallel: Both say the disciples did not understand Jesus' saying and feared asking Him. Luke additionally says it was veiled from them.
    Mark 9:32 But they did not understand the saying, and were afraid to ask Him.

Luke 9:46
A discussion arose among them about which of them might be greatest.
  Mark 9:33–9:34 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples discussing who was greatest. Mark locates the subsequent question at Capernaum after a road discussion; Luke simply introduces the discussion here.
    Mark 9:33 They came to Capernaum. When He was in the house, He asked them, “What were you discussing on the way?”
    Mark 9:34 They remained silent, for on the way they had discussed with one another who was greatest.

Luke 9:47
Jesus knew the thought of their heart. He took a child and placed him beside Himself,
  Mark 9:36 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus placing a child among or beside them for His teaching. Luke names His knowledge of their heart's thought; Mark describes embracing the child.
    Mark 9:36 He took a child, stood the child among them, and, embracing the child, said to them,

Luke 9:48
and said to them, “Whoever receives this child in My name receives Me; whoever receives Me receives the One who sent Me. For the one who is least among you all is great.”
  Mark 9:37 — teaching parallel: Both connect receiving a child in Jesus' name with receiving Jesus and the One who sent Him. Luke adds the least becoming great; Mark's unit does not include that sentence.
    Mark 9:37 “Whoever receives one such child in My name receives Me; and whoever receives Me does not receive Me but the One who sent Me.”

Luke 9:49
John answered, “Master, we saw someone casting out demons in Thy name and tried to stop him, because he does not follow with us.”
  Mark 9:38 — narrative parallel: Both have John report trying to stop a person casting out demons in Jesus' name because he was not following their group.
    Mark 9:38 John said to Him, “Teacher, we saw someone casting out demons in Thy name, and we tried to stop him, because he was not following us.”

Luke 9:50
Jesus said to him, “Do not stop him, for whoever is not against you is for you.”
  Mark 9:39–9:40 — teaching parallel: Both forbid stopping that exorcist and give the not-against/for principle. Luke says against you and for you; Mark says against us and for us and additionally explains the mighty worker's relation to speaking evil of Jesus.
    Mark 9:39 Jesus said, “Do not stop him, for no one who does a mighty work in My name will be able soon afterward to speak evil of Me.
    Mark 9:40 For whoever is not against us is for us.

Luke 9:51
As the days for His being taken up approached their fulfillment, He firmly set His face to go to Jerusalem.
  Luke 24:50–24:51 — same-book anticipation-and-event link: The notice of the approaching time for Jesus' being taken up anticipates Luke's later description of His being carried into heaven. Here He sets out toward Jerusalem; the final ascent is narrated toward Bethany, not on this departure day.
    Luke 24:50 And He led them out as far as toward Bethany, and lifted His hands and blessed them.
    Luke 24:51 And it happened that while He blessed them, He parted from them and was carried up into heaven.

Luke 9:52
He sent messengers before His face. They went and entered a Samaritan village to prepare for Him.
  Luke 10:1 — same-book mission parallel: Both describe people sent ahead of Jesus to prepare places He would visit. Luke9 concerns unnamed messengers in a Samaritan village; Luke10 concerns seventy-two sent in pairs to many places. The missions and personnel are not automatically identified.
    Luke 10:1 After this the Lord appointed seventy-two others also, and sent them two by two before His face into every city and place where He Himself was about to go.

Luke 9:53
But they did not receive Him, because His face was set toward Jerusalem.
  John 4:20–4:22 — thematic setting comparison: Both connect Samaritan-Jewish relations with Jerusalem. John's Samaritan woman discusses competing worship places; Luke narrates a village rejecting Jesus because He was going toward Jerusalem. John does not narrate this rejection or independently identify its villagers, and later describes Samaritans welcoming Jesus.
    John 4:20 Our fathers worshiped on this mountain, and you all say that in Jerusalem is the place where one must worship.”
    John 4:21 Jesus said to her, “Believe Me, woman, an hour is coming when neither on this mountain nor in Jerusalem will you worship the Father.
    John 4:22 You worship what you do not know; we worship what we know, because salvation is from the Jews.

Luke 9:54
Seeing this, the disciples James and John said, “Lord, dost Thou wish us to command fire to come down from heaven and consume them?”
  2 Kings 1:10–1:12 — allusion: The proposed fire from heaven consuming opponents recalls Elijah's fire consuming the captains and their men. Luke's supplied sentence does not explicitly name Elijah; Jesus' subsequent rebuke must not be replaced by an endorsement of the proposed repetition.
    2 Kings 1:10 Elijah answered and said to the captain of fifty, ‘If I am a man of God, fire will come down from heaven and consume you and your fifty men.’ Fire came down from heaven and consumed him and his fifty men.
    2 Kings 1:11 The king again sent to him another captain of fifty with his fifty men. The captain went up and spoke to him, saying, ‘Man of God, thus says the king: Come down quickly!’
    2 Kings 1:12 Elijah answered and spoke to him, saying, ‘If I am a man of God, fire will come down from heaven and consume you and your fifty men.’ Fire came down from heaven and consumed him and his fifty men.

Luke 9:55
But He turned and rebuked them.
  Luke 22:49–22:51 — same-book thematic comparison: Both scenes concern disciples proposing violence near Jesus and His response. Here He rebukes the fire proposal; later followers ask about swords, an ear is cut, and He says Allow this much and heals it. The sayings, weapons and occasions remain distinct.
    Luke 22:49 When those around Him saw what was about to happen, they said, “Lord, shall we strike with a sword?”
    Luke 22:50 And one of them struck the high priest’s slave and took off his right ear.
    Luke 22:51 But Jesus answered, “Allow this much.” And He touched the ear and healed him.

Luke 9:56
And they went to another village.
  Matthew 10:14 — departure after refusal of reception: After a Samaritan village refuses reception and Jesus rebukes the fire proposal, the group goes to another village. Matthew's mission instruction likewise directs departure where messengers are not received. Matthew adds dust-shaking, which Luke9 does not narrate, and the departure is not recast as flight from a violent persecution or an act of destructive retaliation.
    Matthew 10:14 Whoever will not receive you or hear your words, as you leave that house or city, shake the dust off your feet.

Luke 9:57
As they traveled on the road, someone said to Him, “I will follow Thee wherever Thou goest.”
  Matthew 8:19 — teaching-scene parallel: Both give a volunteer's promise to follow Jesus wherever He goes. Matthew identifies him as a scribe and has Teacher; Luke leaves him unnamed in the road scene.
    Matthew 8:19 One scribe came and said to Him, “Teacher, I will follow Thee wherever Thou goest.”

Luke 9:58
Jesus said to him, “Foxes have dens and the birds of the sky have dwelling places, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay His head.”
  Matthew 8:20 — teaching parallel: Both contrast foxes' dens and birds' places with the Son of Man's lack of a place to lay His head. Dwelling and resting places remain the actual English forms.
    Matthew 8:20 Jesus said to him, “Foxes have dens and the birds of the sky have resting places, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay His head.”

Luke 9:59
He said to another, “Follow Me.” But he said, “Lord, permit me first to go and bury my father.”
  Matthew 8:21 — teaching-scene parallel: Both give a prospective follower's request first to bury his father. Luke has Jesus initiate Follow Me; Matthew calls the petitioner another disciple and places the command in Jesus' next reply.
    Matthew 8:21 Another of His disciples said to Him, “Lord, first permit me to go and bury my father.”

Luke 9:60
He said to him, “Let the dead bury their own dead; but you go and proclaim God’s kingdom.”
  Matthew 8:22 — teaching parallel: Both give the dead-burying-the-dead reply in the call to follow Jesus. Luke additionally commands the petitioner to proclaim God's kingdom.
    Matthew 8:22 But Jesus said to him, “Follow Me, and let the dead bury their own dead.”

Luke 9:61
Another also said, “I will follow Thee, Lord; but first permit me to say farewell to those in my house.”
  1 Kings 19:20 — allusion: Both prospective followers first request a family farewell. Elisha asks to kiss his father before following Elijah; Luke's speaker asks to say farewell to those in his house before following Jesus. The answers and people are not made identical.
    1 Kings 19:20 Elisha left the oxen, ran after Elijah, and said, “I will kiss my father and follow you.” Elijah said, “Return, for I have acted for you.”

Luke 9:62
Jesus said to him, “No one who puts his hand to a plow and looks backward is fit for God’s kingdom.”
  1 Kings 19:19–19:21 — thematic imagery comparison: Luke's plowing-and-following saying can be compared with Elisha called while plowing and leaving his oxen to follow Elijah. Kings narrates a particular call and farewell; Luke gives a warning about looking backward and fitness for God's kingdom, not a verbatim quotation.
    1 Kings 19:19 He went from there and found Elisha son of Shaphat, who was plowing with oxen. Twelve pairs of oxen were before him, and he was among the twelve. He came upon him and threw his sheepskin cloak over him.
    1 Kings 19:20 Elisha left the oxen, ran after Elijah, and said, “I will kiss my father and follow you.” Elijah said, “Return, for I have acted for you.”
    1 Kings 19:21 He turned back from following him, took the pairs of oxen, and sacrificed them. With the oxen’s equipment he boiled them, gave them to the people, and they ate. Then he rose, followed Elijah, and ministered to him.

Luke 10:1
After this the Lord appointed seventy-two others also, and sent them two by two before His face into every city and place where He Himself was about to go.
  Mark 6:7 — mission parallel: Both describe Jesus sending followers in pairs. Luke names seventy-two others sent in advance to places He would visit; Mark concerns the Twelve and authority over spirits. The numbers and commissions remain distinct.
    Mark 6:7 He called the Twelve to Him and began sending them out two by two, giving them authority over the unclean spirits.

Luke 10:2
He said to them, “The harvest is plentiful, but the workers are few. Therefore petition the Lord of the harvest to send workers out into His harvest.
  Matthew 9:37–9:38 — teaching parallel: Both call the harvest plentiful, workers few and prayer to the harvest's Lord necessary to send workers.
    Matthew 9:37 Then He says to His disciples, “The harvest indeed is plentiful, but the labourers are few.
    Matthew 9:38 Pray therefore to the Lord of the harvest, that He send out labourers into His harvest.”

Luke 10:3
Go; behold, I send you as lambs among wolves.
  Matthew 10:16 — mission-teaching parallel: Both picture Jesus sending vulnerable sheep or lambs among wolves. Matthew adds prudence as snakes and innocence as doves, which Luke does not repeat.
    Matthew 10:16 Behold, I send you as sheep in the midst of wolves. So become prudent as snakes and innocent as doves.

Luke 10:4
Carry no purse, no bag, no sandals, and greet no one along the road.
  Matthew 10:9–10:10 — mission-teaching parallel: Both restrict journey possessions including bag and sandals. Luke names purse and no road greetings; Matthew specifies metals, tunics and staff without that greeting restriction. These are related instructions for different stated missions.
    Matthew 10:9 Do not acquire gold, silver, or copper for your belts,
    Matthew 10:10 nor a bag for the journey, nor two tunics, nor sandals, nor a staff; for the worker is worthy of his food.

Luke 10:5
Whatever house you enter, first say, ‘Peace to this house.’
  Matthew 10:12–10:13 — mission-teaching parallel: Both instruct an entering household greeting whose outcome is described as peace. Luke gives the spoken Peace to this house; Matthew describes greeting and the worthy household's receipt.
    Matthew 10:12 As you enter the house, greet it.
    Matthew 10:13 If the house is worthy, let your peace come upon it; but if it is not worthy, let your peace return to you.

Luke 10:6
If a son of peace is there, your peace will rest upon him; otherwise it will return to you.
  Matthew 10:13 — mission-teaching parallel: Both describe peace resting on a receptive household or returning if unsuitable. Luke names a son of peace; Matthew describes the house as worthy or unworthy.
    Matthew 10:13 If the house is worthy, let your peace come upon it; but if it is not worthy, let your peace return to you.

Luke 10:7
Remain in that same house, eating and drinking what they provide, for the worker is worthy of his pay. Do not move from house to house.
  1 Timothy 5:18 — teaching parallel: Both state that the worker deserves pay or wage. Luke applies the saying to traveling guests receiving food and drink while staying in one house; Timothy cites it in the context of honoring elders. No identical audience or new independent narrative is claimed.
    1 Timothy 5:18 For Scripture says, “You shall not muzzle an ox that threshes,” and, “The worker is worthy of his wage.”

Luke 10:8
Whatever city you enter and they receive you, eat what is set before you.
  1 Corinthians 10:27 — teaching parallel: Both direct guests to eat what is set before them. Luke concerns cities receiving mission travelers; Paul concerns accepting an unbeliever's invitation and not investigating food for conscience. The hospitality rule has distinct settings and limits.
    1 Corinthians 10:27 If any of the unbelievers invites you and you want to go, eat everything set before you without examining it for conscience’s sake.

Luke 10:9
Heal the sick in it and say to them, ‘God’s kingdom has drawn near to you.’
  Matthew 10:7–10:8 — mission-teaching parallel: Both join healing the sick with announcing the kingdom's nearness. Matthew gives other healing and ministry commands as well; Luke addresses the receiving city.
    Matthew 10:7 As you go, proclaim, ‘The kingdom of the heavens has drawn near.’
    Matthew 10:8 Heal the sick, raise the dead, cleanse lepers, cast out demons. Freely you received; freely give.

Luke 10:10
But whatever city you enter and they do not receive you, go out into its streets and say,
  Matthew 10:14 — mission-teaching parallel: Both address refusal to receive the travelers and their departure response. Luke introduces a public street declaration; Matthew moves directly to shaking off dust.
    Matthew 10:14 Whoever will not receive you or hear your words, as you leave that house or city, shake the dust off your feet.

Luke 10:11
‘Even the dust from your city that clings to our feet we wipe off against you. Nevertheless know this: God’s kingdom has drawn near.’
  Matthew 10:14 — mission-teaching parallel: Both direct dust-shaking or wiping off against a rejecting place. Luke explicitly names the city's clinging dust and repeats that God's kingdom has drawn near, which Matthew's unit does not.
    Matthew 10:14 Whoever will not receive you or hear your words, as you leave that house or city, shake the dust off your feet.

Luke 10:12
I tell you, it will be more bearable for Sodom on that day than for that city.
  Matthew 10:15 — teaching parallel: Both say Sodom will fare more bearably at judgment than the rejecting city. Matthew also names Gomorrah and explicitly says the day of judgment.
    Matthew 10:15 Truly I tell you, it will be more bearable for the land of Sodom and Gomorrah on the day of judgment than for that city.

Luke 10:13
Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty deeds performed among you had happened in Tyre and Sidon, they would long ago have repented, sitting in sackcloth and ashes.
  Matthew 11:21 — teaching parallel: Both pronounce woes on Chorazin and Bethsaida, contrasting Tyre and Sidon's hypothetical repentance in sackcloth and ashes after such mighty works. Luke adds sitting.
    Matthew 11:21 “Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty works that occurred in you had occurred in Tyre and Sidon, they would long ago have repented in sackcloth and ashes.

Luke 10:14
Nevertheless, it will be more bearable for Tyre and Sidon at the judgment than for you.
  Matthew 11:22 — teaching parallel: Both say the judgment will be more bearable for Tyre and Sidon than for the addressed towns.
    Matthew 11:22 Nevertheless I tell you, it will be more bearable for Tyre and Sidon on the day of judgment than for you.

Luke 10:15
And you, Capernaum, will you be exalted as far as heaven? You will go down as far as Hades.
  Matthew 11:23 — teaching parallel: Both oppose Capernaum's expected exaltation to heaven with descent to Hades. Matthew adds a hypothetical comparison with Sodom not present in Luke's unit.
    Matthew 11:23 And you, Capernaum, will you be exalted to heaven? You will descend to Hades! For if the mighty works that occurred in you had occurred in Sodom, it would have remained until today.

Luke 10:16
The one who hears you hears Me, and the one who rejects you rejects Me; the one who rejects Me rejects the One who sent Me.”
  Matthew 10:40 — teaching parallel: Both connect the messengers' reception with Jesus and His Sender. Luke states hearing and rejection, including rejection of the Sender; Matthew states the corresponding positive chain of receiving.
    Matthew 10:40 Whoever receives you receives Me, and whoever receives Me receives the One who sent Me.

Luke 10:17
The seventy-two returned with joy, saying, “Lord, even the demons submit to us in Thy name.”
  Acts 16:18 — thematic parallel: Both associate exorcism with Jesus' name. The seventy-two report demons submitting in His name; Acts narrates Paul commanding a spirit in Jesus Christ's name and its immediate departure. The people and occasions differ.
    Acts 16:18 She kept doing this for many days. Paul, greatly troubled, turned and said to the spirit, “I command you in the name of Jesus Christ to come out of her.” It came out that very hour.

Luke 10:18
He said to them, “I was watching Satan fall from heaven like lightning.
  Isaiah 14:12 — thematic imagery comparison: Both depict a fall from heaven. Luke explicitly names Satan and lightning; Isaiah's dawn-bearer image occurs in a lament against Babylon's king in 14:4. The king is not identified with Satan, nor is a common event or proven literary dependence asserted.
    Isaiah 14:12 How the dawn-bearer who rises in the morning fell from heaven! The one dispatching to all the nations was shattered to earth.

Luke 10:19
Behold, I have given you authority to tread upon snakes and scorpions, and over all the enemy’s power, and nothing will ever harm you.
  Psalms 90:13 — allusion: Both use treading dangerous serpents as protection imagery. The actual psalm names asp, basilisk, lion and dragon; Luke names snakes and scorpions and the enemy's power. The animals and addressed recipients are preserved rather than turned into a universal injury-immunity rule.
    Psalms 90:13 You will step on an asp and a basilisk, and trample a lion and a dragon.

Luke 10:20
Nevertheless, do not rejoice in this, that the spirits submit to you, but rejoice that your names have been written in the heavens.”
  Philippians 4:3 — thematic parallel: Both value the recorded names of God's servants. Luke contrasts heavenly inscription with joy over spirits' submission; Philippians names fellow workers whose names are in the book of life. Luke does not name that book in this verse.
    Philippians 4:3 Yes, I ask you also, true yoke-fellow, help these women, who strove with me in the gospel together with Clement and the rest of my fellow workers, whose names are in the book of life.

Luke 10:21
At that very hour He rejoiced in the Holy Spirit and said, “I acknowledge Thee, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because Thou didst hide these things from the wise and understanding and reveal them to infants. Yes, Father, for such was Thy good pleasure.
  Matthew 11:25–11:26 — teaching parallel: Both thank or acknowledge the Father, Lord of heaven and earth, for hiding from the wise and revealing to infants according to His pleasure. Luke additionally describes Jesus rejoicing in the Holy Spirit.
    Matthew 11:25 At that time Jesus answered, “I acknowledge Thee, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because Thou didst hide these things from the wise and understanding and reveal them to infants.
    Matthew 11:26 Yes, Father, because this was pleasing before Thee.

Luke 10:22
Everything has been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows who the Son is except the Father, or who the Father is except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.”
  Matthew 11:27 — teaching parallel: Both describe all things handed over by the Father, mutual Father-Son knowledge and the Son's disclosure of the Father to whomever He wishes.
    Matthew 11:27 All things have been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.

Luke 10:23
Turning privately to the disciples, He said, “Blessed are the eyes that see what you see.
  Matthew 13:16 — teaching parallel: Both bless the disciples' seeing eyes. Luke describes Jesus turning privately to them; Matthew also blesses their hearing ears.
    Matthew 13:16 But blessed are your eyes, because they see, and your ears, because they hear.

Luke 10:24
For I tell you that many prophets and kings wished to see what you see, and did not see it, and to hear what you hear, and did not hear it.”
  Matthew 13:17 — teaching parallel: Both describe earlier figures desiring but not receiving the disciples' sight and hearing. Luke names prophets and kings; Matthew names prophets and righteous people.
    Matthew 13:17 For truly I tell you, many prophets and righteous people desired to see what you see and did not see it, and to hear what you hear and did not hear it.

Luke 10:25
And behold, an expert in the law stood up to test Him, saying, “Teacher, what shall I do to inherit eternal life?”
  Mark 10:17 — teaching-scene parallel: Both address Jesus as Teacher and ask what to do to inherit eternal life. Luke names a testing law expert; Mark describes a kneeling man saying Good Teacher. The questioners and motives are not conflated.
    Mark 10:17 As He was setting out on the road, a man ran up, knelt before Him, and asked Him, “Good Teacher, what should I do to inherit eternal life?”

Luke 10:26
He said to him, “What is written in the law? How do you read it?”
  Mark 12:28–12:31 — thematic discussion parallel: Both law discussions turn on scriptural love commands. Luke has Jesus ask the law reader to give the written answer before the next verse's two commands; Mark has Jesus supply the commands after a scribe asks which is first. These are related discussions, not identical dialogue.
    Mark 12:28 One of the scribes approached, having heard them disputing and seen that He had answered them well. He asked Him, “Which commandment is first of all?”
    Mark 12:29 Jesus answered, “The first is, ‘Hear, Israel: the Lord our God, the Lord is one.
    Mark 12:30 You shall love the Lord your God with your whole heart, with your whole soul, with your whole mind, and with your whole strength.’
    Mark 12:31 The second is this: ‘You shall love your neighbor as yourself.’ There is no other commandment greater than these.”

Luke 10:27
He answered, “You shall love the Lord your God from your whole heart, with your whole soul, with your whole strength and with your whole mind, and your neighbor as yourself.”
  Deuteronomy 6:5 — quotation: Luke's love-of-God command corresponds to wholehearted, whole-soul and whole-strength love in Deuteronomy. Luke additionally names the mind; the target's shorter list is retained.
    Deuteronomy 6:5 And you shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your strength.
  Leviticus 19:18 — quotation: The command to love the neighbor as oneself repeats Leviticus's wording. Leviticus also forbids vengeance and retained anger against one's people; Luke joins the neighbor command with love of God.
    Leviticus 19:18 And your hand shall not take vengeance, neither shall you remain angry with the sons of your people; and you shall love your neighbour as yourself; I am the Lord.

Luke 10:28
He said to him, “You answered correctly. Do this and you will live.”
  Leviticus 18:5 — allusion: Both connect doing God's requirements with life. Leviticus commands ordinances and judgments that a person does and lives by; Jesus affirms the law reader's love-command answer and says do this and live.
    Leviticus 18:5 And you shall keep all My ordinances and all My judgments, and do them; the man who does them shall live by them; I am the Lord your God.

Luke 10:29
But wishing to justify himself, he said to Jesus, “And who is my neighbor?”
  Luke 16:15 — same-book thematic parallel: Both explicitly name self-justification. Here it motivates the law expert's neighbor question; later Jesus criticizes people justifying themselves before others while God knows hearts. The speakers and circumstances differ.
    Luke 16:15 And He said to them, “You are those who justify yourselves before people, but God knows your hearts, because what is exalted among people is an abomination before God.

Luke 10:30
Jesus replied, “A certain man was going down from Jerusalem to Jericho and fell among robbers. They stripped him, inflicted blows, and went away, leaving him half dead.
  2 Chronicles 28:15 — narrative parallel: The parable begins with a stripped, wounded traveler going toward Jericho; Chronicles describes naked, weakened captives being clothed, helped onto animals, and brought to Jericho. The wider mercy scenes share exposed vulnerability, aid, and Jericho, while the source of harm differs: robbery in Luke, captivity after war in Chronicles. No captive is identified as the parable's traveler.
    2 Chronicles 28:15 The men called by name rose and took charge of the captivity. They covered all the naked from the spoils, dressed them, shod them, and provided for them to eat and anoint themselves. They helped every weak person on pack animals and brought them to their brethren at Jericho, the City of Palms. Then they returned to Samaria.

Luke 10:31
By coincidence a certain priest was going down that road. He saw him and passed by on the opposite side.
  Deuteronomy 22:4 — thematic parallel: The priest sees a half-dead traveler on the road and passes by. Deuteronomy forbids seeing a brother's fallen donkey or calf in the way and disregarding it, requiring help instead. The law's explicit seeing-and-not-disregarding instruction supplies a bounded ethical contrast to passing the injured man; the animals and man are distinct, and no purity motive or knowledge of the priest's resources is invented.
    Deuteronomy 22:4 You shall not see your brother’s donkey or his calf fallen in the way, and disregard them; you shall surely raise them up with him.

Luke 10:32
Likewise a Levite also came to the place, saw him, and passed by on the opposite side.
  Proverbs 24:11–24:12 — thematic parallel: The Levite sees the injured traveler and passes by, whereas Proverbs commands rescue of people facing death and rejects ignorance of the person as an adequate excuse before God. This compares failure to aid a person in mortal need with the rescue instruction. Proverbs' people are being led to death, not victims of the same robbery; Luke does not state the Levite's motive.
    Proverbs 24:11 Rescue those being led to death, and ransom those being killed; do not hold back.
    Proverbs 24:12 But if you say, “I do not know this person,” know that the Lord knows everyone’s hearts, and the One who fashioned breath for all Himself knows everything, and repays each one according to his works.

Luke 10:33
But a certain Samaritan, traveling, came upon him. When he saw him, he was moved with compassion.
  Luke 10:37 — internal interpretation link: The Samaritan's compassionate action is identified in the parable's conclusion as the one showing mercy. This links the narrative's stated response with its interpretive answer, not an independent account of a second traveler.
    Luke 10:37 He said, “The one who showed him mercy.” Jesus said to him, “Go, and you do likewise.”

Luke 10:34
He approached and bound his wounds, pouring on oil and wine. He placed him on his own animal, brought him to an inn, and cared for him.
  2 Chronicles 28:15 — thematic narrative comparison: Both describe bodily care and animal transport for helpless people in a Jericho-related setting. Chronicles concerns captives receiving clothes, food and provision for anointing, with weak people carried on pack animals to Jericho; Luke concerns a wounded traveler bandaged with oil and wine and brought to an inn. The victims, contexts and exact treatments differ.
    2 Chronicles 28:15 The men called by name rose and took charge of the captivity. They covered all the naked from the spoils, dressed them, shod them, and provided for them to eat and anoint themselves. They helped every weak person on pack animals and brought them to their brethren at Jericho, the City of Palms. Then they returned to Samaria.

Luke 10:35
On the next day he took out two denarii, gave them to the innkeeper, and said, ‘Care for him, and whatever more you spend I will repay you when I return.’
  Philemon 1:18–1:19 — thematic parallel: The Samaritan personally guarantees repayment of further costs incurred caring for the injured man; Paul personally offers to have another's wrong or debt charged to him and promises repayment. The concrete connection is assuming another person's financial burden by an explicit repayment pledge. Innkeeper expenses and the possible debt concerning Onesimus differ, and neither pledge determines the other's amount.
    Philemon 1:18 And if he has wronged you in anything, or owes you anything, charge that to my account.
    Philemon 1:19 I, Paul, write this with my own hand: I will repay it—not to mention that you owe me even your own self.

Luke 10:36
Which of these three seems to you to have become a neighbor to the man who fell among the robbers?”
  Luke 10:29 — internal question-and-answer link: The closing question about who became a neighbor answers the earlier question who is my neighbor by shifting attention to the helper's conduct. The wording is not treated as identical or an independent event.
    Luke 10:29 But wishing to justify himself, he said to Jesus, “And who is my neighbor?”

Luke 10:37
He said, “The one who showed him mercy.” Jesus said to him, “Go, and you do likewise.”
  Leviticus 19:18 — law and application: Jesus' instruction to imitate the mercy shown in the neighbor story applies the command to love one's neighbor as oneself. Leviticus gives the command rather than narrating this Samaritan encounter.
    Leviticus 19:18 And your hand shall not take vengeance, neither shall you remain angry with the sons of your people; and you shall love your neighbour as yourself; I am the Lord.

Luke 10:38
As they traveled, He entered a certain village. A woman named Martha welcomed Him.
  John 12:1–12:2 — character-and-hospitality parallel: Both describe Martha offering Jesus hospitality. Luke gives her welcome in an unnamed village; John names a supper at Bethany where she served and Lazarus reclined. The visits and location details are not merged.
    John 12:1 So six days before the Passover, Jesus came to Bethany, where Lazarus was, whom Jesus had raised from the dead.
    John 12:2 They made a supper for Him there, and Martha served, while Lazarus was one of those reclining at table with Him.

Luke 10:39
She had a sister called Mary, who also sat at the Lord’s feet and listened to His word.
  Acts 22:3 — thematic learning-posture comparison: Mary sits at Jesus' feet listening to His word; Paul describes education at Gamaliel's feet. Both connect the feet-of-a-teacher description with learning, without assigning Mary Paul's formal training, institution or teacher.
    Acts 22:3 “I am a Jewish man, born in Tarsus of Cilicia, but brought up in this city at the feet of Gamaliel, educated in the strict observance of the ancestral law, zealous for God as all of you are today.

Luke 10:40
But Martha was distracted by much serving. She came up and said, “Lord, dost Thou not care that my sister has left me to serve alone? Tell her, then, to help me.”
  John 12:2 — character-and-service parallel: Both describe Martha serving Jesus. Luke adds her distraction and complaint about her sister's lack of help; John reports service at a different supper without that complaint.
    John 12:2 They made a supper for Him there, and Martha served, while Lazarus was one of those reclining at table with Him.

Luke 10:41
The Lord answered her, “Martha, Martha, you are anxious and troubled about many things,
  Luke 12:25–12:26 — same-book teaching parallel: Both give Jesus' challenge to anxiety over many or remaining things. Here He addresses Martha's troubled service; later He questions worry's inability to add to one's life. The occasions and particular worries differ.
    Luke 12:25 And which of you by being anxious can add a cubit to his span of life?
    Luke 12:26 So if you cannot do even the smallest thing, why are you anxious about the rest?

Luke 10:42
but one thing is needed; for Mary has chosen the good portion, which shall not be taken from her.”
  Luke 10:39 — internal interpretation link: Jesus' praise of Mary's chosen good portion refers back to her sitting and listening to His word. This makes the story's stated evaluation and action explicit, without inventing a separate event or making another person's service universally wrong.
    Luke 10:39 She had a sister called Mary, who also sat at the Lord’s feet and listened to His word.

Luke 11:1
And it happened that He was praying in a certain place. When He stopped, one of His disciples said to Him, “Lord, teach us to pray, just as John also taught his disciples.”
  Luke 5:33 — same-book ministry link: Both associate John's disciples with prayer. Luke5 reports their petitions alongside fasting; Luke11 records a request for Jesus to teach as John taught his disciples. The earlier report supports their practice, not independent detail of John's actual lesson.
    Luke 5:33 They said to Him, “John’s disciples fast often and offer petitions, and likewise those of the Pharisees, but Thine eat and drink.”

Luke 11:2
He said to them, “When you pray, say: ‘Father, let Thy name be hallowed. Let Thy kingdom come.
  Matthew 6:9–6:10 — prayer parallel: Both invoke the Father, hallowing His name and asking His kingdom to come. Matthew adds Our, in the heavens and the petition for God's will; these are not inserted into Luke's shorter supplied prayer.
    Matthew 6:9 Pray, then, in this way: ‘Our Father who art in the heavens, hallowed be Thy name.
    Matthew 6:10 Thy kingdom come; Thy will be done, as in heaven, so also upon earth.

Luke 11:3
Give us day by day our daily bread.
  Matthew 6:11 — prayer parallel: Both request daily bread. Luke says day by day; Matthew says this day.
    Matthew 6:11 Give us this day our daily bread;

Luke 11:4
And forgive us our sins, for we ourselves also forgive everyone who owes us. And do not bring us into testing.’”
  Matthew 6:12–6:13 — prayer parallel: Both ask forgiveness in relation to forgiving debtors and not being brought into testing or temptation. Luke names sins and everyone owing us; Matthew names debts and adds deliverance from evil, absent here.
    Matthew 6:12 and forgive us our debts, as we also have forgiven our debtors;
    Matthew 6:13 and bring us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil.’

Luke 11:5
And He said to them, “Which of you will have a friend and go to him at midnight and say to him, ‘Friend, lend me three loaves,
  Luke 18:2–18:5 — teaching parallel: The friend-at-midnight story opens with a person asking another for needed help, and Luke11:8 explains persistence; the widow repeatedly petitions a judge who eventually responds because she troubles him. Both parables use persistent human requests in teaching about prayer. Friendship, bread, midnight and hospitality differ from an unjust judge and a demand for justice; God is not thereby identified as unwilling or unjust.
    Luke 18:2 saying, “There was a certain judge in a certain city who neither feared God nor respected any person.
    Luke 18:3 And there was a widow in that city who kept coming to him, saying, ‘Give me justice against my adversary.’
    Luke 18:4 For a time he was unwilling. But afterward he said within himself, ‘Even though I do not fear God or respect any person,
    Luke 18:5 yet because this widow troubles me, I will give her justice, lest by coming continually she wear me down.’”

Luke 11:6
since a friend of mine has arrived at my home from a journey, and I have nothing to set before him,’
  Genesis 19:1–19:3 — thematic parallel: Luke's traveler arrives at his friend's home, whose host has no food to offer; in Genesis evening travelers are invited into Lot's house and he prepares a feast and unleavened bread for them. This compares the concrete responsibility of receiving travelers with food and shelter. Lot's angelic visitors and available provision are not inserted into Luke's friend or his lack of bread.
    Genesis 19:1 And the two angels came to Sodom at evening; and Lot sat beside the gate of Sodom. And when Lot saw them, he rose up to meet them, and bowed his face to the earth,
    Genesis 19:2 and said, “Behold, my lords, turn aside into your servant’s house, and lodge there, and wash your feet; and you shall rise early and go on your way.” And they said, “No; but we will lodge in the square.”
    Genesis 19:3 And he urged them greatly; and they turned aside to him, and entered into his house. And he made them a feast with drink, and baked unleavened bread for them; and they ate.

Luke 11:7
and that man will answer from inside, ‘Do not trouble me. The door is already shut, and my children are in bed with me. I cannot get up and give you anything’?
  Luke 18:4–18:5 — teaching parallel: The man inside initially refuses the midnight request, invoking the shut door and children in bed; Luke's judge is initially unwilling but later yields to the widow's repeated troubling. With Luke 11:8 supplying the first story's eventual response, the comparison concerns resistance overcome by persistence. Household inconvenience and the judge's stated character are different reasons, not proof that the friend is the same unjust figure.
    Luke 18:4 For a time he was unwilling. But afterward he said within himself, ‘Even though I do not fear God or respect any person,
    Luke 18:5 yet because this widow troubles me, I will give her justice, lest by coming continually she wear me down.’”

Luke 11:8
I tell you, even if he will not get up and give to him because he is his friend, yet because of his shameless insistence he will rise and give him as much as he needs.
  Luke 18:1–18:8 — same-book parable comparison: Both use a reluctant human respondent yielding to persistent requests in instruction about prayer. The friend gives needed bread because of shameless insistence; the judge grants a widow justice because she keeps troubling him. Different relationships, motives and requests remain, and neither portrayal simply equates God with the reluctant character.
    Luke 18:1 And He told them a parable about their need to pray always and not lose heart,
    Luke 18:2 saying, “There was a certain judge in a certain city who neither feared God nor respected any person.
    Luke 18:3 And there was a widow in that city who kept coming to him, saying, ‘Give me justice against my adversary.’
    Luke 18:4 For a time he was unwilling. But afterward he said within himself, ‘Even though I do not fear God or respect any person,
    Luke 18:5 yet because this widow troubles me, I will give her justice, lest by coming continually she wear me down.’”
    Luke 18:6 And the Lord said, “Hear what the unrighteous judge says.
    Luke 18:7 And will not God bring about justice for His chosen ones who cry to Him day and night? And will He delay over them?
    Luke 18:8 I tell you, He will bring about justice for them speedily. Nevertheless, when the Son of Man comes, will He find faith on the earth?”

Luke 11:9
And I tell you: ask, and it will be given to you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you.
  Matthew 7:7 — teaching parallel: Both promise a response to asking, seeking and knocking through the same threefold instruction.
    Matthew 7:7 Ask, and it will be given to you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you.

Luke 11:10
For everyone who asks receives, and the one who seeks finds, and to the one who knocks it will be opened.
  Matthew 7:8 — teaching parallel: Both repeat that askers receive, seekers find and knockers receive an opening.
    Matthew 7:8 For everyone who asks receives, the one who seeks finds, and to the one who knocks it will be opened.

Luke 11:11
What father among you, if his son asks for a fish, will give him a snake instead of a fish?
  Matthew 7:10 — teaching parallel: Both reject a father giving his child a snake when asked for a fish. Luke explicitly names the father and substitutes instead of a fish.
    Matthew 7:10 Or if he asks for a fish, will give him a snake?

Luke 11:12
Or again, if he asks for an egg, will he give him a scorpion?
  Matthew 7:9 — teaching-image comparison: Both reject a parent's inappropriate substitute for requested food. Luke uses egg and scorpion; Matthew uses bread and stone. The examples illustrate related reasoning without being made verbally identical.
    Matthew 7:9 Or who among you, if his son asks for bread, will give him a stone?

Luke 11:13
So if you, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will the Father from heaven give the Holy Spirit to those who ask Him!”
  Matthew 7:11 — teaching parallel: Both reason from flawed human parents giving good gifts to the heavenly Father's better giving. Luke specifically names the Holy Spirit; Matthew says good things.
    Matthew 7:11 If you, then, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your Father in the heavens give good things to those who ask Him!

Luke 11:14
And He was driving out a mute demon. When the demon came out, the mute man spoke, and the crowds marveled.
  Matthew 9:32–9:33 — narrative parallel: Both describe a mute demon-afflicted man speaking after exorcism and a marveling crowd. Matthew additionally records the crowd's exclamation about Israel.
    Matthew 9:32 As they were going out, behold, people brought Him a mute man oppressed by a demon.
    Matthew 9:33 When the demon was cast out, the mute man spoke. The crowds marveled, saying, “Never has anything like this appeared in Israel.”

Luke 11:15
But some of them said, “He drives out the demons by Beelzebul, the ruler of the demons.”
  Matthew 12:24 — teaching-scene parallel: Both accuse Jesus of casting out demons by Beelzebul, their ruler. Luke names some people; Matthew identifies Pharisees.
    Matthew 12:24 But when the Pharisees heard it, they said, “This Man casts out demons only by Beelzebul, the ruler of the demons.”

Luke 11:16
Others, testing Him, were seeking from Him a sign from heaven.
  Mark 8:11 — teaching-scene parallel: Both describe seeking a sign from heaven to test Jesus. Mark names Pharisees disputing with Him; Luke names others within this scene.
    Mark 8:11 The Pharisees came out and began disputing with Him, seeking from Him a sign from heaven, to test Him.

Luke 11:17
But He, knowing their thoughts, said to them, “Every kingdom divided against itself is laid waste, and house falls upon house.
  Matthew 12:25 — teaching parallel: Both have Jesus know the questioners' thoughts and answer with a divided kingdom being laid waste and houses being destroyed. Matthew says a divided city or house will not stand; Luke says house falls upon house.
    Matthew 12:25 Knowing their thoughts, He said to them, “Every kingdom divided against itself is laid waste, and every city or house divided against itself will not stand.

Luke 11:18
And if Satan also is divided against himself, how will his kingdom stand? For you say that I drive out the demons by Beelzebul.
  Matthew 12:26 — teaching parallel: Both ask how Satan's kingdom can stand if divided against itself. Luke repeats the Beelzebul accusation; Matthew describes Satan casting out Satan.
    Matthew 12:26 If Satan casts out Satan, he is divided against himself. How then will his kingdom stand?

Luke 11:19
But if I drive out the demons by Beelzebul, by whom do your sons drive them out? Therefore they will be your judges.
  Matthew 12:27 — teaching parallel: Both challenge the accusers about their own sons' exorcisms and say those sons will judge them.
    Matthew 12:27 And if I cast out demons by Beelzebul, by whom do your sons cast them out? Therefore they will be your judges.

Luke 11:20
But if I drive out the demons by the finger of God, then the kingdom of God has come upon you.
  Matthew 12:28 — teaching parallel: Both conclude that God's kingdom has come upon the hearers if Jesus' exorcisms are God's work. Luke says by God's finger; Matthew says by God's Spirit, without silently harmonizing the expressions.
    Matthew 12:28 But if I cast out demons by the Spirit of God, then the kingdom of God has come upon you.

Luke 11:21
When the strong man, fully armed, guards his own courtyard, his possessions are at peace.
  Matthew 12:29 — teaching-image parallel: Both picture a strong man's protected property before dispossession. Luke describes his armor and guarding the courtyard in peace; Matthew asks how someone can plunder his house without first binding him.
    Matthew 12:29 Or how can someone enter the strong man’s house and seize his belongings unless he first binds the strong man? Then he will plunder his house.

Luke 11:22
But when someone stronger than he attacks and defeats him, he takes away the full armor in which he trusted and distributes his spoils.
  Matthew 12:29 — teaching-image parallel: Both describe overpowering the strong man before taking his possessions. Luke names a stronger attacker stripping trusted armor and distributing spoils; Matthew describes binding and plundering the house.
    Matthew 12:29 Or how can someone enter the strong man’s house and seize his belongings unless he first binds the strong man? Then he will plunder his house.

Luke 11:23
Whoever is not with Me is against Me, and whoever does not gather with Me scatters.
  Matthew 12:30 — teaching parallel: Both contrast being with Jesus and gathering with being against Him and scattering.
    Matthew 12:30 Whoever is not with Me is against Me, and whoever does not gather with Me scatters.

Luke 11:24
When the unclean spirit comes out of a person, it passes through waterless places seeking rest. Finding none, it then says, ‘I will return to my house from which I came.’
  Matthew 12:43–12:44 — teaching parallel: Both describe an unclean spirit leaving a person, seeking rest in waterless places and deciding to return to its former house.
    Matthew 12:43 When the unclean spirit leaves a person, it passes through waterless places seeking rest, and finds none.
    Matthew 12:44 Then it says, ‘I will return to my house from which I came.’ When it comes, it finds it vacant, swept, and put in order.

Luke 11:25
And when it comes, it finds it swept and put in order.
  Matthew 12:44 — teaching parallel: Both say the returning spirit finds the house swept and ordered. Matthew additionally calls it vacant.
    Matthew 12:44 Then it says, ‘I will return to my house from which I came.’ When it comes, it finds it vacant, swept, and put in order.

Luke 11:26
Then it goes and takes seven other spirits more evil than itself, and they enter and dwell there. And the last state of that person becomes worse than the first.”
  Matthew 12:45 — teaching parallel: Both describe seven worse spirits taking up residence so the person's final condition is worse. Matthew explicitly applies it to the evil generation; Luke does not repeat that closing sentence here.
    Matthew 12:45 Then it goes and takes with itself seven other spirits more evil than itself; they enter and dwell there, and that person’s last condition becomes worse than the first. So it will also be with this evil generation.”

Luke 11:27
While He was saying these things, a woman from the crowd raised her voice and said to Him, “Blessed is the womb that bore Thee and the breasts at which Thou didst nurse!”
  Luke 1:42 — same-book blessing comparison: Both bless Jesus' mother in relation to bearing Him. Elizabeth addresses Mary and blesses her womb's fruit; the unnamed woman blesses the womb and breasts that bore and nursed Jesus. The women and occasions are not equated.
    Luke 1:42 She cried out with a loud shout and said, “Blessed are you among women, and blessed is the fruit of your womb!

Luke 11:28
But He said, “Rather, blessed are those who hear the word of God and keep it!”
  Luke 8:21 — same-book teaching parallel: Both prioritize hearing God's word and acting on it. Here Jesus calls such people blessed; earlier He calls them His mother and brothers. The blessing and kinship descriptions remain distinct.
    Luke 8:21 He answered them, “My mother and My brothers are these who hear God’s word and do it.”

Luke 11:29
As the crowds were gathering in greater numbers, He began to say, “This generation is an evil generation. It seeks a sign, and no sign will be given to it except the sign of Jonah.
  Matthew 12:39 — teaching parallel: Both call the sign-seeking generation evil and limit its sign to Jonah. Matthew additionally says adulterous and identifies Jonah as the prophet.
    Matthew 12:39 He answered them, “An evil and adulterous generation seeks a sign, but no sign will be given it except the sign of Jonah the prophet.

Luke 11:30
For just as Jonah became a sign to the Ninevites, so the Son of Man will also be to this generation.
  Matthew 12:40 — teaching comparison: Both compare Jonah with the Son of Man as the explanation of the sign. Luke names Jonah's being a sign to Nineveh and Jesus to this generation; Matthew specifies three days and nights in the sea creature and earth. That time-and-location detail is not inserted into Luke.
    Matthew 12:40 For just as Jonah was in the sea creature’s belly three days and three nights, so the Son of Man will be in the heart of the earth three days and three nights.

Luke 11:31
The queen of the south will be raised at the judgment with the men of this generation and will condemn them, because she came from the ends of the earth to hear the wisdom of Solomon, and behold, something greater than Solomon is here.
  Matthew 12:42 — teaching parallel: Both describe the southern queen rising at judgment to condemn this generation because she came to hear Solomon's wisdom, while something greater is present.
    Matthew 12:42 The queen of the south will be raised at the judgment with this generation and condemn it, because she came from the ends of the earth to hear Solomon’s wisdom; and behold, something greater than Solomon is here.

Luke 11:32
The men of Nineveh will rise at the judgment with this generation and condemn it, because they repented at the proclamation of Jonah, and behold, something greater than Jonah is here.
  Matthew 12:41 — teaching parallel: Both describe Ninevites rising at judgment and condemning this generation because they repented at Jonah's preaching, while something greater than Jonah is present.
    Matthew 12:41 Men of Nineveh will rise at the judgment with this generation and condemn it, because they repented at Jonah’s proclamation; and behold, something greater than Jonah is here.

Luke 11:33
No one who lights a lamp puts it in a hidden place or under the modius basket, but on the lampstand, so that those who enter may see its light.
  Matthew 5:15 — teaching parallel: Both reject hiding a lit lamp under the modius and put it on the lampstand for visible light. Luke names entering people and a hidden place; Matthew names everyone in the house.
    Matthew 5:15 Nor do people light a lamp and place it beneath the modius, but upon the lampstand; and it shines for everyone in the house.

Luke 11:34
Your eye is the lamp of the body. When your eye is single, your whole body is also full of light; but when it is evil, your body is also dark.
  Matthew 6:22–6:23 — teaching parallel: Both identify the eye as the body's lamp and relate its quality to a bright or dark body. Luke says single and Matthew sound for the positive condition; both say evil for the negative.
    Matthew 6:22 The lamp of the body is the eye. If therefore your eye is sound, your whole body will be full of light.
    Matthew 6:23 But if your eye is evil, your whole body will be dark. So if the light within you is darkness, how great is the darkness!

Luke 11:35
Watch, therefore, that the light within you is not darkness.
  Matthew 6:23 — teaching parallel: Both warn about the light within being darkness. Luke gives an instruction to watch; Matthew asks how great such darkness is.
    Matthew 6:23 But if your eye is evil, your whole body will be dark. So if the light within you is darkness, how great is the darkness!

Luke 11:36
So if your whole body is full of light, with no part of it dark, it will be wholly full of light, as when a lamp illumines you with its radiance.”
  Matthew 6:22 — teaching parallel: Both describe a wholly light-filled body. Luke expands the absence of darkness and lamp radiance; Matthew connects the condition with a sound eye.
    Matthew 6:22 The lamp of the body is the eye. If therefore your eye is sound, your whole body will be full of light.

Luke 11:37
As He was speaking, a Pharisee invited Him to dine with him. He went in and reclined at the table.
  Luke 7:36 — same-book hospitality parallel: Both describe a Pharisee inviting Jesus to eat and His reclining at table. The earlier meal and this unnamed invitation are not presumed to involve the same host or visit.
    Luke 7:36 One of the Pharisees asked Him to eat with him. He entered the Pharisee’s house and reclined at table.

Luke 11:38
But the Pharisee, seeing this, marveled that He had not first washed before the meal.
  Mark 7:1–7:5 — thematic controversy parallel: Both describe objections concerning washing before food. Luke concerns Jesus and His host's surprise; Mark concerns some disciples and questions from Pharisees and scribes, with fuller tradition explanations. The meals and accused people are distinct.
    Mark 7:1 The Pharisees and some of the scribes who had come from Jerusalem gathered around Him.
    Mark 7:2 They saw that some of His disciples were eating their bread with common hands—that is, unwashed.
    Mark 7:3 For the Pharisees and all the Jews do not eat unless they wash their hands with the fist, holding to the tradition of the elders.
    Mark 7:4 On returning from the marketplace, they do not eat unless they wash themselves. There are many other things they received to observe: washings of cups, jugs, copper vessels, and couches.
    Mark 7:5 The Pharisees and the scribes asked Him, “Why do Thy disciples not walk according to the tradition of the elders, but eat bread with common hands?”

Luke 11:39
And the Lord said to him, “Now you Pharisees cleanse the outside of the cup and the dish, but your inside is full of plunder and wickedness.
  Matthew 23:25 — teaching parallel: Both accuse the addressed Pharisees of cleansing the cup and dish outside while the inside contains plunder. Luke adds wickedness; Matthew adds self-indulgence.
    Matthew 23:25 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you cleanse the outside of the cup and dish, but inside they are full of plunder and self-indulgence!

Luke 11:40
Fools! Did not the One who made the outside also make the inside?
  1 Samuel 16:7 — thematic contrast: Both oppose judging the outside alone to God's concern with the inside. Luke argues that the same Maker made outside and inside; Samuel says humans look at the face while God sees the heart. Samuel is not presented as quoting the Maker argument or this cleansing controversy.
    1 Samuel 16:7 The Lord said to Samuel, “Do not look at his appearance or the bearing of his height, for I have treated him as nothing. God does not see as a human looks: a human looks at the face, but God looks at the heart.”

Luke 11:41
But give what is within as alms, and behold, everything is clean for you.
  Luke 12:33 — same-book teaching parallel: Both direct giving as alms. Here the instruction answers the inner/outer-cleanness issue and says everything is clean; later Jesus commands selling possessions and giving alms for heavenly treasure. The later verse does not independently repeat the cleanness statement.
    Luke 12:33 Sell your possessions and give alms. Make for yourselves purses that do not wear out, an unfailing treasure in the heavens, where no thief draws near and no moth destroys.

Luke 11:42
But woe to you Pharisees, because you tithe mint and rue and every garden herb, and pass by justice and the love of God! You ought to have done these things without leaving those undone.
  Matthew 23:23 — teaching parallel: Both condemn meticulous herb tithes alongside neglected weightier obligations while preserving both duties. Luke names mint, rue, garden herbs, justice and love of God; Matthew names mint, dill, cumin, justice, mercy and faithfulness.
    Matthew 23:23 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you tithe mint, dill, and cumin, but have left aside the weightier matters of the Law: justice, mercy, and faithfulness! These you ought to have done without leaving those aside.

Luke 11:43
Woe to you Pharisees, because you love the foremost seat in the synagogues and greetings in the marketplaces!
  Matthew 23:6–23:7 — teaching parallel: Both condemn desire for foremost synagogue seats and marketplace greetings. Matthew additionally names dinner precedence and being called Rabbi.
    Matthew 23:6 They love the foremost place at dinners and the foremost seats in the synagogues,
    Matthew 23:7 greetings in the marketplaces, and being called ‘Rabbi’ by people.

Luke 11:44
Woe to you, because you are like unmarked tombs, and the people who walk over them do not know it!”
  Matthew 23:27 — teaching-image comparison: Both compare the addressees to tombs, but the images differ. Luke names unmarked graves unknowingly walked over; Matthew names outwardly beautiful whitewashed tombs containing bones and uncleanness. Their visibility and emphasis are not merged.
    Matthew 23:27 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you resemble whitewashed tombs, which outwardly appear beautiful, but inwardly are full of dead people’s bones and all uncleanness!

Luke 11:45
One of the experts in the Law answered Him, “Teacher, by saying these things Thou dost insult us also.”
  Matthew 15:12 — narrative parallel: An expert in the Law tells Jesus that His rebuke insults his group; Matthew's disciples report that the Pharisees stumbled at His saying in a dispute that also begins with handwashing traditions. This compares religious interlocutors' adverse reception of Jesus' purity-related correction. The reporter, wording, teaching sequence and occasion differ; the passages do not independently reproduce the same objection.
    Matthew 15:12 Then the disciples came and said to Him, “Dost Thou know that the Pharisees stumbled when they heard this saying?”

Luke 11:46
But He said, “Woe also to you experts in the Law, because you load people with burdens hard to carry, and you yourselves do not touch the burdens with one of your fingers!
  Matthew 23:4 — teaching parallel: Both accuse religious teachers of loading people with difficult burdens while refusing even finger-level help. Matthew names shoulders and unwillingness to move the burdens; Luke addresses law experts and their not touching them.
    Matthew 23:4 They bind heavy burdens, hard to carry, and lay them upon people’s shoulders, but they themselves are unwilling to move them with their finger.

Luke 11:47
Woe to you, because you build the tombs of the prophets, but your fathers killed them!
  Matthew 23:29 — teaching parallel: Both accuse the audience of building prophets' tombs. Luke immediately names the fathers' killing; Matthew also names adorning monuments of the righteous.
    Matthew 23:29 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you build the prophets’ tombs and adorn the monuments of the righteous,

Luke 11:48
So you are witnesses and approve the deeds of your fathers: they killed them, and you build.
  Matthew 23:30–23:31 — teaching comparison: Both connect tomb-builders with ancestors who killed prophets. Luke explicitly says they witness and approve; Matthew records their denial of complicity before calling them witnesses that they are murderers' sons. Those opposed claims are preserved rather than made identical.
    Matthew 23:30 and say, ‘If we had lived in our fathers’ days, we would not have shared with them in the blood of the prophets.’
    Matthew 23:31 So you testify concerning yourselves that you are sons of those who murdered the prophets.

Luke 11:49
For this reason the wisdom of God also said, ‘I will send them prophets and apostles, and some of these they will kill and persecute,’
  Matthew 23:34 — teaching comparison: Both announce sent messengers and their killing and persecution. Luke attributes the saying to God's wisdom and names prophets and apostles; Matthew has Jesus say I send and names prophets, wise people and scribes, adding crucifixion and flogging.
    Matthew 23:34 Therefore behold, I send you prophets, wise people, and scribes. Some of them you will kill and crucify, and some you will flog in your synagogues and persecute from city to city,

Luke 11:50
so that the blood of all the prophets poured out since the foundation of the world may be required of this generation,
  Matthew 23:35–23:36 — teaching parallel: Both speak of accumulated shed blood being required of this generation. Luke names all prophets since the world's foundation; Matthew names all righteous blood upon earth and the coming upon this generation.
    Matthew 23:35 so that upon you may come all righteous blood poured out upon earth, from the blood of righteous Abel to the blood of Zechariah son of Berechiah, whom you murdered between the sanctuary and the altar.
    Matthew 23:36 Truly I tell you, all these things will come upon this generation.

Luke 11:51
