from the blood of Abel to the blood of Zechariah, who perished between the altar and the house. Yes, I tell you, it will be required of this generation.
  Matthew 23:35–23:36 — teaching parallel: Both range from Abel to Zechariah killed between sacred precincts and apply the reckoning to this generation. Matthew adds righteous Abel and Zechariah son of Berechiah; Luke does not name Zechariah's father. No separate historical identification is imposed.
    Matthew 23:35 so that upon you may come all righteous blood poured out upon earth, from the blood of righteous Abel to the blood of Zechariah son of Berechiah, whom you murdered between the sanctuary and the altar.
    Matthew 23:36 Truly I tell you, all these things will come upon this generation.

Luke 11:52
Woe to you experts in the Law, because you have taken away the key of knowledge! You did not enter yourselves, and you hindered those who were entering.”
  Matthew 23:13 — teaching comparison: Both accuse religious teachers of not entering and hindering others' entry. Luke describes taking away knowledge's key; Matthew describes shutting the kingdom. Their metaphors are related without replacing knowledge with kingdom in Luke's words.
    Matthew 23:13 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you shut the kingdom of the heavens before people! For you do not enter, nor do you permit those entering to enter.

Luke 11:53
When He went out from there, the scribes and Pharisees began to press Him fiercely and question Him closely about many things,
  Luke 20:20–20:23 — same-book hostility parallel: Both describe hostile questioning intended to seize on Jesus' words. Here scribes and Pharisees press Him on many matters after His departure; later observers send falsely righteous spies with a tribute question and a plan to hand Him to the governor. The episodes and agents differ.
    Luke 20:20 And keeping watch, they sent spies who pretended to be righteous, so that they might seize on something He said and hand Him over to the rule and authority of the governor.
    Luke 20:21 They questioned Him, saying, “Teacher, we know that Thou speakest and teachest rightly and dost not show partiality, but teachest the way of God in truth.
    Luke 20:22 Is it lawful for us to give tribute to Caesar, or not?”
    Luke 20:23 But perceiving their cunning, He said to them,

Luke 11:54
lying in wait for Him to catch something from His mouth.
  Matthew 22:15 — thematic hostility parallel: Both describe plotting to catch Jesus in His speech. Matthew names Pharisees taking counsel; Luke describes those lying in wait after the meal controversy, without identifying the scenes as one occasion.
    Matthew 22:15 Then the Pharisees went and took counsel how to trap Him in a statement.

Luke 12:1
Meanwhile, as the crowd gathered in its tens of thousands, so that they were trampling one another, He began to speak first to His disciples: “Guard yourselves against the leaven of the Pharisees, which is hypocrisy.
  Matthew 16:6 — teaching parallel: Both warn against Pharisaic leaven. Luke explicitly identifies it as hypocrisy; Matthew includes the Sadducees and does not use hypocrisy in this verse. The metaphor is not given one meaning in every context.
    Matthew 16:6 Jesus said to them, “Look out and beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees.”

Luke 12:2
Nothing is covered up that will not be uncovered, or hidden that will not become known.
  Matthew 10:26 — teaching parallel: Both say covered things will be revealed and hidden things become known. Matthew uses the assurance against fear; Luke follows the hypocrisy warning.
    Matthew 10:26 So do not fear them. For nothing is covered that will not be revealed, or hidden that will not become known.

Luke 12:3
Therefore whatever you have said in the darkness will be heard in the light, and what you have spoken into the ear in the inner rooms will be proclaimed on the housetops.
  Matthew 10:27 — teaching parallel: Both contrast private speech in darkness with public speech in light and on rooftops. Luke predicts exposure of what hearers said; Matthew commands proclamation of Jesus' teaching.
    Matthew 10:27 What I tell you in the darkness, say in the light; and what you hear in the ear, proclaim upon the housetops.

Luke 12:4
I tell you, My friends, do not fear those who kill the body and after that have nothing more they can do.
  Matthew 10:28 — teaching parallel: Both forbid fearing people whose power reaches bodily death. Matthew also explicitly contrasts inability to kill the soul; Luke states their inability to do more after killing.
    Matthew 10:28 Do not fear those who kill the body but cannot kill the soul; rather fear Him who can destroy both soul and body in Gehenna.

Luke 12:5
But I will show you whom to fear: fear the One who, after killing, has authority to cast into Gehenna. Yes, I tell you, fear Him.
  Matthew 10:28 — teaching parallel: Both direct fear toward the one with power over Gehenna. Matthew explicitly names destruction of soul and body; Luke speaks of authority to cast there after death.
    Matthew 10:28 Do not fear those who kill the body but cannot kill the soul; rather fear Him who can destroy both soul and body in Gehenna.

Luke 12:6
Are not five sparrows sold for two assaria? Yet not one of them is forgotten before God.
  Matthew 10:29 — teaching parallel: Both use cheap sparrows to illustrate God's care. Luke says five for two assaria and none forgotten; Matthew says two for one assarion and none falling apart from the Father.
    Matthew 10:29 Are not two sparrows sold for an assarion? Yet not one of them will fall to the ground apart from your Father.

Luke 12:7
Indeed, even the hairs of your head are all numbered. Do not fear; you are worth more than many sparrows.
  Matthew 10:30–10:31 — teaching parallel: Both join numbered hairs, greater value than sparrows, and freedom from fear. Matthew distributes the assurance across two verses.
    Matthew 10:30 Even the hairs of your head are all numbered.
    Matthew 10:31 So do not fear; you are worth more than many sparrows.

Luke 12:8
And I tell you, everyone who acknowledges Me before people, the Son of Man will also acknowledge before the angels of God.
  Matthew 10:32 — teaching parallel: Both connect public acknowledgment of Jesus with heavenly acknowledgment. Luke names the Son of Man before God's angels; Matthew names Jesus before His Father.
    Matthew 10:32 Therefore everyone who acknowledges Me before people, I too will acknowledge before My Father in the heavens.

Luke 12:9
But whoever denies Me before people will be denied before the angels of God.
  Matthew 10:33 — teaching parallel: Both warn of heavenly denial following denial of Jesus before people. Luke names God's angels; Matthew names His Father as the heavenly audience.
    Matthew 10:33 But whoever denies Me before people, I too will deny before My Father in the heavens.

Luke 12:10
And everyone who speaks a word against the Son of Man will be forgiven, but whoever blasphemes against the Holy Spirit will not be forgiven.
  Matthew 12:32 — teaching parallel: Both distinguish forgivable speech against the Son of Man from unforgivable blasphemy against the Holy Spirit. Matthew adds neither in this age nor the coming one.
    Matthew 12:32 Whoever speaks a word against the Son of Man will be forgiven; but whoever speaks against the Holy Spirit will not be forgiven, either in this age or in the coming one.

Luke 12:11
When they bring you before the synagogues and the rulers and authorities, do not be anxious about how or what you should say in your defense, or what you should say,
  Matthew 10:19 — teaching parallel: Both forbid advance anxiety about speech under hostile proceedings. Luke specifies synagogues, rulers, and authorities; Matthew speaks of being handed over and supplied words.
    Matthew 10:19 When they hand you over, do not be anxious about how or what you should speak, for what you should speak will be given to you at that hour.

Luke 12:12
for the Holy Spirit will teach you in that very hour what must be said.”
  Matthew 10:19–10:20 — teaching parallel: Both promise Spirit-given speech at the needed hour. Luke says the Holy Spirit teaches; Matthew says the Father's Spirit speaks in the disciples.
    Matthew 10:19 When they hand you over, do not be anxious about how or what you should speak, for what you should speak will be given to you at that hour.
    Matthew 10:20 For it is not you who speak, but the Spirit of your Father who speaks in you.

Luke 12:13
Someone from the crowd said to Him, “Teacher, tell my brother to divide the inheritance with me.”
  Luke 15:11–15:12 — comparison of family-property division requests: Both passages contain a request for division of a family's property. The crowd member asks Jesus to tell his brother to divide the inheritance; the younger son asks his father for his share and the father divides his livelihood. The requested authority, people and ensuing responses differ. No birth rank, legal share or identical dispute is inferred.
    Luke 15:11 And He said, “A certain man had two sons.
    Luke 15:12 The younger of them said to his father, ‘Father, give me the share of the property that falls to me.’ And he divided his livelihood between them.

Luke 12:14
But He said to him, “Man, who appointed Me a judge or divider over you?”
  Exodus 2:14 — allusion: Both use the question who appointed someone judge. Luke has Jesus question the requested role of judge or divider; Exodus has a challenger question Moses' authority as ruler and judge. The roles and occasions differ.
    Exodus 2:14 But he said, “Who made you a ruler and judge over us? Do you want to kill me, as you killed the Egyptian yesterday?” And Moses was afraid, and said, “Has this matter become so plainly known?”

Luke 12:15
And He said to them, “See to it that you guard against every kind of greed, because a person’s life does not consist in the abundance of his possessions.”
  1 Timothy 6:7–6:10 — teaching parallel: Both reject treating possessions as life's security. Luke warns against greed; Timothy joins life's transience and contentment with warnings about destructive desire for riches. It does not narrate Luke's inheritance dispute.
    1 Timothy 6:7 For we brought nothing into the world, because neither can we carry anything out.
    1 Timothy 6:8 But having food and coverings, with these we will be content.
    1 Timothy 6:9 But those who want to become rich fall into temptation and a snare and many foolish and harmful desires, which plunge people into ruin and destruction.
    1 Timothy 6:10 For the love of money is a root of all evils, and some, reaching for it, wandered from the faith and pierced themselves with many pains.

Luke 12:16
Then He told them a parable: “The land of a certain rich man yielded abundantly.
  Sirach 11:18–11:19 — thematic parallel: Both introduce a wealthy figure in a warning about imagined security and uncertain remaining life. Luke's wealth comes from an abundant crop; Sirach describes attention and frugality, preserving different means of gain.
    Sirach 11:18 There is someone who grows rich through his attention and frugality, and this is his allotted reward:
    Sirach 11:19 when he says, “I have found rest, and now I will eat from my good things,” he does not know how much time will pass before he leaves them to others and dies.

Luke 12:17
And he reasoned within himself, saying, ‘What shall I do, since I have nowhere to gather my crops?’
  Luke 16:3 — narrative parallel: Both parables give an economic protagonist's private deliberation in the form 'What shall I do?' followed by the difficulty he faces and a plan. The rich man has too much produce to store, while the steward is losing his position and fears digging or begging. This is a specific repeated monologue pattern, not the same predicament or a common approval of the plans.
    Luke 16:3 And the steward said within himself, ‘What shall I do, since my master is taking the stewardship away from me? I am not strong enough to dig; I am ashamed to beg.

Luke 12:18
And he said, ‘This is what I will do: I will pull down my storehouses and build larger ones, and gather all the grain and my goods there.
  Genesis 41:47–41:49 — thematic parallel: Luke's rich man plans larger storehouses to gather abundant grain and his goods; Genesis describes abundant years and Joseph gathering their food into the cities. The concrete comparison is organized grain storage after agricultural abundance. Joseph's preceding plan preserves the land through famine, unlike the rich man's self-directed security; Genesis supplies neither demolition of barns nor Luke's promised years of personal ease.
    Genesis 41:47 And the land brought forth sheaves during the seven years of abundance.
    Genesis 41:48 And he gathered all the food of the seven years in which there was abundance in the land of Egypt, and laid up the food in the cities; the food from the plains around each city he laid up within it.
    Genesis 41:49 And Joseph gathered grain like the sand of the sea, in great abundance, until they could not count it, for it was beyond counting.

Luke 12:19
And I will say to my soul, “Soul, you have many goods laid up for many years. Rest, eat, drink, be glad!”’
  Sirach 11:19 — allusion: Both rich figures speak of rest and enjoying stored good things. Sirach immediately warns of unknown remaining time; Luke's speaker confidently imagines many years before the following divine interruption.
    Sirach 11:19 when he says, “I have found rest, and now I will eat from my good things,” he does not know how much time will pass before he leaves them to others and dies.

Luke 12:20
But God said to him, ‘Fool, this very night they demand your soul from you; and the things you have prepared—whose will they be?’
  Sirach 11:19 — thematic parallel: Both contrast accumulated goods with death and their passing to others. Luke specifies this night and asks whose the goods will be; Sirach gives no fixed night and says they are left to others.
    Sirach 11:19 when he says, “I have found rest, and now I will eat from my good things,” he does not know how much time will pass before he leaves them to others and dies.

Luke 12:21
So it is with the one who stores up treasure for himself and is not rich toward God.”
  Matthew 6:19–6:20 — teaching parallel: Both contrast self-directed earthly accumulation with wealth oriented beyond it. Luke says rich toward God; Matthew contrasts vulnerable earthly treasure with heavenly treasure.
    Matthew 6:19 Do not store up treasures for yourselves upon earth, where moth and consumption destroy and where thieves break through and steal.
    Matthew 6:20 But store up treasures for yourselves in heaven, where neither moth nor consumption destroys and where thieves neither break through nor steal.

Luke 12:22
And He said to His disciples, “For this reason I tell you: do not be anxious about your life, what you will eat, or about your body, what you will wear.
  Matthew 6:25 — teaching parallel: Both tell disciples not to worry over food or clothing for life and body. Matthew also mentions drinking in this verse; Luke does not.
    Matthew 6:25 Therefore I tell you: do not be anxious about your life, what you will eat or what you will drink, or about your body, what you will wear. Is life not more than food, and the body more than clothing?

Luke 12:23
For life is more than food, and the body more than clothing.
  Matthew 6:25 — teaching parallel: Both reason that life is more than food and the body more than clothing. Matthew includes that reasoning with the initial anxiety prohibition.
    Matthew 6:25 Therefore I tell you: do not be anxious about your life, what you will eat or what you will drink, or about your body, what you will wear. Is life not more than food, and the body more than clothing?

Luke 12:24
Consider the ravens: they neither sow nor reap, and they have neither storeroom nor storehouse, yet God feeds them. How much more valuable you are than the birds!
  Matthew 6:26 — teaching parallel: Both contrast birds' lack of sowing and storage with God's feeding them and humans' greater value. Luke names ravens; Matthew refers generally to sky birds.
    Matthew 6:26 Look at the birds of the sky: they do not sow or reap or gather into storehouses, yet your heavenly Father feeds them. Are you not worth more than they?

Luke 12:25
And which of you by being anxious can add a cubit to his span of life?
  Matthew 6:27 — teaching parallel: Both ask whether anxiety can add a cubit to a person's span of life. The unusual measurement image remains as supplied.
    Matthew 6:27 Which of you by being anxious can add one cubit to his lifespan?

Luke 12:26
So if you cannot do even the smallest thing, why are you anxious about the rest?
  Matthew 6:27–6:28 — teaching parallel: Both sequences move from anxiety's inability to alter life's measure to questioning further anxiety. Luke calls this the smallest thing and asks about the rest; Matthew turns specifically to clothing.
    Matthew 6:27 Which of you by being anxious can add one cubit to his lifespan?
    Matthew 6:28 And why are you anxious about clothing? Observe the lilies of the field, how they grow: they neither toil nor spin.

Luke 12:27
Consider the lilies, how they grow. They neither toil nor spin. Yet I tell you, not even Solomon in all his glory was clothed like one of these.
  Matthew 6:28–6:29 — teaching parallel: Both point to lilies that neither toil nor spin and surpass Solomon's clothing in glory. Matthew splits the observation and Solomon comparison into two verses.
    Matthew 6:28 And why are you anxious about clothing? Observe the lilies of the field, how they grow: they neither toil nor spin.
    Matthew 6:29 Yet I tell you that not even Solomon in all his glory clothed himself like one of these.

Luke 12:28
But if God so clothes the grass that is in the field today and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, how much more will He clothe you, you of little faith!
  Matthew 6:30 — teaching parallel: Both reason from God's clothing grass, present today and burned tomorrow, to His greater care for people, addressing their little faith.
    Matthew 6:30 If God so clothes the grass of the field, which exists today and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, will He not much more clothe you, people of little faith?

Luke 12:29
And do not seek what you will eat and what you will drink, and do not be unsettled,
  Matthew 6:31 — teaching parallel: Both reject anxious preoccupation with eating and drinking. Matthew also names clothing; Luke adds not being unsettled.
    Matthew 6:31 So do not be anxious, saying, ‘What shall we eat?’ or ‘What shall we drink?’ or ‘What shall we wear?’

Luke 12:30
for the nations of the world seek after all these things, and your Father knows that you need them.
  Matthew 6:32 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the nations' pursuit of necessities with the Father's knowledge of the disciples' need.
    Matthew 6:32 For the nations seek all these things, and your heavenly Father knows that you need them all.

Luke 12:31
But seek His kingdom, and these things will be added to you.
  Matthew 6:33 — teaching parallel: Both direct seeking God's kingdom with necessities added. Matthew explicitly includes His righteousness; Luke's supplied verse does not.
    Matthew 6:33 But seek first the kingdom of God and His righteousness, and all these things will be added to you.

Luke 12:32
Do not fear, little flock, because your Father was pleased to give you the kingdom.
  Matthew 25:34 — thematic parallel: Both present the kingdom as a benefit from the Father. Luke reassures the little flock that He is pleased to give it; Matthew invites the Father's blessed to inherit the prepared kingdom.
    Matthew 25:34 Then the King will say to those at His right, ‘Come, you blessed by My Father; inherit the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world.

Luke 12:33
Sell your possessions and give alms. Make for yourselves purses that do not wear out, an unfailing treasure in the heavens, where no thief draws near and no moth destroys.
  Matthew 6:19–6:20 — teaching parallel: Both direct attention to heavenly treasure safe from theft and moths. Luke explicitly adds selling possessions, almsgiving, and enduring purses; Matthew does not repeat those instructions here.
    Matthew 6:19 Do not store up treasures for yourselves upon earth, where moth and consumption destroy and where thieves break through and steal.
    Matthew 6:20 But store up treasures for yourselves in heaven, where neither moth nor consumption destroys and where thieves neither break through nor steal.

Luke 12:34
For where your treasure is, there your heart will be also.
  Matthew 6:21 — teaching parallel: Both state that the heart will be where one's treasure is, explaining the preceding choice of treasure.
    Matthew 6:21 For where your treasure is, there your heart will also be.

Luke 12:35
Let your loins be girded and your lamps burning,
  Matthew 25:1–25:4 — thematic parallel: Both use prepared attendants and lamps in watchfulness teaching. Luke commands girded loins and burning lamps before a master's return; Matthew describes virgins with differing oil supplies before the bridegroom. The parables remain distinct.
    Matthew 25:1 Then the kingdom of the heavens will be likened to ten virgins who took their lamps and went out to meet the bridegroom.
    Matthew 25:2 Five of them were foolish and five prudent.
    Matthew 25:3 For the foolish took their lamps but did not take oil with them.
    Matthew 25:4 The prudent took oil in containers along with their lamps.

Luke 12:36
and be like people waiting for their own master to return from the wedding feast, so that when he comes and knocks, they may open to him at once.
  Mark 13:34–13:35 — teaching parallel: Both portray servants and door duties awaiting an absent master. Luke specifies return from a wedding and opening promptly at a knock; Mark specifies travel abroad and an unknown return.
    Mark 13:34 It is like a man going abroad who left his house and gave his slaves authority, each his own work, and commanded the doorkeeper to keep watch.
    Mark 13:35 Therefore keep watch, for you do not know when the master of the house is coming: in the evening, at midnight, at cockcrow, or in the morning.

Luke 12:37
Blessed are those slaves whom the master finds keeping watch when he comes. Amen, I tell you: he will gird himself, have them recline at table, and come and serve them.
  Matthew 24:46 — teaching parallel: Both bless servants whom the returning master finds fulfilling their duty. Luke uniquely adds the master serving the watchful slaves; Matthew's verse does not supply that role reversal.
    Matthew 24:46 Blessed is that slave whom his master, when he comes, will find doing so.

Luke 12:38
Whether he comes in the second watch or in the third and finds them so, blessed are they.
  Mark 13:35–13:36 — teaching parallel: Both connect nighttime return with vigilance. Luke blesses those awake in second or third watches; Mark lists evening, midnight, cockcrow, and morning and warns against being found asleep.
    Mark 13:35 Therefore keep watch, for you do not know when the master of the house is coming: in the evening, at midnight, at cockcrow, or in the morning.
    Mark 13:36 Otherwise he may come suddenly and find you sleeping.

Luke 12:39
But know this: if the master of the house had known at what hour the thief was coming, he would not have allowed his house to be broken into.
  Matthew 24:43 — teaching parallel: Both use the householder who would prevent a burglary if he knew when the thief would come. Matthew explicitly mentions keeping watch and the watch of the night; Luke says hour.
    Matthew 24:43 But know this: if the householder had known in what watch the thief was coming, he would have kept watch and would not have allowed his house to be broken into.

Luke 12:40
You also must be ready, because the Son of Man comes at an hour you do not expect.”
  Matthew 24:44 — teaching parallel: Both require readiness because the Son of Man comes at an unexpected hour.
    Matthew 24:44 Therefore you too must be ready, because the Son of Man comes at an hour you do not expect.

Luke 12:41
Peter said, “Lord, art Thou telling this parable to us, or also to everyone?”
  Mark 13:37 — thematic parallel: Peter asks whether the watchfulness parable addresses the disciples or everyone; Mark explicitly extends watchfulness from immediate hearers to all. Mark does not narrate Peter's question or serve as an identical answer in Luke's scene.
    Mark 13:37 What I say to you, I say to all: keep watch!”

Luke 12:42
And the Lord said, “Who, then, is the faithful steward, the prudent one, whom his master will appoint over his household staff to give out their ration of grain at the proper time?
  Matthew 24:45 — teaching parallel: Both ask about a faithful, prudent servant appointed to distribute household food at the proper time. Luke calls him a steward and names grain rations.
    Matthew 24:45 Who, then, is the faithful and prudent slave whom the master appointed over his household to give them food at the proper time?

Luke 12:43
Blessed is that slave whom his master finds doing so when he comes.
  Matthew 24:46 — teaching parallel: Both bless the servant found carrying out his assigned responsibility when the master arrives.
    Matthew 24:46 Blessed is that slave whom his master, when he comes, will find doing so.

Luke 12:44
Truly, I tell you, he will appoint him over all his possessions.
  Matthew 24:47 — teaching parallel: Both promise appointment over all the master's possessions as the faithful servant's reward.
    Matthew 24:47 Truly I tell you that he will appoint him over all his belongings.

Luke 12:45
But if that slave says in his heart, ‘My master is taking a long time to come,’ and begins to beat the male and female servants, and to eat and drink and become drunk,
  Matthew 24:48–24:49 — teaching parallel: Both depict an assumed delay followed by violence against fellow servants and indulgence. Luke names male and female servants and drunkenness; Matthew names dining with drunkards.
    Matthew 24:48 But if that evil slave says in his heart, ‘My master delays,’
    Matthew 24:49 and begins to beat his fellow slaves and eats and drinks with drunkards,

Luke 12:46
the master of that slave will come on a day he does not expect and at an hour he does not know, and will cut him in two and assign his portion with the unfaithful.
  Matthew 24:50–24:51 — teaching parallel: Both describe unexpected return, cutting the slave in two, and assigning his portion with an excluded group. Luke names the unfaithful; Matthew names hypocrites and adds weeping and grinding teeth.
    Matthew 24:50 the master of that slave will come on a day he does not expect and at an hour he does not know.
    Matthew 24:51 He will cut him in two and assign his portion with the hypocrites. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.

Luke 12:47
That slave who knew his master’s will and did not prepare or act according to his will shall receive many blows.
  James 4:17 — teaching parallel: Both increase accountability through knowing the good but failing to act. Luke gives the informed slave many blows; James calls knowing omission sin without repeating that punishment scheme.
    James 4:17 Therefore, if someone knows the good he ought to do and does not do it, it is sin for him.

Luke 12:48
But the one who did not know and did things deserving blows shall receive few. From everyone to whom much was given, much will be required; and from the one to whom they entrusted much, they will ask all the more.
  James 3:1 — thematic parallel: Both connect greater responsibility with stricter accountability. Luke says more is demanded from greater entrustment; James warns teachers of greater judgment, without repeating the ignorant slave's few blows.
    James 3:1 Not many of you should become teachers, my brothers, knowing that we will receive a greater judgment.

Luke 12:49
I came to cast fire upon the earth, and how I wish it were already kindled!
  Matthew 3:11–3:12 — thematic parallel: Both associate the coming mission with fire. Matthew's John mentions Spirit-and-fire baptism and burning chaff; Luke's Jesus speaks of casting fire on earth. The image comparison does not decide a single meaning of Luke's fire.
    Matthew 3:11 I baptize you in water for repentance, but the One coming after me is stronger than I, whose sandals I am not fit to carry. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.
    Matthew 3:12 His winnowing shovel is in His hand, and He will thoroughly clear His threshing floor and gather His wheat into the storehouse; but He will burn the chaff with unquenchable fire.”

Luke 12:50
But I have a baptism to be baptized with, and how I am constrained until it is accomplished!
  Mark 10:38–10:39 — teaching parallel: Both have Jesus describe His coming experience as a baptism. Mark joins it with the cup and disciples' participation; Luke emphasizes constraint until accomplishment. No identical ritual or chronology is imposed.
    Mark 10:38 Jesus said to them, “You do not know what you are asking. Can you drink the cup that I drink, or be baptized with the baptism with which I am baptized?”
    Mark 10:39 They said to Him, “We can.” Jesus said to them, “You will drink the cup that I drink, and you will be baptized with the baptism with which I am baptized.

Luke 12:51
Do you think I have come to give peace on earth? No, I tell you, but division.
  Matthew 10:34 — teaching parallel: Both deny uncomplicated earthly peace as the result of Jesus' coming. Luke says division; Matthew uses a sword image.
    Matthew 10:34 Do not suppose that I came to bring peace upon the earth. I did not come to bring peace, but a sword.

Luke 12:52
For from now on five in one house will be divided, three against two and two against three.
  Matthew 10:35–10:36 — teaching parallel: Both describe division within a household because of Jesus' mission. Luke specifies five divided three against two; Matthew supplies family pairings and household enemies without those numbers.
    Matthew 10:35 For I came to divide a man against his father, a daughter against her mother, and a daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law,
    Matthew 10:36 and a person’s enemies will be the members of his own household.

Luke 12:53
They will be divided: father against son and son against father, mother against daughter and daughter against mother, mother-in-law against her daughter-in-law and daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law.”
  Matthew 10:35 — teaching parallel: Both list father/son, mother/daughter, and mother-in-law/daughter-in-law divisions. Luke gives reciprocal directions; Matthew expresses the divisions in one direction.
    Matthew 10:35 For I came to divide a man against his father, a daughter against her mother, and a daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law,

Luke 12:54
He also said to the crowds, “When you see a cloud rising in the west, you say at once, ‘A rainstorm is coming,’ and so it happens.
  Matthew 16:2–16:3 — teaching parallel: Both contrast ordinary weather forecasting with failed discernment. Luke's indicator is a cloud rising in the west that signals rain; Matthew's is a red sky with fair or storm weather. The actual indicators are different.
    Matthew 16:2 He answered them, “When evening comes, you say, ‘Fair weather, for the sky is red.’
    Matthew 16:3 And in the morning, ‘Today a storm, for the sky is red and gloomy.’ You know how to distinguish the face of the sky, but can you not distinguish the signs of the times?

Luke 12:55
And when you see a south wind blowing, you say, ‘There will be scorching heat,’ and it happens.
  Matthew 16:2–16:3 — teaching parallel: Both cite recognizable weather signs. Luke names a south wind predicting heat; Matthew names red sky predicting fair weather or storm. Neither forecast is substituted for the other.
    Matthew 16:2 He answered them, “When evening comes, you say, ‘Fair weather, for the sky is red.’
    Matthew 16:3 And in the morning, ‘Today a storm, for the sky is red and gloomy.’ You know how to distinguish the face of the sky, but can you not distinguish the signs of the times?

Luke 12:56
Hypocrites! You know how to assess the appearance of the earth and the sky, but how is it that you do not know how to assess this time?
  Matthew 16:3 — teaching parallel: Both rebuke ability to assess sky appearances alongside inability to discern the present significant time or its signs. Luke names earth and sky; Matthew names sky and signs of the times.
    Matthew 16:3 And in the morning, ‘Today a storm, for the sky is red and gloomy.’ You know how to distinguish the face of the sky, but can you not distinguish the signs of the times?

Luke 12:57
And why do you not also judge for yourselves what is right?
  John 7:24 — teaching parallel: Both call for right judgment. Luke asks why people do not judge what is right for themselves; John commands righteous judgment rather than judgment by appearance, in a different dispute.
    John 7:24 Do not judge according to appearance, but judge with righteous judgment.”

Luke 12:58
For as you go with your adversary before a ruler, make an effort on the way to be released from him, lest he drag you before the judge, and the judge hand you over to the officer, and the officer throw you into prison.
  Matthew 5:25 — teaching parallel: Both urge settlement with an adversary on the way before a judge and officer lead to imprisonment. Luke frames it before a ruler; Matthew says come to terms quickly.
    Matthew 5:25 Come to terms with your adversary quickly, while you are with him on the way, lest your adversary hand you over to the judge, and the judge to the officer, and you be thrown into prison.

Luke 12:59
I tell you, you will certainly not come out from there until you have paid even the last lepton.”
  Matthew 5:26 — teaching parallel: Both warn that release waits until the last small coin is paid. Luke names a lepton; Matthew a quadrans, preserving the distinct coins.
    Matthew 5:26 Truly I tell you: you shall by no means come out of there until you have paid the last quadrans.

Luke 13:1
At that very time some people were present who told Him about the Galileans whose blood Pilate had mixed with their sacrifices.
  Luke 13:2–13:5 — narrative parallel: Jesus's reply explicitly takes up the reported Galileans and rejects the greater-sinners inference, then introduces the eighteen killed by Siloam's falling tower with greater-debtors wording and the repeated repent/perish conclusion. Pilate's mixing blood with sacrifices and the tower's fatal fall concern distinct groups and death mechanisms. This is an internal report-and-response comparison within the same discourse, not independent corroboration of Pilate's action or identification of the Galilean victims.
    Luke 13:2 And He answered them, “Do you think these Galileans were greater sinners than all the other Galileans because they suffered these things?
    Luke 13:3 No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all likewise perish.
    Luke 13:4 Or those eighteen on whom the tower in Siloam fell and killed them—do you think they were greater debtors than all the people living in Jerusalem?
    Luke 13:5 No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all perish in the same way.”

Luke 13:2
And He answered them, “Do you think these Galileans were greater sinners than all the other Galileans because they suffered these things?
  John 9:1–9:3 — teaching parallel: Both challenge an assumed direct inference from a person's suffering to particular guilt. Luke rejects the victims being greater sinners; John rejects the proposed sin explanation of congenital blindness, not a general claim that the family never sinned.
    John 9:1 And as He passed by, He saw a man blind from birth.
    John 9:2 His disciples asked Him, saying, “Rabbi, who sinned, this man or his parents, so that he was born blind?”
    John 9:3 Jesus answered, “Neither this man nor his parents sinned, but this was so that the works of God might be revealed in him.

Luke 13:3
No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all likewise perish.
  Ezekiel 18:30–18:32 — teaching parallel: Both connect repentance with avoiding death. Ezekiel commands turning from impieties and asks why Israel is dying; Luke warns the hearers that without repentance they too will perish.
    Ezekiel 18:30 I will judge you, each according to his way, house of Israel, says the Lord. Turn back and turn away from all your impieties, and they will not become a punishment of injustice for you.
    Ezekiel 18:31 Cast away from yourselves all your impieties with which you acted impiously against Me, and make yourselves a new heart and a new spirit. Why are you dying, house of Israel?
    Ezekiel 18:32 For I do not desire the death of the one who dies, says the Lord.”

Luke 13:4
Or those eighteen on whom the tower in Siloam fell and killed them—do you think they were greater debtors than all the people living in Jerusalem?
  John 9:1–9:3 — teaching parallel: Jesus' question challenges the assumption that the Siloam victims were greater debtors than their neighbors; John's disciples ask whose sin caused a man's congenital blindness, and Jesus rejects their proposed individual-or-parental explanation. Both passages resist a simple inference from suffering to exceptional personal guilt. John does not attest the tower deaths, and neither passage declares everyone morally sinless.
    John 9:1 And as He passed by, He saw a man blind from birth.
    John 9:2 His disciples asked Him, saying, “Rabbi, who sinned, this man or his parents, so that he was born blind?”
    John 9:3 Jesus answered, “Neither this man nor his parents sinned, but this was so that the works of God might be revealed in him.

Luke 13:5
No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all perish in the same way.”
  Luke 13:3 — teaching parallel: This same-chapter refrain repeats the repentance warning after a different tragedy: the tower deaths rather than Pilate's victims. The repeated warning is not independent confirmation of either event.
    Luke 13:3 No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all likewise perish.

Luke 13:6
And He told this parable: “A certain man had a fig tree planted in his vineyard. He came looking for fruit on it and found none.
  Matthew 21:19 — thematic parallel: Both describe fruit sought on a fig tree and not found. Luke tells a parable allowing further time; Matthew narrates Jesus cursing a tree that withers immediately. They are not the same tree or occasion.
    Matthew 21:19 Seeing a fig tree beside the road, He came to it and found nothing on it except leaves alone. He said to it, “Let no fruit ever come from you again.” Immediately the fig tree withered.

Luke 13:7
So he said to the vineyard worker, ‘Behold, for three years I have been coming to look for fruit on this fig tree and have found none. Cut it down, then! Why should it also make the ground unproductive?’
  Luke 3:9 — teaching parallel: Both connect fruitlessness with cutting down a tree. Luke's owner specifies three unsuccessful years; John's warning adds an axe and fire without that period.
    Luke 3:9 Already the axe also lies at the root of the trees. Therefore every tree that does not produce good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.”

Luke 13:8
But he answered him, ‘Master, leave it this year also, until I dig around it and put on manure.
  2 Peter 3:9 — thematic parallel: Both present delay as opportunity before loss or judgment. Luke's worker seeks another fruit season; Peter names divine patience toward repentance. This comparison does not assign identities to the tree and worker or a timetable to God.
    2 Peter 3:9 The Lord is not slow concerning the promise, as some consider slowness, but is patient toward you, not wishing anyone to perish but all to come to repentance.

Luke 13:9
And if it produces fruit in the coming year—but if not, you shall cut it down.’”
  Luke 3:9 — teaching parallel: Both condition a tree's removal on failure to produce fruit. Luke allows a further year first; John's warning does not state that delay and adds fire.
    Luke 3:9 Already the axe also lies at the root of the trees. Therefore every tree that does not produce good fruit is cut down and thrown into fire.”

Luke 13:10
Now He was teaching in one of the synagogues on the Sabbath.
  Mark 3:1–3:2 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Sabbath synagogue healing controversies. Luke introduces the bent woman's setting; Mark introduces a man with a withered hand watched by opponents. The patients and events differ.
    Mark 3:1 He entered a synagogue again, and a man with a withered hand was there.
    Mark 3:2 They watched Him closely to see whether He would heal him on the Sabbath, so that they might accuse Him.

Luke 13:11
And behold, there was a woman who had had a spirit of weakness for eighteen years. She was bent over and could not straighten up completely.
  Luke 13:16 — narrative parallel: The later saying explicitly returns to this woman and the same eighteen years, describing her as a daughter of Abraham whom Satan bound and whose bond ought to be released. It interprets the earlier spirit of weakness within this particular healing controversy. This is an internal explanatory reference, not an independent diagnosis or a rule that every chronic illness has the same cause.
    Luke 13:16 And this woman, who is a daughter of Abraham, whom Satan bound for, behold, eighteen years—ought she not to have been released from this bond on the Sabbath day?”

Luke 13:12
When Jesus saw her, He called to her and said, “Woman, you have been released from your weakness.”
  Luke 4:18 — thematic parallel: Jesus pronounces the woman released from her weakness; His earlier synagogue proclamation announces release to captives and to the oppressed. The following Sabbath argument explicitly describes this woman's release from bondage. This is a narrative illustration of the announced release motif, not a claim that every expression in the earlier proclamation denotes this woman's condition or that the pronouncement is a direct quotation.
    Luke 4:18 “The Spirit of the Lord is upon Me, because He anointed Me to proclaim good news to the poor. He has sent Me to proclaim release to captives and recovery of sight to the blind, to send the oppressed away in release,

Luke 13:13
And He laid His hands on her, and at once she was made straight, and she glorified God.
  Psalms 145:8 — thematic parallel: Luke concretely describes straightening a bent woman who glorifies God; the psalm praises the Lord raising those broken down. The shared raising imagery does not make the psalm name her condition or report her event.
    Psalms 145:8 The Lord raises up those broken down; the Lord makes the blind wise; the Lord loves the righteous.

Luke 13:14
But the synagogue ruler, indignant because Jesus had healed on the Sabbath, said to the crowd, “There are six days on which work ought to be done. So come and be healed on those days, and not on the Sabbath day.”
  Exodus 20:9–20:10 — allusion: The ruler's appeal to six working days recalls the six-day work and seventh-day Sabbath rule. Exodus does not explicitly prohibit healing, so his contested application is not inserted into the law.
    Exodus 20:9 For six days you shall labour and do all your works;
    Exodus 20:10 but the seventh day is a Sabbath to the Lord your God. You shall do no work on it: you, and your son, and your daughter, your male slave, and your female slave, your ox, and your beast of burden, and all your livestock, and the resident foreigner who dwells with you.

Luke 13:15
But the Lord answered him, “Hypocrites! Does not each of you on the Sabbath untie his ox or donkey from the manger and lead it away to give it water?
  Matthew 12:11–12:12 — teaching parallel: Both defend Sabbath relief through accepted care for animals. Luke cites untying ox or donkey for water; Matthew cites rescuing a sheep from a pit and human worth. The examples differ.
    Matthew 12:11 He said to them, “Who among you, if he has one sheep and it falls into a pit on a Sabbath, will not grasp it and lift it out?
    Matthew 12:12 How much more valuable, then, is a human being than a sheep! So it is lawful to do good on Sabbaths.”

Luke 13:16
And this woman, who is a daughter of Abraham, whom Satan bound for, behold, eighteen years—ought she not to have been released from this bond on the Sabbath day?”
  Matthew 12:12 — teaching parallel: Both argue that human relief is appropriate on the Sabbath. Luke emphasizes Abrahamic identity and release from Satan's eighteen-year bond; Matthew emphasizes human value over a sheep and lawful good.
    Matthew 12:12 How much more valuable, then, is a human being than a sheep! So it is lawful to do good on Sabbaths.”

Luke 13:17
As He said these things, all who opposed Him were put to shame, and the whole crowd rejoiced at all the glorious things being done by Him.
  Luke 14:6 — narrative parallel: Both show opponents thwarted after Jesus' Sabbath-healing argument. Luke 13 describes shame and crowd joy; Luke 14 says they cannot answer, without that crowd response. These are different healing scenes.
    Luke 14:6 And they were unable to answer these things.

Luke 13:18
So He said, “What is the kingdom of God like, and to what shall I compare it?
  Matthew 13:31 — teaching parallel: Luke introduces the kingdom comparison continued as mustard seed in the next verse; Matthew introduces the corresponding seed parable directly. The introduction is read with its continuation, not treated as a separate kingdom image.
    Matthew 13:31 He set another parable before them, saying, “The kingdom of the heavens is like a mustard seed, which a man took and sowed in his field.

Luke 13:19
It is like a mustard seed that a man took and cast into his own garden. It grew and became a tree, and the birds of the sky nested in its branches.”
  Matthew 13:31–13:32 — teaching parallel: Both compare the kingdom to a mustard seed growing into a tree with birds in its branches. Luke describes the birds nesting; Matthew describes them coming and resting. Luke names a garden; Matthew a field and additionally contrasts the seed's smallness with its growth.
    Matthew 13:31 He set another parable before them, saying, “The kingdom of the heavens is like a mustard seed, which a man took and sowed in his field.
    Matthew 13:32 It is smaller than all the seeds, but when it grows it is greater than the garden plants and becomes a tree, so that the birds of the sky come and rest in its branches.”

Luke 13:20
And again He said, “To what shall I compare the kingdom of God?
  Matthew 13:33 — teaching parallel: Luke introduces a second kingdom comparison whose continuation is leaven; Matthew introduces the corresponding leaven parable in one verse. The question versus statement form remains distinct.
    Matthew 13:33 He spoke another parable to them: “The kingdom of the heavens is like leaven, which a woman took and hid in three sata of flour until the whole was leavened.”

Luke 13:21
It is like leaven that a woman took and hid in three sata of flour, until the whole was leavened.”
  Matthew 13:33 — teaching parallel: Both compare the kingdom to a woman hiding leaven in three sata of flour until all is leavened. This positive kingdom image is not equated with hypocrisy merely because leaven appears elsewhere.
    Matthew 13:33 He spoke another parable to them: “The kingdom of the heavens is like leaven, which a woman took and hid in three sata of flour until the whole was leavened.”

Luke 13:22
And He traveled through cities and villages, teaching and making His journey toward Jerusalem.
  Luke 9:51 — narrative parallel: The later same-book travel notice continues the Jerusalem direction explicitly set earlier. Luke 13 adds teaching through cities and villages; Luke 9 describes the resolve toward His approaching taking-up, not this exact itinerary.
    Luke 9:51 As the days for His being taken up approached their fulfillment, He firmly set His face to go to Jerusalem.

Luke 13:23
Someone said to Him, “Lord, are those who are being saved few?” And He said to them,
  Matthew 7:13–7:14 — teaching parallel: Luke asks whether the saved are few; Matthew contrasts the few finding the narrow life-way with many on the broad destruction-way. Matthew does not repeat the question or give numerical proportions.
    Matthew 7:13 Enter through the narrow gate, because the gate is wide and the road broad that leads to destruction, and many enter through it.
    Matthew 7:14 How narrow is the gate and how constricted the road that leads to life, and few find it!

Luke 13:24
“Strive to enter through the narrow door, because many, I tell you, will seek to enter and will not be able,
  Matthew 7:13–7:14 — teaching parallel: Both use a narrow entrance and difficult access to life. Luke says many will seek entry unsuccessfully; Matthew contrasts narrow gate and constricted road with a broad road. Door, gate, and road remain different images.
    Matthew 7:13 Enter through the narrow gate, because the gate is wide and the road broad that leads to destruction, and many enter through it.
    Matthew 7:14 How narrow is the gate and how constricted the road that leads to life, and few find it!

Luke 13:25
once the master of the house rises and shuts the door. Then you will begin to stand outside and knock at the door, saying, ‘Lord, open to us!’ And he will answer you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from.’
  Matthew 25:10–25:12 — teaching parallel: Both describe a shut door, later appeals Lord, open to us, and refusal because the speaker does not know them. Matthew frames a wedding; Luke a householder, not one identical scene.
    Matthew 25:10 While they went away to buy, the bridegroom came. Those who were ready entered with him into the wedding feast, and the door was shut.
    Matthew 25:11 Afterward the other virgins also came, saying, ‘Lord, lord, open to us.’
    Matthew 25:12 He answered, ‘Truly I tell you, I do not know you.’

Luke 13:26
Then you will begin to say, ‘We ate and drank in your presence, and you taught in our streets.’
  Matthew 7:22 — teaching parallel: Both portray rejected people appealing to association with Jesus. Luke cites eating and teaching in their streets; Matthew cites claimed prophecy and mighty works. The claimed credentials differ.
    Matthew 7:22 Many will say to Me on that day, ‘Lord, Lord, did we not prophesy in Thy name, cast out demons in Thy name, and do many mighty works in Thy name?’

Luke 13:27
And he will say to you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from. Depart from me, all you workers of unrighteousness!’
  Matthew 7:23 — teaching parallel: Both declare lack of recognition and command workers of wrongdoing to depart. Luke adds not knowing their origin and says unrighteousness; Matthew says never knew and lawlessness.
    Matthew 7:23 Then I will declare to them, ‘I never knew you. Depart from Me, you who practice lawlessness.’

Luke 13:28
There will be weeping and grinding of teeth there, when you see Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of God, but yourselves being thrown outside.
  Matthew 8:11–8:12 — teaching parallel: Both contrast being with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the kingdom with weeping exclusion. Luke includes all prophets; Matthew names sons of the kingdom and outer darkness.
    Matthew 8:11 I tell you that many will come from east and west and recline with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the kingdom of the heavens,
    Matthew 8:12 but the sons of the kingdom will be cast into the outer darkness. There will be weeping and grinding of teeth.”

Luke 13:29
And people will come from east and west and from north and south and recline at table in the kingdom of God.
  Matthew 8:11 — teaching parallel: Both describe people arriving from distant directions to recline in God's kingdom. Luke adds north and south to east and west; Matthew names the patriarchs in that verse.
    Matthew 8:11 I tell you that many will come from east and west and recline with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the kingdom of the heavens,

Luke 13:30
And behold, there are last who will be first, and there are first who will be last.”
  Matthew 19:30 — teaching parallel: Both announce reversal of first and last. Luke says there are last who will be first; Matthew says many first will be last, preserving the different formulations.
    Matthew 19:30 But many who are first will be last, and the last first.

Luke 13:31
At that very hour some Pharisees approached and said to Him, “Leave and go away from here, because Herod wants to kill Thee.”
  Matthew 2:13–2:15 — narrative parallel: Both scenes contain an instruction to leave because Herod seeks Jesus' life. Matthew's angel addresses Joseph about the Child and an Egyptian refuge, while Luke records Pharisees warning the adult Jesus, whose reply follows. The life stages, speakers and Herod settings are distinct; the comparison neither identifies the Herods nor establishes that Jesus fled in response to the Pharisees.
    Matthew 2:13 After they withdrew, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to Joseph in a dream, saying, “Rise, take the Child and His mother, and flee to Egypt. Stay there until I tell you, for Herod is about to seek the Child to destroy Him.”
    Matthew 2:14 He rose, took the Child and His mother by night, and withdrew to Egypt.
    Matthew 2:15 He remained there until Herod’s death, so that what was spoken by the Lord through the prophet might be fulfilled: “Out of Egypt I called My Son.”

Luke 13:32
And He said to them, “Go and tell that fox, ‘Behold, I drive out demons and accomplish healings today and tomorrow, and on the third day I am brought to completion.’
  Luke 7:21–7:22 — narrative parallel: Jesus tells messengers to report His healings in both scenes: to John after works including deliverance from evil spirits, and to the ruler described as a fox with a declaration of ongoing exorcisms and healings. The recipient, tone and setting differ. Luke 7 does not explain the fox epithet or the today/tomorrow/third-day completion language, which is not silently equated with a resurrection timetable.
    Luke 7:21 At that hour He healed many of diseases, afflictions and evil spirits, and graciously gave sight to many blind people.
    Luke 7:22 He answered them, “Go and report to John what you have seen and heard: the blind recover sight, the lame walk, leprous people are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead are raised, and the poor receive good news.

Luke 13:33
Nevertheless, I must travel today and tomorrow and the day following, because it is not possible for a prophet to perish outside Jerusalem.
  Matthew 23:37 — thematic parallel: Both connect Jerusalem with rejection and death of prophets. Luke's rhetorical statement about His destined route is compared with Matthew's lament, not converted into a claim that every prophet historically died only there.
    Matthew 23:37 Jerusalem, Jerusalem, who kills the prophets and stones those sent to her! How often I wished to gather your children as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings, and you were unwilling!

Luke 13:34
Jerusalem, Jerusalem, who kills the prophets and stones those sent to her! How often I wanted to gather your children as a bird gathers her own brood under her wings, and you were not willing!
  Matthew 23:37 — teaching parallel: Both lament Jerusalem's killing of prophets and unwillingness to gather under protective wings. Luke says bird and brood; Matthew says hen and chicks.
    Matthew 23:37 Jerusalem, Jerusalem, who kills the prophets and stones those sent to her! How often I wished to gather your children as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings, and you were unwilling!

Luke 13:35
Behold, your house is left to you. And I tell you, you will certainly not see Me until the time comes when you say, ‘Blessed is He who comes in the name of the Lord!’”
  Matthew 23:38–23:39 — teaching parallel: Both speak of the house being left and future sight of Jesus linked to the Lord-name blessing. Matthew explicitly says desolate; that adjective is absent from Luke's supplied verse.
    Matthew 23:38 Behold, your house is left to you desolate.
    Matthew 23:39 For I tell you, you will certainly not see Me from now until you say, ‘Blessed is the One coming in the name of the Lord.’”

Luke 14:1
And it happened that He went into the house of one of the leaders of the Pharisees on a Sabbath to eat bread, and they were watching Him closely.
  Luke 11:37–11:38 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus at a Pharisee's meal before controversy. Luke 14 specifies a leader's Sabbath meal and close watching; Luke 11 concerns a host's surprise at missing ritual washing. These are different meals.
    Luke 11:37 As He was speaking, a Pharisee invited Him to dine with him. He went in and reclined at the table.
    Luke 11:38 But the Pharisee, seeing this, marveled that He had not first washed before the meal.

Luke 14:2
And behold, a certain man suffering from dropsy was before Him.
  Mark 3:1–3:2 — narrative parallel: A man with dropsy stands before Jesus during a Sabbath meal under close observation; Mark introduces a man with a withered hand in the synagogue while observers watch for a Sabbath healing to accuse Him. These are specific Sabbath-healing controversy settings with different patients, ailments and venues. Mark does not independently attest dropsy or identify its patient as Luke's man.
    Mark 3:1 He entered a synagogue again, and a man with a withered hand was there.
    Mark 3:2 They watched Him closely to see whether He would heal him on the Sabbath, so that they might accuse Him.

Luke 14:3
Jesus spoke to the experts in the Law and the Pharisees, saying, “Is it lawful to heal on the Sabbath, or not?”
  Mark 3:4 — teaching parallel: Both have Jesus ask opponents what is lawful on the Sabbath. Luke asks about healing; Mark contrasts good and evil, saving and killing. The questions precede different patients' healings.
    Mark 3:4 He said to them, “Is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or to do evil, to save a life or to kill?” But they remained silent.

Luke 14:4
But they were silent. And He took hold of the man, healed him, and sent him away.
  Mark 3:4–3:5 — narrative parallel: Both report opponents' silence followed by healing. Luke describes taking hold of and dismissing a man with dropsy; Mark describes commanding a withered hand to stretch. The patients and methods differ.
    Mark 3:4 He said to them, “Is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or to do evil, to save a life or to kill?” But they remained silent.
    Mark 3:5 He looked around at them with anger, grieving at the hardness of their hearts, and said to the man, “Stretch out your hand.” He stretched it out, and his hand was restored.

Luke 14:5
And He said to them, “Which of you, if his son or ox falls into a well, will not pull him out at once on the Sabbath day?”
  Matthew 12:11 — teaching parallel: Both appeal to Sabbath rescue from a pit or well. Luke names a son or ox; Matthew names a sheep. The examples and settings are not made identical.
    Matthew 12:11 He said to them, “Who among you, if he has one sheep and it falls into a pit on a Sabbath, will not grasp it and lift it out?

Luke 14:6
And they were unable to answer these things.
  Mark 3:4 — narrative parallel: Both report opponents not answering in a Sabbath-healing dispute. Mark's silence follows the initial question; Luke's inability follows the rescue comparison, preserving the different sequence.
    Mark 3:4 He said to them, “Is it lawful on the Sabbath to do good or to do evil, to save a life or to kill?” But they remained silent.

Luke 14:7
He told a parable to those who had been invited, noticing how they chose the foremost places at table. He said to them,
  Matthew 23:6 — thematic parallel: Both criticize seeking foremost places at dinners. Luke observes invited guests choosing seats; Matthew describes leaders' love of foremost dinner and synagogue places, without identifying the guest groups as identical.
    Matthew 23:6 They love the foremost place at dinners and the foremost seats in the synagogues,

Luke 14:8
“When someone invites you to a wedding feast, do not recline in the foremost place. Someone more honored than you may have been invited by him,
  Proverbs 25:6–25:7 — allusion: Both warn against taking a high social place before a more honored person. Luke frames wedding seating; Proverbs warns against placing oneself among rulers and being humbled before them.
    Proverbs 25:6 Do not boast before a king, and do not station yourself in the places of rulers;
    Proverbs 25:7 for it is better for you to be told, “Come up to me,” than for him to humble you before a ruler. Say what your eyes have seen.

Luke 14:9
and the one who invited you both will come and say to you, ‘Give this man your place.’ Then you will begin, with shame, to occupy the last place.
  Proverbs 25:7 — allusion: Both warn of humiliating demotion when presuming a high place. Luke's host makes the guest yield to another; Proverbs contrasts being invited up with being humbled before a ruler.
    Proverbs 25:7 for it is better for you to be told, “Come up to me,” than for him to humble you before a ruler. Say what your eyes have seen.

Luke 14:10
But when you are invited, go and recline in the last place, so that when the one who invited you comes, he will say to you, ‘Friend, move up higher.’ Then you will have honor before all who recline at table with you.
  Proverbs 25:7 — allusion: Luke's Friend, move up higher recalls Proverbs' preference for being told Come up to me. Luke adds deliberately choosing the lowest seat and honor before fellow guests.
    Proverbs 25:7 for it is better for you to be told, “Come up to me,” than for him to humble you before a ruler. Say what your eyes have seen.

Luke 14:11
For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and the one who humbles himself will be exalted.”
  Matthew 23:12 — teaching parallel: Both state that self-exaltation leads to humiliation and self-humbling to exaltation. The same reversal appears in different teaching settings.
    Matthew 23:12 Whoever exalts himself will be humbled, and whoever humbles himself will be exalted.

Luke 14:12
He also said to the one who had invited Him, “When you give a midday meal or a supper, do not invite your friends or your brothers or your relatives or rich neighbors, lest they also invite you in return and you receive repayment.
  Luke 6:32–6:35 — teaching parallel: Both reject generosity limited to expected reciprocal benefit. Luke 14 applies it to inviting friends and rich neighbors; Luke 6 applies it to love, doing good, and lending. The specific activities differ.
    Luke 6:32 If you love those who love you, what credit is that to you? For even sinners love those who love them.
    Luke 6:33 If you do good to those who do good to you, what credit is that to you? Even sinners do the same.
    Luke 6:34 If you lend to those from whom you hope to receive, what credit is that to you? Even sinners lend to sinners to receive the equivalent back.
    Luke 6:35 But love your enemies, do good, and lend, expecting nothing back. Your reward will be great, and you will be sons of the Most High, because He is kind to the ungrateful and evil.

Luke 14:13
But when you give a reception, invite the poor, the disabled, the lame, and the blind.
  Luke 14:21 — teaching parallel: This same-chapter instruction's poor, disabled, lame, and blind reappear as the banquet parable's invited groups. The repeated list links practical hospitality with the illustration, not an independently reported guest list.
    Luke 14:21 The slave returned and reported these things to his master. Then the master of the house became angry and said to his slave, ‘Go out quickly into the streets and lanes of the city, and bring the poor, the disabled, the blind, and the lame in here.’

Luke 14:14
And you will be blessed, because they have nothing with which to repay you; for you will be repaid at the resurrection of the righteous.”
  Proverbs 19:17 — teaching parallel: Both connect mercy toward the poor with repayment beyond their resources. Proverbs names God's repayment; Luke specifies the resurrection of the righteous, a timing statement absent from Proverbs.
    Proverbs 19:17 One who has mercy on the poor lends to God, and He will repay him according to his gift.

Luke 14:15
When one of those reclining at table heard these things, he said to Him, “Blessed is whoever shall eat bread in the kingdom of God!”
  Matthew 8:11 — thematic parallel: Both picture eating in God's kingdom. Luke records a guest's blessing on those eating bread there; Matthew's Jesus describes distant arrivals reclining with the patriarchs. The speakers and fuller images differ.
    Matthew 8:11 I tell you that many will come from east and west and recline with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the kingdom of the heavens,

Luke 14:16
But He said to him, “A certain man was giving a great supper, and he invited many.
  Matthew 22:2 — teaching parallel: Both introduce a feast host inviting guests. Luke's host is a man giving a great supper; Matthew's is a king arranging his son's wedding, preserving distinct settings.
    Matthew 22:2 “The kingdom of the heavens was likened to a king who made a wedding feast for his son.

Luke 14:17
At the hour of the supper he sent his slave to tell those who had been invited, ‘Come, because things are now ready.’
  Matthew 22:3–22:4 — teaching parallel: Both send servants to previously invited people with the announcement that the meal is ready. Luke sends one slave; Matthew describes plural messengers and a renewed invitation.
    Matthew 22:3 He sent his slaves to call those invited to the wedding feast, but they were unwilling to come.
    Matthew 22:4 Again he sent other slaves, saying, ‘Tell those invited: Behold, I have prepared my meal. My bulls and fattened animals have been slaughtered, and everything is ready. Come to the wedding feast.’

Luke 14:18
And they all alike began to excuse themselves. The first said to him, ‘I bought a field, and I need to go out and see it. I ask you to excuse me.’
  Matthew 22:5 — teaching parallel: Both portray a field-related pursuit displacing attendance. Luke's invitee excuses himself to inspect a newly bought field; Matthew says a guest disregards the invitation and goes to his field.
    Matthew 22:5 But they disregarded it and went away, one to his own field, another to his business.

Luke 14:19
And another said, ‘I bought five pairs of oxen, and I am going to test them. I ask you to excuse me.’
  Matthew 22:5 — teaching parallel: Both contrast business activity with accepting the feast. Luke specifies testing purchased oxen; Matthew merely says business, not this animal purchase. The relationship compares excuses rather than independently confirming the oxen.
    Matthew 22:5 But they disregarded it and went away, one to his own field, another to his business.

Luke 14:20
And another said, ‘I married a wife, and for this reason I cannot come.’
  Deuteronomy 24:5 — thematic parallel: Both mention obligations associated with a new marriage. Deuteronomy exempts the new husband from war and imposed duty for a year; Luke records a banquet excuse. The law is not made an exemption from this feast or proof that the excuse is valid.
    Deuteronomy 24:5 And if a man has newly taken a wife, he shall not go forth unto war, neither shall any duty be laid upon him. He shall be free in his house one year, and shall gladden his wife whom he has taken.

Luke 14:21
The slave returned and reported these things to his master. Then the master of the house became angry and said to his slave, ‘Go out quickly into the streets and lanes of the city, and bring the poor, the disabled, the blind, and the lame in here.’
  Matthew 22:8–22:10 — teaching parallel: Both replace refusing invitees with people called from public places. Luke specifies poor and disabled people; Matthew gathers everyone found, evil and good, without Luke's exact list.
    Matthew 22:8 Then he said to his slaves, ‘The wedding feast is ready, but those invited were not worthy.
    Matthew 22:9 So go to the outlets of the roads and invite to the wedding feast as many as you find.’
    Matthew 22:10 Those slaves went out into the roads and gathered everyone they found, both evil and good, and the wedding feast was filled with people reclining.

Luke 14:22
And the slave said, ‘Master, what you commanded has been done, and there is still room.’
  Matthew 22:9–22:10 — teaching parallel: Both banquet parables report the result of servants gathering people beyond the original invitees. Luke's slave says the command is fulfilled but room remains, prompting another outreach; Matthew says the wedding feast is filled after gathering people from the roads. This compares the reports and their differing stages, preserving remaining capacity versus fullness rather than replacing Luke's statement with Matthew's.
    Matthew 22:9 So go to the outlets of the roads and invite to the wedding feast as many as you find.’
    Matthew 22:10 Those slaves went out into the roads and gathered everyone they found, both evil and good, and the wedding feast was filled with people reclining.

Luke 14:23
And the master said to the slave, ‘Go out into the roads and along the fences, and compel people to come in, so that my house may be filled.
  Matthew 22:9–22:10 — teaching parallel: Both extend the feast invitation onto roads to fill the banquet. Luke adds fences and compel people to come; Matthew says invite those found. The differing words and rounds of invitation remain distinct.
    Matthew 22:9 So go to the outlets of the roads and invite to the wedding feast as many as you find.’
    Matthew 22:10 Those slaves went out into the roads and gathered everyone they found, both evil and good, and the wedding feast was filled with people reclining.

Luke 14:24
For I tell you, none of those men who were invited will taste my supper.’”
  Matthew 22:8 — teaching parallel: Both exclude the original invited people after their refusal. Luke says none will taste the supper; Matthew calls those invited unworthy.
    Matthew 22:8 Then he said to his slaves, ‘The wedding feast is ready, but those invited were not worthy.

Luke 14:25
Now great crowds were traveling with Him. He turned and said to them,
  Mark 8:34 — narrative parallel: Luke describes Jesus addressing the great crowd traveling with Him before the cross-bearing demands of discipleship; Mark explicitly calls the crowd together with the disciples to hear self-denial and cross-bearing instruction. The connection is the public audience and introduction to the same costly-following theme. Being already on the road and being summoned are distinct narrative arrangements, not an identical occasion asserted.
    Mark 8:34 Calling the crowd to Him with His disciples, He said to them, “If anyone wants to follow behind Me, let him deny himself, take up his cross, and follow Me.

Luke 14:26
“If anyone comes to Me and does not hate his own father and mother and wife and children and brothers and sisters, and even his own life, he cannot be My disciple.
  Matthew 10:37 — teaching parallel: Both demand allegiance to Jesus beyond family loyalty. Luke uses hate and includes spouse, siblings, and one's life; Matthew says loving parents or children more than Him. Their wording is not rewritten into identity.
    Matthew 10:37 Whoever loves father or mother more than Me is not worthy of Me; and whoever loves son or daughter more than Me is not worthy of Me.

Luke 14:27
Whoever does not carry his own cross and come after Me cannot be My disciple.
  Matthew 10:38 — teaching parallel: Both require carrying one's cross and following Jesus. Luke says one otherwise cannot be a disciple; Matthew says one is not worthy of Him.
    Matthew 10:38 Whoever does not take his cross and follow after Me is not worthy of Me.

Luke 14:28
For which of you, wanting to build a tower, does not first sit down and calculate the expense, whether he has enough to finish it?
  Proverbs 24:27 — thematic parallel: Both connect building with prior readiness. Luke counts expense before building a tower; Proverbs commands work and field preparation before rebuilding a house. Proverbs does not mention Luke's budget calculation or tower.
    Proverbs 24:27 Prepare your works for going out, and make yourself ready for the field, and follow behind me, and you will rebuild your house.

Luke 14:29
Otherwise, when he has laid a foundation and is unable to complete it, all who see it may begin to mock him,
  Nehemiah 3:33–3:36 — thematic parallel: The uncompleted building brings spectators' mockery in Luke; Nehemiah records opponents laughing at the Jews' wall building and deriding the wall's strength before a prayer about becoming an object of mockery. This is a concrete construction-and-ridicule comparison. Nehemiah's opponents do not establish an actual abandonment for lack of funds, and their wall is not Luke's tower.
    Nehemiah 3:33 When Sanaballat heard that we were building the wall, it displeased him. He became very angry and laughed at the Jews.
    Nehemiah 3:34 He said before his brothers, “Is this the strength of Somoron, that these Jews are building their own city?”
    Nehemiah 3:35 Tobias the Ammanite came beside him, and they said to one another, “Surely they will not sacrifice or eat in their place? Will not a fox climb up and pull down their stone wall?”
    Nehemiah 3:36 Hear, our God, for we have become an object of mockery. Turn their reproach back upon their own heads, and give them over to mockery in a land of captivity,

Luke 14:30
saying, ‘This man began to build and was unable to finish.’
  Zechariah 4:9 — thematic parallel: Luke's hypothetical builder begins but cannot finish; Zechariah promises that the hands of Zerubbabel which laid the house's foundation will complete it. The contrast concerns beginning and completing a building, with human cost calculation in Luke and a prophetic assurance in Zechariah. No identical project or general promise to complete every undertaking is inferred.
    Zechariah 4:9 “Zerubbabel’s hands laid this house’s foundation, and his hands will complete it. You will recognize that the Lord Almighty has sent me to you.

Luke 14:31
Or what king, going to meet another king in war, will not first sit down and consider whether he is able with ten thousand to meet the one coming against him with twenty thousand?
  Proverbs 24:6 — thematic parallel: Both associate warfare with deliberation. Luke's king assesses unequal forces; the supplied Proverbs text speaks of steering war and a deliberative heart. It does not supply Luke's army numbers or a guaranteed outcome.
    Proverbs 24:6 with steering war takes place, and help is with a deliberative heart.

Luke 14:32
And if not, while the other is still far away, he sends a delegation and asks for terms of peace.
  Deuteronomy 20:10 — thematic parallel: Both describe seeking terms before battle. Luke's weaker king sends a delegation while the opponent is distant; Deuteronomy's approaching attacker offers peace to a city. The military roles and rules are different.
    Deuteronomy 20:10 And when you draw near unto a city to fight against it, you shall call its people forth with an offer of peace.

Luke 14:33
So, therefore, every one of you who does not renounce all his own possessions cannot be My disciple.
  Matthew 19:21 — teaching parallel: Both connect relinquishing possessions with following Jesus. Luke makes a general discipleship demand; Matthew directs the wealthy questioner to sell, give to the poor, and follow, with heavenly treasure.
    Matthew 19:21 Jesus said to him, “If you wish to be perfect, go, sell your belongings, and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in the heavens. Then come, follow Me.”

Luke 14:34
Salt, then, is good. But if even the salt becomes tasteless, with what will it be seasoned?
  Mark 9:50 — teaching parallel: Both call salt good and ask how tasteless salt can be seasoned. Mark additionally commands having salt and peace; Luke continues to disposal.
    Mark 9:50 Salt is good; but if salt becomes saltless, with what will you season it? Have salt in yourselves, and be at peace with one another.”

Luke 14:35
It is fit neither for the soil nor for the manure heap; they throw it outside. Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear.”
  Matthew 5:13 — teaching parallel: Both describe useless salt being thrown away. Luke specifies unfitness for soil or manure and adds the hearing summons; Matthew mentions trampling and does not give those uses.
    Matthew 5:13 You are the salt of the earth. But if the salt becomes tasteless, how shall it be made salty? It is no longer good for anything except to be thrown out and trampled underfoot by people.

Luke 15:1
Now all the tax collectors and sinners were drawing near to hear Him.
  Matthew 9:10 — narrative parallel: Both depict tax collectors and sinners gathering around Jesus. Luke specifies coming to hear; Matthew describes a shared meal with disciples. The scenes and immediate activities differ.
    Matthew 9:10 As He reclined at table in the house, behold, many tax collectors and sinners came and reclined with Jesus and His disciples.

Luke 15:2
And both the Pharisees and the scribes grumbled, saying, “This man welcomes sinners and eats with them.”
  Matthew 9:11 — narrative parallel: Both report Pharisaic objections to Jesus eating with sinners. Luke includes scribes and direct grumbling; Matthew has Pharisees question the disciples about their Teacher.
    Matthew 9:11 When the Pharisees saw this, they said to His disciples, “Why does your Teacher eat with tax collectors and sinners?”

Luke 15:3
So He told them this parable:
  Matthew 18:12–18:14 — teaching parallel: Luke introduces the lost-sheep story following criticism of welcoming sinners; Matthew gives the corresponding illustration with the Father's little ones. The introductory unit is read with its continuation, not as a separate parable.
    Matthew 18:12 What do you think? If someone has a hundred sheep and one of them strays, will he not leave the ninety-nine on the mountains and go seeking the one that strays?
    Matthew 18:13 If he succeeds in finding it, truly I tell you that he rejoices over it more than over the ninety-nine that have not strayed.
    Matthew 18:14 In the same way, it is not the will of your Father in the heavens that one of these little ones perish.

Luke 15:4
“What man among you, having a hundred sheep and losing one of them, does not leave the ninety-nine in the wilderness and go after the lost one until he finds it?
