  Matthew 18:12 — teaching parallel: Both describe one lost sheep out of a hundred and leaving ninety-nine to seek it. Luke says wilderness and until finding; Matthew says mountains and seeking the straying one.
    Matthew 18:12 What do you think? If someone has a hundred sheep and one of them strays, will he not leave the ninety-nine on the mountains and go seeking the one that strays?

Luke 15:5
And when he finds it, he lays it on his shoulders, rejoicing.
  Matthew 18:13 — teaching parallel: Both join finding the sheep with rejoicing. Luke adds carrying it on the shoulders; Matthew compares the joy with the ninety-nine, without that carrying detail.
    Matthew 18:13 If he succeeds in finding it, truly I tell you that he rejoices over it more than over the ninety-nine that have not strayed.

Luke 15:6
When he comes home, he calls his friends and neighbors together and says to them, ‘Rejoice with me, because I found my sheep that was lost!’
  Luke 15:9 — teaching parallel: The same-chapter stories repeat calling friends and neighbors to rejoice over recovering one lost possession. Here it is a man's sheep; there a woman's coin. The owners and objects remain distinct.
    Luke 15:9 And when she finds it, she calls her women friends and neighbors together, saying, ‘Rejoice with me, because I found the drachma that I lost!’

Luke 15:7
I tell you, in the same way there will be more joy in heaven over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous people who have no need of repentance.
  Matthew 18:13–18:14 — teaching parallel: Both make the recovered one an occasion of joy in contrast to ninety-nine. Luke applies it to heavenly joy over repentance; Matthew to the Father's will that no little one perish.
    Matthew 18:13 If he succeeds in finding it, truly I tell you that he rejoices over it more than over the ninety-nine that have not strayed.
    Matthew 18:14 In the same way, it is not the will of your Father in the heavens that one of these little ones perish.

Luke 15:8
Or what woman, having ten drachmas, if she loses one drachma, does not light a lamp and sweep the house and search carefully until she finds it?
  Luke 15:4 — teaching parallel: Both same-chapter stories describe an owner seeking one missing item among a known number until found. Luke contrasts a woman's lamp-and-sweeping coin search with the man's wilderness sheep search, not one event.
    Luke 15:4 “What man among you, having a hundred sheep and losing one of them, does not leave the ninety-nine in the wilderness and go after the lost one until he finds it?

Luke 15:9
And when she finds it, she calls her women friends and neighbors together, saying, ‘Rejoice with me, because I found the drachma that I lost!’
  Luke 15:6 — teaching parallel: This repeated same-chapter celebration formula parallels the sheep owner's call to friends and neighbors. The woman rejoices over one drachma rather than one sheep, preserving the different stories.
    Luke 15:6 When he comes home, he calls his friends and neighbors together and says to them, ‘Rejoice with me, because I found my sheep that was lost!’

Luke 15:10
In the same way, I tell you, there is joy before the angels of God over one sinner who repents.”
  Luke 15:7 — teaching parallel: Both applications state joy over one repentant sinner. Luke 15:10 locates it before God's angels; 15:7 says heaven and adds the comparison with ninety-nine.
    Luke 15:7 I tell you, in the same way there will be more joy in heaven over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous people who have no need of repentance.

Luke 15:11
And He said, “A certain man had two sons.
  Matthew 21:28 — thematic parallel: Both introduce a father with two children as a parable's framework. Matthew tests responses to a vineyard command; Luke develops inheritance, departure, return, and the older son's response. The plots are not one.
    Matthew 21:28 But what do you think? A man had two children. He came to the first and said, ‘Child, go work in the vineyard today.’

Luke 15:12
The younger of them said to his father, ‘Father, give me the share of the property that falls to me.’ And he divided his livelihood between them.
  Deuteronomy 21:15–21:17 — thematic parallel: The father divides property between his sons after the younger requests his share; Deuteronomy regulates a father's assignment of possessions to sons and protects the firstborn's double portion. This is legal background to a paternal inheritance division, not proof of the precise shares or timing in the parable. Luke does not supply the law's two-wives situation or explicitly calculate either son's portion.
    Deuteronomy 21:15 And if a man has two wives, one loved and one hated, and both the loved and the hated bear him children, and the firstborn son is hers that is hated,
    Deuteronomy 21:16 then it shall be, in the day when he gives his sons an inheritance of his possessions, that he may not make the son of the loved wife the firstborn, overlooking the firstborn son of the hated wife.
    Deuteronomy 21:17 But he shall acknowledge the firstborn, the son of the hated wife, by giving him a double portion of all that he has; for he is the beginning of his children, and the right of the firstborn belongs unto him.

Luke 15:13
Not many days later, the younger son gathered everything together and traveled to a distant country. There he scattered his property by living recklessly.
  Luke 16:1 — thematic parallel: Both adjacent stories use scattering possessions to describe economic loss. Luke 15 narrates the son's reckless spending; Luke 16 reports an accusation against a steward over another's property. The charge and the narrated fact, and the roles, differ.
    Luke 16:1 He also said to the disciples, “There was a certain rich man who had a steward, and this man was accused before him of scattering his possessions.

Luke 15:14
When he had spent everything, a severe famine arose throughout that country, and he began to be in need.
  Genesis 47:13–47:15 — famine and exhausted resources: Both narratives combine an acute famine with exhausted resources and need for food. Luke places the son's spending before the country's famine; Genesis describes the severe Egyptian/Canaanite famine and silver spent buying grain before the people ask for bread. Their spending causes and sequences differ, and Genesis does not identify the son's unnamed country or imply his recklessness in its hungry population.
    Genesis 47:13 And there was no grain in all the land, for the famine had become exceedingly severe; and the land of Egypt and the land of Canaan languished because of the famine.
    Genesis 47:14 And Joseph gathered all the silver found in the land of Egypt and in the land of Canaan for the grain which they bought, and he measured it out to them; and Joseph brought all the silver into Pharaoh’s house.
    Genesis 47:15 And all the silver in the land of Egypt and in the land of Canaan was spent. And all the Egyptians came to Joseph, saying, “Give us bread; why should we die before you? For our silver is spent.”

Luke 15:15
So he went and attached himself to one of the citizens of that country, who sent him into his fields to feed pigs.
  Leviticus 11:7–11:8 — thematic parallel: Luke places the needy son in work feeding pigs; Leviticus classifies the pig as unclean for Israel and prohibits eating its flesh or touching its carcass. This provides a specific scriptural background for the animal in the scene. It does not turn a dietary and carcass rule into an express prohibition on this employment or assert that the son ate pork.
    Leviticus 11:7 and the pig, because it divides the hoof and has cleft hoof-claws but does not bring up the cud; it is unclean for you.
    Leviticus 11:8 You shall not eat their flesh or touch their carcasses; they are unclean for you.

Luke 15:16
And he longed to be filled with the carob pods the pigs were eating, and no one gave him anything.
  Luke 16:20–16:21 — narrative parallel: The hungry son longs to be filled with the pigs' food and receives nothing; the poor man at the rich man's gate longs to be filled with what falls from the table. Both nearby parables depict unfulfilled food longing beside abundance and animals. The pigs eat pods, while the dogs lick sores; Luke does not say the dogs eat the table scraps or identify the two hungry men.
    Luke 16:20 And a certain poor man named Lazarus had been laid at his gateway, covered with sores
    Luke 16:21 and longing to be filled with what fell from the rich man’s table. Even the dogs came and licked his sores.

Luke 15:17
But when he came to himself, he said, ‘How many of my father’s hired workers have bread to spare, while I am perishing here from hunger!
  Luke 15:18–15:19 — narrative parallel: The son contrasts his hunger with his father's hired workers having bread to spare, then proposes confession and being made like one of those hired workers. The repeated worker status connects his realization to his planned request. It does not say the father later hires him or reduce his hunger to refusal to work.
    Luke 15:18 I will rise and go to my father and say to him, “Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you.
    Luke 15:19 I am no longer worthy to be called your son. Make me like one of your hired workers.”’

Luke 15:18
I will rise and go to my father and say to him, “Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you.
  Luke 15:20–15:21 — narrative parallel: This same-chapter resolve to rise, go to the father, and confess corresponds to the narrated return and repeated confession. It is a plan-and-action link, not independent attestation.
    Luke 15:20 And he rose and came to his own father. But while he was still far away, his father saw him and was moved with compassion. He ran, fell upon his neck, and kissed him tenderly.
    Luke 15:21 And the son said to him, ‘Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you; I am no longer worthy to be called your son.’

Luke 15:19
I am no longer worthy to be called your son. Make me like one of your hired workers.”’
  Luke 15:21 — narrative parallel: The planned statement of unworthiness is repeated in the son's recorded confession. The planned hired-worker request is not repeated there; no invented cause for that omission is supplied.
    Luke 15:21 And the son said to him, ‘Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you; I am no longer worthy to be called your son.’

Luke 15:20
And he rose and came to his own father. But while he was still far away, his father saw him and was moved with compassion. He ran, fell upon his neck, and kissed him tenderly.
  Genesis 33:4 — allusion: Both family reunions involve running, falling on a neck, and kissing. Genesis describes Esau welcoming his brother Jacob with tears; Luke a compassionate father welcoming his son. The family roles and circumstances remain different.
    Genesis 33:4 And Esau ran to meet him, and embraced and kissed him, and fell upon his neck; and they both wept.

Luke 15:21
And the son said to him, ‘Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you; I am no longer worthy to be called your son.’
  Luke 15:18–15:19 — narrative parallel: The actual confession repeats the earlier planned sin acknowledgment and unworthiness. The earlier servant request is absent from the recorded confession, so the two speeches are not declared identical in every clause.
    Luke 15:18 I will rise and go to my father and say to him, “Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you.
    Luke 15:19 I am no longer worthy to be called your son. Make me like one of your hired workers.”’

Luke 15:22
But the father said to his slaves, ‘Quickly bring out the best robe, and clothe him; and put a ring on his hand, and sandals on his feet.
  Genesis 41:42 — thematic parallel: Both dress a favored recipient and give a ring. Luke's father provides robe and sandals to his son; Pharaoh gives Joseph linen and a gold collar. The honors do not make the offices or recipients identical.
    Genesis 41:42 And Pharaoh took his ring from his hand and put it upon Joseph’s hand, and clothed him in a robe of fine linen, and put a golden collar about his neck.

Luke 15:23
And bring the fattened calf, and slaughter it; and let us eat and rejoice,
  Matthew 22:4 — thematic parallel: Both hosts prepare fattened animals and call for a feast. Luke celebrates a son's return with a calf; Matthew's king announces a wedding meal with bulls and fattened animals. The feasts and sons are not equated.
    Matthew 22:4 Again he sent other slaves, saying, ‘Tell those invited: Behold, I have prepared my meal. My bulls and fattened animals have been slaughtered, and everything is ready. Come to the wedding feast.’

Luke 15:24
because this son of mine was dead and came to life again; he was lost and was found.’ And they began to be glad.
  Luke 15:32 — teaching parallel: The same-chapter refrain repeats dead/alive and lost/found as grounds for celebration. The father first says my son and later your brother, emphasizing the family relation without narrating a literal bodily resurrection.
    Luke 15:32 But we had to be glad and rejoice, because this brother of yours was dead and came to life; he was lost and was found.’”

Luke 15:25
Now his older son was in the field. As he came and drew near the house, he heard music and dancing.
  Exodus 32:17–32:19 — narrative parallel: An approaching person hears a celebration and then reacts angrily in each wider scene: Luke's older son hears music and dancing before resenting his brother's welcome; Joshua and Moses hear camp sounds, and Moses sees dancing and becomes angry. The limited narrative comparison preserves a legitimate family celebration versus idolatrous revelry; no idolatry or moral approval of the brother's anger is imported into Luke.
    Exodus 32:17 When Joshua heard the people shouting, he said to Moses, “There is a sound of war in the camp!”
    Exodus 32:18 He said, “It is not the sound of those leading a song of victory or of defeat; it is the sound of those leading a wine-song that I hear.”
    Exodus 32:19 As he approached the camp, he saw the calf and the dancing. In his anger Moses threw the two tablets from his hands and shattered them at the mountain’s foot.

Luke 15:26
And he called one of the servants and asked what these things might mean.
  Luke 15:27 — internal argument, referent or explicit response: The servant's answer identifies the brother's return and the father's feast as the explanation requested by the older son. This is an internal question-and-answer link, not an independent account of another homecoming.
    Luke 15:27 He said to him, ‘Your brother has come, and your father slaughtered the fattened calf because he received him back in good health.’

Luke 15:27
He said to him, ‘Your brother has come, and your father slaughtered the fattened calf because he received him back in good health.’
  Luke 15:23 — narrative parallel: The servant reports completed slaughter of the calf ordered earlier in this chapter. That command-and-report link does not make the earlier order an independent witness to every detail of the servant's explanation.
    Luke 15:23 And bring the fattened calf, and slaughter it; and let us eat and rejoice,

Luke 15:28
But he became angry and was unwilling to enter. His father came out and pleaded with him.
  Matthew 20:11–20:15 — thematic parallel: Both portray resentment of generous treatment of a late or returning recipient. Luke's older son refuses the feast and is entreated by his father; Matthew's earlier workers grumble and receive the owner's reply.
    Matthew 20:11 On receiving it, they grumbled against the householder,
    Matthew 20:12 saying, ‘These last worked one hour, and you made them equal to us who bore the day’s burden and scorching heat.’
    Matthew 20:13 He answered one of them, ‘Friend, I am not wronging you. Did you not agree with me for a denarius?
    Matthew 20:14 Take what is yours and go. I wish to give to this last one the same as to you.
    Matthew 20:15 Am I not permitted to do what I wish with what is mine? Or is your eye evil because I am good?’

Luke 15:29
But he answered his father, ‘Behold, all these years I have served you as a slave, and I never disobeyed your command; yet you never gave me a young goat so that I might be glad with my friends.
  Matthew 20:12 — thematic parallel: Both complaints contrast the speaker's lengthy labor with favorable treatment of another. Luke cites years of obedient service and no goat; Matthew cites the day's burden and heat versus one hour.
    Matthew 20:12 saying, ‘These last worked one hour, and you made them equal to us who bore the day’s burden and scorching heat.’

Luke 15:30
But when this son of yours came, who devoured your livelihood with prostitutes, you slaughtered the fattened calf for him!’
  Matthew 20:12–20:15 — thematic parallel: Both resentful speakers present another recipient as less deserving. Luke's brother alleges spending on prostitutes; Matthew's workers cite shorter labor. The brother's allegation is not turned into independent narrator confirmation or the workers' story.
    Matthew 20:12 saying, ‘These last worked one hour, and you made them equal to us who bore the day’s burden and scorching heat.’
    Matthew 20:13 He answered one of them, ‘Friend, I am not wronging you. Did you not agree with me for a denarius?
    Matthew 20:14 Take what is yours and go. I wish to give to this last one the same as to you.
    Matthew 20:15 Am I not permitted to do what I wish with what is mine? Or is your eye evil because I am good?’

Luke 15:31
And he said to him, ‘Child, you are always with me, and everything of mine is yours.
  Matthew 20:13–20:15 — thematic parallel: Both benefactors defend generosity while addressing an earlier claimant's rights. Luke's father says his property is the older son's; Matthew's owner honors the agreed wage while freely giving another the same. The property relationships differ.
    Matthew 20:13 He answered one of them, ‘Friend, I am not wronging you. Did you not agree with me for a denarius?
    Matthew 20:14 Take what is yours and go. I wish to give to this last one the same as to you.
    Matthew 20:15 Am I not permitted to do what I wish with what is mine? Or is your eye evil because I am good?’

Luke 15:32
But we had to be glad and rejoice, because this brother of yours was dead and came to life; he was lost and was found.’”
  Luke 15:24 — teaching parallel: This same-chapter return to the dead/alive and lost/found refrain repeats the reason for joy. The father now says your brother rather than my son, applying the same restoration to the older son's response.
    Luke 15:24 because this son of mine was dead and came to life again; he was lost and was found.’ And they began to be glad.

Luke 16:1
He also said to the disciples, “There was a certain rich man who had a steward, and this man was accused before him of scattering his possessions.
  Luke 15:13 — thematic parallel: Both adjacent stories use scattering possessions for economic waste. Luke 16 reports an accusation against a steward over someone else's property; Luke 15 narrates the son's reckless spending. The evidentiary status and family or employment roles differ.
    Luke 15:13 Not many days later, the younger son gathered everything together and traveled to a distant country. There he scattered his property by living recklessly.

Luke 16:2
He called him and said to him, ‘What is this I hear about you? Give an account of your stewardship, for you can no longer be steward.’
  Matthew 25:19 — thematic parallel: Both portray a master calling entrusted servants to account. Luke's steward faces dismissal after an accusation; Matthew's returning master settles accounts after a long absence. The businesses and preceding events differ.
    Matthew 25:19 After a long time, the master of those slaves came and settled accounts with them.

Luke 16:3
And the steward said within himself, ‘What shall I do, since my master is taking the stewardship away from me? I am not strong enough to dig; I am ashamed to beg.
  Luke 12:17 — narrative parallel: The steward and rich man each reason privately with 'What shall I do?' and name an economic difficulty before proposing a solution. The steward faces removal and inability or reluctance to do other work; the rich man faces crop-storage capacity. The recurring deliberation formula connects the parables without making their circumstances, motives, plans or ensuing evaluations identical.
    Luke 12:17 And he reasoned within himself, saying, ‘What shall I do, since I have nowhere to gather my crops?’

Luke 16:4
I know what I will do, so that when I am removed from the stewardship, they may welcome me into their own houses.’
  Luke 16:9 — thematic parallel: The steward plans later welcome into houses, while Jesus' application speaks of welcome into eternal tents through friends made with wealth. This internal reception motif contrasts temporal security and the stated application without endorsing fraud or identifying the dwellings as the same.
    Luke 16:9 And I tell you, make friends for yourselves from the mammon of unrighteousness, so that when it fails, they may welcome you into the eternal tents.

Luke 16:5
And he called each one of his own master’s debtors and said to the first, ‘How much do you owe my master?’
  Matthew 18:23–18:25 — teaching parallel: The steward summons his master's debtors and asks the first what he owes; Matthew's king begins settling accounts and a deeply indebted servant is brought before him. Both parables stage obligations owed to a master through an accounting encounter. The steward is an intermediary, whereas the king is the creditor; debt amounts and the actions that follow are different.
    Matthew 18:23 Therefore the kingdom of the heavens was likened to a king who wished to settle accounts with his slaves.
    Matthew 18:24 And when he began to settle them, one was brought to him who owed ten thousand talents.
    Matthew 18:25 And since he did not have the means to repay, his master commanded that he be sold, with his wife and children and all that he had, and that payment be made.

Luke 16:6
He said, ‘A hundred baths of oil.’ And he said to him, ‘Take your document, sit down quickly, and write fifty.’
  Luke 16:7 — narrative parallel: The two consecutive transactions deliberately repeat the debt question and written-document revision while changing commodity and figures: a hundred baths of oil becomes fifty here, and a hundred kors of wheat becomes eighty in the second. This is an internal structural comparison of the steward's dealings. The different reductions do not establish an interest-rate, commission, or legally authorized remission theory.
    Luke 16:7 Then he said to another, ‘And how much do you owe?’ He said, ‘A hundred kors of wheat.’ He said to him, ‘Take your document and write eighty.’

Luke 16:7
Then he said to another, ‘And how much do you owe?’ He said, ‘A hundred kors of wheat.’ He said to him, ‘Take your document and write eighty.’
  Luke 16:6 — narrative parallel: The wheat transaction repeats the earlier oil debtor's written revision, but changes a hundred to eighty rather than a hundred to fifty. This explicit repeated transaction pattern permits comparison of the different stated reductions without treating the commodities or quantities as identical. No explanation involving removed interest, commission, local prices, or the master's authorization is added.
    Luke 16:6 He said, ‘A hundred baths of oil.’ And he said to him, ‘Take your document, sit down quickly, and write fifty.’

Luke 16:8
And the master praised the unrighteous steward because he had acted prudently. For the sons of this age are more prudent toward their own generation than the sons of light.
  Matthew 10:16 — teaching parallel: Both value prudence, but Matthew expressly also requires innocence. Luke's master praises the unrighteous steward specifically for acting prudently; the comparison does not turn that limited commendation into moral approval of his conduct.
    Matthew 10:16 Behold, I send you as sheep in the midst of wolves. So become prudent as snakes and innocent as doves.

Luke 16:9
And I tell you, make friends for yourselves from the mammon of unrighteousness, so that when it fails, they may welcome you into the eternal tents.
  Luke 12:33 — teaching parallel: Both direct present possessions toward an enduring future. Luke 12 explicitly names alms and heavenly treasure; Luke 16 names friends and eternal tents. The images and instructions are related, not identical transactions.
    Luke 12:33 Sell your possessions and give alms. Make for yourselves purses that do not wear out, an unfailing treasure in the heavens, where no thief draws near and no moth destroys.

Luke 16:10
The one who is faithful in the least is faithful also in much, and the one who is unrighteous in the least is unrighteous also in much.
  Matthew 25:21 — teaching parallel: Both connect faithfulness in little with greater responsibility. Luke also states the negative principle for unrighteousness; Matthew's praise of the faithful slave does not repeat that half.
    Matthew 25:21 His master said to him, ‘Well done, good and faithful slave. You were faithful over a few things; I will appoint you over many. Enter into your master’s joy.’

Luke 16:11
So if you have not been faithful with unrighteous mammon, who will entrust the true riches to you?
  1 Timothy 6:17–6:19 — thematic parallel: Both distinguish present money from a more lasting good requiring responsible use. Luke contrasts unrighteous mammon with true riches; Timothy contrasts uncertain wealth with generous good works and truly living. It does not repeat Luke's exact entrustment question.
    1 Timothy 6:17 Command those who are rich in the present age not to be arrogant nor to set their hope on the uncertainty of wealth, but on God, who richly provides us all things for enjoyment,
    1 Timothy 6:18 to do good, to be rich in good works, to be generous, ready to share,
    1 Timothy 6:19 storing up for themselves a good foundation for the future, so that they may take hold of the life that is truly life.

Luke 16:12
And if you have not been faithful with what belongs to another, who will give you what is your own?
  1 Corinthians 4:1–4:2 — teaching parallel: Both bind stewardship to faithfulness over what is entrusted. Luke asks about another's property and future possession of one's own; Paul names God's mysteries, not the same assets or reward.
    1 Corinthians 4:1 This is how a person should regard us: as servants of Christ and stewards of God’s mysteries.
    1 Corinthians 4:2 Moreover, what is required of stewards is that each be found faithful.

Luke 16:13
No household servant can serve two masters as a slave. For either he will hate the one and love the other, or he will hold to the one and despise the other. You cannot serve God and mammon.”
  Matthew 6:24 — teaching parallel: Both describe the impossibility of serving two masters, opposite loyalties, and the incompatibility of serving God and mammon.
    Matthew 6:24 No one can serve as a slave to two masters: for he will either hate the one and love the other, or hold to one and despise the other. You cannot serve as a slave to God and mammon.

Luke 16:14
Now the Pharisees, who were lovers of money, heard all these things and sneered at Him.
  1 Timothy 6:10 — thematic parallel: Both condemn love of money. Luke identifies sneering opponents as money-lovers; Timothy describes love of money's evils and some people's wandering from faith, not the biography of these Pharisees.
    1 Timothy 6:10 For the love of money is a root of all evils, and some, reaching for it, wandered from the faith and pierced themselves with many pains.

Luke 16:15
And He said to them, “You are those who justify yourselves before people, but God knows your hearts, because what is exalted among people is an abomination before God.
  Matthew 23:27–23:28 — teaching parallel: Both expose outward righteousness before people concealing inward corruption. Luke names God's knowledge of hearts and human exaltation's divine disapproval; Matthew uses whitewashed tombs, hypocrisy, and lawlessness.
    Matthew 23:27 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you resemble whitewashed tombs, which outwardly appear beautiful, but inwardly are full of dead people’s bones and all uncleanness!
    Matthew 23:28 So you too outwardly appear righteous to people, but inwardly you are full of hypocrisy and lawlessness.

Luke 16:16
The Law and the Prophets were until John. From that time the kingdom of God is proclaimed as good news, and everyone presses into it.
  Matthew 11:12–11:13 — teaching parallel: Both place John at a transition involving Law, Prophets, and kingdom. Luke says everyone presses into the proclaimed kingdom; Matthew describes violence and seizure. The expressions are not forced into one interpretation.
    Matthew 11:12 From the days of John the Baptist until now, the kingdom of the heavens suffers violence, and violent men seize it.
    Matthew 11:13 For all the Prophets and the Law prophesied until John.

Luke 16:17
But it is easier for heaven and earth to pass away than for one small stroke of the Law to fall.
  Matthew 5:18 — teaching parallel: Both contrast passing heaven and earth with the Law's enduring smallest written mark. Matthew adds iota and until all is accomplished; Luke names a small stroke falling.
    Matthew 5:18 For truly I tell you: until heaven and earth pass away, not one iota nor one stroke shall pass from the Law, until all things come to pass.

Luke 16:18
Everyone who divorces his wife and marries another commits adultery, and the one who marries a woman divorced from a husband commits adultery.
  Matthew 5:32 — teaching parallel: Both connect divorce and remarriage with adultery and mention marrying a divorced woman. Matthew includes a sexual-immorality exception and causing adultery; Luke's supplied sentence does not repeat those qualifications.
    Matthew 5:32 But I tell you that everyone who divorces his wife, except on account of sexual immorality, causes her to commit adultery; and whoever marries a divorced woman commits adultery.

Luke 16:19
Now there was a certain rich man, and he clothed himself in purple and fine linen, enjoying himself in splendor every day.
  James 5:5 — thematic parallel: Both depict luxurious living within a warning about judgment. Luke specifies purple, linen, and daily splendor; James names indulgence and the slaughter day. James's surrounding accusations are not imported as the rich man's unstated biography.
    James 5:5 You lived luxuriously on the earth and indulged yourselves; you fattened your hearts on a day of slaughter.

Luke 16:20
And a certain poor man named Lazarus had been laid at his gateway, covered with sores
  Acts 3:2–3:3 — needy afflicted men placed at a gateway: Both accounts place a needy, physically afflicted man at an entrance where others may provide aid. Lazarus is laid at the rich man's gateway covered in sores; Acts' man unable to walk from birth is carried to the temple gate and asks for alms. The sites, ailments, requests and outcomes differ. Lazarus is not identified with Acts' man or assigned his inability to walk.
    Acts 3:2 A man unable to walk from his mother’s womb was being carried there. They used to place him each day at the temple gate called Beautiful, to ask for alms from those entering the temple.
    Acts 3:3 When he saw Peter and John about to enter the temple, he asked to receive alms.

Luke 16:21
and longing to be filled with what fell from the rich man’s table. Even the dogs came and licked his sores.
  Matthew 15:27 — thematic parallel: Both place food falling from a table and dogs in a need-related scene. Matthew's little dogs eat crumbs; Luke's dogs lick Lazarus's sores while he longs for fallen food. The actions and human roles are not equated.
    Matthew 15:27 She said, “Yes, Lord, for even the little dogs eat from the crumbs falling from their masters’ table.”

Luke 16:22
And it happened that the poor man died and was carried away by the angels to Abraham’s bosom. The rich man also died and was buried.
  Matthew 8:11 — thematic parallel: Both depict blessed association with Abraham. Luke describes the dead poor man carried by angels to Abraham's bosom; Matthew describes arrivals reclining with patriarchs in the kingdom. The images are not declared identical.
    Matthew 8:11 I tell you that many will come from east and west and recline with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the kingdom of the heavens,

Luke 16:23
And in Hades, being in torment, he lifted his eyes and saw Abraham far away and Lazarus in his bosom.
  Luke 13:28 — thematic parallel: Both portray distressed exclusion alongside sight of Abraham in blessed company. Luke 13 uses kingdom exclusion and weeping; Luke 16 explicitly names Hades, torment, and Lazarus. The distinct images are not turned into one asserted geography.
    Luke 13:28 There will be weeping and grinding of teeth there, when you see Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of God, but yourselves being thrown outside.

Luke 16:24
And he called out, ‘Father Abraham, have mercy on me and send Lazarus to dip the tip of his finger in water and cool my tongue, because I am in pain in this flame.’
  Isaiah 65:13–65:14 — thematic parallel: The formerly feasting rich man asks for water in painful torment while Lazarus is comforted in the surrounding dialogue. Isaiah contrasts the Lord's servants eating, drinking and rejoicing with opponents' hunger, thirst and cries of heart-pain. This is a bounded judgment-and-reversal comparison, not a source for the wet-finger request, flame mechanism, or an identical map of the afterlife.
    Isaiah 65:13 Therefore thus says the Lord: “Behold, those serving Me will eat, but you will hunger. Those serving Me will drink, but you will thirst. Those serving Me will rejoice, but you will be ashamed.
    Isaiah 65:14 Behold, those serving Me will exult with joy, but you will cry out because of your heart’s pain and wail from a broken spirit.

Luke 16:25
But Abraham said, ‘Child, remember that you received your good things during your life, and Lazarus likewise bad things. But now he is comforted here, and you are in pain.
  Luke 6:20–6:25 — teaching parallel: Both contrast poor or suffering people's future comfort with rich people's present good followed by reversal. The beatitudes and woes state the pattern generally; the parable applies it to two figures without supplying identical histories.
    Luke 6:20 Lifting His eyes toward His disciples, He said, “Blessed are you who are poor, because yours is God’s kingdom.
    Luke 6:21 Blessed are you who hunger now, because you will be filled. Blessed are you who weep now, because you will laugh.
    Luke 6:22 Blessed are you when people hate you, when they exclude you, reproach you, and cast out your name as evil because of the Son of Man.
    Luke 6:23 Rejoice on that day and leap, for behold, your reward is great in heaven; their fathers treated the prophets in the same way.
    Luke 6:24 But woe to you who are rich, because you have received your consolation.
    Luke 6:25 Woe to you who are filled now, because you will hunger. Woe to you who laugh now, because you will mourn and weep.

Luke 16:26
And besides all this, a great chasm has been fixed between us and you, so that those who want to cross from here to you cannot, nor may they cross over from there to us.’
  Luke 13:25–13:28 — teaching parallel: The fixed chasm prevents movement between torment and Abraham's company; the earlier teaching depicts a closed door, rejected pleas to enter, and people outside seeing Abraham in God's kingdom. Both depict decisive exclusion alongside awareness of Abraham's blessed company. Chasm and doorway remain different images, with no claim that the scenes give identical geography or add a later crossing possibility.
    Luke 13:25 once the master of the house rises and shuts the door. Then you will begin to stand outside and knock at the door, saying, ‘Lord, open to us!’ And he will answer you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from.’
    Luke 13:26 Then you will begin to say, ‘We ate and drank in your presence, and you taught in our streets.’
    Luke 13:27 And he will say to you, ‘I do not know you or where you come from. Depart from me, all you workers of unrighteousness!’
    Luke 13:28 There will be weeping and grinding of teeth there, when you see Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of God, but yourselves being thrown outside.

Luke 16:27
And he said, ‘Then I ask you, father, to send him to my father’s house,
  Luke 16:24 — narrative parallel: The earlier appeal explicitly asks Father Abraham to send Lazarus with water; the later appeal asks father to send him to the requester's father's house. This identifies the proposed messenger and distinguishes the two requests. No journey is reported as fulfilled, and the parable's Lazarus is not identified with John's Lazarus.
    Luke 16:24 And he called out, ‘Father Abraham, have mercy on me and send Lazarus to dip the tip of his finger in water and cool my tongue, because I am in pain in this flame.’

Luke 16:28
for I have five brothers, so that he may solemnly warn them, lest they also come to this place of torment.’
  Ezekiel 3:17–3:19 — thematic parallel: The rich man wants his living brothers solemnly warned so they may avoid torment; Ezekiel's watchman must convey God's warning so a lawless hearer may turn and live rather than die. The connection is warning before destructive judgment with an opportunity to change. Ezekiel neither identifies the five brothers nor authorizes a visit from the dead; the following Luke dialogue instead points to Moses and the Prophets.
    Ezekiel 3:17 “Son of man, I have appointed you a watchman for the house of Israel. You will hear a word from My mouth and convey My warning to them.
    Ezekiel 3:18 When I say to the lawless person, ‘You will surely be put to death,’ and you do not warn him or speak to warn the lawless person to turn from his ways so that he may live, that lawless person will die in his injustice; but I will require his blood from your hand.
    Ezekiel 3:19 But if you warn the lawless person and he does not turn from his lawlessness and his way, that lawless person will die in his injustice, while you will rescue your life.

Luke 16:29
But Abraham said, ‘They have Moses and the Prophets; let them hear them.’
  John 5:46–5:47 — teaching parallel: Both give Moses' words weight as available testimony. Luke's Abraham directs the brothers to hear Moses and Prophets; John's Jesus links belief in Moses' writings with belief in Himself. The speakers and audiences differ.
    John 5:46 For if you believed Moses, you would believe Me, for he wrote concerning Me.
    John 5:47 But if you do not believe his writings, how will you believe My words?”

Luke 16:30
But he said, ‘No, father Abraham; but if someone goes to them from the dead, they will repent.’
  John 11:43–11:45 — thematic parallel: Luke's rich man proposes a dead visitor as a cause of repentance; John narrates a raising followed by many believing. The parable's beggar and John's Lazarus are not identified from their shared name, and belief is not made a universal response.
    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”
    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”
    John 11:45 So many of the Jews who had come to Mary and beheld what He did believed in Him.

Luke 16:31
And he said to him, ‘If they do not hear Moses and the Prophets, neither will they be persuaded if someone rises from the dead.’”
  John 12:10–12:11 — thematic parallel: Luke rejects resurrection as a guarantee of persuasion when Scripture is ignored; John depicts priests plotting against raised Lazarus while others believe. The response comparison does not identify the beggar with Jesus' Bethany friend.
    John 12:10 But the chief priests planned to kill Lazarus also,
    John 12:11 because on his account many of the Jews were going away and believing in Jesus.

Luke 17:1
And He said to His disciples, “It is impossible for stumbling blocks not to come, but woe to the one through whom they come!
  Matthew 18:7 — teaching parallel: Both acknowledge inevitable stumbling blocks while pronouncing woe on their human source. Matthew additionally says woe to the world; Luke directly addresses disciples.
    Matthew 18:7 Woe to the world because of stumbling blocks! For stumbling blocks must come, but woe to the person through whom the stumbling block comes!

Luke 17:2
It would be better for him to have a millstone placed around his neck and be thrown into the sea than to cause one of these little ones to stumble.
  Matthew 18:6 — teaching parallel: Both prefer a millstone and drowning to causing a little one to stumble. Matthew specifies little ones believing in Jesus; Luke does not repeat that qualification here.
    Matthew 18:6 But whoever causes one of these little ones who believe in Me to stumble, it is better for him that a donkey-driven millstone be hung around his neck and he be sunk in the open sea.

Luke 17:3
Watch yourselves. If your brother sins, rebuke him; and if he repents, forgive him.
  Matthew 18:15 — teaching parallel: Both direct confronting a brother's sin. Matthew specifies private correction and gaining a brother who listens; Luke explicitly links repentance with forgiveness.
    Matthew 18:15 If your brother sins against you, go and reprove him between you and him alone. If he hears you, you have gained your brother.

Luke 17:4
And if he sins against you seven times in the day and turns back to you seven times, saying, ‘I repent,’ you shall forgive him.”
  Matthew 18:21–18:22 — teaching parallel: Both address repeated forgiveness using seven. Luke specifies seven offenses and repentant returns within a day; Matthew records Peter's question and Jesus' greater numerical answer.
    Matthew 18:21 Then Peter came and said to Him, “Lord, how often will my brother sin against me and I forgive him? Up to seven times?”
    Matthew 18:22 Jesus says to him, “I tell you, not up to seven times, but up to seventy times seven.

Luke 17:5
And the apostles said to the Lord, “Increase our faith.”
  Mark 9:24 — thematic parallel: Both appeals ask Jesus for help with deficient faith. Luke's apostles request increased faith; Mark's father confesses belief and asks help for unbelief. The speakers and circumstances differ.
    Mark 9:24 Immediately the father of the child cried out and said, “I believe; help my unbelief!”

Luke 17:6
And the Lord said, “If you have faith like a mustard seed, you would say to this mulberry tree, ‘Be uprooted and planted in the sea,’ and it would obey you.
  Matthew 17:20 — teaching parallel: Both use mustard-seed faith and an apparently impossible command. Luke pictures uprooting a mulberry tree into the sea; Matthew pictures moving a mountain.
    Matthew 17:20 He said to them, “Because of your little faith. For truly I tell you, if you have faith like a mustard seed, you will say to this mountain, ‘Move from here to there,’ and it will move; and nothing will be impossible for you.”

Luke 17:7
Which of you, having a slave plowing or tending sheep, will say to him when he comes in from the field, ‘Come at once and recline at table’?
  Luke 12:37 — same-book contrast: Luke17 questions immediately seating a working slave at the master's table; Luke12 promises the exceptional master serving watchful slaves. The contrasting service arrangements are preserved.
    Luke 12:37 Blessed are those slaves whom the master finds keeping watch when he comes. Amen, I tell you: he will gird himself, have them recline at table, and come and serve them.

Luke 17:8
Will he not instead say to him, ‘Prepare something for my supper, gird yourself, and serve me while I eat and drink; and after that you shall eat and drink’?
  Luke 12:37 — same-book contrast: Both mention girding and meal service, with reversed roles. Here the slave serves before eating; there the returning master girds himself and serves the watchful slaves.
    Luke 12:37 Blessed are those slaves whom the master finds keeping watch when he comes. Amen, I tell you: he will gird himself, have them recline at table, and come and serve them.

Luke 17:9
Does he thank the slave because he did what was commanded?
  Luke 17:10 — internal parable application: The question about thanking a slave for commanded work leads to the same episode's application: servants have only done their duty. The application is not independent attestation of a household custom.
    Luke 17:10 So you also, when you have done everything commanded you, say, ‘We are unprofitable slaves; we have done what we ought to have done.’”

Luke 17:10
So you also, when you have done everything commanded you, say, ‘We are unprofitable slaves; we have done what we ought to have done.’”
  1 Corinthians 9:16 — thematic parallel: Both distinguish required service from a claim to credit. Luke's slaves have done what they ought; Paul has no boast in proclaiming because necessity lies upon him. Their commissions differ.
    1 Corinthians 9:16 For if I proclaim the good news, I have no boast, because necessity lies upon me. For woe to me if I do not proclaim the good news!

Luke 17:11
And it happened that, as He was traveling to Jerusalem, He passed through the midst of Samaria and Galilee.
  Luke 9:51 — same-book journey link: Both locate Jesus' movement toward Jerusalem. Luke17 specifies passage through Samaria and Galilee; Luke9 introduces His resolve and approaching taking-up, without this precise route.
    Luke 9:51 As the days for His being taken up approached their fulfillment, He firmly set His face to go to Jerusalem.

Luke 17:12
As He entered a certain village, ten men with leprosy met Him. They stood at a distance
  Leviticus 13:45–13:46 — law background: The leprous men's distance can be compared with the law's separate dwelling for a person declared unclean. Leviticus does not name these ten men or independently establish their village or conduct.
    Leviticus 13:45 And he that has leprosy, in whom the lesion is, shall have his garments loosened and his head uncovered, and shall put a covering about his mouth, and shall be called unclean;
    Leviticus 13:46 all the days in which the lesion is upon him, being unclean, he shall be unclean; he shall dwell apart; his dwelling shall be outside the camp.

Luke 17:13
and raised their voices, saying, “Jesus, Master, have mercy on us!”
  Luke 18:38 — narrative parallel: Both healing petitions publicly invoke Jesus by name and ask mercy. The leprous group says Master; the blind beggar says Son of David. They are distinct recipients.
    Luke 18:38 And he cried out, saying, “Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Luke 17:14
When He saw them, He said to them, “Go and show yourselves to the priests.” And it happened that as they went, they were cleansed.
  Luke 5:14 — narrative parallel: Both direct leprous recipients to show themselves to priests. Luke17 has ten cleansed while going; Luke5 concerns one already cleansed and additionally specifies Moses' offering.
    Luke 5:14 He commanded him to tell no one: “But go, show yourself to the priest, and make the offering for your cleansing as Moses commanded, for a testimony to them.”

Luke 17:15
One of them, seeing that he had been healed, returned, glorifying God with a loud voice.
  2 Kings 5:14–5:15 — narrative parallel: Both cleansing accounts include a return and acknowledgment of God. Luke's healed man returns praising loudly; Naaman returns after Jordan washing and confesses Israel's God. The healers and occasions differ.
    2 Kings 5:14 And Naaman went down, and dipped himself in the Jordan seven times, according to the word of Elisha; and his flesh was restored as the flesh of a little child, and he was cleansed.
    2 Kings 5:15 He returned to Elisha, he and all his company. He came and stood and said, ‘Behold, now I know that there is no God in all the earth except in Israel. Now accept the blessing from your slave.’

Luke 17:16
And he fell on his face at His feet, giving Him thanks; and he was a Samaritan.
  2 Kings 5:15 — narrative parallel: Both depict a cleansed recipient returning to the healer. Luke's Samaritan falls at Jesus' feet with thanks; Naaman stands before Elisha and acknowledges Israel's God. The persons and gestures differ.
    2 Kings 5:15 He returned to Elisha, he and all his company. He came and stood and said, ‘Behold, now I know that there is no God in all the earth except in Israel. Now accept the blessing from your slave.’

Luke 17:17
Jesus answered, “Were not the ten cleansed? But where are the nine?
  Luke 17:12–17:16 — narrative parallel: The earlier narration gives the ten lepers, their cleansing while going, and one returning to thank Jesus. It supplies the particular ten-and-one background for Jesus' question about the other nine. The comparison does not invent nine names, their subsequent conduct or an independently recorded numerical event.
    Luke 17:12 As He entered a certain village, ten men with leprosy met Him. They stood at a distance
    Luke 17:13 and raised their voices, saying, “Jesus, Master, have mercy on us!”
    Luke 17:14 When He saw them, He said to them, “Go and show yourselves to the priests.” And it happened that as they went, they were cleansed.
    Luke 17:15 One of them, seeing that he had been healed, returned, glorifying God with a loud voice.
    Luke 17:16 And he fell on his face at His feet, giving Him thanks; and he was a Samaritan.

Luke 17:18
Were none found returning to give glory to God except this foreigner?”
  2 Kings 5:15 — thematic parallel: Both connect healing with honoring God. Jesus notes the returning foreigner's gratitude; Naaman acknowledges no God except in Israel. Kings does not supply Luke's ten recipients or missing nine.
    2 Kings 5:15 He returned to Elisha, he and all his company. He came and stood and said, ‘Behold, now I know that there is no God in all the earth except in Israel. Now accept the blessing from your slave.’

Luke 17:19
And He said to him, “Rise and go; your faith has saved you.”
  Mark 5:34 — teaching parallel: Both tell a healed recipient that faith has saved them and send them onward. Luke concerns a leprous man and says rise and go; Mark concerns bleeding and says go in peace.
    Mark 5:34 And He said to her, “Daughter, your faith has saved you; go in peace, and be well, free from your affliction.”

Luke 17:20
When the Pharisees asked Him when the kingdom of God was coming, He answered them, “The kingdom of God does not come in a way that can be observed,
  Acts 1:6–1:7 — thematic parallel: Both address a question about kingdom timing without supplying the requested schedule. Luke answers Pharisees about observable coming; Acts answers disciples about the Father's appointed times. The questions and replies differ.
    Acts 1:6 So those who had come together asked Him, “Lord, is this the time when Thou art restoring the kingdom to Israel?”
    Acts 1:7 He said to them, “It is not for you to know times or seasons that the Father has set by His own authority.

Luke 17:21
nor will they say, ‘Behold, here!’ or ‘There!’ For behold, the kingdom of God is within you.”
  Luke 11:20 — same-book teaching comparison: Both describe God's kingdom in relation to the present hearers. Luke17 says within you; Luke11 says has come upon you through God's exorcising work. The expressions are preserved without assigning them identical spatial meanings.
    Luke 11:20 But if I drive out the demons by the finger of God, then the kingdom of God has come upon you.

Luke 17:22
And He said to the disciples, “Days will come when you will long to see one of the days of the Son of Man, and you will not see it.
  John 13:33 — disciples' anticipated seeking during absence: Both sayings to disciples anticipate Jesus' absence and an unfulfilled seeking or desire. Luke speaks of longing to see a day of the Son of Man without seeing it; John says they will seek Him and cannot come where He goes. The days/seeing and seeking/going formulations and settings differ; this comparison does not impose an identical timetable or settle the meaning of Luke's days.
    John 13:33 Little children, I am with you a little longer. You will seek Me, and just as I told the Jews, ‘Where I go, you cannot come,’ I now tell you also.

Luke 17:23
And they will say to you, ‘Behold, there!’ or ‘Behold, here!’ Do not go away or pursue them.
  Matthew 24:23–24:26 — teaching parallel: Both warn against pursuing localized claims about Christ or His presence. Matthew adds false messiahs, signs, wilderness and inner rooms; Luke simply rejects here-or-there reports.
    Matthew 24:23 Then if anyone tells you, ‘Behold, here is the Christ!’ or ‘Here!’ do not believe it.
    Matthew 24:24 For false christs and false prophets will arise and give great signs and wonders, so as to lead astray, if possible, even the chosen.
    Matthew 24:25 Behold, I have told you beforehand.
    Matthew 24:26 So if they tell you, ‘Behold, He is in the wilderness,’ do not go out; or ‘Behold, in the inner rooms,’ do not believe it.

Luke 17:24
For just as lightning flashes and shines from one region under heaven to another under heaven, so will the Son of Man be in His day.
  Matthew 24:27 — teaching parallel: Both compare the Son of Man's manifestation with widely visible lightning. Matthew specifies east and west; Luke describes one region under heaven shining to another.
    Matthew 24:27 For just as lightning comes from the east and shines to the west, so will be the coming of the Son of Man.

Luke 17:25
But first He must suffer many things and be rejected by this generation.
  Mark 8:31 — passion-prediction parallel: Both predict the Son of Man's prior suffering and rejection. Luke names this generation; Mark specifies elders, chief priests and scribes, then death and resurrection.
    Mark 8:31 He began teaching them that the Son of Man must suffer many things, be rejected by the elders, the chief priests, and the scribes, be killed, and rise after three days.

Luke 17:26
And just as it happened in the days of Noah, so it will also be in the days of the Son of Man.
  Matthew 24:37 — teaching parallel: Both compare Noah's days with the Son of Man's future manifestation. Matthew calls it His coming; Luke speaks of His days.
    Matthew 24:37 For just as the days of Noah were, so will be the coming of the Son of Man.

Luke 17:27
They were eating, drinking, marrying, and being given in marriage, until the day Noah entered the ark, and the flood came and destroyed them all.
  Matthew 24:38–24:39 — teaching parallel: Both describe ordinary eating, drinking and marriage until Noah entered the ark and the flood swept people away. Matthew additionally emphasizes their lack of knowledge before the flood.
    Matthew 24:38 For in those days before the flood they were eating and drinking, marrying and giving in marriage, until the day Noah entered the ark.
    Matthew 24:39 They did not know until the flood came and took them all away. So also will be the coming of the Son of Man.

Luke 17:28
Likewise, just as it happened in the days of Lot: they were eating, drinking, buying, selling, planting, and building;
  Matthew 24:38 — teaching comparison: Both list ordinary activities before judgment. Luke adds Lot's setting, buying, selling, planting and building; Matthew names Noah's days and marriage. Matthew does not independently attest Luke's Lot-specific activity list.
    Matthew 24:38 For in those days before the flood they were eating and drinking, marrying and giving in marriage, until the day Noah entered the ark.

Luke 17:29
but on the day Lot went out from Sodom, fire and sulfur rained from heaven and destroyed them all.
  Genesis 19:23–19:25 — allusion: Luke recalls Lot's departure followed by sulfur and fire destroying Sodom. Genesis places Lot in Zoar before the rain on Sodom and Gomorrah and describes the wider destruction.
    Genesis 19:23 The sun rose upon the earth, and Lot entered into Zoar.
    Genesis 19:24 And the Lord rained upon Sodom and Gomorrah sulphur and fire from the Lord out of the heaven;
    Genesis 19:25 and He overthrew those cities, and all the surrounding country, and all that lived in the cities, and all that sprang from the earth.

Luke 17:30
It will be the same on the day the Son of Man is revealed.
  Matthew 24:39 — teaching parallel: Both apply an ancient sudden-destruction comparison to the Son of Man's future day. Luke follows Lot and says revealed; Matthew follows Noah and says coming.
    Matthew 24:39 They did not know until the flood came and took them all away. So also will be the coming of the Son of Man.

Luke 17:31
On that day, whoever is on the housetop, with his belongings in the house, must not go down to take them; likewise, whoever is in the field must not turn back.
  Matthew 24:17–24:18 — teaching parallel: Both forbid descending from a housetop for belongings or returning from a field. Matthew specifies taking a cloak; Luke's field instruction simply forbids turning back.
    Matthew 24:17 Let the one on the housetop not go down to take things from his house,
    Matthew 24:18 and let the one in the field not turn back to take his cloak.

Luke 17:32
Remember Lot’s wife.
  Genesis 19:26 — allusion: Jesus' command to remember Lot's wife points to Genesis' account of her looking back and becoming a salt pillar. Luke does not repeat those narrative details.
    Genesis 19:26 And his wife looked behind her, and became a pillar of salt.

Luke 17:33
Whoever seeks to preserve his life will lose it, but whoever loses it will keep it alive.
  Mark 8:35 — teaching parallel: Both contrast saving life through losing it with losing life through trying to save it. Mark expressly names Jesus and the gospel as the reason; Luke's supplied saying omits that clause.
    Mark 8:35 For whoever wants to save his life will lose it; but whoever loses his life for My sake and for the gospel will save it.

Luke 17:34
I tell you, on that night two will be on one bed: one will be taken and the other left.
  Matthew 24:40 — teaching comparison: Both picture two people separated into taken and left. Luke places them on one bed at night; Matthew places them in a field. The settings are not merged.
    Matthew 24:40 Then two men will be in the field: one is taken and one is left.

Luke 17:35
Two women will be grinding together: one will be taken and the other left.”
  Matthew 24:41 — teaching parallel: Both describe two women grinding together, one taken and the other left. Neither verse names the women or specifies a separate destination.
    Matthew 24:41 Two women will be grinding at the mill: one is taken and one is left.

Luke 17:37
And they answered Him, “Where, Lord?” He said to them, “Where the body is, there the eagles will also gather.”
  Matthew 24:28 — teaching parallel: Both connect a body or corpse with gathered eagles. Luke answers a Where question and says body; Matthew says corpse. Eagles is retained rather than replaced by another animal.
    Matthew 24:28 Wherever the corpse is, there the eagles will gather.

Luke 18:1
And He told them a parable about their need to pray always and not lose heart,
  1 Thessalonians 5:17 — teaching parallel: Both urge persistent prayer. Luke introduces a parable about praying always without losing heart; Thessalonians gives the brief command to pray without ceasing.
    1 Thessalonians 5:17 Pray unceasingly.

Luke 18:2
saying, “There was a certain judge in a certain city who neither feared God nor respected any person.
  2 Chronicles 19:5–19:7 — judicial-character contrast: fear of God: The parable's judge lacks fear of God, whereas Jehoshaphat's appointed judges are explicitly told that they judge for the Lord and must act in His fear without injustice, status favoritism or gifts. This is a concrete judicial-character contrast. The parable judge is not identified as one of those officials or accused of an unstated particular bribe.
    2 Chronicles 19:5 He appointed judges in all the fortified cities of Judah, city by city.
    2 Chronicles 19:6 He said to the judges, “Consider what you are doing, because you judge for the Lord, not for a human being, and the matters of judgment are with you.
    2 Chronicles 19:7 Now let the fear of the Lord be upon you. Keep watch and act, because with the Lord our God there is no injustice, admiration of a person’s status, or taking of gifts.”

Luke 18:3
And there was a widow in that city who kept coming to him, saying, ‘Give me justice against my adversary.’
  Isaiah 1:17 — thematic parallel: The widow's petition for justice corresponds to Isaiah's command to vindicate the widow and rescue the wronged. Isaiah gives a duty, not an independent report of this judge or case.
    Isaiah 1:17 Learn to do good, seek judgment, rescue the wronged. Give judgment for the orphan and vindicate the widow.

Luke 18:4
For a time he was unwilling. But afterward he said within himself, ‘Even though I do not fear God or respect any person,
  Luke 11:7 — same-book parable comparison: Both prayer illustrations include an initially unwilling respondent. Here an unjust judge delays; there a friend refuses a nighttime bread request. Their relationships and reasons differ.
    Luke 11:7 and that man will answer from inside, ‘Do not trouble me. The door is already shut, and my children are in bed with me. I cannot get up and give you anything’?

Luke 18:5
yet because this widow troubles me, I will give her justice, lest by coming continually she wear me down.’”
  Luke 11:8 — same-book parable comparison: Both reluctant respondents yield to insistent requests. The judge grants justice to avoid continual trouble; the friend supplies bread because of shameless insistence. Neither figure is simply identified with God.
    Luke 11:8 I tell you, even if he will not get up and give to him because he is his friend, yet because of his shameless insistence he will rise and give him as much as he needs.

Luke 18:6
And the Lord said, “Hear what the unrighteous judge says.
  Luke 18:4–18:5 — internal quotation reference: Jesus directs attention to the unjust judge's stated reasoning: unwillingness gives way to the widow's persistence. This identifies the parable's quoted explanation rather than another judge or occasion.
    Luke 18:4 For a time he was unwilling. But afterward he said within himself, ‘Even though I do not fear God or respect any person,
    Luke 18:5 yet because this widow troubles me, I will give her justice, lest by coming continually she wear me down.’”

Luke 18:7
And will not God bring about justice for His chosen ones who cry to Him day and night? And will He delay over them?
  Sirach 35:17–35:19 — thematic parallel: Both associate persevering prayer with divine justice and deny that God simply delays it. Sirach describes humble prayer continuing until the Most High judges; Luke names chosen people crying day and night.
    Sirach 35:17 A humble person’s prayer has passed through the clouds, and until it draws near, he will certainly not be comforted.
    Sirach 35:18 He will certainly not withdraw until the Most High visits, judges in favor of the righteous, and executes judgment.
    Sirach 35:19 The Lord will certainly not delay or be patient with them,

Luke 18:8
I tell you, He will bring about justice for them speedily. Nevertheless, when the Son of Man comes, will He find faith on the earth?”
  Sirach 35:19–35:23 — thematic parallel: Both promise divine justice without delay. Sirach describes retribution and judgment for God's people; Luke promises speedy justice and adds the question of faith at the Son of Man's coming.
    Sirach 35:19 The Lord will certainly not delay or be patient with them,
    Sirach 35:20 until He crushes the loins of the merciless and repays the nations with retribution,
    Sirach 35:21 until He removes the multitude of the insolent and crushes the scepters of the unjust,
    Sirach 35:22 until He repays a person according to his actions and judges human works according to their thoughts,
    Sirach 35:23 until He judges His people’s case and gladdens them with His mercy.

Luke 18:9
He also told this parable to some who trusted in themselves that they were righteous and regarded the rest as nothing:
  Luke 16:15 — same-book teaching parallel: Both challenge self-justification before others. Luke18 introduces people trusting their own righteousness and despising others; Luke16 contrasts human self-justification with God's knowledge of hearts.
    Luke 16:15 And He said to them, “You are those who justify yourselves before people, but God knows your hearts, because what is exalted among people is an abomination before God.

Luke 18:10
“Two men went up into the temple to pray, one a Pharisee and the other a tax collector.
  1 Kings 8:29–8:30 — scriptural background: temple-directed prayer: The two men's going to the temple to pray has a concrete scriptural setting in Solomon's prayer that God hear His servant and Israel when praying toward this house. This is background for temple-directed prayer and divine hearing/mercy. Kings neither narrates this pair of worshipers nor establishes their Pharisee/tax-collector identities or the contents of their prayers.
    1 Kings 8:29 so that Thine eyes may be open toward this house day and night, toward the place of which Thou saidst, ‘My name shall be there,’ to hear the prayer Thy servant prays toward this place day and night.
    1 Kings 8:30 Hear the supplication of Thy servant and Thy people Israel when they pray toward this place. Hear in Thy dwelling place, in heaven; act and be merciful.

Luke 18:11
The Pharisee stood and prayed these things to himself: ‘God, I thank Thee that I am not like the rest of humanity—plunderers, unrighteous people, adulterers—or even like this tax collector.
  Isaiah 65:5 — religious self-distancing and claimed distinction: Both speeches distance the speaker from other people through a claim to religious or moral distinction. The Pharisee thanks God that he is unlike listed wrongdoers and the tax collector; Isaiah's speakers tell others not to approach because they are clean. Isaiah's actual clean wording is retained. Its surrounding idolatry and food offenses are not attributed to the Pharisee, and it does not quote his prayer.
    Isaiah 65:5 They say, ‘Far away from me! Do not approach me, for I am clean.’ This is smoke of My wrath; fire burns in it all the days.

Luke 18:12
I fast twice a week; I tithe everything I acquire.’
  Matthew 23:23 — teaching comparison: Both discuss meticulous tithing in religious self-presentation or criticism. Luke's speaker claims tithing everything and twice-weekly fasting; Matthew names herb tithes and neglected weightier duties, without that fasting schedule.
    Matthew 23:23 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you tithe mint, dill, and cumin, but have left aside the weightier matters of the Law: justice, mercy, and faithfulness! These you ought to have done without leaving those aside.

Luke 18:13
But the tax collector, standing far away, would not even lift his eyes to heaven, but beat his breast, saying, ‘God, be merciful to me, the sinner.’
  Psalms 50:3 — prayer parallel: Both prayers address God for mercy in acknowledgment of sin. The tax collector calls himself the sinner; the psalm requests mercy and blotting out a lawless deed.
    Psalms 50:3 Have mercy on me, God, according to Thy great mercy, and according to the abundance of Thy compassions blot out my lawless deed.

Luke 18:14
I tell you, this man went down to his house justified rather than the other. For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, but the one who humbles himself will be exalted.”
  Matthew 23:12 — teaching parallel: Both declare humiliation for self-exaltation and exaltation for self-humbling. Luke additionally gives the verdict that the tax collector, rather than the other man, went home justified.
    Matthew 23:12 Whoever exalts himself will be humbled, and whoever humbles himself will be exalted.

Luke 18:15
And they were bringing even infants to Him so that He might touch them. But when the disciples saw it, they rebuked them.
  Mark 10:13 — narrative parallel: Both describe children being brought for Jesus' touch and the disciples' rebukes. Luke explicitly includes infants; Mark uses children.
    Mark 10:13 People brought children to Him so that He might touch them, but the disciples rebuked them.

Luke 18:16
But Jesus called the children to Himself, saying, “Let the children come to Me, and do not hinder them, for the kingdom of God belongs to such as these.
  Mark 10:14 — teaching parallel: Both command allowing children to come without hindrance because God's kingdom belongs to such. Mark adds Jesus' indignation; Luke describes His calling them.
    Mark 10:14 When Jesus saw it, He was indignant and said to them, “Let the children come to Me; do not stop them, for the kingdom of God belongs to such as these.

Luke 18:17
Amen, I tell you: whoever does not receive the kingdom of God as a child will certainly not enter it.”
  Mark 10:15 — teaching parallel: Both declare that receiving God's kingdom as a child is necessary for entering it.
    Mark 10:15 Truly I tell you, whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a child will certainly not enter it.”

Luke 18:18
And a certain ruler questioned Him, saying, “Good Teacher, what must I do to inherit eternal life?”
  Mark 10:17 — narrative parallel: Both record the Good Teacher question about inheriting eternal life. Luke identifies a ruler; Mark describes a man running and kneeling without that title.
    Mark 10:17 As He was setting out on the road, a man ran up, knelt before Him, and asked Him, “Good Teacher, what should I do to inherit eternal life?”

Luke 18:19
Jesus said to him, “Why do you call Me good? No one is good except One—God.
  Mark 10:18 — teaching parallel: Both question being called good and state that only God is good. No further identification or denial is added to the supplied words.
    Mark 10:18 Jesus said to him, “Why do you call Me good? No one is good except One—God.

Luke 18:20
You know the commandments: ‘Do not commit adultery; do not murder; do not steal; do not bear false witness; honor your father and mother.’”
  Mark 10:19 — teaching parallel: Both list commandments on adultery, murder, theft, false witness and honoring parents. The order differs, and Mark additionally includes do not defraud.
    Mark 10:19 You know the commandments: ‘Do not murder; do not commit adultery; do not steal; do not bear false witness; do not defraud; honor your father and mother.’”

Luke 18:21
And he said, “All these things I have kept from my youth.”
  Mark 10:20 — narrative parallel: Both have the questioner claim observance of these commandments since youth. Mark additionally addresses Jesus as Teacher.
    Mark 10:20 He said to Him, “Teacher, I have kept all these from my youth.”

Luke 18:22
When Jesus heard this, He said to him, “You still lack one thing: sell everything you have and distribute it to the poor, and you will have treasure in the heavens; and come, follow Me.”
  Mark 10:21 — teaching parallel: Both name one remaining lack and command sale, distribution to the poor and following Jesus, promising heavenly treasure. Mark additionally describes Jesus looking at and loving the man.
    Mark 10:21 Jesus looked at him, loved him, and said to him, “You lack one thing. Go, sell what you have and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven. Then come, follow Me.”

Luke 18:23
But when he heard these things, he became deeply grieved, for he was exceedingly rich.
  Mark 10:22 — narrative parallel: Both describe grief at the instruction because of great wealth. Mark also narrates departure; Luke's supplied verse does not say he left.
    Mark 10:22 But his face fell at the word, and he went away grieved, for he had many possessions.

Luke 18:24
Seeing that he had become deeply grieved, Jesus said, “How difficult it is for those who have wealth to enter the kingdom of God!
  Mark 10:23 — teaching parallel: Both declare difficulty for wealthy people entering God's kingdom. Luke relates it to the man's grief; Mark describes Jesus looking around at His disciples.
    Mark 10:23 Jesus looked around and said to His disciples, “How hard it will be for those who have wealth to enter the kingdom of God!”

Luke 18:25
For it is easier for a camel to enter through the eye of a needle than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of God.”
  Mark 10:25 — teaching parallel: Both compare a camel passing through a needle's eye with a rich person's entering God's kingdom. No alternative gate or altered animal is supplied.
    Mark 10:25 It is easier for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle than for a rich person to enter the kingdom of God.”

Luke 18:26
And those who heard said, “Then who can be saved?”
  Mark 10:26 — narrative parallel: Both report the hearers asking who can be saved. Mark describes heightened astonishment; Luke simply identifies those who heard.
    Mark 10:26 They were exceedingly astonished and said to one another, “Then who can be saved?”

Luke 18:27
But He said, “The things impossible with people are possible with God.”
  Mark 10:27 — teaching parallel: Both contrast human impossibility with divine possibility. Mark explicitly says all things are possible with God; Luke says the things impossible with people are possible with God.
    Mark 10:27 Jesus looked at them and said, “With humans it is impossible, but not with God; for all things are possible with God.”

Luke 18:28
And Peter said, “Behold, we have left what was our own and followed Thee.”
  Mark 10:28 — narrative parallel: Both have Peter speak of leaving possessions or everything and following Jesus. Luke says what was our own; Mark says everything.
    Mark 10:28 Peter began saying to Him, “Look, we have left everything and followed Thee.”

Luke 18:29
And He said to them, “Amen, I tell you: there is no one who has left house or wife or brothers or parents or children for the sake of the kingdom of God
  Mark 10:29 — teaching parallel: Both promise recompense for leaving household and family for Jesus' mission. Luke names wife and God's kingdom; Mark names fields and Jesus and the gospel. The lists remain distinct.
    Mark 10:29 Jesus said, “Truly I tell you, there is no one who has left house, brothers, sisters, mother, father, children, or fields for My sake and for the sake of the gospel,

Luke 18:30
who will not certainly receive many times as much in this time, and in the age to come eternal life.”
  Mark 10:30 — teaching parallel: Both promise present recompense and eternal life in the coming age. Mark specifies a hundredfold, family and fields, and persecutions; Luke says many times as much without those additions.
    Mark 10:30 who will not receive a hundredfold now, in this time—houses, brothers, sisters, mothers, children, and fields, with persecutions—and in the coming age, eternal life.

Luke 18:31
Taking the Twelve aside, He said to them, “Behold, we are going up to Jerusalem, and everything written through the prophets will be accomplished for the Son of Man.
  Mark 10:32 — passion-prediction parallel: Both describe the Jerusalem journey and taking the Twelve aside before a prediction. Luke expressly identifies fulfillment of prophetic writings; Mark does not repeat that formulation.
    Mark 10:32 They were on the road going up to Jerusalem. Jesus was going ahead of them, and they were astounded, while those following were afraid. Taking the Twelve aside again, He began telling them what was going to happen to Him:

Luke 18:32
For He will be handed over to the nations and will be mocked and insulted and spat upon.
  Mark 10:33–10:34 — passion-prediction parallel: Both predict handing Jesus to the nations or Gentiles, mocking and spitting. Luke also says insulted; Mark names the preceding priestly condemnation and continues with flogging, death and rising.
    Mark 10:33 “Look, we are going up to Jerusalem, and the Son of Man will be handed over to the chief priests and the scribes. They will condemn Him to death and hand Him over to the Gentiles.
    Mark 10:34 They will mock Him, spit on Him, flog Him, and kill Him, and after three days He will rise.”

Luke 18:33
And after flogging Him, they will kill Him, and on the third day He will rise.”
  Mark 10:34 — passion-prediction parallel: Both predict flogging, killing and resurrection. Luke says on the third day; Mark says after three days. Those formulations are preserved.
    Mark 10:34 They will mock Him, spit on Him, flog Him, and kill Him, and after three days He will rise.”

Luke 18:34
But they understood none of these things. This saying was hidden from them, and they did not understand what was being said.
  Luke 9:45 — same-book narrative parallel: Both state that disciples did not understand the saying and that it was hidden or veiled. Luke9 additionally describes their fear of asking, absent from this unit.
    Luke 9:45 But they did not understand this saying; it was veiled from them so that they might not perceive it, and they were afraid to ask Him about it.

Luke 18:35
And it happened that as He drew near to Jericho, a certain blind man was sitting beside the road, begging.
  Mark 10:46 — narrative parallel: Both introduce a blind beggar at Jericho. Luke describes approaching the city; Mark describes departing and names Bartimaeus. No two-city explanation or identification of Luke's unnamed man is imposed.
    Mark 10:46 They came to Jericho. As He was leaving Jericho with His disciples and a considerable crowd, Bartimaeus, the son of Timaeus, a blind beggar, was sitting beside the road.

Luke 18:36
Hearing a crowd passing through, he asked what this might be.
  Mark 10:47–10:48 — narrative parallel: Luke's blind man asks about the passing crowd and is told next that Jesus the Nazarene is passing; Mark narrates the blind man hearing that it is Jesus the Nazarene and crying for mercy. This compares the auditory information preceding the appeal while retaining approaching versus leaving Jericho and not importing Luke's question into Mark or Mark's personal name into Luke.
    Mark 10:47 Hearing that it was Jesus the Nazarene, he began crying out, “Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on me!”
    Mark 10:48 Many rebuked him so that he would be silent, but he cried out much more, “Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Luke 18:37
They told him, “Jesus the Nazarene is passing by.”
