  Mark 10:47 — narrative parallel: Both identify the passing Jesus as the Nazarene. Luke reports others telling the beggar; Mark describes his hearing that identification before crying out.
    Mark 10:47 Hearing that it was Jesus the Nazarene, he began crying out, “Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Luke 18:38
And he cried out, saying, “Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on me!”
  Mark 10:47 — narrative parallel: Both give the blind beggar's appeal to Jesus, Son of David, for mercy. Mark introduces his cry after hearing Jesus was passing.
    Mark 10:47 Hearing that it was Jesus the Nazarene, he began crying out, “Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Luke 18:39
Those going ahead rebuked him so that he would be silent, but he cried out much more, “Son of David, have mercy on me!”
  Mark 10:48 — narrative parallel: Both describe rebukes intended to silence the beggar and his louder repetition of the Son-of-David mercy appeal.
    Mark 10:48 Many rebuked him so that he would be silent, but he cried out much more, “Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Luke 18:40
Jesus stopped and commanded that he be brought to Him. When he drew near, He asked him,
  Mark 10:49 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus stop and arrange the man's approach. Luke commands him to be brought; Mark commands a call and records the crowd's encouragement.
    Mark 10:49 Jesus stopped and said, “Call him.” They called the blind man, saying to him, “Take courage! Get up; He is calling you.”

Luke 18:41
“What do you want Me to do for you?” And he said, “Lord, that I may receive my sight.”
  Mark 10:51 — narrative parallel: Both give Jesus' question about the desired action and the man's request for sight. Luke's address is Lord; Mark's is Rabbouni.
    Mark 10:51 Jesus answered him, “What do you want Me to do for you?” The blind man said to Him, “Rabbouni, that I may see again.”

Luke 18:42
And Jesus said to him, “Receive your sight; your faith has saved you.”
  Mark 10:52 — narrative parallel: Both declare that faith has saved the man. Luke commands restored sight; Mark says go, followed by the narrated recovery.
    Mark 10:52 Jesus said to him, “Go; your faith has saved you.” Immediately he saw again and followed Him on the road.

Luke 18:43
And at once he received his sight and followed Him, glorifying God. And all the people, seeing this, gave praise to God.
  Mark 10:52 — narrative parallel: Both narrate immediate sight and following Jesus. Luke adds the man's glorifying God and the people's praise, which Mark does not repeat.
    Mark 10:52 Jesus said to him, “Go; your faith has saved you.” Immediately he saw again and followed Him on the road.

Luke 19:1
And He entered Jericho and was passing through it.
  Mark 10:46 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus at Jericho. Luke narrates entry after the blind man's healing; Mark narrates arrival and departure before that healing. The differing sequences are not harmonized.
    Mark 10:46 They came to Jericho. As He was leaving Jericho with His disciples and a considerable crowd, Bartimaeus, the son of Timaeus, a blind beggar, was sitting beside the road.

Luke 19:2
And behold, there was a man called Zacchaeus, who was a chief tax collector and was rich.
  Luke 18:23–18:27 — same-book narrative contrast: Luke introduces another wealthy encounter after the rich ruler's grief and Jesus' teaching on wealth, salvation and divine possibility. Zacchaeus's response and household salvation unfold in19:8-9. The named collector is not identified with the unnamed ruler, whose departure Luke does not narrate.
    Luke 18:23 But when he heard these things, he became deeply grieved, for he was exceedingly rich.
    Luke 18:24 Seeing that he had become deeply grieved, Jesus said, “How difficult it is for those who have wealth to enter the kingdom of God!
    Luke 18:25 For it is easier for a camel to enter through the eye of a needle than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of God.”
    Luke 18:26 And those who heard said, “Then who can be saved?”
    Luke 18:27 But He said, “The things impossible with people are possible with God.”

Luke 19:3
He was seeking to see who Jesus was, but could not because of the crowd, since he was short in stature.
  Luke 5:18–5:19 — internal crowd-impeded access pattern: Both stories explicitly say that a crowd prevents access to Jesus. Zacchaeus cannot see because of the crowd and his short stature; the bearers cannot bring their paralyzed companion before Jesus because of the crowd. The impediment pattern is concrete, while the people, physical conditions, desired sight/healing and means of access remain different.
    Luke 5:18 And behold, men were carrying on a bed a man who was paralyzed. They sought to bring him in and place him before Him.
    Luke 5:19 Finding no way to bring him in because of the crowd, they went up onto the roof and lowered him through the tiles with his little bed into their midst, before Jesus.

Luke 19:4
So he ran ahead and climbed a sycamore fig tree to see Him, because He was about to pass that way.
  Luke 5:19 — internal comparison: elevated access past a crowd: Both crowd-impeded encounters use an elevated route to reach a position relative to Jesus. Zacchaeus climbs a sycamore fig tree to see Him; the bearers go onto a roof and lower their companion through its tiles before Him. A tree is not a roof, seeing is not the stated request for healing, and the people and occasions are not identified.
    Luke 5:19 Finding no way to bring him in because of the crowd, they went up onto the roof and lowered him through the tiles with his little bed into their midst, before Jesus.

Luke 19:5
When Jesus came to the place, He looked up and said to him, “Zacchaeus, hurry and come down, for today I must stay in your house.”
  Luke 5:27–5:30 — internal tax-collector call and hospitality pattern: Both Lukan narratives join Jesus' personal call of a tax collector with hospitality in that man's house. Zacchaeus is called down from the tree for a stay today; Levi is called from the booth to follow and gives Jesus a reception at home. The directions, timing and men differ; Levi's call is not made an independent record of Jesus' words to Zacchaeus.
    Luke 5:27 After this He went out and saw a tax collector named Levi sitting at the tax booth, and said to him, “Follow Me.”
    Luke 5:28 Leaving everything, he rose and followed Him.
    Luke 5:29 Levi gave a great reception for Him in his house. A large crowd of tax collectors and others was reclining with them.
    Luke 5:30 The Pharisees and their scribes grumbled to His disciples, saying, “Why do you eat and drink with tax collectors and sinners?”

Luke 19:6
And he hurried down and welcomed Him, rejoicing.
  Luke 5:29 — same-book hospitality parallel: Both tax-collector scenes involve welcoming Jesus. Zacchaeus comes down rejoicing; Levi gives a great reception at home. The men and the details of their responses remain distinct.
    Luke 5:29 Levi gave a great reception for Him in his house. A large crowd of tax collectors and others was reclining with them.

Luke 19:7
When they saw this, they all grumbled, saying, “He has gone in to lodge with a sinful man.”
  Luke 15:2 — same-book narrative parallel: Both describe grumbling over Jesus' hospitality with sinners. Here people object to lodging with Zacchaeus; Luke15 names Pharisees and scribes objecting to welcome and eating.
    Luke 15:2 And both the Pharisees and the scribes grumbled, saying, “This man welcomes sinners and eats with them.”

Luke 19:8
But Zacchaeus stood and said to the Lord, “Behold, Lord, I give half of my possessions to the poor, and if I have extorted anything from anyone, I repay it fourfold.”
  Luke 3:12–3:13 — same-book ethical comparison: Both concern tax collectors and unjust gain. John forbids collecting beyond the prescribed amount; Zacchaeus says he gives half his goods to the poor and repays extortion fourfold. John does not prescribe those amounts.
    Luke 3:12 Tax collectors also came to be baptized and said to him, “Teacher, what should we do?”
    Luke 3:13 He said to them, “Collect nothing beyond what has been appointed for you.”

Luke 19:9
Jesus said to him, “Today salvation has come to this house, because he too is a son of Abraham.
  Luke 13:16 — same-book teaching comparison: Both affirm Abrahamic identity in connection with deliverance. Zacchaeus is called a son of Abraham as salvation reaches his house; the healed woman is Abraham's daughter released from Satan's bond.
    Luke 13:16 And this woman, who is a daughter of Abraham, whom Satan bound for, behold, eighteen years—ought she not to have been released from this bond on the Sabbath day?”

Luke 19:10
For the Son of Man came to seek and to save what was lost.”
  Luke 15:4–15:7 — same-book teaching link: Jesus' stated mission to seek and save the lost corresponds to the earlier lost-sheep search and its application to joy over repentance. Zacchaeus is not assigned an exclusive identity as the parable's sheep.
    Luke 15:4 “What man among you, having a hundred sheep and losing one of them, does not leave the ninety-nine in the wilderness and go after the lost one until he finds it?
    Luke 15:5 And when he finds it, he lays it on his shoulders, rejoicing.
    Luke 15:6 When he comes home, he calls his friends and neighbors together and says to them, ‘Rejoice with me, because I found my sheep that was lost!’
    Luke 15:7 I tell you, in the same way there will be more joy in heaven over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous people who have no need of repentance.

Luke 19:11
While they were hearing these things, He went on to tell a parable, because He was near Jerusalem and they thought that the kingdom of God was about to appear immediately.
  Luke 17:20 — same-book kingdom-timing comparison: Both address expectations about the kingdom's arrival. Luke19 explains an expectation of immediate appearance near Jerusalem; Luke17 records Pharisees' timing question and Jesus' reply about observable coming. The audiences and answers differ.
    Luke 17:20 When the Pharisees asked Him when the kingdom of God was coming, He answered them, “The kingdom of God does not come in a way that can be observed,

Luke 19:12
So He said, “A certain nobleman went to a distant country to receive a kingdom for himself and return.
  Matthew 25:14 — parable parallel: Both introduce an absent master before entrusted servants' activity. Luke's nobleman travels to receive a kingdom and return; Matthew's traveler entrusts possessions without that royal mission.
    Matthew 25:14 For it is like a man traveling abroad, who called his own slaves and handed over his belongings to them.

Luke 19:13
He called ten of his own slaves, gave them ten minas, and said to them, ‘Do business until I come.’
  Matthew 25:14–25:15 — parable parallel: Both entrust money to slaves before departure. Luke gives ten minas to ten slaves with a business command; Matthew distributes five, two and one talents according to ability. No currency conversion or identical allocation is inferred.
    Matthew 25:14 For it is like a man traveling abroad, who called his own slaves and handed over his belongings to them.
    Matthew 25:15 To one he gave five talents, to another two, to another one, each according to his own ability, and traveled abroad. Immediately

Luke 19:14
But his citizens hated him and sent a delegation after him, saying, ‘We do not want this man to reign over us.’
  Luke 19:27 — internal narrative callback: The later command against enemies who refused his reign recalls the citizens' rejecting delegation. The command is not a narration of an execution already carried out, and citizens are not automatically equated with the entrusted slaves.
    Luke 19:27 But those enemies of mine who did not want me to reign over them—bring them here and slaughter them before me.’”

Luke 19:15
And it happened that when he returned, having received the kingdom, he ordered those slaves to whom he had given the money to be called to him, so that he might know what business they had done.
  Matthew 25:19 — parable parallel: Both describe a returned master calling entrusted servants to account. Luke expressly says he received the kingdom and asks about business; Matthew specifies a long absence before settling accounts.
    Matthew 25:19 After a long time, the master of those slaves came and settled accounts with them.

Luke 19:16
The first came, saying, ‘Master, your mina has gained ten minas.’
  Matthew 25:20 — parable parallel: Both servants report profitable use of entrusted money. Luke's mina gains ten minas; Matthew's five talents gain five more. The currencies, initial amounts and ratios differ.
    Matthew 25:20 The one who received five talents came and brought another five talents, saying, ‘Master, you handed over five talents to me. Look, I gained another five talents.’

Luke 19:17
And he said to him, ‘Well done, good slave! Because you have been faithful in a very small thing, have authority over ten cities.’
  Matthew 25:21 — parable parallel: Both praise a good servant's faithfulness in little and grant greater responsibility. Luke specifies ten cities; Matthew promises appointment over many things and entry into the master's joy.
    Matthew 25:21 His master said to him, ‘Well done, good and faithful slave. You were faithful over a few things; I will appoint you over many. Enter into your master’s joy.’

Luke 19:18
The second came, saying, ‘Your mina, master, has made five minas.’
  Matthew 25:22 — parable parallel: Both second servants report gain. Luke reports one mina making five; Matthew reports two talents gaining two more. These are not identical investments or returns.
    Matthew 25:22 The one with two talents also came and said, ‘Master, you handed over two talents to me. Look, I gained another two talents.’

Luke 19:19
And he said to this one also, ‘You too shall be over five cities.’
  Matthew 25:23 — parable parallel: Both reward the second profitable servant with responsibility. Luke grants five cities; Matthew repeats praise and appointment over many things, with the master's joy.
    Matthew 25:23 His master said to him, ‘Well done, good and faithful slave. You were faithful over a few things; I will appoint you over many. Enter into your master’s joy.’

Luke 19:20
And the other came, saying, ‘Master, behold your mina, which I kept laid away in a cloth.
  Matthew 25:25 — parable parallel: Both servants return money kept unused. Luke's mina was laid away in a cloth; Matthew's talent was hidden in the ground, with fear stated in that verse.
    Matthew 25:25 I was afraid, so I went away and hid your talent in the ground. Look, you have what is yours.’

Luke 19:21
For I was afraid of you, because you are a severe man: you take up what you did not lay down and reap what you did not sow.’
  Matthew 25:24–25:25 — parable parallel: Both fearful servants describe the master as reaping without sowing. Luke says severe and taking what was not laid down; Matthew says hard and gathering where not scattered. These remain the servants' descriptions.
    Matthew 25:24 The one who received one talent also came and said, ‘Master, I knew you to be a hard man, reaping where you did not sow and gathering from where you did not scatter.
    Matthew 25:25 I was afraid, so I went away and hid your talent in the ground. Look, you have what is yours.’

Luke 19:22
He said to him, ‘From your own mouth I will judge you, evil slave. You knew that I am a severe man, taking up what I did not lay down and reaping what I did not sow?
  Matthew 25:26 — parable parallel: Both masters answer the servant through his own depiction of their severity. Luke explicitly judges from his mouth and calls him evil; Matthew calls him evil and idle. The accusation is not independently verified as the master's character.
    Matthew 25:26 His master answered him, ‘Evil and idle slave! You knew that I reap where I did not sow and gather from where I did not scatter?

Luke 19:23
Then why did you not put my money on deposit? And when I came, I would have collected it with interest.’
  Matthew 25:27 — parable parallel: Both ask why money was not deposited so it could be received with interest on return. The comparison concerns the parables' rebukes, not a separate financial recommendation.
    Matthew 25:27 Then you ought to have deposited my silver with the bankers, and when I came I would have recovered what is mine with interest.

Luke 19:24
And he said to those standing by, ‘Take the mina from him and give it to the one who has the ten minas.’
  Matthew 25:28 — parable parallel: Both order the unused money taken and given to the servant with ten. Luke names a mina and ten minas; Matthew names a talent and ten talents. The units are preserved.
    Matthew 25:28 So take the talent from him and give it to the one who has the ten talents.

Luke 19:25
And they said to him, ‘Master, he has ten minas!’
  Matthew 25:28 — comparison of the recipient's prior holding: Both related parables explicitly identify the transfer's beneficiary as already having ten units. Luke's bystanders say he has ten minas; Matthew's command names the one with ten talents. Talents and minas and their distributions differ, and Matthew does not contain the bystanders' interjection. This compares the stated prior holding, not the presence of an identical objection in both stories.
    Matthew 25:28 So take the talent from him and give it to the one who has the ten talents.

Luke 19:26
‘I tell you that to everyone who has, more will be given; but from the one who does not have, even what he has will be taken away.
  Matthew 25:29 — teaching parallel: Both state that one who has receives more while one who lacks loses even what he has. Matthew adds abundance; Luke introduces the saying with I tell you.
    Matthew 25:29 For to everyone who has, more will be given and he will have abundance; but from the one who does not have, even what he has will be taken.

Luke 19:27
But those enemies of mine who did not want me to reign over them—bring them here and slaughter them before me.’”
  Luke 19:14 — internal narrative callback: The command concerning enemies who rejected the king's reign explicitly recalls the earlier delegation's refusal. The text reports the story character's command rather than narrating its execution, and does not identify every entrusted slave with the rejecting citizens.
    Luke 19:14 But his citizens hated him and sent a delegation after him, saying, ‘We do not want this man to reign over us.’

Luke 19:28
And after saying these things, He went on ahead, going up to Jerusalem.
  Mark 10:32 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus going ahead on the ascent toward Jerusalem. Mark additionally describes followers' amazement and fear and introduces a prediction; Luke places this movement after the mina parable.
    Mark 10:32 They were on the road going up to Jerusalem. Jesus was going ahead of them, and they were astounded, while those following were afraid. Taking the Twelve aside again, He began telling them what was going to happen to Him:

Luke 19:29
And it happened that as He drew near Bethphage and Bethany, at the mount called Olivet, He sent two of the disciples,
  Mark 11:1 — narrative parallel: Both locate the approach near Bethphage, Bethany and the Mount of Olives and describe sending two disciples. Luke calls the mount Olivet; Mark names it in its own supplied form.
    Mark 11:1 As they drew near Jerusalem, to Bethphage and Bethany at the Mount of Olives, He sent two of His disciples

Luke 19:30
saying, “Go into the village opposite. As you enter it, you will find a colt tied, on which no person has ever sat. Untie it and bring it.
  Mark 11:2 — narrative parallel: Both direct the disciples to a nearby village, where an unridden colt is tied, and command its untying and bringing. Luke says on entering; Mark says immediately on entering.
    Mark 11:2 and said to them, “Go into the village opposite you. Immediately on entering it, you will find a colt tied, on which no human has ever sat. Untie it and bring it.

Luke 19:31
And if anyone asks you, ‘Why are you untying it?’ you shall say this: ‘The Lord has need of it.’”
  Mark 11:3 — narrative parallel: Both prescribe answering a challenge by saying the Lord needs the colt. Mark additionally mentions sending it back immediately; Luke's supplied instruction does not.
    Mark 11:3 If anyone says to you, ‘Why are you doing this?’ say, ‘The Lord has need of it, and He will send it back here immediately.’”

Luke 19:32
Those who were sent went away and found it just as He had told them.
  Mark 11:4 — narrative parallel: Both narrate the disciples finding the colt after Jesus' instruction. Luke says just as told; Mark specifies a door outside on the street and begins the untying.
    Mark 11:4 They went away and found a colt tied at a door outside on the street, and untied it.

Luke 19:33
As they were untying the colt, its owners said to them, “Why are you untying the colt?”
  Mark 11:5 — narrative parallel: Both describe an objection while the colt is untied. Luke identifies the questioners as its owners; Mark calls them people standing there.
    Mark 11:5 Some of those standing there said to them, “What are you doing, untying the colt?”

Luke 19:34
And they said, “The Lord has need of it.”
  Mark 11:6 — narrative parallel: Both describe answering as Jesus instructed. Luke repeats the Lord's need; Mark summarizes the instructed answer and says the bystanders let them go.
    Mark 11:6 They answered them just as Jesus had said, and the people let them go.

Luke 19:35
They brought it to Jesus, threw their garments on the colt, and set Jesus upon it.
  Mark 11:7 — narrative parallel: Both bring the colt to Jesus and put garments on it for Him. Luke says they set Jesus on it; Mark says He sat on it.
    Mark 11:7 They brought the colt to Jesus, threw their garments on it, and He sat on it.

Luke 19:36
As He went, they spread their own garments on the road.
  Mark 11:8 — narrative parallel: Both describe garments spread on the road. Mark additionally mentions leafy branches cut from fields; Luke does not supply them here.
    Mark 11:8 Many spread their garments on the road; others spread leafy branches they had cut from the fields.

Luke 19:37
As He was already drawing near the descent of the Mount of Olives, the whole multitude of the disciples began to rejoice and praise God with a loud voice for all the mighty works they had seen,
  Mark 11:9–11:10 — narrative parallel: Both describe acclaim during Jesus' approach. Luke specifies disciples descending the mount and praising for seen mighty works; Mark describes those ahead and behind acclaiming the coming one and Davidic kingdom.
    Mark 11:9 Those going ahead and those following cried out, “Hosanna! Blessed is the One who comes in the name of the Lord!
    Mark 11:10 Blessed is the coming kingdom of our father David! Hosanna in the highest!”

Luke 19:38
saying, “Blessed is the King who comes in the name of the Lord! Peace in heaven, and glory in the highest!”
  Psalms 117:26 — quotation: Luke's blessing of the King coming in the Lord's name recalls the psalm's blessing of the one coming in His name. Luke adds King, heavenly peace and highest glory; the psalm continues with blessing from the Lord's house.
    Psalms 117:26 Blessed is the one who comes in the name of the Lord. We have blessed you from the house of the Lord.
  Luke 2:14 — same-book praise comparison: Both acclamations join peace with glory in the highest. The infancy angels say peace on earth; the entering disciples say peace in heaven. The speakers and locations of peace are distinguished.
    Luke 2:14 “Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace among people of His favour.”

Luke 19:39
And some of the Pharisees from the crowd said to Him, “Teacher, rebuke Thy disciples.”
  Matthew 21:15–21:16 — narrative comparison: Both describe objections to praise of Jesus. Luke names Pharisees demanding rebuke of disciples during the approach; Matthew names priests and scribes reacting to children's temple cries. The groups, settings and replies differ.
    Matthew 21:15 But when the chief priests and scribes saw the wonders He did and the children crying in the temple, “Hosanna to the Son of David,” they became indignant
    Matthew 21:16 and said to Him, “Dost Thou hear what these are saying?” Jesus said to them, “Yes. Have you never read, ‘Out of the mouth of infants and nursing children Thou didst prepare praise’?”

Luke 19:40
And He answered, “I tell you, if these fall silent, the stones will cry out.”
  Habakkuk 2:11 — imagery comparison: Both personify a stone crying out. Habakkuk's wall-stone witnesses within a woe against unjust gain; Jesus says stones will cry if disciples fall silent. Their occasions and purposes are not identified.
    Habakkuk 2:11 For a stone will cry from a wall, and a beetle from wood will utter these things.

Luke 19:41
As He drew near and saw the city, He wept over it,
  Matthew 23:37 — thematic parallel: Both portray Jesus grieving over Jerusalem. Luke narrates His weeping at the city's sight; Matthew gives His lament over rejected gathering and killed prophets without independently reporting these tears.
    Matthew 23:37 Jerusalem, Jerusalem, who kills the prophets and stones those sent to her! How often I wished to gather your children as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings, and you were unwilling!

Luke 19:42
saying, “If only you too had known on this day the things that lead to peace—but now they have been hidden from your eyes.
  Isaiah 48:18 — thematic parallel: Both lament missed peace associated with failure to heed. Isaiah pictures peace like a river if commands had been heard; Jesus speaks of the city's failure to know peace now hidden. The occasions and imagery differ.
    Isaiah 48:18 If you had listened to My commands, your peace would have become like a river and your righteousness like a sea wave.

Luke 19:43
For days will come upon you when your enemies will build a siege barrier around you and surround you and hem you in on every side.
  Isaiah 29:3 — allusion: Both describe a city surrounded by siege works. Isaiah's divine speaker promises encirclement, a palisade and towers; Luke names enemies hemming the city in with a siege barrier. The speakers and exact details differ.
    Isaiah 29:3 I will surround you like David, set a palisade around you and set towers around you,

Luke 19:44
They will dash you to the ground, and your children within you, and will not leave stone upon stone within you, because you did not know the time of your visitation.”
  Luke 21:6 — same-book prediction comparison: Both predict that stone will not remain upon stone. Luke19 concerns the city, its children and missed visitation; Luke21 addresses the temple buildings. The scopes are not silently equated.
    Luke 21:6 “As for these things you see, days will come in which stone will not be left upon stone without being torn down.”

Luke 19:45
And He entered the temple and began to drive out those who were selling,
  Mark 11:15 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus driving sellers from the temple. Mark also mentions buyers, money-changers' tables and dove-sellers' seats; Luke's present unit does not.
    Mark 11:15 They came to Jerusalem. He entered the temple and began driving out those selling and those buying in the temple. He overturned the tables of the money changers and the seats of those selling doves,

Luke 19:46
saying to them, “It is written, ‘And My house shall be a house of prayer,’ but you have made it a den of robbers.”
  Isaiah 56:7 — quotation: Both call God's house a house of prayer. Isaiah adds for all nations and describes offerings; Luke's supplied citation does not repeat that phrase or the entire verse.
    Isaiah 56:7 I will bring them into My holy mountain and make them rejoice in My house of prayer. Their whole burnt offerings and sacrifices will be acceptable on My altar, for My house will be called a house of prayer for all nations,”
  Jeremiah 7:11 — quotation: Jesus' den-of-robbers rebuke recalls Jeremiah's question about the house called by God's name becoming a robbers' cave. Jeremiah supplies the temple critique, not an account of this later expulsion.
    Jeremiah 7:11 is My house, over which My name has been called there, a robbers’ cave before you? And behold, I have seen, says the Lord.

Luke 19:47
And He was teaching daily in the temple. But the chief priests and the scribes, and the leading men of the people, were seeking to destroy Him.
  Mark 11:18 — narrative parallel: Both describe chief priests and scribes seeking to destroy Jesus in the temple-teaching context. Luke adds daily teaching and leading men; Mark explains the leaders' fear and the crowd's amazement.
    Mark 11:18 The chief priests and the scribes heard, and sought how to destroy Him; for they feared Him, because the whole crowd was astonished at His teaching.

Luke 19:48
Yet they could not find what to do, for all the people hung on His words as they listened.
  Mark 11:18 — narrative parallel: Both connect the leaders' difficulty acting with the crowd's response to Jesus' teaching. Luke says people hung on His words; Mark says the crowd was astonished, causing the leaders' fear.
    Mark 11:18 The chief priests and the scribes heard, and sought how to destroy Him; for they feared Him, because the whole crowd was astonished at His teaching.

Luke 20:1
And it happened on one of the days, as He was teaching the people in the temple and proclaiming good news, that the chief priests and the scribes came up with the elders
  Mark 11:27 — narrative parallel: Both introduce chief priests, scribes and elders approaching Jesus in the temple. Luke describes teaching and good-news proclamation; Mark describes His walking there.
    Mark 11:27 They came again to Jerusalem. As He walked in the temple, the chief priests, the scribes, and the elders came to Him

Luke 20:2
and said to Him, “Tell us by what authority Thou doest these things, or who it is that gave Thee this authority.”
  Mark 11:28 — narrative parallel: Both question Jesus' authority and who gave it to Him. Neither verse itself defines the challengers' demanded authorization.
    Mark 11:28 and said to Him, “By what authority art Thou doing these things? Or who gave Thee this authority to do them?”

Luke 20:3
He answered them, “I too will ask you a question; tell Me:
  Mark 11:29 — narrative parallel: Both answer the authority challenge with Jesus proposing a question. Mark explicitly says their answer will precede His explanation; Luke's shorter introduction continues into the baptism question.
    Mark 11:29 Jesus said to them, “I will ask you one question. Answer Me, and I will tell you by what authority I do these things.

Luke 20:4
was John’s baptism from heaven or from people?”
  Mark 11:30 — narrative parallel: Both ask whether John's baptism came from heaven or from people. Mark adds answer Me.
    Mark 11:30 Was John’s baptism from heaven or from humans? Answer Me.”

Luke 20:5
And they reasoned among themselves, saying, “If we say, ‘From heaven,’ He will say, ‘Why did you not believe him?’
  Mark 11:31 — narrative parallel: Both give the leaders' reasoning that admitting heavenly origin invites Jesus' question why they did not believe John.
    Mark 11:31 They reasoned with one another, saying, “If we say, ‘From heaven,’ He will say, ‘Why then did you not believe him?’

Luke 20:6
But if we say, ‘From people,’ all the people will stone us, for they are convinced that John was a prophet.”
  Mark 11:32 — narrative parallel: Both fear the public response to denying John's prophetic status. Luke expressly predicts stoning; Mark describes fear of the crowd because all considered John truly a prophet.
    Mark 11:32 But should we say, ‘From humans’?” They feared the crowd, for everyone held that John truly was a prophet.

Luke 20:7
So they answered that they did not know where it was from.
  Mark 11:33 — narrative parallel: Both report the leaders answering that they do not know. Luke explicitly names uncertainty about the baptism's source; Mark also gives Jesus' ensuing refusal.
    Mark 11:33 They answered Jesus, “We do not know.” Jesus said to them, “Neither do I tell you by what authority I do these things.”

Luke 20:8
And Jesus said to them, “Neither do I tell you by what authority I do these things.”
  Mark 11:33 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus refuse to disclose His authority after their nonanswer. Mark combines their answer and His refusal in one unit.
    Mark 11:33 They answered Jesus, “We do not know.” Jesus said to them, “Neither do I tell you by what authority I do these things.”

Luke 20:9
And He began to tell the people this parable: “A certain man planted a vineyard, leased it to tenant farmers, and went away for a considerable time.
  Mark 12:1 — parable parallel: Both introduce a planted vineyard leased to farmers during the owner's absence. Mark adds fence, winepress and tower; Luke specifies a considerable time away.
    Mark 12:1 He began speaking to them in parables: “A man planted a vineyard, put a fence around it, dug a winepress vat, and built a tower. He leased it to tenant farmers and went abroad.

Luke 20:10
At the proper season he sent a slave to the farmers, so that they would give him some of the vineyard’s fruit. But the farmers beat him and sent him away empty.
  Mark 12:2–12:3 — parable parallel: Both describe a seasonal servant sent for vineyard produce, beaten and sent away empty. Mark divides the sending and mistreatment across two units.
    Mark 12:2 At the proper season he sent a slave to the farmers, to receive from them some of the vineyard’s fruit.
    Mark 12:3 They seized him, beat him, and sent him away empty-handed.

Luke 20:11
And he went on to send another slave. But they beat and dishonored that one also and sent him away empty.
  Mark 12:4 — parable parallel: Both describe a second servant being beaten and dishonored. Mark specifies a head injury; Luke additionally says he was sent away empty. The injuries are not made identical.
    Mark 12:4 Again he sent another slave to them; they struck that one on the head and dishonored him.

Luke 20:12
And he went on to send a third. But they wounded that one also and threw him out.
  Mark 12:5 — parable comparison: Both describe another servant sent and mistreated. Luke's third is wounded and thrown out; Mark says the next was killed and adds many others beaten or killed. The severity and sequence differ.
    Mark 12:5 He sent another, and they killed that one; and many others—some they beat, and others they killed.

Luke 20:13
Then the master of the vineyard said, ‘What shall I do? I will send my beloved son; perhaps they will respect him.’
  Mark 12:6 — parable parallel: Both turn to sending a beloved son with an expectation of respect. Luke says perhaps; Mark states they will respect him and calls him the last sending.
    Mark 12:6 He still had one, a beloved son. He sent him to them last, saying, ‘They will respect my son.’

Luke 20:14
But when the farmers saw him, they reasoned with one another, saying, ‘This is the heir. Let us kill him, so that the inheritance may become ours.’
  Mark 12:7 — parable parallel: Both have the farmers identify the heir and propose killing him to obtain the inheritance.
    Mark 12:7 But those farmers said to one another, ‘This is the heir. Come, let us kill him, and the inheritance will be ours.’

Luke 20:15
And they threw him out of the vineyard and killed him. What, then, will the master of the vineyard do to them?
  Mark 12:8–12:9 — parable parallel: Both narrate the son's killing and expulsion and ask what the owner will do. Luke puts expulsion before killing; Mark puts killing before expulsion. The action order is preserved.
    Mark 12:8 They seized him, killed him, and threw him outside the vineyard.
    Mark 12:9 What, then, will the owner of the vineyard do? He will come and destroy the farmers and give the vineyard to others.

Luke 20:16
He will come and destroy those farmers and give the vineyard to others.” When they heard this, they said, “May it never happen!”
  Mark 12:9 — parable parallel: Both answer that the owner destroys the farmers and gives the vineyard to others. Luke additionally records the hearers' May it never happen response.
    Mark 12:9 What, then, will the owner of the vineyard do? He will come and destroy the farmers and give the vineyard to others.

Luke 20:17
But He looked at them and said, “What, then, is this that is written: ‘The stone the builders rejected—this became the head of the corner’?
  Psalms 117:22 — quotation: Both identify the builders' rejected stone as the head of the corner. Luke introduces the written saying as a question to the hearers; the psalm does not narrate their parable.
    Psalms 117:22 The stone the builders rejected— this has become the head of the corner.

Luke 20:18
Everyone who falls upon that stone will be shattered; but whoever it falls upon, it will crush him.”
  Matthew 21:44 — teaching parallel: Both describe a person falling on the stone being shattered and a person under the falling stone being crushed. The saying is retained without assigning an extra physical location or historical victim.
    Matthew 21:44 And the one falling upon this stone will be shattered; but on whomever it falls, it will crush him.”

Luke 20:19
And the scribes and the chief priests sought to lay hands on Him at that very hour, but they feared the people, for they knew that He had spoken this parable against them.
  Mark 12:12 — narrative parallel: Both describe leaders seeking to seize Jesus, fearing the crowd and recognizing the vineyard parable against them. Mark adds their departure; Luke specifies that very hour.
    Mark 12:12 They sought to seize Him, but feared the crowd, for they knew that He had spoken the parable against them. They left Him and went away.

Luke 20:20
And keeping watch, they sent spies who pretended to be righteous, so that they might seize on something He said and hand Him over to the rule and authority of the governor.
  Mark 12:13 — narrative parallel: Both describe agents sent to trap Jesus in speech. Luke calls them spies feigning righteousness and names handing Him to the governor as the aim; Mark names Pharisees and Herodians.
    Mark 12:13 They sent some of the Pharisees and the Herodians to Him, to catch Him in a word.

Luke 20:21
They questioned Him, saying, “Teacher, we know that Thou speakest and teachest rightly and dost not show partiality, but teachest the way of God in truth.
  Mark 12:14 — narrative parallel: Both preface the tax question by calling Jesus a truthful, impartial teacher of God's way. Mark also includes the tax question in the same unit.
    Mark 12:14 They came and said to Him, “Teacher, we know that Thou art truthful and dost not defer to anyone, for Thou dost not look at people’s outward standing, but dost teach God’s way in truth. Is it lawful to give tribute to Caesar or not? Should we give it or not?”

Luke 20:22
Is it lawful for us to give tribute to Caesar, or not?”
  Mark 12:14–12:15 — narrative parallel: Both ask whether paying Caesar's tribute is lawful. Mark adds whether they should pay and Jesus' recognition and request for a denarius; Luke places those developments in following units.
    Mark 12:14 They came and said to Him, “Teacher, we know that Thou art truthful and dost not defer to anyone, for Thou dost not look at people’s outward standing, but dost teach God’s way in truth. Is it lawful to give tribute to Caesar or not? Should we give it or not?”
    Mark 12:15 Knowing their hypocrisy, He said to them, “Why are you testing Me? Bring Me a denarius so that I may see it.”

Luke 20:23
But perceiving their cunning, He said to them,
  Mark 12:15 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus recognize the questioners' duplicity. Luke says cunning; Mark says hypocrisy and quotes His challenge about testing and His coin request.
    Mark 12:15 Knowing their hypocrisy, He said to them, “Why are you testing Me? Bring Me a denarius so that I may see it.”

Luke 20:24
“Show Me a denarius. Whose image and inscription does it have?” They answered, “Caesar’s.”
  Mark 12:16 — narrative parallel: Both ask whose image and inscription the denarius bears and receive Caesar as the answer. Luke includes Jesus' request to show the coin; Mark narrates its production.
    Mark 12:16 They brought one. He said to them, “Whose image and inscription is this?” They said to Him, “Caesar’s.”

Luke 20:25
And He said to them, “Then give back to Caesar the things that are Caesar’s, and to God the things that are God’s.”
  Mark 12:17 — teaching parallel: Both command rendering Caesar's things to Caesar and God's things to God. Mark also reports the hearers' amazement in that unit.
    Mark 12:17 Jesus said to them, “Give back Caesar’s things to Caesar, and God’s things to God.” They marveled greatly at Him.

Luke 20:26
And they were unable to seize on the saying before the people. Marveling at His answer, they fell silent.
  Mark 12:17 — narrative parallel: Both describe amazement at Jesus' answer. Luke additionally states they could not seize His words before the people and fell silent; Mark does not repeat those details.
    Mark 12:17 Jesus said to them, “Give back Caesar’s things to Caesar, and God’s things to God.” They marveled greatly at Him.

Luke 20:27
Some of the Sadducees, who deny that there is a resurrection, came up and questioned Him,
  Mark 12:18 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Sadducees who deny resurrection approaching with a question. The denial is theirs, not the narrator's endorsement.
    Mark 12:18 Sadducees, who say there is no resurrection, came to Him and questioned Him, saying,

Luke 20:28
saying, “Teacher, Moses wrote for us that if a man’s brother dies, having a wife but being childless, his brother should take the wife and raise up offspring for his brother.
  Deuteronomy 25:5–25:6 — quotation: The cited Mosaic rule corresponds to a dead childless brother's widow being taken by his brother to continue the dead man's line. Deuteronomy adds co-residing brothers and establishing the child's name for the dead; Luke summarizes the rule in the challengers' question.
    Deuteronomy 25:5 And if brethren dwell together, and one of them dies, and has no seed, the wife of the dead shall not be married outside unto a man who is not near of kin. Her husband’s brother shall go in unto her, and take her unto himself for a wife, and dwell with her as husband.
    Deuteronomy 25:6 And it shall be, that the child whom she bears shall be established in the name of the dead, and his name shall not be blotted out of Israel.

Luke 20:29
Now there were seven brothers. The first took a wife and died childless,
  Mark 12:20 — narrative parallel: Both begin the hypothetical case with seven brothers and the first marrying and dying without offspring.
    Mark 12:20 There were seven brothers. The first took a wife, and died leaving no offspring.

Luke 20:30
and the second,
  Mark 12:21 — narrative continuation: Luke's short continuation names the second brother; Mark explicitly narrates his marriage and childless death before the third. Luke's saved fragment is read with its surrounding units, not expanded in the source.
    Mark 12:21 The second took her and died leaving no offspring, and the third likewise.

Luke 20:31
and the third took her. Likewise, all seven left no children and died.
  Mark 12:21–12:22 — narrative parallel: Both continue through the third and the seven brothers, all without offspring. Mark also states the woman's final death, which Luke gives in the following unit.
    Mark 12:21 The second took her and died leaving no offspring, and the third likewise.
    Mark 12:22 The seven left no offspring. Last of all, the woman also died.

Luke 20:32
Afterward the woman also died.
  Mark 12:22 — narrative parallel: Both conclude the hypothetical sequence with the woman's death after the brothers. Mark combines it with the seven leaving no offspring.
    Mark 12:22 The seven left no offspring. Last of all, the woman also died.

Luke 20:33
So in the resurrection, whose wife does the woman become? For all seven had her as wife.”
  Mark 12:23 — narrative parallel: Both ask whose wife the woman will be in the resurrection because all seven married her.
    Mark 12:23 In the resurrection, when they rise, whose wife will she be? For the seven had her as wife.”

Luke 20:34
And Jesus said to them, “The sons of this age marry and are given in marriage.
  Luke 20:35 — internal teaching contrast: Jesus' statement about present-age marriage is explicitly contrasted with the next unit's resurrection-age absence of marriage. This preserves the distinction between ages rather than applying the later condition indiscriminately to present life.
    Luke 20:35 But those counted worthy to attain that age and the resurrection from the dead neither marry nor are given in marriage.

Luke 20:35
But those counted worthy to attain that age and the resurrection from the dead neither marry nor are given in marriage.
  Mark 12:25 — teaching parallel: Both say those raised neither marry nor are given in marriage. Luke names the coming age and those counted worthy; Mark proceeds to the angel comparison.
    Mark 12:25 For when they rise from the dead, they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but are like angels in the heavens.

Luke 20:36
For they can no longer die either, because they are equal to angels and are sons of God, being sons of the resurrection.
  Mark 12:25 — teaching comparison: Both compare resurrection life with angels. Luke adds inability to die and divine sonship through resurrection; Mark says like angels in the heavens. Luke's additional claims are not inserted into Mark.
    Mark 12:25 For when they rise from the dead, they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but are like angels in the heavens.

Luke 20:37
And that the dead are raised, even Moses disclosed in the passage about the bush, when he calls the Lord the God of Abraham and God of Isaac and God of Jacob.
  Exodus 3:6 — quotation and argument: Jesus invokes the bush passage's identification of God with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. Exodus gives that divine self-identification; Luke uses it in a resurrection argument not stated in full within Exodus.
    Exodus 3:6 And He said to him, “I am the God of your father, the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob.” And Moses turned his face away, for he feared to look before God.

Luke 20:38
Now He is God not of the dead but of the living, for all live to Him.”
  Mark 12:27 — teaching parallel: Both call God the God of living rather than dead people. Luke adds that all live to Him; Mark concludes that the questioners are greatly mistaken.
    Mark 12:27 He is not God of the dead, but of the living. You are greatly mistaken.”

Luke 20:39
And some of the scribes answered, “Teacher, Thou hast spoken well.”
  Mark 12:32 — teaching-response comparison: Both have scribes commend Jesus as Teacher for speaking well. Luke has several respond after the resurrection discussion; Mark has one respond after the love-command discussion. Their occasions and statements are not merged.
    Mark 12:32 The scribe said to Him, “Well said, Teacher! Thou hast truly said that He is one, and there is no other besides Him.

Luke 20:40
For they no longer dared to question Him about anything.
  Mark 12:34 — narrative comparison: Both conclude that people no longer dared question Jesus. Luke follows the resurrection exchange; Mark follows the love-command discussion. The differing sequence is preserved.
    Mark 12:34 Seeing that he answered thoughtfully, Jesus said to him, “You are not far from the kingdom of God.” No one dared to question Him any further.

Luke 20:41
But He said to them, “How do they say that the Christ is David’s son?
  Mark 12:35 — teaching parallel: Both ask how the Christ can be called David's son. Mark explicitly attributes that description to scribes while Jesus teaches in the temple.
    Mark 12:35 Jesus responded as He taught in the temple, “How do the scribes say that the Christ is David’s son?

Luke 20:42
For David himself says in the book of Psalms, ‘The Lord said to my Lord, “Sit at My right hand
  Psalms 109:1 — quotation: Luke quotes the psalm's Lord-to-my-lord speech and invitation to sit at God's right hand. The same psalm verse continues with the enemies' footstool clause quoted in Luke's next unit.
    Psalms 109:1 Of David. A psalm. The Lord said to my lord: Sit at My right hand until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet.

Luke 20:43
until I make Thy enemies a footstool for Thy feet.”’
  Psalms 109:1 — quotation: Luke's enemies-as-footstool clause continues the same psalm verse quoted immediately before. The target retains its Davidic heading and complete speech rather than being renumbered to another Psalter.
    Psalms 109:1 Of David. A psalm. The Lord said to my lord: Sit at My right hand until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet.

Luke 20:44
David therefore calls Him Lord; how, then, is He his son?”
  Mark 12:37 — teaching parallel: Both ask how David's calling the Christ Lord relates to calling Him David's son. Mark additionally describes the crowd's glad hearing; no extra genealogical or chronological conclusion is imposed.
    Mark 12:37 David himself calls Him Lord; how then is He his son?” The large crowd listened to Him gladly.

Luke 20:45
While all the people were listening, He said to His disciples,
  Mark 12:37–12:38 — teaching-scene comparison: Both place a public teaching warning about scribes after the David discussion. Luke distinguishes listening people from addressed disciples; Mark mentions the crowd's glad hearing and introduces the warning without that same audience distinction.
    Mark 12:37 David himself calls Him Lord; how then is He his son?” The large crowd listened to Him gladly.
    Mark 12:38 In His teaching He said, “Beware of the scribes who like to walk about in long robes and receive greetings in the marketplaces,

Luke 20:46
“Beware of the scribes who want to walk about in robes and love greetings in the marketplaces and foremost seats in the synagogues and foremost places at suppers,
  Mark 12:38–12:39 — teaching parallel: Both warn about scribes' robes, marketplace greetings, foremost synagogue seats and foremost places at meals. Mark distributes the list across two units.
    Mark 12:38 In His teaching He said, “Beware of the scribes who like to walk about in long robes and receive greetings in the marketplaces,
    Mark 12:39 the foremost seats in the synagogues, and the foremost reclining places at dinners.

Luke 20:47
who devour widows’ houses and pray at length for a pretext. These will receive greater judgment.”
  Mark 12:40 — teaching parallel: Both condemn devouring widows' houses and lengthy prayers as pretext, followed by greater judgment. Their supplied formulations agree on the warning; no different victim group is inferred.
    Mark 12:40 They devour widows’ houses and make long prayers as a pretense. These will receive greater judgment.”

Luke 21:1
Looking up, He saw the rich putting their gifts into the treasury.
  Mark 12:41 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus observing rich people putting money or gifts into the treasury. Mark places Him seated opposite it and names many large contributions; Luke says He looked up.
    Mark 12:41 He sat opposite the treasury and watched how the crowd put copper money into the treasury. Many rich people put in much.

Luke 21:2
And He saw a certain needy widow putting two lepta in there.
  Mark 12:42 — narrative parallel: Both describe a poor widow giving two lepta. Mark additionally gives the quadrans equivalent; Luke calls her needy without that explanation.
    Mark 12:42 And one poor widow came and put in two lepta, which make a quadrans.

Luke 21:3
And He said, “Truly, I tell you, this poor widow put in more than all of them.
  Mark 12:43 — teaching parallel: Both declare that the poor widow gave more than the other contributors. Mark explicitly calls the disciples before saying this; Luke does not state that summons.
    Mark 12:43 He called His disciples to Him and said to them, “Truly I tell you, this poor widow has put in more than all those putting into the treasury.

Luke 21:4
For all these put into the gifts out of their abundance, but she, out of her lack, put in all she had to live on.”
  Mark 12:44 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the others' surplus with the widow giving her entire livelihood from poverty. Neither account's comparison is converted into a different monetary amount.
    Mark 12:44 For they all put in from their surplus, but she, from her need, put in everything she had—her whole livelihood.”

Luke 21:5
And as some were speaking about the temple, how it was adorned with beautiful stones and dedicated offerings, He said,
  Mark 13:1 — narrative parallel: Both introduce admiration for the temple's stones. Luke names some speakers and dedicated offerings; Mark names one disciple and the great buildings. The speakers and details remain distinct.
    Mark 13:1 As He was leaving the temple, one of His disciples said to Him, “Teacher, look! What stones and what buildings!”

Luke 21:6
“As for these things you see, days will come in which stone will not be left upon stone without being torn down.”
  Mark 13:2 — teaching parallel: Both predict destruction so complete that no stone will remain on another. Mark points to the great buildings; Luke describes coming days concerning what the listeners see.
    Mark 13:2 Jesus said to him, “Do you see these great buildings? Not one stone will be left here on another that will not be torn down.”

Luke 21:7
They questioned Him, saying, “Teacher, when, then, will these things be, and what will be the sign when these things are about to happen?”
  Mark 13:3–13:4 — discourse parallel: Both ask when the predicted events will occur and what sign will precede them. Mark names four questioners privately on Olivet; Luke has an unnamed group address Teacher without supplying that setting here.
    Mark 13:3 As He sat on the Mount of Olives opposite the temple, Peter, James, John, and Andrew questioned Him privately:
    Mark 13:4 “Tell us, when will these things be, and what will be the sign when all these things are about to be fulfilled?”

Luke 21:8
And He said, “See that you are not led astray. For many will come in My name, saying, ‘I am he,’ and, ‘The time has drawn near.’ Do not go after them.
  Mark 13:5–13:6 — teaching parallel: Both warn against deception by people coming in Jesus' name and saying I am he. Luke additionally gives the time-has-drawn-near claim and forbids following them.
    Mark 13:5 Jesus began saying to them, “Watch out that no one leads you astray.
    Mark 13:6 Many will come in My name, saying, ‘I am he,’ and will lead many astray.

Luke 21:9
When you hear of wars and disturbances, do not be terrified, for these things must happen first, but the end will not come immediately.”
  Mark 13:7 — teaching parallel: Both forbid alarm at wars because these must occur before the end. Luke names disturbances and says the end is not immediate; Mark names reports of wars and says not yet.
    Mark 13:7 When you hear of wars and reports of wars, do not be alarmed. These must happen, but the end is not yet.

Luke 21:10
Then He said to them, “Nation will rise against nation and kingdom against kingdom.
  Mark 13:8 — teaching parallel: Both predict nation against nation and kingdom against kingdom. Mark includes earthquakes and famines within that same unit.
    Mark 13:8 For nation will rise against nation, and kingdom against kingdom. There will be earthquakes in various places; there will be famines. These things are the beginning of birth pains.

Luke 21:11
There will be great earthquakes, and famines and plagues in various places. There will also be terrors and great signs from heaven.
  Mark 13:8 — teaching parallel: Both predict earthquakes in different places and famines. Luke additionally names plagues, terrors and heavenly signs; Mark calls its listed troubles the beginning of birth pains.
    Mark 13:8 For nation will rise against nation, and kingdom against kingdom. There will be earthquakes in various places; there will be famines. These things are the beginning of birth pains.

Luke 21:12
But before all these things they will lay their hands on you and persecute you, handing you over to synagogues and prisons, and you will be led before kings and governors because of My name.
  Mark 13:9 — teaching parallel: Both warn of persecution involving synagogues and appearances before kings and governors because of Jesus. Luke adds prisons and before-all-these timing; Mark names councils and synagogue beatings. Their lists and sequence language are preserved.
    Mark 13:9 But watch yourselves. They will hand you over to councils; you will be beaten in synagogues, and you will stand before governors and kings for My sake, as a testimony to them.

Luke 21:13
It will turn out as an opportunity for you to bear witness.
  Mark 13:9 — teaching parallel: Both present hostile hearings as an occasion for testimony. Luke states the opportunity separately; Mark includes it within the warning about governors and kings.
    Mark 13:9 But watch yourselves. They will hand you over to councils; you will be beaten in synagogues, and you will stand before governors and kings for My sake, as a testimony to them.

Luke 21:14
Therefore settle it in your hearts not to rehearse beforehand how to defend yourselves,
  Mark 13:11 — teaching parallel: Both tell disciples not to prepare their defensive speech anxiously beforehand. Mark also names what will be given in that hour and the Holy Spirit's speaking; Luke continues its promise in the next unit.
    Mark 13:11 When they lead you away to hand you over, do not worry beforehand about what to say. Whatever is given you in that hour, say that; for it is not you who speak, but the Holy Spirit.

Luke 21:15
for I will give you a mouth and wisdom that all who oppose you will be unable to resist or contradict.
  Acts 6:9–6:10 — promise and reported example: Jesus promises wisdom opponents cannot withstand; Acts describes Stephen's disputants unable to withstand the wisdom and Spirit with which he spoke. This is a specific comparable outcome, not proof that every confrontation or speaker has identical circumstances.
    Acts 6:9 But some from the synagogue called the Freedmen’s, including Cyrenians and Alexandrians and people from Cilicia and Asia, rose up and disputed with Stephen.
    Acts 6:10 They could not withstand the wisdom and the Spirit with which he spoke.

Luke 21:16
You will be handed over even by parents and brothers and relatives and friends, and they will put some of you to death.
  Mark 13:12 — teaching parallel: Both predict betrayal within families and resulting deaths. Luke also names relatives and friends; Mark specifies brother against brother, father and child, and children against parents.
    Mark 13:12 Brother will hand brother over to death, and a father his child. Children will rise against parents and have them put to death.

Luke 21:17
And you will be hated by all because of My name.
  Mark 13:13 — teaching parallel: Both predict being hated by all because of Jesus' name. Mark adds the endurance-and-salvation conclusion in the same unit.
    Mark 13:13 You will be hated by all because of My name. But the one who endures to the end will be saved.

Luke 21:18
Yet certainly not a hair from your head will perish.
  Acts 27:34 — thematic assurance parallel: Both use the assurance that not a hair from a head will be lost. Paul addresses a shipwreck company; Luke21 addresses persecuted followers and nearby explicitly says some will be killed. The shared wording is not made a universal guarantee against physical death.
    Acts 27:34 Therefore I urge you to take food, for this is for your safety. Not a hair from the head of any of you will be lost.”

Luke 21:19
By your endurance gain your souls.
  Mark 13:13 — teaching parallel: Both connect endurance with salvation or gaining one's soul. Mark says enduring to the end will be saved; Luke says by endurance gain your souls. The supplied formulations remain distinct.
    Mark 13:13 You will be hated by all because of My name. But the one who endures to the end will be saved.

Luke 21:20
But when you see Jerusalem surrounded by armies, then know that her desolation has drawn near.
  Mark 13:14 — discourse comparison: Both introduce a visible desolation warning before instructions to flee Judea. Luke names Jerusalem surrounded by armies; Mark names the abomination standing where it should not. The armies and abomination are not simply declared identical.
    Mark 13:14 When you see the abomination of desolation standing where it must not—let the reader understand—then let those in Judea flee to the mountains.

Luke 21:21
Then let those in Judea flee to the mountains, and let those within her depart, and let those in the countryside not enter her,
  Mark 13:14–13:16 — teaching parallel: Both direct those in Judea to flee to the mountains urgently. Luke distinguishes people inside the city and countryside; Mark forbids house or field returns for belongings.
    Mark 13:14 When you see the abomination of desolation standing where it must not—let the reader understand—then let those in Judea flee to the mountains.
    Mark 13:15 Let the one on the housetop not go down or enter to take anything from his house,
    Mark 13:16 and let the one in the field not turn back to take his garment.

Luke 21:22
because these are days of vengeance, for all that is written to be fulfilled.
  Hosea 9:7 — allusion: Both describe days of vengeance and repayment in judgment concerning Israel. Luke connects the days with fulfillment of written things; Hosea names Israel's injustices and affliction. This compares the judgment formula without declaring one historical occasion or an exhaustive quotation.
    Hosea 9:7 The days of vengeance have come; the days of your repayment have come. Israel will be afflicted like the deranged prophet, a spirit-bearing man. Because your injustices were many, your madness increased.

Luke 21:23
Woe to those who are pregnant and those nursing infants in those days! For there will be great distress upon the land and wrath against this people.
  Mark 13:17 — teaching parallel: Both pronounce woe on pregnant women and nursing mothers in those days. Luke additionally names distress on the land and wrath against this people; Mark's cited unit does not repeat those clauses.
    Mark 13:17 Woe to those who are pregnant and those nursing infants in those days!

Luke 21:24
They will fall by the edge of the sword and be led captive into all the nations. And Jerusalem will be trampled by nations until the times of nations are fulfilled.
  Deuteronomy 28:64 — prophetic thematic parallel: Both describe scattering among all nations in a judgment setting. Deuteronomy additionally names foreign gods; Luke adds sword, captivity and Jerusalem's trampling until the nations' times are fulfilled. No date or full equivalence of every judgment detail is asserted.
    Deuteronomy 28:64 And the Lord your God shall scatter you among all the nations, from one end of the earth unto the other; and there you shall serve other gods, wood and stone, which neither you nor your fathers have known.

Luke 21:25
And there will be signs in sun and moon and stars, and on the earth distress among nations in perplexity at the roar of the sea and its surging,
  Mark 13:24–13:25 — discourse parallel: Both describe signs in sun, moon and stars. Mark specifies darkening and falling stars; Luke adds nations' distress at the sea's roar and surging. The differing imagery is not rewritten.
    Mark 13:24 But in those days, after that distress, the sun will be darkened and the moon will not give its light.
    Mark 13:25 The stars will be falling from heaven, and the powers in the heavens will be shaken.

Luke 21:26
people fainting from fear and expectation of what is coming upon the inhabited world, for the powers of the heavens will be shaken.
  Mark 13:25 — discourse parallel: Both say the heavenly powers will be shaken. Luke additionally names people fainting in fear of coming events; Mark additionally describes falling stars.
    Mark 13:25 The stars will be falling from heaven, and the powers in the heavens will be shaken.

Luke 21:27
And then they will see the Son of Man coming in a cloud with power and great glory.
  Mark 13:26 — teaching parallel: Both describe the Son of Man coming in cloud or clouds with power and glory. Singular and plural cloud wording remain the supplied forms.
    Mark 13:26 Then they will see the Son of Man coming in clouds with great power and glory.

Luke 21:28
But when these things begin to happen, straighten up and lift your heads, because your redemption is drawing near.”
  Romans 13:11 — thematic exhortation parallel: Both connect alert hope with approaching salvation or redemption. Luke urges lifted heads as the events begin; Paul urges waking because salvation is nearer than at belief. Their audiences and temporal comparisons are not turned into a common calendar date.
    Romans 13:11 And do this knowing the time, that it is already the hour for you to rise from sleep; for now salvation is nearer to us than when we believed.

Luke 21:29
And He told them a parable: “Look at the fig tree and all the trees.
  Mark 13:28 — parable parallel: Both introduce the fig tree as an observable seasonal comparison. Luke also names all the trees; Mark immediately describes the tender branch and leaves.
    Mark 13:28 Learn the parable from the fig tree: when its branch has already become tender and puts out leaves, you know that summer is near.

Luke 21:30
When they already put out shoots, you see and know for yourselves that summer is already near.
  Mark 13:28 — parable parallel: Both use new growth as a sign that summer is near. Luke names shoots; Mark names a tender branch putting out leaves.
    Mark 13:28 Learn the parable from the fig tree: when its branch has already become tender and puts out leaves, you know that summer is near.

Luke 21:31
So also you, when you see these things happening, know that the kingdom of God is near.
  Mark 13:29 — teaching parallel: Both apply observed events to recognizing nearness. Luke explicitly names God's kingdom; Mark says it is near, at the doors. Mark's supplied pronoun is not silently replaced with a named person.
    Mark 13:29 So also, when you see these things happening, know that it is near, at the doors.

Luke 21:32
Amen, I tell you: this generation will certainly not pass away until all things happen.
  Mark 13:30 — teaching parallel: Both declare that this generation will not pass away until the predicted things occur. Neither quoted unit supplies an additional numerical date.
    Mark 13:30 Truly I tell you, this generation will certainly not pass away until all these things happen.

Luke 21:33
Heaven and earth will pass away, but My words will certainly not pass away.
  Mark 13:31 — teaching parallel: Both contrast passing heaven and earth with Jesus' words certainly not passing away.
    Mark 13:31 Heaven and earth will pass away, but My words will certainly not pass away.

Luke 21:34
But watch yourselves, lest your hearts be weighed down with carousing and drunkenness and the cares of life, and that day come upon you suddenly
  1 Thessalonians 5:6–5:7 — thematic exhortation parallel: Both oppose drunkenness and inattentiveness to alertness in the surrounding day-of-the-Lord teaching. Luke names carousing, life's cares and a sudden day; Paul contrasts watchfulness and sobriety with nighttime sleeping and drunkenness.
    1 Thessalonians 5:6 So then, let us not sleep as the rest do, but let us keep watch and be sober.
    1 Thessalonians 5:7 For those who sleep sleep at night, and those who get drunk are drunk at night.

Luke 21:35
like a trap. For it will come upon all who dwell on the face of the whole earth.
  Isaiah 24:17 — allusion: Both place a trap upon earth's inhabitants in judgment imagery. Isaiah also names fear and pit; Luke says the day comes like a trap upon everyone on the whole earth. The comparison does not identify a historical date.
    Isaiah 24:17 Fear, pit and trap are upon you who dwell on the earth!

Luke 21:36
But stay awake at every season, praying that you may have strength to escape all these things that are about to happen and to stand before the Son of Man.”
  Mark 13:33 — teaching parallel: Both command continued wakefulness. Luke adds prayer for strength to escape and stand before the Son of Man; Mark emphasizes not knowing the time without adding those prayer clauses.
    Mark 13:33 Watch out; stay awake, for you do not know when the time is.

Luke 21:37
By day He was teaching in the temple, but at night He went out and lodged on the mount called Olivet.
  Luke 22:39 — internal ministry-pattern link: Both describe Jesus going to Olivet as a recurring practice. The earlier summary contrasts daytime temple teaching with nighttime lodging; the later notice describes His customary journey there with disciples. The later verse does not independently repeat the full daily schedule.
    Luke 22:39 And He went out and traveled, as was His custom, to the Mount of Olives; and the disciples also followed Him.

Luke 21:38
And all the people would come early in the morning to Him in the temple to hear Him.
  Luke 19:47–19:48 — same-book ministry parallel: Both describe repeated temple teaching and people's eagerness to listen. Luke21 specifies early morning; Luke19 adds the leaders' hostility and the people's hanging on Jesus' words.
    Luke 19:47 And He was teaching daily in the temple. But the chief priests and the scribes, and the leading men of the people, were seeking to destroy Him.
    Luke 19:48 Yet they could not find what to do, for all the people hung on His words as they listened.

Luke 22:1
Now the feast of Unleavened Bread, called Passover, was drawing near.
  Mark 14:1 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Passover and Unleavened Bread approaching. Mark specifies two days and also introduces the murder plot; Luke says the feast was drawing near.
    Mark 14:1 The Passover and the Feast of Unleavened Bread were two days away. The chief priests and the scribes sought how to seize Him by deceit and kill Him.

Luke 22:2
And the chief priests and the scribes were seeking how to kill Him, for they feared the people.
  Mark 14:1–14:2 — narrative parallel: Both describe chief priests and scribes plotting Jesus' death while concerned about the people. Mark names deceptive seizure and a disturbance during the feast; Luke says they feared the people.
    Mark 14:1 The Passover and the Feast of Unleavened Bread were two days away. The chief priests and the scribes sought how to seize Him by deceit and kill Him.
    Mark 14:2 For they said, “Not during the feast, lest there be a disturbance among the people.”

Luke 22:3
Then Satan entered Judas, who was called Iscariot and was one of the number of the Twelve.
  John 13:27 — narrative comparison: Both explicitly describe Satan entering Judas. Luke places it before his bargain with the authorities; John places it after the morsel during supper. No single merged timing is imposed.
    John 13:27 And after the morsel, then Satan entered him. So Jesus said to him, “What you do, do more quickly.”

Luke 22:4
He went away and spoke with the chief priests and the captains about how he might hand Him over to them.
  Mark 14:10 — narrative parallel: Both describe Judas, one of the Twelve, going to the chief priests about handing Jesus over. Luke additionally names the captains and the discussion of how to do it.
    Mark 14:10 Judas Iscariot, one of the Twelve, went away to the chief priests to hand Him over to them.

Luke 22:5
And they rejoiced and agreed to give him money.
  Mark 14:11 — narrative parallel: Both describe the authorities rejoicing and promising money. Mark names silver money; Luke records agreement to give money without specifying an amount.
    Mark 14:11 When they heard, they rejoiced and promised to give him silver money. He sought how to hand Him over at a suitable time.

Luke 22:6
He consented, and sought an opportunity to hand Him over to them when no crowd was present.
  Mark 14:11 — narrative parallel: Both describe Judas seeking an opportunity to hand Jesus over. Luke adds his consent and absence of a crowd; Mark says a suitable time without that explicit crowd condition.
    Mark 14:11 When they heard, they rejoiced and promised to give him silver money. He sought how to hand Him over at a suitable time.

Luke 22:7
Then the day of Unleavened Bread came, on which the Passover sacrifice had to be slaughtered.
  Mark 14:12 — narrative parallel: Both identify the Unleavened Bread day with slaughter of the Passover sacrifice. Mark additionally introduces the disciples' preparation question in this unit.
    Mark 14:12 On the first day of Unleavened Bread, when they sacrificed the Passover, His disciples said to Him, “Where dost Thou want us to go and prepare the Passover for Thee to eat?”

Luke 22:8
And He sent Peter and John, saying, “Go and prepare the Passover for us, so that we may eat.”
  Mark 14:13 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus sending two disciples to prepare. Luke names Peter and John; Mark leaves the two unnamed and begins the city instructions.
    Mark 14:13 He sent two of His disciples and said to them, “Go into the city, and a man carrying an earthenware jar of water will meet you. Follow him.

Luke 22:9
They said to Him, “Where dost Thou want us to prepare it?”
  Mark 14:12 — narrative parallel: Both give the disciples' question about where Jesus wants the Passover prepared. Mark places that question before the sending; Luke narrates it after naming the two sent.
    Mark 14:12 On the first day of Unleavened Bread, when they sacrificed the Passover, His disciples said to Him, “Where dost Thou want us to go and prepare the Passover for Thee to eat?”

Luke 22:10
He said to them, “Behold, as you enter the city, a man carrying a jar of water will meet you. Follow him into the house he enters.
  Mark 14:13–14:14 — narrative parallel: Both direct following a man with a water jar into the house he enters. Luke describes the encounter upon entering the city; Mark specifies an earthenware jar and continues to the master-of-house instruction.
    Mark 14:13 He sent two of His disciples and said to them, “Go into the city, and a man carrying an earthenware jar of water will meet you. Follow him.
    Mark 14:14 Wherever he enters, say to the master of the house, ‘The Teacher says: Where is My guest room, where I may eat the Passover with My disciples?’

Luke 22:11
And you shall say to the master of the house, ‘The Teacher says to you, “Where is the lodging room in which I may eat the Passover with My disciples?”’
  Mark 14:14 — narrative parallel: Both give the Teacher's question to the householder about a room for eating Passover with His disciples. Guest room and lodging room remain their supplied formulations.
    Mark 14:14 Wherever he enters, say to the master of the house, ‘The Teacher says: Where is My guest room, where I may eat the Passover with My disciples?’

Luke 22:12
And he will show you a large, furnished upper room. Prepare it there.”
  Mark 14:15 — narrative parallel: Both predict a large furnished upper room and command preparation there. Mark also calls the room ready.
    Mark 14:15 He will show you a large upper room, furnished and ready. Prepare for us there.”

Luke 22:13
They went and found things just as He had told them, and they prepared the Passover.
  Mark 14:16 — narrative parallel: Both narrate the disciples finding matters just as Jesus told them and preparing Passover.
    Mark 14:16 The disciples went out, came to the city, and found it just as He had told them. They prepared the Passover.

Luke 22:14
When the hour came, He reclined at table, and the apostles with Him.
  Mark 14:17–14:18 — narrative parallel: Both introduce Jesus at the supper with the apostolic group. Luke says when the hour came and names the apostles; Mark names evening and the Twelve, then reclining and eating.
    Mark 14:17 When evening came, He arrived with the Twelve.
    Mark 14:18 As they reclined and ate, Jesus said, “Truly I tell you, one of you will hand Me over—one who is eating with Me.”

Luke 22:15
And He said to them, “With desire I have desired to eat this Passover with you before I suffer.
  Matthew 26:2 — Passover and approaching Passion: Both statements connect Passover with Jesus' approaching suffering or death. Luke expresses His desire to eat this Passover with the apostles before suffering; Matthew announces Passover after two days and the Son of Man's handover for crucifixion. Matthew does not repeat the desire or narrate the supper in that verse; their speech occasions are not collapsed.
    Matthew 26:2 “You know that after two days the Passover takes place, and the Son of Man is handed over to be crucified.”

Luke 22:16
For I tell you that I will certainly not eat it until it is fulfilled in the kingdom of God.”
  Mark 14:25 — supper-teaching comparison: Both anticipate renewed participation in a kingdom meal after present abstention. Luke speaks of not eating this Passover until fulfillment in God's kingdom; Mark speaks of not drinking the vine's fruit until drinking it new there. Food, drink and fulfillment wording remain distinct.
    Mark 14:25 Truly I tell you, I will certainly no longer drink of the fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new in the kingdom of God.”

Luke 22:17
And He received a cup, gave thanks, and said, “Take this and divide it among yourselves.
  Mark 14:23 — supper-action comparison: Both describe Jesus receiving or taking a cup, giving thanks and sharing it with the group. Luke places this cup before the bread and later narrates another cup after supper; Mark's cited cup follows bread. Their sequences are not collapsed.
    Mark 14:23 He took a cup, gave thanks, and gave it to them, and they all drank from it.

Luke 22:18
For I tell you, from now on I will certainly not drink from the fruit of the vine until the kingdom of God comes.”
  Mark 14:25 — teaching parallel: Both declare no further drinking of the vine's fruit until God's kingdom. Mark says drinking it new in that day; Luke says until the kingdom comes.
    Mark 14:25 Truly I tell you, I will certainly no longer drink of the fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new in the kingdom of God.”

Luke 22:19
