And He took bread, and having given thanks, He broke it and gave it to them, saying, “This is My body, which is given for you; do this in remembrance of Me.”
  1 Corinthians 11:23–11:24 — supper-tradition parallel: Both describe taking bread, giving thanks, breaking and identifying it as Jesus' body for the recipients, with remembrance commanded. Luke says given for you; Corinthians says for you. No extra broken-body wording is inserted.
    1 Corinthians 11:23 For I received from the Lord what I also handed over to you: that the Lord Jesus, on the night when He was being handed over, took bread,
    1 Corinthians 11:24 and after giving thanks He broke it and said, “This is My body, which is for you. Do this in remembrance of Me.”

Luke 22:20
And likewise the cup after supper, saying, “This cup is the new covenant in My blood, poured out for you.
  1 Corinthians 11:25 — supper-tradition parallel: Both identify the after-supper cup as the new covenant in Jesus' blood. Luke adds poured out for you; Corinthians adds repeated drinking in remembrance. Those additional clauses remain distinct.
    1 Corinthians 11:25 Likewise He also took the cup after supper, saying, “This cup is the new covenant in My blood. Do this, as often as you drink, in remembrance of Me.”

Luke 22:21
But behold, the hand of the one who hands Me over is with Me on the table.
  Mark 14:18–14:20 — supper-teaching parallel: Both identify the betrayer as someone sharing Jesus' meal. Luke names the hand on the table; Mark names eating and dipping in the dish and records the disciples' distress.
    Mark 14:18 As they reclined and ate, Jesus said, “Truly I tell you, one of you will hand Me over—one who is eating with Me.”
    Mark 14:19 They began to be grieved and said to Him one by one, “Surely not I?”
    Mark 14:20 He said to them, “One of the Twelve, the one dipping with Me into the dish.

Luke 22:22
For the Son of Man indeed goes according to what has been determined, but woe to that man through whom He is handed over!”
  Mark 14:21 — teaching parallel: Both combine the Son of Man's appointed course with woe to the betrayer. Luke says determined; Mark says written and adds that not being born would have been better for that man.
    Mark 14:21 For the Son of Man goes just as it is written about Him, but woe to that man through whom the Son of Man is handed over! It would have been good for that man if he had not been born.”

Luke 22:23
And they began to discuss among themselves which of them it could be who was about to do this.
  Mark 14:19 — narrative parallel: Both describe the disciples responding by questioning who the betrayer could be. Luke describes discussion among themselves; Mark describes grief and individual questions to Jesus.
    Mark 14:19 They began to be grieved and said to Him one by one, “Surely not I?”

Luke 22:24
A dispute also arose among them about which of them was considered to be greatest.
  Mark 9:33–9:34 — discipleship-pattern comparison: Both describe a dispute about which disciple is greatest. Luke places this at supper; Mark describes an earlier road discussion recalled in a Capernaum house. The occasions are distinct.
    Mark 9:33 They came to Capernaum. When He was in the house, He asked them, “What were you discussing on the way?”
    Mark 9:34 They remained silent, for on the way they had discussed with one another who was greatest.

Luke 22:25
But He said to them, “The kings of the nations lord it over them, and those who exercise authority over them are called benefactors.
  Mark 10:42 — teaching parallel: Both contrast Gentile rulers' domination with the disciples' coming instruction. Luke additionally says powerful rulers are called benefactors; Mark does not supply that title.
    Mark 10:42 Jesus called them to Him and said to them, “You know that those regarded as rulers of the nations lord it over them, and their great ones exercise authority over them.

Luke 22:26
But you are not to be so. Rather, let the greatest among you become as the youngest, and the leader as the one who serves.
  Mark 10:43–10:44 — teaching parallel: Both deny ordinary domination among disciples and define greatness through service. Luke contrasts greatest with youngest and leader with server; Mark contrasts greatness with servant and first place with slave of all.
    Mark 10:43 But it is not so among you. Whoever wants to become great among you shall be your servant,
    Mark 10:44 and whoever wants to be first among you shall be slave of all.

Luke 22:27
For who is greater, the one reclining at table or the one serving? Is it not the one reclining? But I am among you as the One who serves.
  Mark 10:45 — teaching parallel: Both identify Jesus with service rather than superior treatment. Luke gives the table's server/recliner comparison and says He serves among them; Mark describes His mission to serve and give His life as ransom.
    Mark 10:45 For even the Son of Man did not come to be served but to serve, and to give His life as a ransom in exchange for many.”

Luke 22:28
And you are those who have remained with Me in My trials.
  John 15:27 — discipleship comparison: Both have Jesus recognize followers' continuing presence with Him. Luke names remaining in His trials; John names being with Him from the beginning as grounds for witness. The duration descriptions and ensuing assignments differ.
    John 15:27 And you also bear witness, because you have been with Me from the beginning.

Luke 22:29
And I assign to you a kingdom, just as My Father assigned one to Me,
  Luke 12:32 — same-book kingdom-gift link: Both portray the kingdom as a gift to followers rather than an achievement of domination. Luke12 says the Father gladly gives it to the little flock; here Jesus assigns it as the Father assigned it to Him. The exact speakers and formulations remain distinct.
    Luke 12:32 Do not fear, little flock, because your Father was pleased to give you the kingdom.

Luke 22:30
so that you may eat and drink at My table in My kingdom, and you will sit on thrones judging the twelve tribes of Israel.
  Matthew 19:28 — promise parallel: Both promise the disciples thrones judging Israel's twelve tribes. Luke additionally names eating and drinking at Jesus' kingdom table; Matthew names the Son of Man's glorious throne and the renewal.
    Matthew 19:28 Jesus said to them, “Truly I tell you that you who have followed Me, in the regeneration, when the Son of Man sits upon the throne of His glory, shall also sit upon twelve thrones, judging the twelve tribes of Israel.

Luke 22:31
Simon, Simon, behold, Satan demanded to have you all, to sift you like wheat.
  Job 1:9–1:12 — thematic testing comparison: Both portray the adversary demanding a test of God's servant or servants. Luke names Satan's request to sift the disciples like wheat; Job's devil challenges Job's integrity and receives power over his possessions. The persons, imagery and permitted trials differ.
    Job 1:9 The devil answered before the Lord, “Does Job worship God for nothing?
    Job 1:10 Hast Thou not fenced in what is outside him and what is inside his house, and everything around all that belongs to him? Thou hast blessed the works of his hands and made his livestock numerous in the land.
    Job 1:11 But stretch out Thy hand and touch everything he has; surely he will curse Thee to Thy face.”
    Job 1:12 Then the Lord said to the devil, “Behold, everything he has I give into your hand, but do not touch him.” And the devil went out from the Lord.

Luke 22:32
But I prayed for you, that your faith might not fail; and you, once you have turned back, strengthen your brothers.”
  John 21:15–21:17 — promise-and-later-commission comparison: Jesus' prayer for Simon and command to strengthen brothers after turning back can be compared with His later commission to Peter to feed and shepherd His flock. John narrates love questions and pastoral commands, not this prayer or the same words. No additional account of the prayer's timing or result is invented.
    John 21:15 So when they had eaten breakfast, Jesus says to Simon Peter, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me more than these?” He says to Him, “Yes, Lord, Thou knowest that I love Thee.” He says to him, “Feed My lambs.”
    John 21:16 He says to him again a second time, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me?” He says to Him, “Yes, Lord, Thou knowest that I love Thee.” He says to him, “Shepherd My sheep.”
    John 21:17 He says to him the third time, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me?” Peter was grieved because He said to him the third time, “Do you love Me?” And he said to Him, “Lord, Thou knowest all things; Thou knowest that I love Thee.” Jesus says to him, “Feed My sheep.

Luke 22:33
He said to Him, “Lord, I am ready to go with Thee both to prison and to death.”
  Mark 14:31 — narrative parallel: Both give Peter's insistence on readiness to die with Jesus. Luke adds prison; Mark explicitly promises not to deny Him and says the others spoke similarly.
    Mark 14:31 He spoke all the more insistently, “Even if I must die with Thee, I will certainly not deny Thee.” They all said the same.

Luke 22:34
But He said, “I tell you, Peter, a rooster will not crow today until you deny three times that you know Me.”
  Mark 14:30 — prediction parallel: Both predict Peter's three denials before cockcrow. Luke says a rooster will not crow today until three denials; Mark specifies this night and two crows. No crow-count reconciliation is supplied.
    Mark 14:30 Jesus said to him, “Truly I tell you, today, this very night, before a rooster crows twice, you will deny Me three times.”

Luke 22:35
And He said to them, “When I sent you without purse and bag and sandals, did you lack anything?” They said, “Nothing.”
  Luke 10:4 — internal commission callback: Jesus explicitly recalls the earlier instruction to carry no purse, bag or sandals. Luke10 supplies those restrictions; it does not independently narrate the later answer that nothing was lacking.
    Luke 10:4 Carry no purse, no bag, no sandals, and greet no one along the road.

Luke 22:36
And He said to them, “But now let the one who has a purse take it, and likewise a bag; and let the one who does not have sell his garment and buy a sword.
  Luke 22:35 — internal contrast: The now instruction to take purse and bag contrasts with the immediately recalled earlier mission without them. The garment-for-sword instruction is additional, not a command found in the earlier unit or a universal weapons rule inferred from this link.
    Luke 22:35 And He said to them, “When I sent you without purse and bag and sandals, did you lack anything?” They said, “Nothing.”

Luke 22:37
For I tell you that this which is written must be accomplished in Me: ‘And He was counted with the lawless.’ For what concerns Me is reaching its completion.”
  Isaiah 53:12 — quotation and claimed fulfillment: Jesus cites being numbered with the lawless as what must be accomplished in Him. Isaiah supplies that phrase amid death and sin-bearing language; Luke's application is Jesus' declaration rather than an independent historical proof.
    Isaiah 53:12 Therefore he will inherit many, and will divide the spoils of the mighty; for his soul was delivered to death, and he was numbered among the lawless. And he bore the sins of many, and for their sins he was delivered up.

Luke 22:38
They said, “Lord, behold, here are two swords.” And He said to them, “It is enough.”
  John 18:10–18:11 — Passion narratives: swords and Jesus' response: Both Passion scenes explicitly place swords among Jesus' companions and record His response. Luke says they present two swords and He says it is enough; John narrates Peter using a sword and Jesus commanding it into its sheath. John neither establishes the total of two nor interprets enough, and the link imposes no general weapons policy or identity for the second sword holder.
    John 18:10 Then Simon Peter, having a sword, drew it and struck the high priest’s slave and cut off his right ear. The slave’s name was Malchus.
    John 18:11 So Jesus said to Peter, “Put the sword into the sheath. The cup that the Father has given Me—shall I not drink it?”

Luke 22:39
And He went out and traveled, as was His custom, to the Mount of Olives; and the disciples also followed Him.
  Mark 14:26 — narrative parallel: Both narrate departure to the Mount of Olives after the supper context. Mark mentions singing a hymn; Luke describes the customary journey and the disciples following.
    Mark 14:26 After singing a hymn, they went out to the Mount of Olives.

Luke 22:40
When He came to the place, He said to them, “Pray that you may not enter into testing.”
  Mark 14:38 — teaching parallel: Both command prayer against entering testing or temptation. Mark adds watchfulness and the willing-spirit/weak-flesh explanation; Luke gives the command on arrival before His withdrawal.
    Mark 14:38 Keep watch and pray, so that you may not enter into temptation. The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak.”

Luke 22:41
And He withdrew from them about a stone’s throw, knelt down, and prayed,
  Mark 14:35 — prayer-scene parallel: Both describe Jesus withdrawing a short distance to pray. Luke specifies a stone's throw and kneeling; Mark says a little forward and falling on the ground. The postures are not harmonized.
    Mark 14:35 Going forward a little, He fell on the ground and prayed that, if possible, the hour might pass from Him.

Luke 22:42
saying, “Father, if Thou art willing, remove this cup from Me. Nevertheless, let not My will but Thine be done.”
  Mark 14:36 — prayer parallel: Both address the Father, ask removal of the cup and subordinate Jesus' will to the Father's. Mark has Abba and all things possible; Luke has if Thou art willing.
    Mark 14:36 He said, “Abba, Father, all things are possible for Thee. Take this cup away from Me. Yet not what I will, but what Thou wilt.”

Luke 22:43
And an angel from heaven appeared to Him, strengthening Him.
  Matthew 4:11 — angelic-help comparison: Both describe angels ministering to Jesus in trial-related settings. Luke has one heavenly angel strengthening Him during passion prayer; Matthew has angels arriving after the devil leaves the wilderness test. These are distinct episodes, not independent attestation of this angel's appearance.
    Matthew 4:11 Then the devil left Him, and behold, angels came and ministered to Him.

Luke 22:44
And being in agony, He prayed more earnestly, and His sweat became like clots of blood falling to the ground.
  Hebrews 5:7 — thematic prayer comparison: Both portray intense petitions by Jesus facing death. Luke describes agony, intensified prayer and sweat like clots of blood; Hebrews describes loud crying and tears and being heard. Hebrews does not attest the sweat, identify this particular prayer or support a medical diagnosis.
    Hebrews 5:7 In the days of His flesh, He offered petitions and supplications, with loud crying and tears, to the One able to save Him out of death, and was heard because of His reverent fear.

Luke 22:45
Rising from prayer, He came to the disciples and found them sleeping from grief.
  Mark 14:37 — prayer-scene parallel: Both describe Jesus returning from prayer and finding disciples asleep. Luke attributes sleep to grief; Mark addresses Simon and asks about keeping watch for an hour.
    Mark 14:37 He came and found them sleeping, and said to Peter, “Simon, are you sleeping? Could you not keep watch for one hour?

Luke 22:46
And He said to them, “Why are you sleeping? Rise and pray, so that you may not enter into testing.”
  Mark 14:37–14:38 — teaching-scene parallel: Both join a question about sleeping with instructions to watch or pray against testing. Luke tells them to rise; Mark addresses Peter and adds willing spirit and weak flesh.
    Mark 14:37 He came and found them sleeping, and said to Peter, “Simon, are you sleeping? Could you not keep watch for one hour?
    Mark 14:38 Keep watch and pray, so that you may not enter into temptation. The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak.”

Luke 22:47
While He was still speaking, behold, a crowd came, and the one called Judas, one of the Twelve, was going before them. He drew near to Jesus to kiss Him.
  Mark 14:43–14:45 — arrest-scene parallel: Both describe Judas of the Twelve arriving with a crowd and using a kiss in the betrayal. Luke narrates his approach to kiss; Mark explicitly records the arranged sign, greeting and kiss. The narration is not silently expanded in Luke.
    Mark 14:43 Immediately, while He was still speaking, Judas, one of the Twelve, arrived, and with him a crowd with swords and clubs from the chief priests, the scribes, and the elders.
    Mark 14:44 The one handing Him over had given them an agreed sign, saying, “The One I kiss is He. Seize Him and lead Him away securely.”
    Mark 14:45 He came and immediately approached Him, saying, “Rabbi!” Then he kissed Him.

Luke 22:48
But Jesus said to him, “Judas, do you hand over the Son of Man with a kiss?”
  Matthew 26:48–26:49 — arrest-scene comparison: Both focus on a kiss used to betray Jesus. Luke gives Jesus' question naming Judas and the Son of Man; Matthew gives the prearranged sign and performed kiss. Jesus' distinctive question is not quoted as though present in Matthew.
    Matthew 26:48 The one handing Him over had given them a sign, saying, “The One I kiss is He; seize Him.”
    Matthew 26:49 Immediately he came to Jesus and said, “Greetings, Rabbi,” and kissed Him.

Luke 22:49
When those around Him saw what was about to happen, they said, “Lord, shall we strike with a sword?”
  Luke 9:54–9:55 — same-book request comparison: Both have followers ask Jesus about violent action against opponents. Earlier James and John propose consuming fire and are rebuked; here unnamed companions ask about a sword. The people, circumstances, weapons and response wording are not equated.
    Luke 9:54 Seeing this, the disciples James and John said, “Lord, dost Thou wish us to command fire to come down from heaven and consume them?”
    Luke 9:55 But He turned and rebuked them.

Luke 22:50
And one of them struck the high priest’s slave and took off his right ear.
  John 18:10 — arrest-scene parallel: Both describe a sword blow cutting off the high priest's slave's right ear. John identifies Peter and Malchus; Luke leaves both unnamed. Those names are not inserted into Luke's text.
    John 18:10 Then Simon Peter, having a sword, drew it and struck the high priest’s slave and cut off his right ear. The slave’s name was Malchus.

Luke 22:51
But Jesus answered, “Allow this much.” And He touched the ear and healed him.
  Mark 7:32–7:35 — restorative touch applied to an ear: Both narrate Jesus applying physical touch to an ear as part of restoration. Luke follows the slave's ear being cut off and reports its healing; Mark describes fingers placed in a deaf man's ears, tongue-touching and the ears opening. The causes, patients, settings and speech differ. Mark does not independently attest the arrest healing, specify a repair mechanism or interpret Allow this much.
    Mark 7:32 They brought Him a man who was deaf and had difficulty speaking, and begged Him to lay His hand on him.
    Mark 7:33 Taking him aside privately, away from the crowd, He put His fingers into his ears, spat, and touched his tongue.
    Mark 7:34 Looking up to heaven, He sighed and said to him, “Ephphatha,” that is, “Be opened.”
    Mark 7:35 Immediately his ears were opened, the bond of his tongue was loosened, and he spoke correctly.

Luke 22:52
And Jesus said to the chief priests and captains of the temple and elders who had come against Him, “Have you come out as against a robber, with swords and clubs?
  Mark 14:48 — arrest-scene parallel: Both challenge coming with swords and clubs as against a robber. Luke addresses chief priests, temple captains and elders present; Mark addresses the arresting party after identifying who sent it.
    Mark 14:48 Jesus answered them, “Have you come out with swords and clubs to arrest Me as though against a robber?

Luke 22:53
When I was with you daily in the temple, you did not stretch out your hands against Me. But this is your hour and the authority of darkness.”
  Mark 14:49 — arrest-scene parallel: Both say Jesus was with them teaching in the temple daily without being seized. Luke adds their hour and darkness's authority; Mark adds scriptural fulfillment. The closing explanations remain distinct.
    Mark 14:49 Day after day I was with you in the temple, teaching, and you did not seize Me. But this is so that the Scriptures may be fulfilled.”

Luke 22:54
They seized Him and led Him away and brought Him into the high priest’s house. But Peter was following at a distance.
  Mark 14:53–14:54 — arrest-scene parallel: Both describe taking Jesus to the high priest and Peter following at a distance. Mark also names the assembled authorities and Peter's entering the courtyard.
    Mark 14:53 They led Jesus away to the high priest, and all the chief priests, the elders, and the scribes assembled.
    Mark 14:54 Peter followed Him from a distance, as far as inside the high priest’s courtyard. He sat with the attendants and warmed himself at the firelight.

Luke 22:55
When they had kindled a fire in the middle of the courtyard and sat down together, Peter sat among them.
  Mark 14:54 — denial-scene parallel: Both place Peter seated with others at a courtyard fire. Luke narrates their kindling it; Mark describes attendants and his warming himself at the firelight.
    Mark 14:54 Peter followed Him from a distance, as far as inside the high priest’s courtyard. He sat with the attendants and warmed himself at the firelight.

Luke 22:56
A certain servant girl saw him sitting by the light and, looking intently at him, said, “This man also was with Him.”
  Mark 14:66–14:67 — denial-scene parallel: Both have a servant girl look at Peter by the fire and identify him as accompanying Jesus. Mark says Jesus the Nazarene; Luke describes her intent look and his being lit by the fire.
    Mark 14:66 While Peter was below in the courtyard, one of the high priest’s female slaves came.
    Mark 14:67 Seeing Peter warming himself, she looked at him and said, “You too were with Jesus the Nazarene.”

Luke 22:57
But he denied it, saying, “I do not know Him, woman.”
  Mark 14:68 — denial-scene parallel: Both narrate Peter's first denial of the servant girl's charge. Luke says he does not know Jesus; Mark says he neither knows nor understands what she says and records an exit and rooster crow.
    Mark 14:68 He denied it, saying, “I neither know nor understand what you are saying.” He went outside into the forecourt, and a rooster crowed.

Luke 22:58
After a short time another saw him and said, “You too are one of them.” But Peter said, “Man, I am not.”
  Mark 14:69–14:70 — denial-scene comparison: Both narrate another identification and denial. Luke names another person addressed as Man; Mark describes the girl speaking again to bystanders and Peter denying. The speakers and delivery are not harmonized.
    Mark 14:69 The female slave saw him and again began saying to those standing nearby, “This man is one of them.”
    Mark 14:70 Again he denied it. After a little while, those standing nearby again said to Peter, “Truly you are one of them, for you are a Galilean.”

Luke 22:59
After about one hour had passed, someone else insisted, saying, “Truly, this man also was with Him, for he too is a Galilean.”
  Mark 14:70 — denial-scene parallel: Both use Peter's Galilean identity in the renewed accusation. Luke specifies about an hour and one insistent speaker; Mark says after a little while and names bystanders.
    Mark 14:70 Again he denied it. After a little while, those standing nearby again said to Peter, “Truly you are one of them, for you are a Galilean.”

Luke 22:60
But Peter said, “Man, I do not know what you are saying.” And at once, while he was still speaking, a rooster crowed.
  Mark 14:71–14:72 — denial-scene comparison: Both narrate Peter's further denial followed by cockcrow. Mark includes curses and swearing and calls this a second crow; Luke gives a different denial and an immediate crow while he is speaking.
    Mark 14:71 He began invoking a curse and swearing, “I do not know this Man you are speaking of.”
    Mark 14:72 Immediately a rooster crowed a second time. Peter remembered the word Jesus had spoken to him: “Before a rooster crows twice, you will deny Me three times.” He broke down and wept.

Luke 22:61
And the Lord turned and looked at Peter. And Peter remembered the Lord’s word, how He had said to him, “Before a rooster crows today, you will deny Me three times.”
  Mark 14:72 — denial-scene parallel: Both have Peter remember Jesus' three-denial prediction at cockcrow. Luke adds the Lord turning and looking at him and says before a rooster crows today; Mark retains twice. No unrecorded gaze or harmonized count is added.
    Mark 14:72 Immediately a rooster crowed a second time. Peter remembered the word Jesus had spoken to him: “Before a rooster crows twice, you will deny Me three times.” He broke down and wept.

Luke 22:62
And he went outside and wept bitterly.
  Matthew 26:75 — denial-scene parallel: Both conclude Peter's recollection with going outside and weeping bitterly.
    Matthew 26:75 Peter remembered Jesus’ word: “Before a rooster crows, you will deny Me three times.” He went outside and wept bitterly.

Luke 22:63
Now the men who were holding Him mocked Him and beat Him.
  Mark 14:65 — custody-abuse comparison: Both describe men holding Jesus subjecting Him to blows and mockery. Mark adds spitting, covering His face and a prophecy challenge; Luke's sequence places these abuses after Peter's denials.
    Mark 14:65 Some began spitting on Him, covering His face, striking Him with their fists, and saying to Him, “Prophesy!” The attendants received Him with slaps.

Luke 22:64
They blindfolded Him and questioned Him, saying, “Prophesy! Who is it that struck Thee?”
  Matthew 26:67–26:68 — custody-abuse parallel: Both describe blows followed by a demand to prophesy who struck Him. Luke explicitly describes blindfolding; Matthew's cited units describe spitting and striking without that blindfold detail.
    Matthew 26:67 Then they spat into His face and struck Him with their fists; others slapped Him,
    Matthew 26:68 saying, “Prophesy to us, Christ! Who is the one who struck Thee?”

Luke 22:66
When day came, the council of elders of the people gathered, both chief priests and scribes, and they led Him into their council,
  Mark 15:1 — council-scene comparison: Both describe a dawn gathering of chief priests, scribes and elders. Luke proceeds to council interrogation; Mark narrates consultation followed by delivery to Pilate after its earlier questioning. The proceedings are not made one detailed chronology.
    Mark 15:1 Immediately in the morning, the chief priests held a consultation with the elders, the scribes, and the whole council. They bound Jesus, led Him away, and handed Him over to Pilate.

Luke 22:67
saying, “If Thou art the Christ, tell us.” But He said to them, “If I tell you, you will certainly not believe.
  Mark 14:61 — council-question comparison: Both question whether Jesus is the Christ. Mark's high priest adds Son of the Blessed One; Luke's council requests an answer and Jesus warns they will not believe. The questions and replies are not made verbatim.
    Mark 14:61 He remained silent and answered nothing. Again the high priest questioned Him and said to Him, “Art Thou the Christ, the Son of the Blessed One?”

Luke 22:68
And if I ask, you will certainly not answer.
  Luke 20:3–20:7 — internal authority-questioning and noncommittal response: Luke20 provides a specific earlier scene of Jesus questioning religious authorities who decline the requested choice by saying they do not know. Luke22 conditionally says that if He asks, they will not answer. Their earlier reply is professed ignorance, not literal silence; the controversies and questions differ, and no unrecorded trial question is supplied.
    Luke 20:3 He answered them, “I too will ask you a question; tell Me:
    Luke 20:4 was John’s baptism from heaven or from people?”
    Luke 20:5 And they reasoned among themselves, saying, “If we say, ‘From heaven,’ He will say, ‘Why did you not believe him?’
    Luke 20:6 But if we say, ‘From people,’ all the people will stone us, for they are convinced that John was a prophet.”
    Luke 20:7 So they answered that they did not know where it was from.

Luke 22:69
But from now on the Son of Man will be seated at the right hand of the power of God.”
  Mark 14:62 — teaching parallel: Both speak of the Son of Man seated at the Power's right hand. Mark also includes coming with heaven's clouds; Luke's unit does not repeat that clause.
    Mark 14:62 Jesus said, “I am. And you will see the Son of Man sitting at the right of the Power and coming with the clouds of heaven.”

Luke 22:70
They all said, “Then art Thou the Son of God?” And He said to them, “You say that I am.”
  Matthew 26:63–26:64 — council-question parallel: Both ask whether Jesus is God's Son and receive a you-say formulation. Matthew names the high priest's adjuration and a longer reply; Luke names the whole group's question and You say that I am.
    Matthew 26:63 But Jesus remained silent. The high priest said to Him, “I adjure Thee by the living God to tell us whether Thou art the Christ, the Son of God.”
    Matthew 26:64 Jesus said to him, “You have said it. Nevertheless I tell you, from now on you will see the Son of Man seated at the right of the Power and coming upon the clouds of heaven.”

Luke 22:71
They said, “What further need do we have of testimony? For we ourselves have heard from His own mouth.”
  Mark 14:63–14:64 — council-scene parallel: Both conclude that further testimony is unnecessary because they themselves heard Jesus. Mark labels the speech blasphemy and records torn clothing and a death verdict; Luke's cited conclusion does not repeat those actions.
    Mark 14:63 The high priest tore his tunics and said, “What further need have we of witnesses?
    Mark 14:64 You heard the blasphemy. What is your judgment?” They all condemned Him as deserving death.

Luke 23:1
Then their whole assembly rose and led Him before Pilate.
  Mark 15:1 — trial-scene parallel: Both describe the council group delivering Jesus to Pilate. Mark specifies morning, consultation and binding; Luke describes the whole assembly rising and leading Him.
    Mark 15:1 Immediately in the morning, the chief priests held a consultation with the elders, the scribes, and the whole council. They bound Jesus, led Him away, and handed Him over to Pilate.

Luke 23:2
And they began to accuse Him, saying, “We found this man turning our nation astray and forbidding tribute to be given to Caesar, and saying that He Himself is Christ, a king.”
  Luke 20:22–20:25 — internal accusation-and-teaching contrast: The accusation that Jesus forbade Caesar's tribute can be checked against His earlier instruction to give Caesar what is Caesar's. This compares the accusation with the narrative's recorded teaching rather than endorsing the charge or making every political allegation identical.
    Luke 20:22 Is it lawful for us to give tribute to Caesar, or not?”
    Luke 20:23 But perceiving their cunning, He said to them,
    Luke 20:24 “Show Me a denarius. Whose image and inscription does it have?” They answered, “Caesar’s.”
    Luke 20:25 And He said to them, “Then give back to Caesar the things that are Caesar’s, and to God the things that are God’s.”

Luke 23:3
Pilate questioned Him, saying, “Art Thou the King of the Jews?” He answered him, “You say so.”
  Mark 15:2 — trial-scene parallel: Both give Pilate's King-of-the-Jews question and Jesus' You say so answer.
    Mark 15:2 Pilate questioned Him, “Art Thou the King of the Jews?” He answered him, “You say so.”

Luke 23:4
And Pilate said to the chief priests and the crowds, “I find no ground for a charge against this man.”
  John 18:38 — trial-scene parallel: Both have Pilate declare he finds no charge against Jesus. Luke names chief priests and crowds; John describes Pilate going out to the Jews after his truth question.
    John 18:38 Pilate says to Him, “What is truth?” And having said this, he went out again to the Jews, and says to them, “I find no charge against Him.

Luke 23:5
But they insisted, saying, “He stirs up the people, teaching throughout all Judea, beginning from Galilee even to this place.”
  Acts 10:37–10:38 — ministry-geography comparison: Both trace Jesus' activity through Judea from a Galilean beginning. Luke reports an accusation of stirring people through teaching; Acts gives a positive account of anointed healing and doing good. The accusation is not accepted as the narrator's moral judgment.
    Acts 10:37 You know what took place throughout all Judea, beginning from Galilee after the baptism John proclaimed:
    Acts 10:38 Jesus of Nazareth—how God anointed Him with the Holy Spirit and power. He went about doing good and healing all who were oppressed by the devil, because God was with Him.

Luke 23:6
When Pilate heard this, he asked whether the man was a Galilean.
  Luke 3:1 — internal jurisdictional background: The earlier dating notice explicitly assigns Pilate to Judea and Herod to Galilee. It provides jurisdictional background for Pilate's Galilean inquiry and the following referral to Herod. It does not independently narrate the inquiry, date the trial to Tiberius' fifteenth year or corroborate every other ruler in the list.
    Luke 3:1 In the fifteenth year of Tiberius Caesar’s rule, when Pontius Pilate governed Judea, Herod was tetrarch of Galilee, his brother Philip tetrarch of the region of Iturea and Trachonitis, and Lysanias tetrarch of Abilene,

Luke 23:7
And learning that He belonged to Herod’s jurisdiction, he sent Him to Herod, who was himself also in Jerusalem in those days.
  Acts 4:27 — passion-retrospect comparison: Both connect Herod and Pilate with Jesus' treatment in Jerusalem. Luke narrates a jurisdictional referral to Herod; Acts names both among those gathered against Jesus in this city. Acts does not independently attest the referral's procedure or Herod's exact jurisdiction.
    Acts 4:27 For truly, in this city, both Herod and Pontius Pilate, with the Gentiles and the peoples of Israel, gathered against Thy holy Servant Jesus, whom Thou didst anoint,

Luke 23:8
When Herod saw Jesus, he rejoiced greatly, for he had long wanted to see Him because he had heard about Him, and he hoped to see some sign performed by Him.
  Luke 9:9 — internal narrative callback: Herod's long desire to see Jesus recalls the earlier notice that he sought to see Him after hearing reports. The later scene adds rejoicing and hope for a sign; the earlier verse does not narrate this appearance.
    Luke 9:9 Herod said, “John I beheaded. But who is this about whom I hear such things?” And he sought to see Him.

Luke 23:9
He questioned Him at length, but He gave him no answer.
  Isaiah 53:7 — allusion: Jesus' silence under Herod's questioning can be compared with the afflicted figure who does not open his mouth. Isaiah uses sheep and lamb imagery and does not name Herod or this hearing; no verbatim quotation or identical setting is claimed.
    Isaiah 53:7 And because he has been afflicted, he does not open his mouth. Like a sheep he was led to slaughter, and as a lamb is silent before its shearer, so he does not open his mouth.

Luke 23:10
And the chief priests and scribes stood there, accusing Him vehemently.
  Mark 15:3 — trial-scene comparison: Both describe chief priests accusing Jesus. Luke adds scribes, vehemence and the Herod hearing; Mark places many accusations before Pilate. The locations and stages of the proceedings remain distinct.
    Mark 15:3 The chief priests accused Him of many things.

Luke 23:11
Herod also, with his troops, treated Him with contempt and mocked Him. He clothed Him in splendid clothing and sent Him back to Pilate.
  Mark 15:16–15:20 — mock-royalty comparison: Both describe troops mocking Jesus and dressing Him in special clothing. Luke names Herod's troops, splendid clothing and return to Pilate; Mark names the governor's cohort and purple clothing before crucifixion. The groups, garments and stages are not identified.
    Mark 15:16 The soldiers led Him inside the courtyard—that is, the praetorium—and called together the whole cohort.
    Mark 15:17 They clothed Him in purple and, after weaving a crown of thorns, put it on Him.
    Mark 15:18 They began saluting Him, “Hail, King of the Jews!”
    Mark 15:19 They kept striking His head with a reed and spitting on Him, and knelt down to bow before Him.
    Mark 15:20 After they had mocked Him, they stripped off the purple and put His own garments on Him. They led Him out to crucify Him.

Luke 23:12
Herod and Pilate became friends with one another on that very day, for previously they had been at enmity with each other.
  Acts 4:27 — Herod and Pilate in the Jesus Passion narratives: Both passages name the Herod–Pilate pair in relation to Jesus' Passion. Luke states that they became friends after enmity; Acts names them together among those gathered against Jesus. The actor-pair connection is explicit, while Acts does not establish the beginning of friendship or the preceding feud. It is not offered as independent evidence for that change in relationship.
    Acts 4:27 For truly, in this city, both Herod and Pontius Pilate, with the Gentiles and the peoples of Israel, gathered against Thy holy Servant Jesus, whom Thou didst anoint,

Luke 23:14
and said to them, “You brought this man to me as one turning the people astray. And behold, I examined Him before you and found no ground in this man for the accusations you bring against Him.
  John 19:4 — trial-scene parallel: Both have Pilate publicly affirm that he finds no charge against Jesus. Luke explicitly reports examination and the people's agitation accusation after the Herod return; John introduces Jesus' presentation outside. Their sequences are not combined.
    John 19:4 And Pilate went outside again and says to them, “See, I am bringing Him outside to you so that you may know that I find no charge against Him.”

Luke 23:15
Nor did Herod, for he sent Him back to us. And behold, nothing deserving death has been done by Him.
  Acts 13:28 — passion-retrospect comparison: Both state that no death-worthy cause was found for Jesus. Luke reports Pilate's interpretation of Herod's return; Acts describes Jerusalem's leaders asking Pilate for death despite finding no cause. Acts does not independently report Herod's assessment.
    Acts 13:28 Although they found no cause for death, they asked Pilate to have Him killed.

Luke 23:16
Therefore I will punish Him and release Him.”
  John 19:1 — trial-punishment comparison: Luke records Pilate proposing punishment followed by release; John narrates Pilate actually having Jesus scourged. This compares punishment in the same passion context without rewriting Luke's general punish word as a detailed scourging report or claiming release occurred.
    John 19:1 Then Pilate took Jesus and had Him scourged.

Luke 23:18
But they cried out all together, saying, “Take this man away, and release Barabbas to us!”
  Mark 15:11 — trial-scene parallel: Both describe demand for Barabbas's release instead of Jesus. Mark explicitly names the priests stirring the crowd; Luke quotes the combined cry to take Jesus away.
    Mark 15:11 But the chief priests stirred up the crowd to have him release Barabbas for them instead.

Luke 23:19
He had been thrown into prison for a certain uprising that took place in the city and for murder.
  Mark 15:7 — trial-scene parallel: Both associate Barabbas's imprisonment with an uprising and murder. Mark also names the other rebels; Luke specifies the city uprising.
    Mark 15:7 The man called Barabbas was imprisoned with the rebels who had committed murder in the uprising.

Luke 23:20
Pilate addressed them again, wanting to release Jesus.
  John 19:12 — trial-scene parallel: Both explicitly describe Pilate seeking to release Jesus. Luke narrates another address; John adds the political pressure concerning friendship with Caesar.
    John 19:12 From this Pilate sought to release Him; but the Jews cried out, saying, “If you release this Man, you are not Caesar’s friend. Everyone who makes himself a king speaks against Caesar.”

Luke 23:21
But they kept shouting, saying, “Crucify, crucify Him!”
  Mark 15:13 — trial-scene parallel: Both quote the demand Crucify Him. Luke repeats Crucify and describes continued shouting; Mark describes another cry.
    Mark 15:13 They cried out again, “Crucify Him!”

Luke 23:22
And for the third time he said to them, “Why, what evil has this man done? I found no ground for death in Him. Therefore I will punish Him and release Him.”
  Mark 15:14 — trial-scene parallel: Both have Pilate ask what evil Jesus has done. Luke specifies a third appeal, no death-worthy charge and proposed punishment/release; Mark moves to louder crucifixion demands.
    Mark 15:14 Pilate said to them, “Why, what evil has He done?” They cried out all the more, “Crucify Him!”

Luke 23:23
But they pressed him with loud voices, demanding that He be crucified, and their voices prevailed.
  Mark 15:14–15:15 — trial-scene parallel: Both describe intensified crucifixion demands overcoming Pilate's resistance. Luke says their voices prevailed; Mark says Pilate wanted to satisfy the crowd and narrates his action.
    Mark 15:14 Pilate said to them, “Why, what evil has He done?” They cried out all the more, “Crucify Him!”
    Mark 15:15 Pilate, wanting to satisfy the crowd, released Barabbas for them. After having Jesus scourged, he handed Him over to be crucified.

Luke 23:24
And Pilate decided that their demand should be granted.
  Mark 15:15 — trial-scene parallel: Both describe Pilate yielding to the crowd's desired outcome. Mark additionally names Barabbas's release, scourging and handing Jesus over.
    Mark 15:15 Pilate, wanting to satisfy the crowd, released Barabbas for them. After having Jesus scourged, he handed Him over to be crucified.

Luke 23:25
He released the man who had been thrown into prison for uprising and murder, whom they were demanding; but he handed Jesus over to their will.
  Mark 15:15 — trial-scene parallel: Both contrast releasing Barabbas with handing Jesus over. Luke recalls the prisoner's uprising and murder and says their will; Mark adds scourging and the explicit crucifixion purpose.
    Mark 15:15 Pilate, wanting to satisfy the crowd, released Barabbas for them. After having Jesus scourged, he handed Him over to be crucified.

Luke 23:26
As they led Him away, they seized a certain Simon of Cyrene coming from the countryside, and laid the cross on him to carry behind Jesus.
  Mark 15:21 — passion-scene parallel: Both name Simon of Cyrene arriving from the countryside and being compelled to carry the cross. Luke specifies behind Jesus; Mark names Alexander and Rufus as his sons.
    Mark 15:21 They compelled a passerby, Simon of Cyrene, coming from the country, the father of Alexander and Rufus, to carry His cross.

Luke 23:27
And a great multitude of the people followed Him, and women who beat themselves in mourning and lamented Him.
  Zechariah 12:10–12:11 — bounded prophetic imagery: lament in Jerusalem: Both depict lament in a Jerusalem setting. Luke's women mourn Jesus in the procession; Zechariah addresses David's house and Jerusalem's residents and pictures grief as for a beloved one or firstborn. This is a bounded prophetic-image comparison, not identification of every person or proof that the entire prophecy is fulfilled here. The actual Greek-based danced-in-mockery and pomegranate-grove wording is kept, without importing pierced or another edition's place name.
    Zechariah 12:10 I will pour upon David’s house and Jerusalem’s residents a spirit of grace and compassion. They will look toward Me because they danced in mockery, and mourn over him as over a beloved one; they will suffer grief as over a firstborn.
    Zechariah 12:11 On that day mourning in Jerusalem will grow great, like mourning over a pomegranate grove cut down in a plain,

Luke 23:28
But Jesus turned toward them and said, “Daughters of Jerusalem, do not weep over Me, but weep over yourselves and over your children.
  Jeremiah 22:10 — thematic lament comparison: Both redirect weeping away from one object toward another facing loss. Jeremiah contrasts the dead with the person going into exile; Jesus tells Jerusalem's daughters to weep for themselves and their children rather than Him. The persons and predicted losses are distinct.
    Jeremiah 22:10 Do not weep for the dead or lament him; weep bitterly for the one going out, because he will never return or see his homeland.

Luke 23:29
For behold, days are coming in which they will say, ‘Blessed are the barren, and the wombs that did not bear, and the breasts that did not nourish.’
  Luke 21:23 — internal judgment comparison: Both warn of coming distress especially affecting pregnancy and nursing. Luke21 pronounces woe on such mothers; Luke23 envisages people calling never-bearing wombs and never-nursing breasts blessed. These opposite formulations express related danger, not identical quoted words.
    Luke 21:23 Woe to those who are pregnant and those nursing infants in those days! For there will be great distress upon the land and wrath against this people.

Luke 23:30
Then they will begin to say to the mountains, ‘Fall upon us,’ and to the hills, ‘Cover us.’
  Hosea 10:8 — quotation allusion: Both picture people asking mountains and hills for covering or a fall upon them. Luke assigns Fall to mountains and Cover to hills; Hosea assigns Cover to mountains and Fall to hills. The actual reversed pairings are preserved.
    Hosea 10:8 The shrines of On, Israel’s sins, will be removed; thorns and thistles will grow on their altars. They will say to the mountains, ‘Cover us!’ and to the hills, ‘Fall upon us!’

Luke 23:31
For if they do these things with the green wood, what will happen with the dry?”
  Ezekiel 21:3 — thematic judgment imagery: Both use green and dry wood in judgment imagery. Ezekiel predicts a fire consuming both kinds; Jesus asks what will happen to dry wood if such things are done with green. The comparison does not assign historical identities to each wood merely from the image.
    Ezekiel 21:3 and say to the Nageb forest, ‘Hear the Lord’s word! Thus says the Lord, the Lord: Behold, I am kindling a fire within you. It will devour every green tree and every dry tree in you. The kindled flame will not be quenched, and every face from east to north will be burned by it.

Luke 23:32
Two others also, criminals, were being led with Him to be put to death.
  Mark 15:27 — passion-scene parallel: Both associate two condemned offenders with Jesus' execution. Luke describes their being led to die; Mark narrates crucifixion and calls them robbers. The stages and labels remain distinct.
    Mark 15:27 They crucified two robbers with Him, one at His right and one at His left.

Luke 23:33
When they came to the place called Skull, there they crucified Him and the criminals, one on the right and the other on the left.
  Mark 15:22–15:27 — passion-scene parallel: Both locate the crucifixion at Skull/Golgotha and place two offenders at Jesus' right and left. Mark's contiguous narrative also adds wine, garments, an inscription and the third hour; those extra details are not inserted into Luke's unit.
    Mark 15:22 They brought Him to the place Golgotha, which translated means Place of a Skull.
    Mark 15:23 They offered Him wine mixed with myrrh, but He did not take it.
    Mark 15:24 They crucified Him and divided His garments, casting a lot over them to determine who would take what.
    Mark 15:25 It was the third hour when they crucified Him.
    Mark 15:26 The inscription of the charge against Him was written: “The King of the Jews.”
    Mark 15:27 They crucified two robbers with Him, one at His right and one at His left.

Luke 23:34
But Jesus said, “Father, forgive them, for they do not know what they are doing.” And dividing His garments, they cast lots.
  Acts 7:59–7:60 — thematic prayer parallel: Both dying sufferers petition forgiveness for their killers. Jesus addresses Father and names their ignorance; Stephen addresses the Lord in his prayer after naming Lord Jesus and asks that the sin not be held against them. The speakers, addressees and exact requests are distinct.
    Acts 7:59 They stoned Stephen as he called out, saying, “Lord Jesus, receive my spirit.”
    Acts 7:60 Kneeling down, he cried with a loud voice, “Lord, do not hold this sin against them.” After saying this, he fell asleep.
  Psalms 21:19 — allusion: Luke's dividing Jesus' garments and casting lots corresponds to the psalm's garments divided and a lot cast for clothing. This concerns the unit's second clause, not independent attestation of the preceding forgiveness prayer.
    Psalms 21:19 They divided my garments among themselves, and cast a lot for my clothing.

Luke 23:35
The people stood watching. The rulers also sneered, saying, “He saved others; let Him save Himself, if this is the Christ of God, the chosen One.”
  Mark 15:31–15:32 — passion-scene parallel: Both have authorities mock Jesus with saving others but not Himself and appeal to His Christ or king identity. Luke names God's Christ, the chosen One; Mark names Christ, King of Israel and a demand to come down so they may believe.
    Mark 15:31 Likewise the chief priests, mocking Him among themselves with the scribes, said, “He saved others; He cannot save Himself.
    Mark 15:32 Let the Christ, the King of Israel, come down now from the cross, so that we may see and believe.” Those crucified with Him also reproached Him.

Luke 23:36
The soldiers also mocked Him, coming up and offering Him sour wine
  John 19:29–19:30 — passion-scene comparison: Both describe sour wine offered at the cross. Luke explicitly names mocking soldiers; John describes a sponge on hyssop and Jesus receiving it before His final words. The actors, implements and narrative placement are not merged.
    John 19:29 A vessel full of sour wine was standing there. So, putting a sponge full of the sour wine on hyssop, they brought it to His mouth.
    John 19:30 When therefore Jesus had received the sour wine, He said, “It is finished.” And bowing His head, He gave up His spirit.

Luke 23:37
and saying, “If Thou art the King of the Jews, save Thyself.”
  Mark 15:32 — mockery comparison: Both taunt Jesus' kingship with a challenge to rescue Himself. Luke gives soldiers' King-of-the-Jews wording; Mark gives authorities' Christ/King-of-Israel and come-down challenge. The titles and speakers remain distinct.
    Mark 15:32 Let the Christ, the King of Israel, come down now from the cross, so that we may see and believe.” Those crucified with Him also reproached Him.

Luke 23:38
There was also an inscription above Him: “This is the King of the Jews.”
  Mark 15:26 — passion-scene parallel: Both give a written King-of-the-Jews inscription. Luke includes This is and says above Him; Mark calls it the charge's inscription.
    Mark 15:26 The inscription of the charge against Him was written: “The King of the Jews.”

Luke 23:39
One of the criminals who were hanging there blasphemed Him, saying, “Art Thou not the Christ? Save Thyself and us!”
  Mark 15:32 — passion-scene comparison: Both describe people crucified with Jesus reproaching Him. Luke singles out one criminal and quotes a Christ/save-us challenge; Mark uses the plural. No unrecorded sequence of both mocking and then one changing sides is supplied.
    Mark 15:32 Let the Christ, the King of Israel, come down now from the cross, so that we may see and believe.” Those crucified with Him also reproached Him.

Luke 23:40
But the other answered, rebuking him, “Do you not even fear God, since you are under the same judgment?
  Luke 12:4–12:5 — fear of divine authority beside human killing: Both passages bring fear of divine authority into a setting involving human power to kill. The criminal invokes fear of God while sharing the human sentence; Jesus' earlier teaching distinguishes bodily killing from the One with authority over Gehenna. The shared God-fear theme does not turn the criminal's same judgment into an explicit statement of both men's final destiny or reproduce his specific rebuke elsewhere.
    Luke 12:4 I tell you, My friends, do not fear those who kill the body and after that have nothing more they can do.
    Luke 12:5 But I will show you whom to fear: fear the One who, after killing, has authority to cast into Gehenna. Yes, I tell you, fear Him.

Luke 23:41
And we justly so, for we receive what our deeds deserve. But this man did nothing out of place.”
  1 Peter 2:21–2:22 — thematic innocence parallel: Both assert Jesus' innocence in a suffering context. The criminal contrasts his own deserved sentence with Jesus doing nothing wrong; Peter says Christ committed no sin or deceit. Peter does not independently attest this criminal's confession.
    1 Peter 2:21 For to this you were called, because Christ also suffered for you, leaving you an example, so that you might follow in His footsteps.
    1 Peter 2:22 He committed no sin, nor was deceit found in His mouth.

Luke 23:42
And he said, “Jesus, remember me when Thou comest into Thy kingdom.”
  Psalms 105:4 — thematic petition parallel: Both petition remembrance in connection with saving favor. The criminal asks Jesus to remember me in His kingdom; the psalm asks the Lord to remember us with His people's favor and visit with salvation. Singular/plural wording, addressees and circumstances remain distinct.
    Psalms 105:4 Remember us, Lord, with the favor of Thy people; visit us with Thy salvation,

Luke 23:43
And He said to him, “Amen, I tell you: today you will be with Me in Paradise.”
  2 Corinthians 5:6–5:8 — thematic presence-with-the-Lord parallel: Jesus promises the dying criminal that today he will be with Him in Paradise; Paul prefers being away from the body and at home with the Lord. Both concern desired communion with the Lord beyond present bodily circumstances; Paul's words do not reproduce Paradise or today's specific promise.
    2 Corinthians 5:6 Therefore we are always confident, knowing that while at home in the body we are away from the Lord—
    2 Corinthians 5:7 for we walk by faith, not by sight—
    2 Corinthians 5:8 we are confident, and prefer rather to be away from the body and at home with the Lord.

Luke 23:44
And it was already about the sixth hour, and darkness came over the whole land until the ninth hour,
  Mark 15:33 — passion-scene parallel: Both describe darkness across the land from the sixth to the ninth hour. Luke says about the sixth; Mark says when it came.
    Mark 15:33 When the sixth hour came, darkness came over the whole land until the ninth hour.

Luke 23:45
as the sun’s light failed. And the curtain of the sanctuary was split down the middle.
  Mark 15:38 — passion-scene comparison: Both describe the sanctuary curtain splitting. Luke places it with failing sunlight before the narrated death and says down the middle; Mark places it after death and says in two from top to bottom. The sequence and direction wording are preserved.
    Mark 15:38 The curtain of the sanctuary was torn in two from top to bottom.

Luke 23:46
And Jesus cried out with a loud voice and said, “Father, into Thy hands I entrust My spirit.” Having said this, He breathed His last.
  Psalms 30:6 — quotation: Jesus' entrusting His spirit into the Father's hands corresponds to the psalm's entrusting one's spirit into the Lord's hands. Luke adds Father and narrates His death; the psalm continues with redemption and God of truth.
    Psalms 30:6 Into Thy hands I will entrust my spirit; Thou didst redeem me, Lord, God of truth.

Luke 23:47
When the centurion saw what had happened, he glorified God, saying, “Truly, this man was righteous.”
  Mark 15:39 — passion-scene comparison: Both give a centurion's favorable declaration after witnessing Jesus' death. Luke says righteous and glorifying God; Mark says God's Son. The confessions are not substituted for one another.
    Mark 15:39 When the centurion standing opposite Him saw that He breathed His last in this way, he said, “Truly this Man was God’s Son.”

Luke 23:48
And all the crowds who had gathered for this spectacle, seeing what had happened, returned beating their breasts.
  Luke 18:13 — same-book gesture comparison: Both describe beating one's breast. The tax collector couples it with a mercy prayer; the crucifixion crowds return after the spectacle. A shared gesture does not establish identical motives or a conversion by every crowd member.
    Luke 18:13 But the tax collector, standing far away, would not even lift his eyes to heaven, but beat his breast, saying, ‘God, be merciful to me, the sinner.’

Luke 23:49
All His acquaintances stood at a distance, and the women who had followed Him from Galilee, watching these things.
  Mark 15:40–15:41 — passion-scene parallel: Both describe women who accompanied Jesus from Galilee watching at a distance. Luke also names all His acquaintances without listing the women; Mark lists several women and describes service.
    Mark 15:40 There were also women watching from a distance, among them Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James the Little and Joses, and Salome.
    Mark 15:41 When He was in Galilee, they had followed Him and served Him; there were also many other women who had come up with Him to Jerusalem.

Luke 23:50
And behold, there was a man named Joseph, a council member, a good and righteous man.
  Mark 15:43 — burial-scene parallel: Both identify Joseph as a council member. Luke calls him good and righteous; Mark calls him respected and adds Arimathea, kingdom expectation and the body request. The epithets are not treated as identical words.
    Mark 15:43 Joseph of Arimathea, a respected council member who himself was also awaiting the kingdom of God, came and dared to enter before Pilate and ask for Jesus’ body.

Luke 23:51
He had not consented to their decision and action. He was from Arimathea, a city of the Jews, and was awaiting the kingdom of God.
  Mark 15:43 — burial-scene parallel: Both identify Joseph with Arimathea and expectation of God's kingdom. Luke additionally states his dissent from the decision and action; Mark does not independently give that dissent in this unit.
    Mark 15:43 Joseph of Arimathea, a respected council member who himself was also awaiting the kingdom of God, came and dared to enter before Pilate and ask for Jesus’ body.

Luke 23:52
This man approached Pilate and asked for the body of Jesus.
  Mark 15:43 — burial-scene parallel: Both describe Joseph approaching Pilate and asking for Jesus' body. Mark adds the courage involved; Luke keeps the request concise.
    Mark 15:43 Joseph of Arimathea, a respected council member who himself was also awaiting the kingdom of God, came and dared to enter before Pilate and ask for Jesus’ body.

Luke 23:53
Taking it down, he wrapped it in linen and laid Him in a rock-cut tomb where no one had yet been laid.
  Mark 15:46 — burial-scene parallel: Both narrate taking Jesus down, wrapping Him in linen and laying Him in a rock-cut tomb. Luke additionally says it had never been used; Mark narrates purchase of linen and rolling a stone without that unused-tomb detail.
    Mark 15:46 Joseph bought a linen cloth, took Him down, wrapped Him in the cloth, and laid Him in a tomb cut out of rock. He rolled a stone against the entrance of the tomb.

Luke 23:54
It was the day of Preparation, and the Sabbath was beginning.
  Mark 15:42 — burial-scene parallel: Both identify Preparation immediately before the Sabbath. Mark says evening had already come and explains the term; Luke says the Sabbath was beginning.
    Mark 15:42 Evening had already come, and since it was Preparation—that is, the day before the Sabbath—

Luke 23:55
The women who had come with Him from Galilee followed and saw the tomb and how His body was laid.
  Mark 15:47 — burial-scene parallel: Both describe women observing where Jesus was laid. Mark names Mary Magdalene and Mary mother of Joses; Luke identifies the women by their Galilean accompaniment and additionally describes seeing how His body was laid.
    Mark 15:47 Mary Magdalene and Mary the mother of Joses watched where He was laid.

Luke 23:56
Then they returned and prepared spices and perfumed ointments. And on the Sabbath they rested according to the commandment.
  Exodus 20:8–20:10 — law and observance: The women's Sabbath rest according to the commandment corresponds to the command to sanctify the seventh day and do no work. Exodus does not independently narrate their spice preparation or identify these women.
    Exodus 20:8 Remember the Sabbath day, to keep it holy.
    Exodus 20:9 For six days you shall labour and do all your works;
    Exodus 20:10 but the seventh day is a Sabbath to the Lord your God. You shall do no work on it: you, and your son, and your daughter, your male slave, and your female slave, your ox, and your beast of burden, and all your livestock, and the resident foreigner who dwells with you.

Luke 24:1
But on the first day of the week, at early dawn, they came to the tomb, carrying the spices they had prepared.
  Mark 16:1–16:2 — resurrection-scene comparison: Both describe women coming with the purpose of anointing on the week's first day. Luke says early dawn with prepared spices; Mark describes buying spices after the Sabbath and arrival after sunrise. Their preparation and light notices are not merged.
    Mark 16:1 When the Sabbath had passed, Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James, and Salome bought spices so that they might come and anoint Him.
    Mark 16:2 Very early on the first day of the week, they came to the tomb when the sun had risen.

Luke 24:2
They found the stone rolled away from the tomb,
  Mark 16:4 — resurrection-scene parallel: Both describe finding the tomb stone already rolled away. Mark additionally calls it very large; neither unit narrates the rolling action itself.
    Mark 16:4 Looking up, they saw that the stone had been rolled away, for it was very large.

Luke 24:3
but when they entered, they did not find the body of the Lord Jesus.
  Mark 16:5–16:6 — resurrection-scene comparison: Both narrate entry into the tomb and Jesus' absence. Mark describes one young man seated on the right and his declaration He is not here; Luke states the body's absence before describing two men. Numbers and sequence remain distinct.
    Mark 16:5 They entered the tomb and saw a young man sitting on the right, clothed in a white robe, and they were greatly alarmed.
    Mark 16:6 He said to them, “Do not be alarmed. You seek Jesus the Nazarene, who was crucified. He has been raised; He is not here. Look, the place where they laid Him.

Luke 24:4
And it happened that, while they were perplexed about this, behold, two men stood beside them in flashing clothing.
  John 20:11–20:12 — resurrection-messenger comparison: Both describe two heavenly messengers at the empty tomb. Luke calls them men in flashing clothes standing beside the women; John calls them angels in white seated at the body's former head and feet as Mary looks in. The positions, observers and sequence are not harmonized.
    John 20:11 But Mary was standing outside beside the tomb, weeping. So, as she wept, she stooped to look into the tomb,
    John 20:12 and beholds two angels in white, sitting one at the head and one at the feet, where the body of Jesus had lain.

Luke 24:5
They became afraid and bowed their faces to the ground, and the men said to them, “Why do you seek the living One among the dead?
  Mark 16:5–16:6 — resurrection-message comparison: Both describe fear before the tomb messenger and a message redirecting the search for crucified Jesus to His resurrection. Luke adds bowing and the living-among-the-dead question; Mark adds reassurance against alarm and points to where He lay.
    Mark 16:5 They entered the tomb and saw a young man sitting on the right, clothed in a white robe, and they were greatly alarmed.
    Mark 16:6 He said to them, “Do not be alarmed. You seek Jesus the Nazarene, who was crucified. He has been raised; He is not here. Look, the place where they laid Him.

Luke 24:6
He is not here, but has been raised. Remember how He spoke to you while He was still in Galilee,
  Mark 16:6–16:7 — resurrection-message comparison: Both announce that Jesus has risen and is not there and refer to Galilee. Luke says remember words spoken while in Galilee; Mark promises a forthcoming meeting there. Remembering a past place is not replaced with travel instructions.
    Mark 16:6 He said to them, “Do not be alarmed. You seek Jesus the Nazarene, who was crucified. He has been raised; He is not here. Look, the place where they laid Him.
    Mark 16:7 But go, tell His disciples and Peter, ‘He is going ahead of you into Galilee. There you will see Him, just as He told you.’”

Luke 24:7
saying that the Son of Man must be handed over into the hands of sinful people and be crucified and rise on the third day.”
  Luke 9:22 — internal prediction callback: The resurrection reminder repeats the earlier necessity of the Son of Man's death and third-day rising. Luke24 adds sinful hands and crucifixion; Luke9 names rejection and killing. This is related remembered teaching, not a claim of a verbatim full quotation.
    Luke 9:22 saying, “The Son of Man must suffer many things, be rejected by the elders, chief priests and scribes, be killed, and on the third day be raised.”

Luke 24:8
And they remembered His words,
  John 2:22 — thematic remembrance parallel: Both connect the resurrection with disciples remembering Jesus' earlier words. Luke's women recall the passion prediction; John's disciples recall the temple saying and believe Scripture and His word. The sayings and groups are distinct.
    John 2:22 When, therefore, He was raised from the dead, His disciples remembered that He had said this, and they believed the Scripture and the word Jesus had spoken.

Luke 24:9
and returned from the tomb and reported all these things to the Eleven and to all the rest.
  Matthew 28:8 — resurrection-report parallel: Both describe women leaving the tomb and reporting to the disciples. Luke names the Eleven and the rest; Matthew names fear, great joy and running.
    Matthew 28:8 They departed quickly from the tomb with fear and great joy and ran to report to His disciples.

Luke 24:10
It was Mary Magdalene and Joanna and Mary of James and the other women with them who told these things to the apostles.
  Mark 16:1 — resurrection-women comparison: Both lists name Mary Magdalene and a Mary associated with James. Luke adds Joanna and others and says Mary of James; Mark adds Salome and explicitly calls that Mary James's mother while describing spice purchase. The lists, actions and relationship wording are preserved.
    Mark 16:1 When the Sabbath had passed, Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James, and Salome bought spices so that they might come and anoint Him.

Luke 24:11
But these words seemed to them like nonsense, and they did not believe the women.
  Mark 16:10–16:11 — resurrection-report comparison: Both describe disbelief when a woman or women report Jesus alive. Luke's group reports the tomb message; Mark's Mary reports seeing Him. The Mark target is within the double-bracketed longer ending and does not supply identical witnesses or an identical report.
    Mark 16:10 She went and reported to those who had been with Him, as they mourned and wept.
    Mark 16:11 When they heard that He was alive and had been seen by her, they disbelieved.

Luke 24:12
Yet Peter rose and ran to the tomb. Stooping to look in, he saw the linen cloths alone, and went away, wondering to himself at what had happened.
  John 20:3–20:7 — resurrection-inspection comparison: Both describe Peter running to the tomb and seeing linen cloths. John also names another disciple arriving first and stooping, then Peter entering and inspecting the face cloth; Luke describes Peter stooping and leaving in wonder. The actions and accompanying people are not merged.
    John 20:3 Then Peter and the other disciple went out, and they were coming to the tomb.
    John 20:4 The two were running together, and the other disciple ran ahead faster than Peter and came to the tomb first;
    John 20:5 and, stooping to look in, he sees the linen cloths lying there; yet he did not enter.
    John 20:6 Then Simon Peter also comes, following him, and entered the tomb; and he beholds the linen cloths lying there,
    John 20:7 and the face cloth that had been on His head, not lying with the linen cloths, but wrapped up separately in one place.

Luke 24:13
And behold, two of them were traveling that same day to a village named Emmaus, sixty stadia away from Jerusalem.
  Mark 16:12 — resurrection-road comparison: Both describe two followers traveling in a countryside setting after the resurrection. Luke names Emmaus, the day and sixty stadia; Mark names none of those details and says Jesus appeared in another form. Mark's target belongs to the double-bracketed longer ending.
    Mark 16:12 After these things, He appeared in another form to two of them as they were walking, going into the country.

Luke 24:14
They were talking with one another about all these things that had happened.
  Luke 24:19–24:24 — internal expansion of a reported event-topic: The narrator says the two travelers discuss the recent happenings; their later report to Jesus expressly recounts His ministry, condemnation, crucifixion, third-day hopes and the women's tomb report. This is an internal expansion of the event-topic under discussion. It does not claim that the earlier unquoted exchange contained these exact later words or supply an independent witness.
    Luke 24:19 And He said to them, “What things?” They said to Him, “The things concerning Jesus the Nazarene, who was a prophet mighty in deed and word before God and all the people,
    Luke 24:20 and how our chief priests and rulers handed Him over to a judgment of death and crucified Him.
    Luke 24:21 But we were hoping that He was the One about to redeem Israel. Yet indeed, besides all these things, this is the third day since these things happened.
    Luke 24:22 Moreover, some women from among us astonished us. They went to the tomb early
    Luke 24:23 and did not find His body, and came saying that they had also seen a vision of angels who say that He is alive.
    Luke 24:24 And some of those with us went to the tomb and found it just as the women had said, but Him they did not see.”

Luke 24:15
And it happened that, as they talked and discussed, Jesus Himself drew near and traveled with them.
  Mark 16:12 — resurrection-road comparison: Both describe Jesus appearing or drawing near to two traveling followers. Luke narrates His walking with them during their discussion; Mark says another form in its double-bracketed longer ending. No explanation of Luke's recognition restraint is imported from that phrase.
    Mark 16:12 After these things, He appeared in another form to two of them as they were walking, going into the country.

Luke 24:16
But their eyes were held back from recognizing Him.
  John 20:14–20:15 — resurrection-recognition comparison: Both describe people seeing the risen Jesus without recognizing Him. Luke says the travelers' eyes were held back; John says Mary supposed He was the gardener. The people and stated explanations remain distinct.
    John 20:14 Having said these things, she turned backward and beholds Jesus standing there, and did not know that it was Jesus.
    John 20:15 Jesus says to her, “Woman, why do you weep? Whom do you seek?” She, supposing that He is the gardener, says to Him, “Sir, if Thou didst carry Him away, tell me where Thou didst lay Him, and I will take Him away.”

Luke 24:17
And He said to them, “What are these words you are exchanging with one another as you walk?” And they stood still, downcast.
  John 20:14–20:16 — unrecognized risen Jesus questioning distressed followers: Both resurrection encounters have the still-unrecognized Jesus question distressed followers. Luke's two travelers stop downcast when He asks about their words; John's Mary weeps, mistakes Him for the gardener and hears questions before He names her. Road and tomb settings, questions, participants and recognition sequences remain distinct.
    John 20:14 Having said these things, she turned backward and beholds Jesus standing there, and did not know that it was Jesus.
    John 20:15 Jesus says to her, “Woman, why do you weep? Whom do you seek?” She, supposing that He is the gardener, says to Him, “Sir, if Thou didst carry Him away, tell me where Thou didst lay Him, and I will take Him away.”
    John 20:16 Jesus says to her, “Mary.” Turning, she says to Him in Hebrew, “Rabbouni!”—which means Teacher.

Luke 24:18
One named Cleopas answered Him, “Art Thou the only visitor in Jerusalem who does not know the things that have happened there in these days?”
  Acts 26:22–26:26 — thematic parallel: Cleopas expects a Jerusalem visitor to know the recent events concerning Jesus; Paul later appeals to the king's knowledge of the proclaimed events and says they were not done in a corner. Both speakers appeal to their public character, in different encounters. The comparison does not establish that every individual knew them or identify Cleopas with Clopas.
    Acts 26:22 Having therefore received help from God, I stand to this day, testifying to small and great, saying nothing beyond what the prophets and Moses said would happen:
    Acts 26:23 that the Christ would suffer and, as the first to rise from the dead, would proclaim light both to the people and to the Gentiles.”
    Acts 26:24 As he was making this defense, Festus said with a loud voice, “You are mad, Paul! Your great learning is turning you to madness.”
    Acts 26:25 Paul said, “I am not mad, most excellent Festus, but I speak words of truth and sound judgment.
    Acts 26:26 For the king knows about these things, and I speak freely to him. I am persuaded that none of these things has escaped his notice, for this has not been done in a corner.

Luke 24:19
And He said to them, “What things?” They said to Him, “The things concerning Jesus the Nazarene, who was a prophet mighty in deed and word before God and all the people,
  Acts 7:22 — thematic prophetic-profile comparison: Jesus is described as a prophet mighty in deed and word; Acts describes Moses as powerful in words and deeds. This compares the stated profile, not the people's identities or an explicit quotation. Luke's before-God-and-people phrase is not attributed to Acts.
    Acts 7:22 Moses was educated in all the wisdom of the Egyptians and was powerful in his words and deeds.

Luke 24:20
and how our chief priests and rulers handed Him over to a judgment of death and crucified Him.
  Acts 2:23 — passion-retrospect parallel: Both recount Jesus being handed over and killed. Luke's travelers name chief priests and rulers condemning and crucifying; Peter's speech names determined divine purpose and lawless hands. Those agent descriptions and explanatory emphases remain distinct.
    Acts 2:23 This man, handed over according to God’s determined purpose and foreknowledge, you nailed up and killed through the hands of lawless men.

Luke 24:21
But we were hoping that He was the One about to redeem Israel. Yet indeed, besides all these things, this is the third day since these things happened.
  Luke 2:38 — same-book redemption-hope link: Both connect Jesus with hopes for Israel or Jerusalem's redemption. Anna speaks of the Child to those awaiting Jerusalem's redemption; the travelers describe their disappointed hope and the third day. The stages of understanding and locations of expectation are not equated.
    Luke 2:38 Coming up at that very hour, she gave thanks to God and spoke about Him to everyone awaiting Jerusalem’s redemption.

Luke 24:22
Moreover, some women from among us astonished us. They went to the tomb early
  Luke 24:1 — internal report-and-event link: The travelers' report of women going early to the tomb corresponds to the earlier dawn visit with prepared spices. This identifies the event being recounted, not a second independent confirmation of their astonishment.
    Luke 24:1 But on the first day of the week, at early dawn, they came to the tomb, carrying the spices they had prepared.

Luke 24:23
and did not find His body, and came saying that they had also seen a vision of angels who say that He is alive.
  Luke 24:3–24:7 — internal report-and-event link: The travelers recap the body's absence and messengers' resurrection announcement. The original narration calls the figures men in flashing clothes, while their report calls it a vision of angels. The later designation is not silently substituted into the earlier text.
    Luke 24:3 but when they entered, they did not find the body of the Lord Jesus.
    Luke 24:4 And it happened that, while they were perplexed about this, behold, two men stood beside them in flashing clothing.
    Luke 24:5 They became afraid and bowed their faces to the ground, and the men said to them, “Why do you seek the living One among the dead?
    Luke 24:6 He is not here, but has been raised. Remember how He spoke to you while He was still in Galilee,
    Luke 24:7 saying that the Son of Man must be handed over into the hands of sinful people and be crucified and rise on the third day.”

Luke 24:24
And some of those with us went to the tomb and found it just as the women had said, but Him they did not see.”
  John 20:3–20:8 — resurrection-inspection comparison: Both describe followers inspecting the tomb after the women's news. John names Peter and another disciple seeing cloths; Luke's speakers summarize plural visitors confirming the tomb report without seeing Jesus. This does not identify every visitor or narrator, or transfer John's other disciple's belief into Luke's statement.
    John 20:3 Then Peter and the other disciple went out, and they were coming to the tomb.
    John 20:4 The two were running together, and the other disciple ran ahead faster than Peter and came to the tomb first;
    John 20:5 and, stooping to look in, he sees the linen cloths lying there; yet he did not enter.
    John 20:6 Then Simon Peter also comes, following him, and entered the tomb; and he beholds the linen cloths lying there,
    John 20:7 and the face cloth that had been on His head, not lying with the linen cloths, but wrapped up separately in one place.
