    John 20:8 Then the other disciple who had come to the tomb first also entered, and he saw and believed;

Luke 24:25
And He said to them, “O foolish ones, and slow in heart to believe all that the prophets spoke!
  Mark 16:14 — resurrection-reproof comparison: Both have risen Jesus rebuke unbelief. Luke addresses slowness to believe the prophets; Mark addresses the Eleven's disbelief of eyewitnesses in its double-bracketed longer ending. Objects of belief and encounter settings differ.
    Mark 16:14 Later He appeared to the Eleven themselves as they reclined at table, and reproached their unbelief and hardness of heart because they had not believed those who had seen Him raised.

Luke 24:26
Was it not necessary for the Christ to suffer these things and enter into His glory?”
  1 Peter 1:10–1:11 — thematic exposition parallel: Both connect Christ's sufferings with subsequent glory in a prophetic context. Peter describes prophets investigating the Spirit's testimony; Jesus explains the necessity of suffering and entering glory. The speakers and form of exposition differ.
    1 Peter 1:10 Concerning this salvation, the prophets who prophesied about the grace for you searched and investigated,
    1 Peter 1:11 investigating what time or what kind of time the Spirit of Christ within them was indicating, as He testified beforehand to the sufferings destined for Christ and the glories after them.

Luke 24:27
And beginning from Moses and from all the prophets, He interpreted to them in all the Scriptures the things concerning Himself.
  John 5:39–5:40 — scriptural-witness comparison: Both identify the Scriptures as concerning or bearing witness to Jesus. Luke describes His exposition beginning with Moses and prophets; John records His challenge to searchers unwilling to come to Him. John does not independently narrate the road lesson.
    John 5:39 You search the Scriptures because you think that in them you have eternal life; and it is they that bear witness concerning Me.
    John 5:40 Yet you are unwilling to come to Me so that you may have life.

Luke 24:28
They drew near the village to which they were traveling, and He acted as though He would travel farther.
  Mark 6:48–6:51 — bounded movement-past-companions comparison: Both scenes narrate an apparent or intended movement onward by Jesus before He stays with companions. Luke says He acts as though going farther; Mark says He intends to pass by the rowing disciples before speaking and entering their boat. Apparent continuation and stated intention are different, as are road and sea. No shared hidden motive, feigned intention in Mark or identical occasion is inferred.
    Mark 6:48 Seeing them straining at the rowing, for the wind was against them, He came toward them about the fourth watch of the night, walking on the sea. He intended to pass by them.
    Mark 6:49 When they saw Him walking on the sea, they thought it was a phantom and cried out,
    Mark 6:50 for they all saw Him and were troubled. Immediately He spoke with them and said to them, “Take courage! It is I; do not fear.”
    Mark 6:51 He climbed into the boat with them, and the wind ceased. They were utterly astounded within themselves,

Luke 24:29
But they urged Him strongly, saying, “Stay with us, because it is toward evening and the day has already waned.” And He went in to stay with them.
  Genesis 19:2–19:3 — thematic hospitality comparison: Both describe urging visitors to enter a home and stay, followed by a meal. Lot urges two visitors after their proposed stay in the square; Luke's two travelers urge one unrecognized Jesus as evening falls. The hosts, guests and circumstances are not identified or made proof of literary dependence.
    Genesis 19:2 and said, “Behold, my lords, turn aside into your servant’s house, and lodge there, and wash your feet; and you shall rise early and go on your way.” And they said, “No; but we will lodge in the square.”
    Genesis 19:3 And he urged them greatly; and they turned aside to him, and entered into his house. And he made them a feast with drink, and baked unleavened bread for them; and they ate.

Luke 24:30
And it happened that as He reclined at table with them, He took the bread, blessed it, broke it, and gave it to them.
  Luke 22:19 — internal meal-action echo: Both narrate taking bread, blessing or thanking, breaking and giving it. Luke22 additionally interprets the body and commands remembrance; Luke24 links the Emmaus meal to recognition without repeating institution words. The meals are distinct.
    Luke 22:19 And He took bread, and having given thanks, He broke it and gave it to them, saying, “This is My body, which is given for you; do this in remembrance of Me.”

Luke 24:31
Then their eyes were opened and they recognized Him, and He vanished from their sight.
  Luke 24:16 — internal recognition contrast: The travelers' opened eyes and recognition reverse their earlier held-back eyes and failure to recognize Him. The link tracks the story's explicit progression; the earlier unit does not independently attest His vanishing.
    Luke 24:16 But their eyes were held back from recognizing Him.

Luke 24:32
And they said to one another, “Was not our heart burning within us as He spoke to us on the road, as He opened the Scriptures to us?”
  Luke 24:27 — internal report-and-event link: Their report of opened Scriptures recalls Jesus' actual exposition of Moses and the prophets on the road. The earlier narrator does not independently state their burning-heart response; that response belongs to this later recollection.
    Luke 24:27 And beginning from Moses and from all the prophets, He interpreted to them in all the Scriptures the things concerning Himself.

Luke 24:33
They rose that very hour and returned to Jerusalem, and found the Eleven and those with them gathered together,
  Mark 16:13 — resurrection-return comparison: Both describe the two travelers returning to the others. Luke names Jerusalem, the Eleven and an assembled company; Mark's double-bracketed longer ending describes their report and unbelieving response. Luke's next announcement that Jesus appeared to Simon is not rewritten to match that response.
    Mark 16:13 They went away and reported to the rest; they did not believe them either.

Luke 24:34
saying, “The Lord has truly been raised and has appeared to Simon!”
  1 Corinthians 15:5 — appearance-tradition parallel: Both preserve an appearance to Simon or Cephas; John1:42 explicitly links Simon, Cephas and Peter as contextual identity evidence. Corinthians then narrates an appearance to the twelve; Luke's gathered group, called the Eleven, reports the appearance to Simon. Their group titles and narrative sequence are not made identical.
    1 Corinthians 15:5 and that He appeared to Cephas, then to the twelve.

Luke 24:35
And they related the things that happened on the road, and how He was made known to them in the breaking of the bread.
  Luke 24:30–24:31 — internal report-and-event link: The travelers' account of recognition in bread-breaking directly recaps the bread actions and opened eyes just narrated. This is the episode's own summary, not an independent witness to another meal.
    Luke 24:30 And it happened that as He reclined at table with them, He took the bread, blessed it, broke it, and gave it to them.
    Luke 24:31 Then their eyes were opened and they recognized Him, and He vanished from their sight.

Luke 24:36
While they were speaking these things, He Himself stood among them and said to them, “Peace be with you.”
  John 20:19 — resurrection-appearance parallel: Both describe Jesus standing among gathered disciples and greeting them with peace on the first-day evening context. John adds shut doors and fear of the Jews; Luke introduces the appearance while the travelers are reporting.
    John 20:19 Then, when it was evening on that day, the first day of the week, and the doors were shut where the disciples were, because of fear of the Jews, Jesus came and stood in the midst, and says to them, “Peace to you.”

Luke 24:37
But they were startled and became afraid, thinking they were seeing a spirit.
  Matthew 14:26 — thematic fear comparison: Both describe disciples fearing they are seeing a spirit or apparition when Jesus appears. Matthew concerns walking on the sea before the passion; Luke concerns a risen-Jesus appearance. Different occasions and the actual spirit/apparition terms are preserved.
    Matthew 14:26 When the disciples saw Him walking upon the sea, they were troubled and said, “It is an apparition!” They cried out in fear.

Luke 24:38
And He said to them, “Why are you troubled, and why do doubts arise in your heart?
  John 20:27 — resurrection-doubt comparison: Both have Jesus address doubt alongside bodily evidence. Luke questions the assembled group's troubled thoughts; John later commands Thomas to become believing and offers hands and side. Neither Thomas's presence at this meeting nor one identical occasion is inferred.
    John 20:27 Then He says to Thomas, “Bring your finger here and see My hands, and bring your hand and put it into My side; and do not become unbelieving, but believing.”

Luke 24:39
See My hands and My feet, that it is I Myself. Feel Me and see, because a spirit does not have flesh and bones as you see that I have.”
  John 20:27 — resurrection-body comparison: Both invite inspection of Jesus' hands and bodily contact. Luke names hands, feet, flesh and bones in contrast to a spirit; John invites Thomas's finger and hand to hands and side. Side is not inserted into Luke, nor feet and the spirit statement into John.
    John 20:27 Then He says to Thomas, “Bring your finger here and see My hands, and bring your hand and put it into My side; and do not become unbelieving, but believing.”

Luke 24:40
And having said this, He showed them His hands and feet.
  John 20:20 — resurrection-body parallel: Both describe Jesus showing His hands. Luke additionally names feet; John additionally names side and the disciples' joy. The bodily details remain as supplied.
    John 20:20 And having said this, He showed them His hands and His side. Then the disciples rejoiced when they saw the Lord.

Luke 24:41
While they still did not believe for joy and were marveling, He said to them, “Do you have anything to eat here?”
  John 21:5 — resurrection-food comparison: Both have risen Jesus ask a food-related question. Luke asks whether food is available while disciples marvel; John asks fishermen whether they have fish and receives No. Their purposes, locations and responses are not equated.
    John 21:5 Then Jesus says to them, “Children, you do not have any fish to eat, do you?” They answered Him, “No.”

Luke 24:42
They gave Him a portion of broiled fish.
  John 21:9 — resurrection-meal comparison: Both resurrection-meal settings include fish. Luke's group gives Jesus a broiled portion; John's disciples find fish on a charcoal fire with bread. John does not independently narrate giving this portion to Jesus.
    John 21:9 So when they got out onto the land, they see a charcoal fire laid there, and fish lying on it, and bread.

Luke 24:43
And He took it and ate before them.
  Acts 10:40–10:41 — resurrection-meal testimony link: Luke narrates Jesus eating before the disciples; Acts describes chosen witnesses who ate and drank with Him after His raising. Acts does not specify this particular fish meal or supply a drink to Luke's scene.
    Acts 10:40 God raised Him on the third day and granted that He should appear,
    Acts 10:41 not to all the people, but to witnesses chosen beforehand by God—to us, who ate and drank with Him after He rose from the dead.

Luke 24:44
And He said to them, “These are My words that I spoke to you while I was still with you: everything written about Me in the Law of Moses and the Prophets and Psalms must be fulfilled.”
  Acts 28:23 — scriptural-exposition comparison: Both connect Jesus' significance with Moses' law and the prophets. Luke has Jesus declare fulfillment and also names Psalms; Acts has Paul explain and persuade concerning Jesus from law and prophets. Psalms and the exact fulfillment declaration are not inserted into Acts.
    Acts 28:23 They appointed a day with him and came to his lodging in greater numbers. From morning until evening he explained to them, bearing solemn witness to the kingdom of God and seeking to persuade them concerning Jesus from both the law of Moses and the prophets.

Luke 24:45
Then He opened their mind to understand the Scriptures
  Acts 16:14 — thematic understanding comparison: Both describe the Lord opening an inner faculty to receive instruction. Jesus opens disciples' minds to Scripture; the Lord opens Lydia's heart to attend Paul's words. Recipients, mind/heart language and instruction settings remain distinct.
    Acts 16:14 A woman named Lydia, a seller of purple goods from the city of Thyatira, who worshiped God, was listening. The Lord opened her heart to attend to what Paul was saying.

Luke 24:46
and said to them, “Thus it is written that the Christ should suffer and rise from the dead on the third day,
  1 Corinthians 15:3–15:4 — resurrection-teaching parallel: Both connect Christ's suffering or death and third-day rising with Scripture. Corinthians adds for our sins and burial; Luke introduces the statement with thus it is written.
    1 Corinthians 15:3 For I handed over to you as matters of first importance what I also received: that Christ died for our sins according to the Scriptures,
    1 Corinthians 15:4 and that He was buried, and that He has been raised on the third day according to the Scriptures,

Luke 24:47
and that repentance for the forgiveness of sins should be proclaimed in His name to all the nations—beginning from Jerusalem.
  Acts 2:38 — commission-and-proclamation link: Peter's Jerusalem proclamation of repentance and forgiveness in Jesus' name is a specific beginning corresponding to this commission. Acts also adds baptism and the Spirit's gift; one Jerusalem address is not treated as completing the mission to all nations.
    Acts 2:38 Peter said to them, “Repent, and let each one of you be baptized upon the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of your sins, and you will receive the gift of the Holy Spirit.

Luke 24:48
You are witnesses of these things.
  Acts 1:8 — commission parallel: Both appoint the followers as Jesus' witnesses. Acts adds Spirit-given power and the outward geography from Jerusalem; Luke refers to the preceding events and teaching as these things.
    Acts 1:8 But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem and [in] all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.”

Luke 24:49
And behold, I am sending out the promise of My Father upon you; but remain in the city until you are clothed with power from on high.”
  Acts 1:4–1:5 — promise parallel: Both command remaining in Jerusalem for the Father's promise. Luke describes clothing with power from above; Acts explicitly identifies coming Spirit baptism. That explanation is compared without rewriting Luke's actual wording.
    Acts 1:4 While gathered with them, He ordered them not to leave Jerusalem, but to wait for the Father’s promise, “which,” He said, “you heard from Me;
    Acts 1:5 for John baptized with water, but you will be baptized in the Holy Spirit not many days from now.”

Luke 24:50
And He led them out as far as toward Bethany, and lifted His hands and blessed them.
  Leviticus 9:22 — thematic blessing-posture comparison: Both describe lifting hands over people and blessing them. Aaron's blessing follows specified offerings; Jesus' blessing accompanies His departure toward Bethany. The figures, rites and settings are not equated.
    Leviticus 9:22 And Aaron lifted his hands over the people and blessed them. He came down after preparing the offering for sin, the whole burnt offerings, and the deliverance offerings.

Luke 24:51
And it happened that while He blessed them, He parted from them and was carried up into heaven.
  Acts 1:9 — ascension parallel: Both describe Jesus being lifted or carried up and departing from their sight. Luke places it during blessing; Acts adds the cloud and, in its wider context, forty days of appearances. No extra date is imposed on Luke's narration.
    Acts 1:9 After saying these things, He was lifted up as they watched, and a cloud took Him from their sight.

Luke 24:52
And they, having worshiped Him, returned to Jerusalem with great joy,
  Acts 1:12–1:14 — ascension-return comparison: Both describe the followers returning to Jerusalem after Jesus' ascent. Luke adds worship and great joy; Acts names Olivet, an upper room and the praying company. The distinct location notices are not replaced with one another.
    Acts 1:12 Then they returned to Jerusalem from the mountain called Olivet, which is near Jerusalem, a Sabbath’s journey away.
    Acts 1:13 When they entered, they went up to the upper room where they were staying: Peter and John and James and Andrew, Philip and Thomas, Bartholomew and Matthew, James son of Alphaeus, Simon the Zealot, and Judas son of James.
    Acts 1:14 All these devoted themselves with one accord to prayer, together with women, Mary the mother of Jesus, and His brothers.

Luke 24:53
and were continually in the temple, blessing God.
  Acts 2:46–2:47 — same-author worship-pattern comparison: Both summaries describe continuing temple worship and praise of God. Acts adds shared meals, favor and the growing gathering after Pentecost; Luke concludes with temple blessing after the ascent. The summaries are not declared one dated occasion.
    Acts 2:46 Day by day they devoted themselves with one accord to gathering in the temple, and breaking bread in their homes, they shared food with gladness and simplicity of heart,
    Acts 2:47 praising God and enjoying favor with all the people. And day by day the Lord added those who were being saved to their gathering.

Reviewed units still unfinished
Luke 1:21: The waiting congregation wonders at a sanctuary delay before Zechariah emerges mute. A generic delay or later vision report does not reproduce this particular congregation's initial experience.
Luke 1:23: Completion of the service days precedes Zechariah's return and Elizabeth's conception. The sequence does not give a computable calendar date or a second matching priestly journey.
Luke 2:44: The parents' mistaken company assumption and relatives search are specific. The subsequent return supplies narrative continuation but no independent match for that assumption or traveling group.
Luke 2:45: The unsuccessful search leads back to Jerusalem and then the temple finding. The later three-day notice is not used to reconstruct unreported daily travel or another matching return journey.
Luke 3:24: The actual Matthat–Levi–Melchi–Jannai–Joseph sequence is read between Heli and Mattathias. Names recurring in other positions do not establish a second matching ancestral chain.
Luke 3:25: Mattathias–Amos–Nahum–Esli–Naggai is the actual local sequence. Prophetic book names do not identify these ancestors as those authors or supply missing connecting generations.
Luke 3:26: Maath–Mattathias–Semein–Josech–Joda remains a consecutive pedigree segment. Nearby Joanan and Rhesa do not license merging it into an unrelated same-name genealogy.
Luke 3:28: The Melchi–Addi–Cosam–Elmadam–Er chain is retained in its position after Neri. No actual parallel succession has been established merely by common ancestral names.
Luke 3:29: The named Jesus precedes Eliezer, Jorim, Matthat and Levi in the ancestry. That position does not identify him as the Christ or connect him to a different same-name person.
Luke 3:30: Simeon–Judah–Joseph–Jonam–Eliakim precedes the sequence toward Nathan and David. Familiar patriarchal names in other generations are not adequate identity evidence.
Luke 22:65: The narrator reports additional blasphemous words without quoting them. The preceding prophesy taunt cannot be reclassified as the exact content of all the other sayings.
Luke 23:13: The summons names chief priests, rulers and people before Pilate's announced findings. Another trial scene does not independently reproduce this precise summons or establish every attendee's identity.
